# A FORERUNNER TO THE VITAL PHILOSOPHY OF THE PARACELSIANS

### Concerning the Authority of Ancient Pagan Literature, and the Ability to Explain the Causes of Human Affairs.

---

->1.<-

Since we see that in this final age of the world <note>A common 17th-century belief that the end of the world was approaching, leading to a surge in new intellectual movements.</note> not a few people have emerged who partly nitpick at the doctrines of the ancient pagans and partly condemn them entirely, desiring them to be exchanged for new opinions; and since these ancient doctrines are nevertheless still proposed to students; we must diligently consider the nature of this criticism or condemnation. We must ensure that we do not appear to be deceived ourselves—mistaking a lie for the truth—and that we are not leading our own students astray.

2. This dispute is concerned mostly with matters of Philosophy and medicine. For although there is also a contention regarding ancient Theology, those who know that God should be believed rather than men can have less doubt about this; thus, they can easily dismiss the arguments brought forward concerning non-authentic interpreters of the scriptures.

3. Many have set themselves in opposition to Aristotle, among whom are Bernardino Telesio, Jakob Martini the Scot, Petrus Ramus, and others; but none admire anyone more, or think anyone more worthy of being followed, than Paracelsus. These followers, much like that "False Monarch" <note>original: "Pseudomonarcha"</note> Paracelsus himself, desire all old studies concerning Philosophy and Galenic medicine <note>The traditional medical system based on the Greek physician Galen and the balance of the four humors.</note> to be erased. They pursue this in their writings: Johannes Hartmann in his works on <term>Vital Philosophy</term> <note>A school of thought that viewed nature as being permeated by a living, spiritual force rather than just dead matter.</note>, Oswald Croll, Henning Scheunemann in his little book on the plague (in which he prays that Galen’s long-winded rules be sent to hell), Joachim Tanck in his preface to the first collection of Alchemy, and others of that same faction. These have recently been followed by the "fame of the brotherhood of the Rosy Cross," <note>The Rosicrucians, a secret society of doctors and philosophers claiming ancient mystical knowledge.</note> which Adam Haselmeier has celebrated in his own judgment, while simultaneously launching a sharp attack on the disciplines of the ancients. So too did Croll, who did not fear to write on page 67 of his "Admonitory Preface" to the *Chemical Basilica* that every error has flowed from Pagan Philosophy, because it passed over the dual body of creatures—namely the visible elemental body and the invisible, unknown astral body—in silence. He claims that no one has yet reached Paracelsus's level of skill, let alone been able to surpass it.

4. First, you might marvel at what kind of reasoning this condemnation uses, since whatever these moderns have, they learned from the ancients. Even Croll, the critic himself, on pages 39 and 40, hammers home contemplations sought from antiquity. Those who call themselves "Chemical Physicians" <note>original: "Chymiatros"</note> and <term>Spagyrists</term> <note>Alchemists who specialized in separating and recombining plant and mineral extracts for medicine.</note> want to be recommended by no greater argument than that of antiquity, to such an extent that they even drag Aristotle, Plato, Democritus, and others into their Chemical Society. Following the craft of the Dane, Petrus Severinus, they transform the whole of Hippocrates, so that Galen and Hippocrates are no longer said to practice the same medical art; instead, Hippocrates practiced the "Hermetic Spagyric" art, while Galen practiced his own "false medicine" <note>original: "pseudoiatrian"</note>. They also have that well-worn saying on their lips: "Nothing can be said that has not been said before," and the sentiment of the Preacher: "There is nothing new under the sun." <note>A reference to Ecclesiastes 1:9.</note>

5. However, because they divide antiquity into two factions, they make one side out to be Magic, Kabbalistic, Hermetic, etc., originating from Chaldea and Egypt, and recently from Germany with Paracelsus as the author (from which it is called the "Theophrastan-Paracelsian" way, as if it were also ancient). The other side they make out to be Peripatetic <note>Followers of Aristotle.</note>, Galenic, Arabic, etc., so that they include Pliny, Dioscorides, Avicenna, Mesue, and whoever else Paracelsus rejects behind his back. Since they use several arguments, it will be worth the effort to see on what foundations they stand.

<summary>This page introduces the intellectual battle between traditional Aristotelian/Galenic science and the rising Paracelsian movement, which seeks to replace ancient pagan learning with a "Vital Philosophy" rooted in alchemy and spiritualism.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, Vital Philosophy, Galen, Aristotle, Rosicrucians, Oswald Croll, Alchemy, Spagyric medicine, Astral body, Hermeticism</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Forerunner to the Vital Philosophy," specifically focusing on the academic and theological arguments against the followers of Paracelsus (the Paracelsians). It transitions from the general disdain for ancient learning mentioned on the previous page to specific critiques by famous scholars like Thomas Erastus and Jacob Zwinger.</meta>

<header>A Forerunner to the Vital Philosophy</header>

6. About forty or fifty years ago, Thomas Erastus, a physician and philosopher from Heidelberg, wrote a professional critique against Paracelsus. He refuted almost all of his paradoxes and empty claims, calling his teachings absurd, impious, and dangerous. He gave credit only to those chemical processes involving distillation, which Paracelsus did not actually invent, though he did help make them famous. Johannes Crato von Krafftheim—a physician to three Emperors who can never be praised enough—calls those Paracelsian times "ages of darkness," in which certain people profess a "tartarean" medicine <note>Paracelsus used the term "Tartar" to describe stony or gravel-like deposits in the body, such as gout or kidney stones, comparing them to the crust found in wine barrels.</note> wrapped in new deceptions and empty words. He judges these to be "Thessalian manners" (named after a certain Thessalus who lived in the time of Nero and despised everyone who came before him). Pliny mentions in Book 29, Chapter 1, that in the age of Nero, people flocked to Thessalus, who wiped away all the established views of the elders and ranted with a kind of madness against the doctors of every age. The level of his prudence and character can be judged by a single fact: on his monument on the Appian Way, he inscribed himself as the <term>Conqueror of Doctors</term> <note>original: "LATRONICEN." This Greek-derived term was used by Thessalus to claim he had "overcome" or "hunted down" all other physicians, though critics like Erastus used it to imply he was a "robber" of the profession.</note>, by which he placed himself above all antiquity and the excellence of our own century (from the dedicatory epistle of the *Methodical Summary of Galen*, 1563). The same Crato, in a letter to Erastus in 1570, has much to say about the perverse cures of Paracelsus; he adds that the Emperor Ferdinand called Paracelsus a most lying and shameless impostor—a judgment confirmed by many highly learned men. Not long ago, in 1609, Jacob Zwinger, a very celebrated philosopher and medical professor at the Academy of Basel, wrote to Wilhelm Fabricius Hildanus that in his opinion, the philosophies of Paracelsus were either identical to those of the Greeks or worse. He argued they were sometimes the same but changed with an affected use of foreign words to make it seem like he was saying something new. Other times they were worse, obscuring the truth with false opinions and relying on irrelevant allegories, especially on the foundation of the **microcosm** <note>The "small world"—the idea that the human body is a miniature map of the entire universe (the macrocosm).</note>, as if everything in the "great world" were contained within the human body. If this foundation is destroyed, everything built upon it collapses. Let us be content with the judgment and critique of these three most distinguished men.

7. Oswald Croll and other Paracelsians certainly object to Erastus; but as is the custom with all Paracelsian factions, they do so by heaping a great number of insults, slanders, and accusations upon him. If they even wish to reason, they only bring forward things from their Magic, Kabbalah, Astrology, Chiromancy (palm reading), Chemistry, and other subjects they profess. They distinguish these into two general headings: the **Light of Nature** (derived from the "great" and "small" worlds) and the **Light of Grace** (derived from hidden revelation). They do not draw their conclusions from fixed principles or proven facts, but from opinion and imagination regarding "similarities" in the worldly sphere or "wheel." In this wheel, they say everything fits together so harmoniously that once the "center" is known, everything else lies open and can be understood. This is why they claim there is no need for any books by the ancients; Paracelsus himself wrote that he had not looked at a book for ten years.

8. But let us review their most important arguments, which they use as principles against the ancient books and explanations of philosophers and physicians. Their strongest argument, which they object with great arrogance, is this: "If a natural philosopher or physician must be educated from the 'book of nature and grace' (that is, by the light of nature and spiritual revelation), then it is useless to seek learning from the writings of Aristotle, Plato, Galen, Avicenna, Pliny, etc. The first part is true. Therefore, the second follows."

<margin>
Romans 1:19 and 20. <note>These Bible verses discuss how God’s invisible qualities are visible through His creation, a point the author uses to argue that the ancients were capable of learning truth through nature.</note>
</margin>
*Response.* This connection is denied because it is a logical fallacy. We argue the opposite: if the light of nature and grace is to be consulted, then the writings of the ancients must *not* be ignored. This is our reasoning: The ancients investigated as much as could possibly be found in physical and medical matters through the light of nature—so much so that even the Apostle Paul concedes that the Pagans knew God from the creation of the world. By what argument can these newcomers show that the ancients lacked the "light of nature," or that everyone must be <term>self-taught</term> <note>original: "αὐτοδίδακτον" (autodidaktos)</note>? Regarding the "light of grace," the Pagans certainly did not know as much as the Israelites and Christians; yet some streams of the ancients also flowed to them, traces of which appear in their writings. However, now that both lights are joined in Christianity, and Pagan philosophy has been thoroughly examined and illuminated by the principles of divine revelation in the Scriptures, the ancient writings should certainly not be rejected.

<margin>
Judgment on Plato: with a few changes, he could become a Christian. Numenius compared him to Moses; others to Philo, the wise Hebrew.
</margin>
9. If they still wish to press their <term>rejection of education</term> <note>original: "ἀποδιδαξίαν" (apodidaxian), literally a "non-teaching" or "un-learning."</note> and their knowledge from the light of nature and grace, we say that by the same argument, the commentaries of Paracelsus, Severinus, Hermes, Quercetanus, and their like should also be rejected. Furthermore, among so many conflicting opinions and diverse judgments, one must ask whose "light of nature and grace" is true and whose is merely an illusion. It is well known that the devil can disguise himself as an angel of light and present specters that look like the truth, as happens in the "similarities," "analogies," and "harmonies" of Paracelsus. Plato knew both the "barbarian" wisdom of the Egyptians and the Greek wisdom of Homer and the philosophers; nonetheless, he removed the knowledge of true things from the realm of physical interaction, just as the author of the Book of Wisdom does in Chapter 9, verse 15. Thirdly, the entire power of experience and the scientific foundation of the disciplines perishes when the knowledge of no man...

<meta>catchword: man</meta>

<summary>This page presents a scholarly defense of ancient learning (Aristotle, Galen, Plato) against the "new" medicine of Paracelsus. It cites historical critics like Thomas Erastus and argues that the "Light of Nature" claimed by Paracelsians was already understood and better articulated by the ancients and the Bible.</summary>
<keywords>Thomas Erastus, Paracelsus, Johannes Crato von Krafftheim, Jacob Zwinger, light of nature, light of grace, microcosm, macrocosm, Thessalus, medical philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of Paracelsian philosophy, moving from general historical comparisons to specific refutations of their "Light of Nature," their use of occult arts like Magic and Kabbalah, and their claims regarding alchemical principles.</meta>

# [Of the] Paracelsians
<sig>A 3</sig>

...human life and capacity can be so great that one person might know all things and confirm them by experience. **Fourthly**, if the **light of nature** <note>original: "lumen naturae"; the Paracelsian idea that nature contains a divine spark of knowledge accessible to the human mind</note> is sufficient for an argument, one must not assume that nature which is depraved in divine and human things by the Fall and the curse, and subjected to vanity; but rather that nature which the innocent Adam possessed, and which the Saints will have in the state of perfection. Nor should you seek a supplement for perfection in this age from the **light of grace** <note>original: "lumine gratiae"</note>. For in this life, that light is only partial, incipient, and imperfect, as the Apostle writes in 1 Corinthians 13 and as the matter itself demonstrates. Carnal affections so obscure the light of the mind that even an enlightened person wavers, let alone one left to himself. Those who attribute the light of grace to themselves explain it in a wondrous way, and stitch onto it I know not what "revelations." It remains, therefore, that we should join the science of the ancients with our own, and "test all things; hold fast to what is good." <note>A reference to 1 Thessalonians 5:21</note>

10. The second argument by which they confidently boast proceeds from the defenses of **Magic**, **Kabbalah**, and **Chiromancy** <note>Palm reading</note>: but it begs the question. For that Magic which Paracelsus, Croll, and their like profess is that impure and diabolical magic of Trithemius, Cornelius Agrippa, Artephius, Techellus, and the like. In this, there are mere deceptions, as their works and dogmas show. Read the **Archidoxis of Magic** <note>original: "archodoxin Magicam"</note> added to the works of Paracelsus; likewise on the **Renovation and Mysteries of Worms**, on **Adept Philosophy**, and those things regarding Magic found in the *Berthonii*, and in the books on **Sagacious Philosophy**, on the pestilence, and others. **Kabbalah** is a vain game, which almost always ends in impiety and monstrous opinions, as examples show in the Jewish "character-logic" <note>Symbolic interpretation of letters and numbers</note> regarding **Bereshit** <note>original: "Berefchith"; the first word of the Hebrew Bible, "In the beginning"</note>, regarding the **Merkavah** <note>original: "mercana" (likely Merkavah); chariot mysticism</note>, etc., where letters and numbers, and especially divine names and sentences, are wonderfully distorted. **Chiromancy** is entirely conjectural and relies on uncertain significations; a single mark can be as much a sign of something contrary as of something beneficial. If those arts were treated not superstitiously, but **Theologically** and **Physically**, they would bring us nothing new that must be sought from the Paracelsian "light of nature." For the same is found entirely among the ancient Theologians, Physicists, and Physicians, mostly disagreeing in all points with the opinions of Paracelsus.

11. In **Astrology** and **Chemistry**, they triumph; for they think these two—whether arts or sciences—were unknown to the ancient **Peripatetics** <note>Followers of Aristotle</note>, **Galenists**, and others whom they despise. However, they do not understand the Philosophical Astrology of the ancients, but that which Paracelsus, Severinus, Hartmann, Croll, Tanck, and others fashioned for themselves. Truly, if the Peripatetics and Galenists are to be rejected for this reason, then the **Hermetics** and **Hippocratics** must be as well. For before Isaac Holland and Basil Valentine the Monk, no one proposed three or four (for the Paracelsians do not yet agree on the number) **principles** or **stars**: **Salt**, **Sulfur**, and **Mercury**. Before Paracelsus and Severinus, no one applied these to all natural things, nor pronounced upon everything according to principles and elements based on a fictional harmony or analogy. None of them sought a **microcosmic Astrology**.

12. As for true **Astrology**, who does not know it to be Peripatetic? If those things are the "stars of the microcosm" which are drawn out from mixtures through **Chemistry**—which they call by the new name "spoken-out anatomy" <note>original: "anatomiam effatam"; likely referring to chemical analysis or separation</note>—they essentially exist also in Aristotle, Avicenna, Mesue, Galen, and all who described oils, liquors, and salts, or extracts and balsams. These men transferred essence and virtue through infusions, decoctions, expressions, combustions, the collection of salts, alterations, and other modes of separation. By what name do the Chemists seek to be commended more than by the name of antiquity? The only difference is that the recent age is more accurate and lucid in all things, although someone did not write badly concerning the **Philosopher’s Stone** when they said most people of our time cannot do what the ancients used to do; and the Paracelsians especially are prone to every imposture, so much so that they have even become a laughingstock among the common people.

13. Another argument of Croll’s seems to be that all error flowed from Gentile philosophy because it did not recognize the **double body** of creatures: one corporeal, mortal, and composed of elements—Physical and visible from the elements; the other astral, sidereal, and invisible from the firmament and the stars. For thus he speaks on page 35: *If the **Olympic Spirit** <note>The invisible, celestial part of man</note>, or the invisible man, had been observed by Aristotle and esteemed by Galen, such a notable error flowing from the Pagans, the masters of error, would not have been brought into Philosophy and Medicine, to say nothing of Theology, etc.* This is what he says. But Hermes and other ancients whom they drag into their Chemical society were not Gentiles <note>The author is likely being sarcastic here, as Hermes Trismegistus was a pagan figure</note>. Truly, this argument is the same as the preceding one. For by "astral body" nothing else is understood than the **chemical essence**, by which three or four species are separated from mixtures. By "elements," nothing else is meant than earth and water in which there is no secret virtue; and when they wish that virtue to be derived from the stars, we can grant it only in a certain respect: namely, insofar as God in creation so mixed the celestial and elemental nature that there resulted from it that **Aristotelian analogue** <note>original: "analogos illa Aristotelica"</note>, the **innate heat** of Hippocrates, and the **specific form** of Mesue.

14. That today in generations <note>Biological reproduction or the coming-to-be of things</note> they no longer fall from heaven except in a certain respect,

<summary>The author systematically rejects Paracelsian claims that their "new" sciences of Magic, Kabbalah, and Chemistry are superior to ancient learning, arguing instead that their "Three Principles" (Salt, Sulfur, Mercury) are recent inventions and that their "Astral Body" is merely a rebranding of ancient Aristotelian concepts.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsianism, Light of Nature, Magic, Kabbalah, Chiromancy, Astrology, Chemistry, Three Principles, Salt, Sulfur, Mercury, Oswald Croll, Peripatetics, Aristotle, Galen, Astral Body</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the systematic rebuttal of Paracelsianism. The author is refuting the claim that ancient "pagan" philosophy (Aristotle, Galen, etc.) should be abandoned in favor of the "Christian" or "Vital" philosophy of Paracelsus and his followers like Croll and Hartmann. The previous page ended with a critique of Paracelsian magic.</meta>

->### Forerunner of the Vital Philosophy<-

...they may slip away; the reason is that God gave each thing endowed with vegetative power its own nature, through which like procreates like. Then He diffused a spirit through all things for their sustenance. Moreover, the earth and water—the matrices of generation and nutrition—have held a blessing from of old, and from that, a nature analogous to heaven. Along with the fertile rains, some of the worldly spirit descends, which we also draw in by breathing. Herbs and minerals certainly had no need for the stars for their creation and nature. God indeed subjected the earth to a curse and vanity (Genesis 3:17, Romans 8:20) so that it cannot produce food suitable for human nature unless cultivated; but that the whole blessing was not removed is shown by the conservation of nature and the remaining power of propagation. Furthermore, that the sentence was mitigated by the intercession of the Son of God is evident because after the flood, God blessed humans again, and often gave abundant crops and a land flowing with milk and honey, promising this to the obedient in the first verses of Deuteronomy 28.

15. However, it is clear from the writings of the Arabs, Aristotle, Hippocrates, and others that those things later attributed to the stars regarding growing things were not entirely unknown to the **Ancient Physicists**. Pliny—whose name the Paracelsians <note>Followers of the Swiss physician and occultist Paracelsus</note> can barely stand—writes in book 18, chapter 29 about the **Pleiades** <note>original: "Virgiliis"; the cluster of stars often used to mark the seasons</note> that they specifically relate to fruits, as summer begins at their rising, etc. Likewise, he says of the **Milky Way**: "There is in the sky what is called the Milky Circle, easily visible. From its downflow, as if from some udder, all things become milky [or filled with life-giving sap]." And a little later: "The effects of the Eagle and the Dog Star pertain to all fruit-bearing lands. Therefore, during the days of these constellations, if the air is pure and mild and transmits that generative and milky juice to the earth, the crops grow joyfully." See the rest. Aristotle maintains that all power of the lower things is ruled by the higher, and therefore the world is a continuum. Regarding **Astrology**, how much the heavens and stars confer to the lowest things, the books of the pagans are full, and the Chaldeans, Egyptians, and Greeks were hardly more superstitious in any other part. Therefore, both the Hippocratics and Galenics <note>Followers of the ancient Greek physicians Hippocrates and Galen</note> have Astrological precepts regarding diseases, crises, and other matters. Nor should these things be called "unknown" just because the stars were not named [in a Paracelsian way] or distinguished by the "three principles" <note>The Paracelsian Tria Prima: Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury</note>. If they knew the thing itself, the power, and the efficient causes, they were not ignorant of that standard Astrology, simply speaking.

<margin>
2 Timothy 2.
Isaiah 47:12-13.
Jeremiah 10.
Hippocrates, *On Airs, Waters, and Places*, around the middle; at the beginning of the book *On Flesh*; in *On Diseases* book 4, regarding the heat and cold that cause sickness from the sky. At the beginning of the book *On Ancient Medicine*, he rejects the Astrology of Paracelsus as not being based on certain principles.
</margin>

16. Furthermore, **Crollius** <note>Oswald Croll, a Paracelsian alchemist</note> also argues thus: The intellectual soul of man, a divine light emanating from the breath of God and divine fountains, belongs to invisible Philosophy, whose foundation is Christ. Therefore, we ought to philosophize in a Christian way and not an "ethnic" [pagan] way in fleeting and temporary languages and arts, preferring the eternal to the mortal and transitory. From this, he thinks it follows that the writings of Aristotle, Pliny, Galen, and other pagans should be ejected from the schools, and the Paracelsian fictions—Magic, **Kabbalah**, Astrology, etc.—should be substituted according to the "attained and sagacious" (that is, diabolical) Philosophy, as they call it.

17. We respond first: even granting the premise (which we by no means grant), the conclusion does not follow. For it does not follow from that: If the human soul is metaphysical by its very nature of contemplation, it does not mean that nothing besides metaphysics should be taught in Philosophy. Philosophy's subject and matter are not only that, but whatever can be acquired by the study of wisdom. Secondly, the reasoning is equal: The visible external world, and man insofar as he is subject to the senses, belongs to the **Physics** of natural bodies and the virtues within bodies. Physics, moreover, is the first part of human Philosophy, clarified by the studies and books of many pagans, and even of Jews and Christians. Therefore, one must philosophize in the manner of pagans, Jews, and Christians, and at the same time, join eternal things with transitory things in the schools of the world.

<margin>
Acts 4:12.
1 Corinthians 3:11.
Hebrews 3:1 and 12:2.
</margin>

18. Thirdly: if we must philosophize in a Christian way according to the foundation of faith and its author, Christ, then we must not do so magically, kabbalistically, chemically, astrologically, chiromantically, etc.—and certainly not in the manner of Paracelsus, Croll, Hartmann, or Scheunemann, etc. Because in the books about Christ, the Prophets, and the Apostles, none of these things are found; indeed, most Paracelsian principles can be overturned by the principles of Scripture. On the contrary, the **Peripatetic** <note>The school of Aristotle</note> discipline (excepting those things that pertain to faith) fights least with the scriptures among all philosophies unknown outside the Church. Thus, if we are to philosophize with Christ, we must philosophize peripatetically—specifically, a Philosophy illuminated by the light of the Holy Spirit shining in the sacred texts. (For note: if our carnal reason is not spiritually illuminated and perfected by the truth, it is indeed reason, but not divine and true, but foolish.) 1 Corinthians chapters 1 & 2. <gloss>Claiming to be wise, they became fools.</gloss> <note>original Greek: "σοφοὶ εἶναι φάσκοντες ἐμωράνθησαν"</note> Romans 1.

19. We use a **prosyllogism** <note>A preliminary logical argument</note> fetched from not far away. For let only the warnings of Croll and the "Vital Philosophy" of Hartmann be inspected and compared with the sacred letters. One will find the principles of Paracelsus, Severinus, Hermes (the impious pagan), Cornelius Agrippa the Magician, and their like—not the principles of Christ. Indeed, this very argument of Croll refutes Croll himself. See page 46 of Croll and 44 concerning Kabbalists, magicians, names, etc.; regarding the word of God incarnate in creatures: p. 38 on the **Gabalia** <note>A term for secret tradition or mystical interpretation</note>; p. 35 on the double body of Adam, visible and invisible; p. 34 on the true Magician. Where in this...

<meta>This page continues the systematic critique of Paracelsian philosophy, specifically attacking Oswald Croll’s "Preface Admonitory." The author is refuting the idea that human wisdom and the soul's nature are derived from the stars rather than from traditional Christian and Aristotelian foundations.</meta>

->### Of the Paracelsians<-
<note>Paracelsians: followers of Paracelsus, who combined chemistry, medicine, and occult philosophy.</note>

->A 4<-

...is this found in the Christian testament, confessions, or symbolic declarations? Where did Christ, the Prophets, or the Apostles teach that the intelligent soul of man is a "divine light flowing from the breath of God and divine fountains"? Or, as he writes on page 33, that "the spirit of man, inspired by the Most High, is nothing other than **GOD** dwelling in us"? Or, as on page 31: "Man has two spirits, one **sidereal** <note>sidereal: relating to the stars or constellations</note> from the firmament, and the other from the breath of life, which is the inspired soul, or the invisible body of man"?

20. *On page 25, he says that man draws wisdom from the stars. Therefore, his philosophy was based on astrology. It is manifest, however, that this is not Christian.* In the stars of the firmament, he says on page 37, "lies hidden all animal wisdom, crafts, arts, sciences, and the knowledge of all things. To the heavens, all things are most well-known," etc. Why then do we not cast away not only the pagan <note>original: "ethnica"</note> writings, but also the Paracelsian and Theological ones, and draw wisdom from the stars by the power of our "magnet"? The Holy Spirit says that not all things are known to the heavens in Jeremiah 10:2-3: "Learn not the way of the heathen, and be not dismayed at the signs of heaven," etc. Isaiah 41:22: "Let them bring them forth, and show us what shall happen," etc. Chapter 47:12: "Stand now with thine enchantments" (the magicians). And verse 10: "Thy wisdom and thy knowledge, it hath perverted thee" (this wisdom is far later than the **Hermetic** <note>Hermetic: referring to the mystical/alchemical tradition of Hermes Trismegistus</note> sort, and indeed is drawn from it). Verse 13: "Let now the astrologers <note>original: "vates coeli"</note> save thee..." But from where does true wisdom come? See Proverbs 1:7 and <margin>Wisdom 9, throughout.</margin> following; Isaiah 6; Matthew 11:25; chapter 7:7 and following; Luke 11:9 and following; James 1:6. **Note:** The Paracelsians claim they do not wish to philosophize in a pagan manner. Yet here, Croll even asserts the pagan superstition regarding the intelligence of the stars, which Plato taught in the *Epinomis*; Galen in the book *That the Mental Faculties Follow the Bodily Temperament*; Hippocrates in the book *On Fleshes*, and others. The words of Galen: <note>original Greek: "αὐτὴ ἡ ξηρὰ ψυχὴ σοφωτάτη... καὶ τοὺς ἀστέρας αὐγοειδεῖς ἅμα κ’ ξηροὺς ὅλας ἄκραν σύνεσιν ἔχειν."</note> "This dry soul is the wisest (following Hippocrates), again considering dryness to be of an invisible constitution. For the name of 'radiance' <note>original: "αὐγῆς"</note> indicates this, and it is most reasonable to think this is a better opinion: that the stars, being radiant and dry, possess supreme intelligence. For if no one says they possess it, he will seem insensible to the excellence of the gods," etc. If Croll denies this Galenic view, he must also deny his own positions, because they contain nothing else; and if Hippocrates, Plato, Galen, etc., were to come back to life, they would subscribe to them.

21. *Another Argument: The Paracelsians accuse the Peripatetics <note>Peripatetics: followers of Aristotle</note> and Galenists of being "elemental philosophers" who refer everything to the qualities of the elements—the "asylum of ignorance"—and know nothing of the essential principles in which science and the causes of things are founded.* Hence Croll on page 59 and elsewhere, and throughout Severinus, Hartmann, and others, rail against Galen, as if he mainly adapted the Hippocratic "opposition" <note>the theory of curing by opposites (e.g., cold cures hot)</note> to the "bare qualities" condemned by Hippocrates, and recalled the primary ideas of cures to this through an unhappy error. In the same place, he explains the "Nature" of Hippocrates in a Paracelsian way, like Severinus, concerning **mumia**, **balsam**, etc. <note>mumia and balsam: Paracelsian terms for the vital preservative force in living things</note> Because this same discipline is Platonic, Aristotelian, Arabic, and belongs to absolutely everyone after Galen and Aristotle who follows their decrees, they are all condemned with a single vote: and the new Paracelsian Philosophy and Medicine is commended. But we respond that these Paracelsians do not understand the Aristotelian and Galenic teachings, or if they do understand, they studiously slander them; and they treat Hippocrates fraudulently. This is shown by a comparison made elsewhere.

22. *Another point from page 45: "The Word of God is the true medicine healing all things."* This was not known to all, nor was it given to just anyone "submerged in scholastic dust" to taste of it or write about it. "Only Paracelsus, a disciple of Mosaic and Living Philosophy, found the incarnate Word in creatures," etc. From this, they thunder against Academic Doctors, whom they call **Scholastics**, **Braggarts** <note>original: "Thrafones"</note>, **Athenians**, and other names, and they conclude that one must philosophize and practice medicine in the Paracelsian way, casting everything else aside, since only a single "Monarch" <note>A common title for Paracelsus among his followers</note> discovered it. Their syllogism would be: If the Word of God is true medicine, then Paracelsian astral medicines or principles are true medicine. The first is true; therefore, the second is. The reason for the connection: because the principles of Paracelsus and the stars are the "incarnate Word of God." Truly, the connection with its premise is most vain. Indeed, we do not condemn everything in the books of Paracelsus. For his pharmacy contains many good things; although the "raving" Croll thinks that Paracelsus's mind is not clear from his literal text, which he calls "dead." But his philosophy is nothing but pure imposture and a forest of lies for the most part, and for the greater part, only adulterated conjecture based on similarities. Since there is no science of things that are false and accidental, we confess that those "scholastics" cannot know Paracelsian things, and for that reason, they most justly reject them. But they should not be despised for this, but praised: and the arguer is laughable for finding it strange that we do not know Paracelsian things, since they are subject to no real science.

23. *Regarding the Word of God healing all things, we know the author of the Book of Wisdom ascribes healing to Wisdom itself in chapter 10, as also in chapter 9, verse 19 and chapter 15, verse 12: "Neither herb nor poultice healed them, but your Word, O Lord, which heals all things," etc. In the meantime, it does not exclude natural things; it shows healing through a fig to Hezekiah, and in Sirach chapter 38, through fifteen verses. But that Word is certainly not what Croll calls "incarnate," as if there were spiritual essences in the elements...*

He mocks the Paracelsian rejection of traditional university learning and their claim that Paracelsus alone discovered the "true medicine" of the Word of God.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of the Paracelsian worldview, moving from the general theories of Oswald Croll to the specific alchemical and philosophical arguments of Joachim Tancke. It addresses the tension between traditional Aristotelian logic and the "new" chemical philosophy of the early 17th century.</meta>

->### Forerunner of Vital Philosophy<-

*...you might say they have acquired the body of the mind, so that Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury <note>The "Tria Prima" or three primary elements of Paracelsian alchemy.</note> may be understood. Is it not a certain impiety to accommodate the most holy words of John 1 <note>The Gospel of John, beginning "In the beginning was the Word."</note> to the things of nature and the dreams of art, to Paracelsus, and thus, as it were, to mock God and make common that which belongs to the only Son of God? Behold for yourself the method of philosophizing. Concerning the arguments of the Hartmannians <note>Followers of Johannes Hartmann, the first professor of Chymiatry.</note> and the Rose Cross <note>The Rosicrucians, a secret brotherhood of sages and alchemists.</note>, it has been dealt with elsewhere. Let us see those of Tancke.*

24. *We do not envy the fame of Tancke <note>Joachim Tancke (1557–1609), a professor of medicine and proponent of alchemy.</note>, nor do we recall a man now dead to war. He lives by his wit and reasons. What these are like must be considered, lest we be led into error by love for the person. However, we freely acknowledge that he did not entirely condemn Aristotle and other physicists, nor Galen with the physicians of his faction, as far as words are concerned. Therefore, we examine his critique lest he seem to favor the Paracelsians too much and shake other authors out of the hands of learners. In the year 1610, the first Repository of Alchemy <note>original: "promtuarium Alchimiæ"</note> was printed. In its preface to the Lovers of Chemistry, he writes thus:* **Not all arts are contained or described in the biblical book.** *By this sentiment, he refutes Croll's fantasy about philosophizing in a Christian manner only from the foundation of the invisible philosophy, Christ, who fundamentally is known nowhere except from the writings and doctrine of the Prophets, Evangelists, and Apostles. But if he draws that divine philosophy from the foundation of Christ to the magic of Paracelsus, to Astrology, Spagyria <note>Alchemical production of herbal medicines.</note>, Gabalia <note>A term for occult or mystical science.</note>, Cabala, Character-magic, etc., it is clear how much he departs from the truth, even just from reading the Bibles. Solomon did not learn wisdom from Moses, says Tancke, although Christ commands us to learn therefrom.*

26. *Moreover, he cites here the testimony of Luther, who called philosophy "the play of apes" <note>original: "Lufum Simiarum"</note>, and wants it not to have to persist in the general knowledge of natural things as in the Organon <note>Aristotle's works on logic.</note>, the Acroamatic works <note>original: "Acroamaticis" - refers to Aristotle's lectures on Physics.</note> and similar Aristotelian writings. Further, Luther holds the associates of Aristotle for pure fools; Tancke reports that Luther looked more deeply into the nature of the Aristotelians and knew that gold could be made from copper, etc. If he opposed these things to the Peripatetic <note>Aristotelian</note> discipline and wanted to lead students away from it by the authority of Luther and Catholic <note>Here meaning "universal" or "general."</note> logic, they are ignorant of the Method of Refutation <note>original: "Elenchum"</note>. For Luther disapproved of philosophy only insofar as it is wickedly mixed with Theology by the sophists, as if there were an equal dignity of [human] light and Scripture, or that the latter should yield to the former, or receive light from it: which is false. He also disapproves of those who, in their own times, sounded nothing in their lecture halls besides Logic, Metaphysics, and the like. But far different is the form of philosophizing by the study of great men, which is not so much invented as recalled from antiquity. If philosophy be a handmaid and allows itself to be led captive into the obedience of faith, Luther praised it.*

26. *As for those who persist in the universal sciences, these are not so much to be rebuked, unless they think it must be done entirely and simply. The reason is: because the gifts of the Holy Spirit are not of the same measure. To one are committed general things, or all, or some—so that one might be a Professor of Logic, another of Physics; to another special things, such as to Gesner <note>Conrad Gessner, a Swiss naturalist.</note>, Ulisse Aldrovandi <note>An Italian naturalist and father of modern zoology.</note>, Agricola <note>Georgius Agricola, father of mineralogy.</note>, Rentmann, and so on; to others both, such as to Scaliger <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger, a renowned polymath.</note> and the like. He who has received more should not pursue with hatred him who has received less; but let him acknowledge the grace of God, and in the distribution of the talent, let him approve of God's counsel. Luther also sought that in physics one should be so engaged that they might be adapted to Theology. Truly this is the duty of the interpreter and the preacher, which we do not disapprove of.*

27. *Aristotle did not describe all things, nor have all his books reached us. Should we therefore not seek from Aristotle what is in him? We confess that Aristotle neither wrote everything, nor have all his writings reached us, if you speak of particulars. And who is omniscient except God? Scripture testifies concerning the wisdom of Solomon. If his books remained, we would see that even then he did not know all things. Meanwhile, however, those things which we can enjoy are not to be cast away. Is the Bible to be laid aside for that reason? We do not think, however, that Tancke wanted to undermine the Peripatetic doctrine by this argument. For if Aristotle himself did not describe everything, he had many successors who also contributed their own. Those things which complete philosophy are to be joined, not separated. Add this also: from Aristotle's universal principles, whether directly or by analogy, most things which are disputed in the schools of physics can be explained. Theology, Medicine, Astrology, and other things we learn from others.*

28. *Many things from the contemplation of the property of natures have been little cared for by the Philosophers, such as those which behave <gloss>antipathetically <note>original: ἀντιπαθῶς (antipathōs) - acting with a natural aversion or opposite force.</note></gloss>. Such is the power of the plantain against the venom of the spider, and the enmity itself between both creatures; likewise, what the reason is that vitriol <note>Sulfuric acid or its salts.</note> expels earthworms; what the power is of the specific purge of Paracelsus; by what virtue serpentine powder resists the venom of snakes and defends a man from being harmed by them. No one can bring forth the true causes of these mysteries from the Philosophy of Aristotle and the medicine of Galen. What then? Should they therefore be condemned?*

29. *We do not think Tancke wanted this, but only denied [that they spoke] sufficiently concerning all things*

The author argues for a middle ground where traditional logic and new natural observations (like medicinal antipathies) complement rather than exclude one another.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of Joachim Tancke and the Paracelsian school. It focuses on the tension between "Natural Magic" and traditional Aristotelian/Galenic medicine, specifically addressing the "weapon salve" and the chemical principles of Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury. The author argues that Paracelsians often claim divine inspiration to hide a lack of scientific proof.</meta>

->### Of the Paracelsians ###<-

...cannot be deferred from their principles. For he does not entirely reject the philosophy of Aristotle; at least, in his judgment, no one can be a perfect physician who is not skilled in the science of Hippocratic and Galenic doctrine, as well as being experienced in Alchemy <note>original: "Alchymiæ"</note>. One must not remain with Aristotle and Galen as if you had merely sworn an oath to the words of a master. Therefore, he wishes that "natural magic"—that which those Easterners who brought gifts to Christ <note>The Three Magi or Wise Men.</note> practiced—should be known. He does not think it is enough to say: "The cause is hidden." He also brings forward the weapon salve <term>unguentum armarium: a "sympathetic" ointment applied to the weapon that caused a wound rather than the wound itself, believed to heal at a distance</term>, as if its power were unknown to the Aristotelians and Galenists, even though no one can deny its effectiveness.

30. But that good man <note>Referring to Tancke.</note> is too lenient a judge of his own people. For as far as the sympathies and antipathies of things are concerned, these are not unknown to the Peripatetics <term>Peripatetics: followers of Aristotle, who famously walked while teaching</term> and Galenists; so that, with the exception of those contained in new accounts, most examples of them were noted down by Aristotle, Galen, and their disciples, and from there drawn into Paracelsianism. It is impossible to note down every single instance. One must judge based on general principles and examples of things similarly affected, and discern the accident from the essence. If you look at the causes, the Paracelsian spagyrists <term>spagyrists: alchemical pharmacists who "separate and recombine" plant or mineral essences</term> think they have reached the starting principle if they say: "this happens because of sulfur, salt, or mercury, or all of them, or some of them"; or "that happens because of the elements"; or "another thing comes from the stars, from chiromancy, magic, or the cabala," and so on. But if you ask another person for things that might provide assurance that it is as they say, the flow of logic stops <note>original: "haeret aqua," literally "the water sticks," a Latin idiom for being at a loss or stuck</note>, and they flee to flawed metaphors, specific examples, and their own opinions. Some even tell lies, such as those concerning the weapon salve <margin>*</margin> and the blood-stanching stone <note>original: "ischæmo," a stone or remedy believed to stop bleeding</note>; some things are even attributed to attendant spirits <note>original: "pærtredros spiritus," familiar spirits or demons believed to assist a magician</note>. Regarding these, we freely confess we have no knowledge, because they lack philosophical principles; if you want sulfur to be the cause of a thing, it must be stated scientifically *why* it is so. Otherwise, it fails just as much as Aristotelianism and Galenism.

<margin>*</margin> > Lest he say this was inserted: the weapon salve is so common today that almost anyone knows and sees its power. But who understands the cause of its operation? If they understood the harmony of the macrocosm and the microcosm—that is, of heaven with the lower world—they could provide the cause for this wondrous operation. But I say: we hear words, but no proof. How do you know that this harmony is the cause? A fallacy is committed by mistaking an accident for a cause. For healing occurs in a far different way than by heat. This must happen spiritually, you say, because they do not touch physically. But I say they touch in neither way; rather, the effect depends on another reason, and that not always, nor in all cases: a matter discussed elsewhere.

31. God (he says) gives this understanding not to everyone, but only to the good who ask Him. Well said, my dear Tancke! If you were alive, I could easily prove by your own argument that you were neither good nor did you ask, because you were just as ignorant of the primary and unmoved causes as the Galenists and Aristotelians. Concerning purgatives, they say the power is in the spirit. I do not disagree. But tell me *why* it is in the spirit? Why in the juice? You flee to the stars. I resist you with the argument of minerals and plants, which had their power before the stars existed. But also, what have you said that is different from Aristotle and other Peripatetics, who themselves attribute much to the heavens, but like you, are unable to explain it? Must we believe you just because you say it and think it so? Not at all.

32. You say that those who have explored the "book of the great and small world" <note>The macrocosm (universe) and microcosm (man).</note> know all things, of which we spoke above. You attribute this knowledge mostly to your own kind: cabalists, astrologers, alchemists, etc. But prove this very thing. The Paracelsian astrologers lie just as much—indeed, far more shamelessly—than any Aristotelian, Galenist, Arab, Chaldean, or Egyptian. Therefore, the argument from the stars falls, and that specific knowledge remains with Paul until the creature shall be delivered from vanity <note>A reference to Romans 8:21.</note>. If the heaven works upon plants, much more so upon minerals, says Tancke. But where is the principle? For the connection is denied. Regarding the familiarity of the sun and the sunflower <note>original: "solsequij," plants that follow the sun</note>, the agreement of the Moon and the sea, and so on, the Paracelsians chatter much, but they fail in their analysis of causes. There is hardly anyone from a trivial school <note>The "trivium" was the introductory level of education (grammar, logic, rhetoric).</note> who would not see the argument should be inverted: if it works on minerals, it works even more on vegetation, which is more capable of receiving an impression.

33. Why then, Tancke, did you not propose those infallible, unmoved, primary causes of your magical experiments? Why can we learn nothing from you, who were undoubtedly a disciple of magic? Why did you not perfect "potable silver," "potable gold," <note>Chemical elixirs where metals are made drinkable, often sought as universal medicines.</note> and other things, if you were so conscious of nature that you dared to bring a lawsuit against Aristotle and Galen? To tell stories—whether true or likely, judged rightly or wrongly—is of no great weight in philosophy.

34. You deny that it can be seen from Aristotle how a grain of wheat is a symbol of the resurrection of our bodies. But I believe we could say that to you from your entire philosophy: unless you had "plowed with the heifers" of Christ and Paul <note>A biblical idiom meaning to solve a riddle using someone else's secrets; here, using scripture to bolster his weak philosophy.</note>, you would have been as mute as a fish.
> Discern between Theological and Philosophical mysteries, and seek the principles for each from its own science. When a bearded woman is born, tell us, O Magus, without error and scientifically: what mystery does it hold? We can tell various stories based on comparison, but not from absolute knowledge.

35. You think Solomon knew such Magic. I see you writing that. But—

<meta>This page continues the critique of Joachim Tancke and the Paracelsian school. It moves from the specific example of the "weapon salve" to a broader discussion of how Paracelsians use (and misuse) Scripture and the authority of Martin Luther to justify their chemical philosophies over traditional Aristotelian logic.</meta>

# Vital Prelude to Philosophy #

...you might say they are bodies acquired by the mind, so that Salt, Sulphur, and Mercury <note>The "Tria Prima" or three primary principles of Paracelsian alchemy.</note> may be understood. Is it not a certain impiety to adapt the most holy words of John 1 <note>The opening of the Gospel of John: "In the beginning was the Word..."</note> to the things of nature and the dreams of art, as the Paracelsians do, and thus, as if to mock God, make common that which belongs only to the Son of God? Here is your reason for philosophy. Regarding the arguments of the followers of Hartmann and the Rosy Cross <note>The Rosicrucians, a mysterious esoteric society of the era.</note>, they have been dealt with elsewhere. Let us now see Tancke’s arguments.

24. We do not envy Tancke’s fame, nor do we call a man who is now dead back into the theater of war. He lives through his talent and his reasoning. We must consider what these reasons are, lest we be led into error by our affection for the man. However, we willingly acknowledge that he did not entirely condemn Aristotle and other natural philosophers, nor Galen and the physicians of that school, at least in words. We examine his judgment so that he does not seem to favor the Paracelsians too much or shake other authors out of the hands of students. In the year 1610, the first *Storehouse of Alchemy* <note>original: "promtuarium Alchimiæ"</note> was printed. In its preface to the "Lovers of Chemistry," he writes: "Not all arts are contained or described in the Biblical book." With this opinion, he refutes the fantasy of Oswald Croll <note>A prominent Paracelsian physician.</note> about philosophizing in a "Christian" manner based solely on the foundation of the invisible philosophy of Christ—who fundamentally is known nowhere except from the writings and doctrine of the Prophets, Evangelists, and Apostles. But if he drags that divine philosophy from the foundation of Christ into the magic of Paracelsus, into Astrology, Spagyria <term>Spagyria: the alchemical art of separating and recombining the components of a substance</term>, the Cabala, the use of magical characters, and so on, it is clear how far he departs from the truth, provided one has at least read the Bible. Tancke says that Solomon did not learn his wisdom from Moses, even though Christ commands us to learn from the Scriptures.

25. He also brings forward the testimony of Luther, who called [scholastic] philosophy a "Play of Apes" <note>original: "Lusum Simiarum"</note>, and he argues that philosophy should not persist in the general knowledge of natural things as found in the *Organon*, the *Acroamatics* <note>Esoteric works of Aristotle, such as the *Physics*.</note>, and similar Aristotelian works. Below the associates of Aristotle, Luther holds those to be pure fools who claim Luther looked deeper into Aristotle's light or knew that gold could be made from copper, as Tancke reports. If Tancke opposed these views to the Peripatetic discipline <note>The traditional school of Aristotle.</note> and wanted to lead students away from it using the authority of Luther and universal logic, he has failed to understand the "Refutation" <note>original: "Elenchum," a logical term for a proof that contradicts an opponent’s thesis.</note>. For Luther disapproved of philosophy only insofar as it is wrongly mixed with Theology by sophists—as if the light of reason and Scripture were of equal dignity, or as if Scripture should yield to reason or receive its light from it. This is false. He also disapproved of those who, in his time, spoke of nothing in their lecture halls except Logic, Metaphysics, and the like. But the form of philosophizing found in the study of great men is far different—it is not so much a new invention as a recovery of antiquity. If philosophy acts as a handmaid <note>The medieval concept of "Philosophia ancilla theologiae," or philosophy as the servant of theology.</note> and allows herself to be led captive in obedience to faith, then Luther praised it.

26. Regarding those who remain within the universal sciences, they are not to be rebuked unless they think that science must consist of *only* those things. The reason is this: the gifts of the Holy Spirit are not given in the same measure to everyone. To one, general things are committed—either all or some—so that one person might be a Professor of Logic, and another a Professor of the *Acroamatics*. To others, specialized things are given, such as to Gessner <note>Conrad Gessner, a pioneer of modern zoology and botany.</note>, Ulisse Aldrovandi, Agricola, or Rentmann. To others, both are given, as with Scaliger <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger, a famous polymath.</note> and his like. He who has received more should not pursue with hatred the one who has received less; rather, he should acknowledge the grace of God and approve of God’s plan in the distribution of talents. Luther also sought that physics should be handled in such a way that it was accommodated to Theology. Indeed, this is the duty of the interpreter and the preacher, which we do not disapprove of.

27. Aristotle did not describe everything, nor have all his books reached us. Should we not seek what is in Aristotle because of the number of lost works? We admit that Aristotle neither wrote everything, nor did all his writings reach us, if you are speaking of particular details. And who is omniscient except God? Scripture testifies to the wisdom of Solomon; if his books survived, we would see that even he did not know everything. In the meantime, however, we must not cast away those things we can still enjoy. Should the Bible therefore be set aside? We do not think Tancke wanted to undermine Aristotelian doctrine with this argument. For even if Aristotle did not describe everything himself, he had many successors who contributed their own findings. The things that complete philosophy should be joined together, not torn apart. Add to this: from Aristotle’s universal principles—whether directly or by analogy—most things disputed in the schools of physics can be explained. We learn Theology, Medicine, Astrology, and other things from other sources.

28. Many things observed in the properties of nature are little cared for by [traditional] Philosophers, such as those which behave <unclear>analogously</unclear> <note>original Greek: ἀνάλογα</note>. For example: the power of the plantain herb against the venom of a spider, and the natural enmity between those two; likewise, the reason why vitriol <note>Sulfuric acid or sulfate minerals.</note> expels earthworms; the force of the specific "purge" used by Paracelsus; and by what virtue "serpent powder" resists the venom of snakes and protects a man from their harm. No one can produce the true causes of these mysteries from the Philosophy of Aristotle or the medicine of Galen. What then? Are they therefore to be condemned?

29. We do not think Tancke wanted this, but only denied that [Aristotle] had written sufficiently regarding all things...

<summary>This page critiques the Paracelsian attempt to replace Aristotelian philosophy with a "Christian" chemistry, arguing that while Aristotle was not omniscient, his logic remains a necessary "handmaid" to faith and science. The author balances traditional scholasticism with an acknowledgment of natural mysteries (like herbal cures) that traditional medicine cannot yet explain.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of Joachim Tancke and the Paracelsian school. It moves from general accusations of impiety to a specific debate over the "weapon salve" and the limits of natural magic versus traditional Aristotelian and Galenic science.</meta>

# Of the Paracelsians #

*...cannot be set aside based on their principles. For he does not entirely reject the philosophy of Aristotle; at least, in his judgment, no one can be a perfect physician who is not skilled in the science of Hippocratic and Galenic doctrine and also experienced in Alchemy. One must not remain with Aristotle and Galen as if you had merely sworn an oath to the words of a master. Therefore, he wishes that "natural magic"—the kind known to those Easterners <note>The Magi or Three Wise Men.</note> who brought gifts to Christ—should be understood. He does not think it is enough to say: "The cause is hidden." He also brings up the "weapon salve," <note>original: "vnguentum armarium." A controversial 17th-century medicinal ointment believed to heal a wound by being applied to the weapon that caused it rather than the patient.</note> as if its power were unknown to the Aristotelians and Galenists, even though no one can deny its effectiveness.*

30. But that good man <note>Referring to Joachim Tancke.</note> is too lenient a judge of his own people. As for the "sympathies and antipathies" of things, these are not unknown to the Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle’s philosophy.</note> and Galenists; indeed, except for those found in newer accounts, most examples were noted by Aristotle, Galen, and their disciples, and were drawn from there into Paracelsian thought. It is impossible to note every single detail. One must judge based on general principles and examples of similar effects, and discern the accidental from the essential. If you look at the causes, and the Paracelsian "spagyrists" <note>Alchemists who specialized in separating and recombining plant or mineral essences.</note> say a cause has reached its principle: they claim this happens because of "sulfur, salt, or mercury"—all or some of them; or that happens because of the elements; another thing because of the stars, or from chiromancy <note>palm reading</note>, magic, cabalism, and so on. But if you ask another person why they believe it is as they say, "the water sticks" <note>An idiom meaning they are at a loss for words.</note> and they flee to flawed metaphors, specific examples, and their own opinions. Some even tell lies, such as what they say about the weapon salve <margin>*</margin> and the blood-stopping stone <note>original: "ischæmo"</note>; some things are even attributed to deceptive spirits. Regarding these, we freely confess we have no science, because they lack philosophical principles. If you want "sulfur" to be the cause of a thing, you must say scientifically *why* it is so. Otherwise, it fails just as much as Aristotelianism and Galenism.

<margin>*</margin> *Lest this be omitted, he says: The weapon salve is so common today that almost anyone knows and sees its power. But who understands the cause of its operation? If they understood the harmony between the macrocosm <note>the universe</note> and the microcosm <note>the human body</note>, or of heaven with the lower world, they could provide the cause of this wondrous operation. But I say: we hear words, but see no proof. How do you know that this "harmony" is the cause? The fallacy of "non-cause" and "accident" is being committed. For healing happens far differently than through such a substitute. "This must happen spiritually," you say, "because they do not touch physically." But I say: they touch in neither way; the effect happens for another reason, and that not always, nor in all cases: a matter discussed elsewhere.*

31. "God," he says, "gives this understanding not to all, but only to the good who ask Him." Well said, my dear Tancke. If you were alive, I would easily prove by your own argument that you were neither good nor did you ask, because you remained just as ignorant of the primary and unchanging causes as the Galenists and Aristotelians. Regarding purgatives, they say the power is in the "spirit." I do not disagree. But tell me, *why* in the spirit? *Why* in the juice? You flee to the stars. I resist you with the argument of minerals and plants, which had their power before the stars were created <note>A reference to the Biblical creation narrative where plants are created on the third day and stars on the fourth.</note>. But also, what have you said that is different from Aristotle and other Peripatetics, who themselves attribute much to the heavens, but like you, are unable to explain it? Must we believe you just because you say it and because you think so? Not at all.

32. You say that those who have explored the "book of the great and small world" <note>The macrocosm and microcosm.</note> know everything, as mentioned above. You attribute this knowledge to your own kind: the cabalists, astrologers, spagyrists, etc. But prove this very thing. Paracelsian astrologers tell lies—indeed, far more shamelessly than any Aristotelian, Galenist, Arab, Chaldean, or Egyptian. Therefore, the argument from the stars fails, and that "particular knowledge" remains with Paul until the "creature shall be delivered from vanity." <note>A reference to Romans 8:21.</note> "If the heaven acts upon plants, much more so upon minerals," says Tancke. But where is the principle? For the connection is denied. The Paracelsians babble many things about the "familiarity" of the sun and the sunflower, of the agreement between the moon and the sea, etc., but they fail in their "Refutation of Causes" <note>original: "Elencho causarum."</note>. There is hardly anyone from a trivial school who would not see the argument should be inverted: if it works on minerals, it should work even more on vegetation, which is more capable of receiving impressions.

33. Why then, Tancke, did you not propose those infallible, unchanging, primary causes of your magical experiments? Why can we learn nothing from you, who were undoubtedly a disciple of magic? Why did you not perfect "potable silver," "potable gold," and other things, if you were so conscious of nature that you dared to write an indictment of ignorance against Aristotle and Galen? To tell stories—whether true or likely, whether correctly or wrongly indicated—is of no great weight in philosophy.

34. You deny that it can be seen from Aristotle how a grain of wheat is a symbol of the resurrection of our bodies. But I believe we could say that from your entire philosophy, and unless you had "plowed with the heifers" <note>A biblical idiom meaning to solve a riddle using someone else's help (Judges 14:18).</note> of Christ and Paul, you would have been as mute as a fish.

*Distinguish between Theological and Philosophical mysteries, and seek the principles for each from its own science. When a bearded woman is born, tell me, without error and scientifically, what mystery does it hold? We can describe things in various ways based on comparison, but not out of absolute science.*

35. You think that Solomon possessed that Magic. I see you writing that. But—

<summary>This page presents a sharp critique of Joachim Tancke's Paracelsian ideas, specifically attacking the "weapon salve" and the "macrocosm-microcosm" theory as lacking scientific proof. The author argues that Paracelsians rely on religious metaphors and empty terminology rather than the rigorous causal logic found in traditional Aristotelian philosophy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's point-by-point rebuttal of Joachim Tancke and the Paracelsian school. The previous page discussed the "weapon salve" and the limits of natural magic; here, the author transitions into a historical and theological critique of magic, gold-making, and the supposed "ancient wisdom" of the Egyptians and Persians.</meta>

# Forerunner of Vital Philosophy #

...why do you not prove it infallibly? Why were his magic books burned? Why were the magicians' books burned by the Christians in the Acts of the Apostles, *Acts 19:19*, which Baronius <note>Caesar Baronius (1538–1607) was a Cardinal and historian who wrote the *Annales Ecclesiastici*.</note> likely proves were magic books or closely related to magic? Was it permissible to cast divine mysteries to Vulcan? <note>original: "Vulcano obijcere." Vulcan was the Roman god of fire; here it means to burn the books.</note> But even if Solomon knew the magic arts, does that mean whatever is physical does not fall under the Peripatetic <note>Aristotelian.</note> and Medical rules? Just because Simon Magus, Artephius, Trithemius, Paracelsus, and similar men did many things through commerce with spirits—or said they could be done—this does not mean that Aristotle’s physics is useless, nor that Galen’s medicine should be rejected or placed second to Paracelsian magic, simply because neither Aristotle nor Galen addressed these things explicitly or implicitly.

36. You ought to have proven with a scientific argument that God does not want the "mystery of the wise" regarding gold-making to be common knowledge. Otherwise, God, who is good and "rains upon the just and the unjust," <note>A reference to Matthew 5:45.</note> and who has liberally revealed the mystery of the Gospel, does not seem to begrudge or wish to withhold that which is less important for human comfort. The *Fama of the Rosy Cross* <note>original: "Fama crucis Roseæ." This refers to the *Fama Fraternitatis*, a Rosicrucian manifesto published around 1614 which claimed a secret brotherhood possessed ancient wisdom.</note> also denies that this age is worthy of it. Therefore, in the future eternity, we shall be vigorous goldsmiths and physicians, when "there is nothing that must not be revealed." Whatever is under the sun, "time brings into the open." *Matthew 10:26.* We do not care for naked assertions. We demand the unshakable principles behind what is said. If everyone could make gold, you would fear some kind of confusion in vain; even now, no evils arise simply because air, water, fire, and earth are for common use. The kingdom of Solomon did not perish because he "made silver as common as stones." *3 Kings 10:27.* <note>In modern Bibles, this is 1 Kings 10:27.</note>

37. You say we think we have "devoured everything" when we have looked into Aristotle (whom alone we now treat), even though much is lacking in us and we are as far from the secrets of nature as heaven is from earth. Yet you attribute that faculty to the Magi of Persia and Egypt, to whom the Greeks traveled. It does not matter what you think or don’t think. We ascribe no more to Aristotle than what we find in his work. If your medical or philosophical faculty held that opinion, why did it not profess Aristotle alone? If the Greeks acquired wisdom from the Egyptians and Persians, why do you admire the latter while disparaging the former? Oh, the "Magic" of the true wisdom of Hermes in the *Poimandres* and *Asclepius*; <note>Two foundational texts of Hermeticism, attributed to the mythical Hermes Trismegistus.</note> yet, by Paul’s witness, it is "foolishness." A miserable judgment: if someone refers certain things to God’s providence, Tancke says: "What nature can do ought not to be ascribed immediately to God; God ordered the course of nature through his *FIAT*, <note>original: "FIAT." The Latin command for "Let it be," used by God during creation.</note> but Art surpasses nature." Certainly, there are many things that have causes neither in nature nor in art, but are to be attributed to "higher motors" <note>Celestial or divine influences.</note>; however, he does not sin who immediately attributes the effects of art and nature to God, as many such things are read in Holy Scripture, especially in Job and the Psalms.

38. He commends Democritus, Pythagoras, and Plato for the magic of the Egyptians. But Aristotle knew this better than any Paracelsian. Therefore, that was not "Magic," nor were the secrets of Magic hidden from Aristotle. The Paracelsian responds from the decrees of those magicians: and by comparison with Aristotelian institutions, we shall see whose reasons are better, though examples of this matter are not lacking in Aristotle, Pliny, and others.

39. It escaped his notice that he makes Numa Pompilius <note>The legendary second king of Rome, credited with establishing many religious rites.</note> an expert in that most secret magic. But Numa was a gentile idolater, a worshiper of the diabolical, and a practitioner of impious magic. Does anyone require this magic for Aristotelian and Galenic Christians? They are pious if they wish neither to learn nor to practice it. Therefore, they detest the magical vanity of Croll, Paracelsus, Artephius, Trithemius, Agrippa, and similar men, along with God. That Pythagoras was addicted to diabolical magic is proven by his life. Numa's secret science was conformed to his decrees—so much so that he was considered a Pythagorean, even though he lived earlier in time. If, therefore, the mysteries of Numa were diabolical, so were those of Pythagoras. With what face can these things be thrust upon Christian schools, so that while Aristotelians are suspended, the magic of Pythagoras must also be taught? Things certainly turned out poorly for Tullus <note>Tullus Hostilius, the third king of Rome, who allegedly died in a fire while trying to perform one of Numa's rituals.</note> regarding Numa's *Commentaries* on "drawing down Jupiter" through occult sacrifices. Those books were found later: seven were Latin concerning Pontifical law, and seven were Greek concerning the "discipline of wisdom" of those times; by judgment of the Senate and the Praetor, they were publicly burned in the assembly. It is a reckless claim that Numa commanded Pythagoras's books to be buried with him in his tomb, since that is called a lie in Livy and is refuted by Cicero, because Pythagoras lived after Numa. *Cicero, Tusculan Disputations, Book 4.*

<margin>
*Livy, Book 1.*
Year of the World 3785.
*Livy, Book 40, decade 4.*
Death of Numa, year of the world 3193.
Pythagoras was in Italy in the year 3457.
</margin>

40. The masters of the Philosopher’s Stone are rightly commended. But what else do they bring forward other than Peripatetic foundations? This is why Aristotle was later counted among the wise men and mystic philosophers. We do not indeed learn the "Stone" <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the goal of alchemy.</note> from Aristotle according to the method of the art; but we have demonstrated against Guibert <note>Nicolas Guibert (c. 1547–1620), a physician and chemist who wrote against alchemy.</note> that the foundations are in him. How many people are there, however, who have learned gold-making from the books of the mystic philosophers? The defect is equal in both the Peripatetics and the Magicians.

41. It would be just if Princes ordered physicians and philosophers to read chemical authors no less than Aristotle and Galen. For from that, we would obtain a more constant and correct Philosophy and Medicine, which comparison will be made—

<summary>This page presents a rigorous defense of traditional Aristotelian science against the "ancient magic" claims of the Paracelsians, arguing that alchemical and magical secrets are either diabolical or already contained within classical philosophy.</summary>
