<meta>This page continues the author's systematic refutation of the Paracelsian school, specifically targeting Joachim Tancke’s claims. The previous page ended with a discussion on the burning of magical books and the limits of Solomon's wisdom. This page moves into the debate over "husks vs. kernels" in nature and the validity of Paracelsian astrology and alchemy.</meta>

### Of the Paracelsians ###

...it was intended that we should not remain in the husks <note>The "husk" (cortex) refers to the superficial or outer appearance of a substance.</note> of things, but rather attain the kernel <note>The "kernel" (nucleus) refers to the inner essence or medicinal power.</note> of nature, etc. *I do not find fault with this goal, provided that Paracelsian methods are not mandated. However, because there are professors and students in the Universities who already do this diligently, there is no need for a decree from princes. If anything should be commanded, let it be commanded that all of Paracelsus's books be burned—those in which he describes magic, Cabala <note>A form of Jewish mysticism that Paracelsians adapted into a "Christian Cabala" to explain natural secrets.</note>, and similar diabolical, foolish, vain, impious, and absurd things. Chemistry is now so well-illuminated that we have no need for Paracelsus's writings.*

42. But what is this that is said about the husk? The Paracelsians claim the kernel for themselves and assign the husk to Aristotle and Galen: *but they are mistaken. For both parties possess both the husk and the kernel together, just as God joined them and nature produced them, and the whole world uses them in food and medicine. Chemical masterworks <term>magisteria: highly refined or "mastered" chemical preparations</term> also preserve both—namely the elements and the essence, which cannot be totally separated from the elements. The Arabs <note>Referring to medieval Islamic physicians like Avicenna and Rhazes.</note> long ago taught about essences, and thus they expanded the Galenic tradition. Later, work and experience followed so that every cause for complaint was removed. Tancke <note>Joachim Tancke (1557–1609), a professor at Leipzig and a defender of alchemical medicine.</note> wanted the schools to teach about sulfur, salt, and mercury <note>The "Tria Prima" or three primary principles of Paracelsian chemistry.</note> as the "kernels." Fine. But we cannot entirely take these kernels away from Galen or Aristotle, even if they did not possess them in such a pure and refined state. If you combine the works of the Arabs and the labors of the moderns, you can desire nothing more.*

<margin>
*Paracelsian Astrology.*
</margin>
43. He writes that "true astrology"—which consists of the sympathy and antipathy of the stars of heaven and the lower world, and how all things are produced and created through the heavens—has been extinguished among us. He claims that the philosophers of our time are known to know nothing of it; or at least only that the natural philosophers and alchemists practiced it, etc. *But the first question is: is this "true and ancient Astrology" that which is described by Paracelsus, Croll <note>Oswald Croll (c. 1563–1609), a prominent Paracelsian physician and chemist.</note>, Severinus <note>Petrus Severinus (1542–1602), a Danish physician whose "Idea Medicinae Philosophicae" popularized Paracelsian thought.</note>, etc.? The Babylonians, Egyptians, Greeks, and Arabs practiced a far different kind, as their writings testify. Thus, the argument is flawed from the very start <note>original: "Petitur ergo principium," referring to the logical fallacy of begging the question.</note> when he gives the name "Astrology" to that segregatory spagyria <term>spagyria: the alchemical art of "separating and recombining" to create medicines</term> which belongs to physics and medicine. They sew on magic, Cabala, and other deceits, through which they invent a certain harmony or a "sphere and wheel" for the reconciliation of the highest things with the lowest—not in the manner of the ancients, but according to the recent followers of Paracelsus.*

44. Furthermore, the principles of true Astrology are found in Aristotle in the first book of *Meteorology*, and in his books *On the Heavens* and *On the Generation of Animals*: *since philosophers embrace these, it is false to say that they and the physicians know nothing of that ancient Physics concerning the kinship of celestial and elementary things. It is not that they are ignorant of Astrology or Medical Physics (if it should be so named); rather, they do not accept, but instead condemn, the Paracelsian, Severinian, and Crollian follies—the dreams of delirious men. Nor are Peripatetic <note>Aristotelian</note> philosophers lacking who do not condemn symbols, parables, or analogies when understood in a healthy sense. There are those who know how to combine the essences of lower things with celestial fires and heat in the manner of Hippocrates. Again, therefore, we are not ignorant of that Astrology when it is rightly understood and used, but we reject it as declared by Paracelsus. From Paracelsus, there is nothing in this matter but what is foolish and lying.*

45. He claims Alchemy is neglected, except for the greed for riches, and that without it, nature cannot be known. Since Aristotle, Galen, and the rest did not know Alchemy, they did not know nature; thus, he claims no one can profit from their writings, etc. *The answer to this has long been that this is a fallacious argument <note>original: "Elenchum esse à dicto secundum quid," a logic term for a generalization that ignores essential qualifications.</note>. For even if they did not know or cultivate Alchemy in the methodical way it was later understood, they still possessed its principles and many rudiments. Furthermore, while the art is not found exactly described in their writings, the Arabs and other physicians who enriched medicine did not fail in this regard. Join what you divide, and you will have no cause to complain. Why do you treat our age with such disgrace, in which many Galenists have also cultivated Alchemy? Do you know nothing of Crato <note>Johannes Crato von Krafftheim (1519–1585), imperial physician.</note>, Gesner <note>Conrad Gessner (1516–1565), a Swiss polymath and naturalist.</note>, and a great number of others? Why do you not fill the void yourself? Who practices Alchemy for the sake of money other than the Paracelsians, who sell their own preparations and, to lure buyers, scatter flyers like charlatans <note>original: "argytarum," referring to mountebanks or itinerant sellers of silver/medicines.</note>?*

46. True Philosophy and Alchemy, along with the medicine it encompasses, requires spiritual eyes that can look into nature—not just to survey earthly things, but to see what lies hidden spiritually in every thing. Such eyes were possessed by Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note>, Democritus, Pythagoras, and Plato, who saw with celestial or angelic eyes that nature had implanted and clothed all things with a hidden and spiritual power, seed, and virtue <note>In this context, "virtue" refers to a specific medicinal or natural efficacy.</note>. Once freed from bodily impediments, this power can perform wonders that cannot be explained by Galenic responses regarding "occult properties" and "temperament," nor seen with bodily eyes. For example: the magnet attracts iron, and the iron to which it is joined turns toward the North Pole; if rubbed with garlic, it loses its power; the scorpion is the remedy for its own wound; man loves gold with a familiar...

<meta>catchword: ...ity of...</meta>

<summary>This page contains a vigorous defense of Aristotelian and Galenic traditions against Paracelsian accusations of ignorance. The author argues that classical philosophy already contains the "kernels" of nature and astrology, and dismisses Paracelsus's specific magical and "spagyric" additions as foolish or fraudulent.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, Galen, Aristotle, alchemy, astrology, spagyria, Joachim Tancke, Oswald Croll, essence, occult properties, magnet, charlatans</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of the Paracelsian school and its departure from Aristotelian philosophy. The previous page discussed the "kernel" and "husk" of nature, arguing that Paracelsians dismiss traditional learning too quickly. Here, the author defends Aristotle’s relevance to chemistry and critiques the "magical" foundations of the Paracelsian tradition.</meta>

# Preliminary Treatise on Vital Philosophy #

[Signature mark] EX
<meta>catchword: EX</meta>

...through a hidden power. So wine gladdens, and is familiar to man; gold takes up quicksilver <note>original: *argentum vivum*; the metal mercury</note>, and with it is extended for gilding, and so on. Goldsmiths know these things, but not the Aristotelians, who cannot give the reasons for these occurrences from Aristotle's *Acroamatics* <note>The *Acroamatica* refers to Aristotle’s advanced or esoteric lectures intended for his inner circle of students.</note>. *What follows from this?* Should Aristotle’s philosophy therefore be stripped of its feathers <note>An idiom meaning to be shorn of prestige or authority.</note>? We deny that this follows. *For even if military discipline cannot be learned from the philosophy of Aristotle, it does not mean his philosophy is to be despised. It is a ridiculous argument: Goldsmiths know things in their craft that Aristotelians do not; therefore, the goldsmiths have more enlightened eyes and minds.*

47. *Regarding Plato, Democritus, Pythagoras, and Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of Hermeticism/alchemy.</note>, it is false that they saw with celestial and angelic eyes—unless you take devils for angels—since they were wicked magicians and idol worshipers, whose whole wisdom is foolishness before God in divine matters. In human matters, they so overflow with vices and absurdities that they have been rebuked by many; in particular, the Hermetic Dialogues are full of errors. It is obvious that the foundations of impious Magic and the "suspension of judgment" <note>original: *acatalepsia*; a skeptical doctrine that things cannot be fully known.</note> of the Pyrrhonian skeptics are found in Plato’s dialogues, as is witnessed by the writings of the Platonists: Iamblichus, Proclus, Pico della Mirandola, Pistorius, Marsilio Ficino, etc. Where is it proved that they recognized a "seminary virtue" <note>An internal power or "seed" within matter that directs its growth and properties.</note> in all things? Whatever they knew, they received either through instruction, or learned from attendant spirits <note>original: *spiritibus Parhedris*; supernatural entities or "familiars" thought to assist magicians.</note>, or in a natural way through the senses, reason, and experience. If you look at instruction, Aristotle does not yield to them. If you look at Magic, they were wicked. If you look at the ordinary way of learning, the Peripatetic school <note>The followers of Aristotle.</note> leaves them far behind, as the facts and controversies prove.*

48. *The examples brought forward do not prove the point they are meant to support. Regarding the Magnet, the Paracelsians hold a very false opinion: namely, that iron is attracted by it because of an "iron spirit" which pulls more strongly in a foreign body than in its own. Even if there is a spirit—which the Peripatetics and followers of Galen gladly concede—these "new philosophers" ought to say why it attracts. Is it because it is sulfurous? But why does sulfur attract? Because it is "familiar." But why? Is it not because their "mixture" <term>crasis: the specific balance of qualities or humors in a substance</term> and property is simply unexplainable? Therefore, the Paracelsians are stuck in the same—indeed, deeper—mud. As for what they claim comes "from heaven," they are bound to prove this too, since it is not true simply because it seems so to them. Regarding the scorpion, the Paracelsians can give no better reason than the Galenists. In both cases, Sympathy and similarity have their reasons in hidden things. That a man loves gold is a specific and accidental case. He also loves girls, glory, estates, and many other things he thinks are good and pleasing, and often not for their own sake but by accident. Therefore, many who were quite famous for their wisdom have despised gold. If small stones were valuable in trade, gold, silver, and the like would become very cheap. Wine is so familiar to human nature that it sometimes even becomes a poison, whether you provide the whole substance or just its essence. Was this unknown to the ancients? Do the Paracelsians not "beg the question" <note>original: *petunt principium*; a logical fallacy where the conclusion is assumed in the premise.</note> when they say the spirit of wine is the cause? For why is this the cause? You will say "Heaven." But I ask for the principle of this as well. Thus, the Galenists can discuss the familiarity of gold and quicksilver better than the Paracelsians, even though the nature of mercury was unexplored by Galen himself.*

49. *That man estimated the power of others by his own impotence. If he had correctly understood Aristotle’s esoteric works, he could have discussed the nature of gold and quicksilver from them formally <note>original: *μοικῶς* (likely a typo for *νομικῶς*); meaning systematically or according to established principles.</note>. He could have taken specific details from the books "On Generation and Corruption," the fourth book of "Meteorology," and other Peripatetic explanations. Tell me, chemists, why do gold and quicksilver love each other? Is it not because they are of the same root, and differ only in imperfection and perfection, dissolution and coagulation, cooking and rawness—just like water and ice? Why is this? Is it not because their mixture and whole nature is such? Who are these Aristotelian philosophers who are "ignorant of friendship or Sympathy"? They knew it from the specific nature of things; they know about gunpowder and "fulminating gold" <note>original: *auro fulmineo*; an explosive chemical compound made from gold.</note>, and can give reasons for them from Aristotle. Therefore, the Peripatetics see far more sharply than the Paracelsians, who imagine they see what they do not see at all. The Paracelsian would like to hear the cause of these things from an Aristotelian philosopher. But you "know-it-alls" <note>original: *scioli*; people who have only a superficial knowledge of things.</note>, who think you are sharper than Aristotle, you speak first! We have read Croll <note>Oswald Croll, a famous Paracelsian physician and chemist.</note> on the hostility of Tartaric salt and sal ammoniac, but he does not satisfy.*

50. *If the chemical art is not added, the causes of all things cannot be explained from Aristotle’s philosophy. Indeed, I say, not even if the chemical art is added, as far as specific and proper causes are concerned. Regarding general principles, we hold only as much as can be proved. We have seen various writings of the Paracelsians on the causes of things. Truly, we have not found wisdom and truth in all of them.*

-> But so much for this. <-

-> End of the Preliminary Treatise on Vital Philosophy. <-

The author asserts that while chemistry is useful, it does not replace the foundational wisdom of Aristotle and Galen.</summary>

->To God, the Best and Greatest, with His Help<-

->*From the Preface of Oswald Croll to his Chemical Basilica*<-

# ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF PHILOSOPHY AND MEDICINE,

### THE FIRST INQUIRY.

#### SECTION I.

<margin>Croll's Astronomy.</margin>
<margin>Philosophy.</margin>
*Crollius* believes that to become and be judged a physician depends on the science of Philosophy and Astronomy (otherwise known as Physical and Surgical Medicine), according to the decrees of Paracelsus. He calls Astronomy the science of the upper mundane globe, which consists of the firmament or fire (for to Paracelsus, fire and heaven are the same) and air. Philosophy, however, he defines as the knowledge of the lower globe, consisting of earth and water; thus, all human knowledge is contained within those four mentioned elements. But as to where this knowledge is to be acquired, he neither agrees with himself, nor is Paracelsus certain. These are the Crollian tenets: 1. A physician is not made from books, because nature instructs him, NOT MAN, and it is necessary for him to grow out of nature. (Here, the learned Doctors—both living and dead—are rejected.) He elsewhere makes an exception for Theology and sacred justice, which cannot be known from nature. 2. It is necessary for a physician to know the invisible internal parts of man, which are likewise divided into two globes, one astral and the other earthly. Everything invisible in man becomes known from the visible things of the greater, or external, world. For everything is manifested in this. Therefore, the knowledge of all things hidden in man must be sought from without, through the contemplation of the Macrocosm <note>The "Macrocosm" refers to the universe as a whole, which Paracelsians believed was perfectly mirrored within the "Microcosm," or the human being.</note>. 3. It arises from the light of nature: that is, the analogy of the greater and lesser worlds, like the proportion between a Father (the greater world) and a son (man). 4. Man belongs to two worlds, and is a hidden world. The Macrocosm is the open world, etc. He disputes these and similar things with great pride, so that he may exclude the books of the ancients—Hippocrates, Galen, Aristotle, Avicenna, etc.—and oppose himself to the academic disciplines and lectures of both physicians and philosophers, from whom he denies that Philosophy and Medicine can be learned.

<margin>How one becomes a physician.</margin>
<margin>From where the invisible is revealed.</margin>
<margin>The analogy of the two worlds.</margin>
<margin>What is man? The Macrocosm.</margin>

---

#### SECTION II.

There is no need for a lengthy argument to refute these points. For Crollius struggles even against himself. For he writes on page 26: *Essential, intrinsic knowledge is not from flesh and blood, nor in a multitude of books and reading, nor in a plurality of experience and the antiquity of days, nor in the persuasions of the human word and the contention of reason: but in the very suffering of divine things;* NOT IN LEARNING: *but in suffering, the divine Science of man is perfected, etc.* As if indeed, when Enthusiasts <note>In this period, "Enthusiasts" referred to those claiming direct, private divine inspiration, often viewed with suspicion by the orthodox.</note> imagine spiritual inspirations for themselves, or as if by learning from the book *Arbatel* <note>A famous 16th-century grimoire of "spiritual magic" that claimed to teach how to summon planetary spirits.</note>—having rejected all ordinary Doctors and books—they seek for themselves assistant spirits <term>parolous</term> <note>original: *παρόλους*, likely a transcription error for *parhedros* (παρέδρους), meaning "familiars" or "supernatural assistants."</note>, that this is not "learning" but "suffering." This "Enthusiasm" is a diabolical suggestion. Furthermore, if that divine and intrinsic knowledge is to be acquired by suffering, it is also acquired by learning. For to learn is, in a way, to be acted upon and changed. Likewise: if this is the essential and intrinsic science, that which is sought from the macrocosm will not be *essential*, but *accidental*, and thus it will constitute neither a Philosopher nor a Physician in themselves, but only by chance. But if that knowledge of the invisible aspects of man through the visible things of the external world is also essential, it is clear that the "Enthusiastic" information is false. But this is not all: soon *he seeks the beginning of all knowledge not from without—whether from God or from the contemplation of the great world—but from man himself*, and that according to the opinion of *Cornelius Agrippa* <note>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa (1486–1535), a famous German polymath and occult writer.</note>, that wicked Magician. For he writes: *Man has within himself that by which he may know God and himself. Therefore Agrippa discourses piously and learnedly that the true way to attain true wisdom and perpetual happiness is if you know yourself, etc.* If true wisdom is born from man himself—which he previously called Philosophy and Astronomy—then it will not be true wisdom, or it is not to be sought from without. Again, he contradicts himself more openly on page 25: *The starry man draws wisdom and arts from the firmament. The firmament is the light of nature, which flows all things naturally into man. For the starry spirit of man attracts ALL ARTS, sciences, all faculties, and all wisdom from the rays of the higher stars, etc.* This is indeed seeking wisdom from without, from a created thing, but not through the analogy of the greater and lesser world, which is nothing other than the reasonings of both in some way...

<margin>The principle of all science is from man.</margin>
<margin>All wisdom is from the stars of the firmament.</margin>

<summary>This page critiques the Paracelsian physician Oswald Croll, highlighting his contradictions regarding whether true medical and philosophical knowledge is found within man, learned from the stars (Macrocosm), or received via direct divine inspiration.</summary>

The author contrasts the Paracelsian idea of innate, magical knowledge with the traditional view that wisdom must be acquired through effort, teachers, and established books.</meta>

<header>On Scientific Principles</header>

<margin>Knowledge from the light of Grace and nature.</margin> ...provided there is a comparison with what is already known. Otherwise, as stated on page 13: *The physician must be born from the light of Grace and nature from within, and in the invisible man. By the internal angel, the light of nature, which instructs man like a Teacher.* Also: *In man there is a true and real possession of all things and natures, and a PERFECT image of the creator.* 
<margin>Man understands all things.</margin> Therefore, it is necessary that the knowledge of all things, their natures, and the creator himself must first begin from oneself. *A man understanding himself understands all things in himself, because we are made of all things, and we carry all things within us, etc.* <note>original: "Homo intelligens ſeipſum, in ſeipſum intelligit omnia, quia ſumus ex omnibus, & omnia in nobis portamus, &c."</note> Here you might think that this knowledge of oneself is indeed necessary, but that it ought to be sought from the outside—from God, from the stars, from the contemplation of the great world—unless he said FIRST, and unless he made a progression from the things familiar to us toward other things which 
<margin>External things must be judged from what is implanted in us.</margin> are outside of us. From this, however, it follows that one ought to judge external things from those which are within man, and not the other way around, as the Paracelsians <term>Paracelsians: followers of the Swiss physician Paracelsus who emphasized alchemy and the "inner light" over traditional book-learning</term> wish. Add to these also these Paracelsian notions: that man received wisdom from the serpent of Paradise, according to the dogma of the <term>Ophites: an early Gnostic sect that believed the serpent in Eden was a bringer of knowledge rather than a tempter</term>, and that even today, by taking the medicine of the serpent elsewhere, he can become wise; and that Paracelsus seeks instructions from hags, executioners, flute-players, travelers, and even the devil himself, etc. He also orders the preparation of "constellated mirrors," <note>magical mirrors supposed to be forged under specific astrological alignments</note> in one of which all thoughts may be seen, in the second all deeds, and in the third all writings. Similar trifles concerning "constellated coins" were transferred from Agrippa, Trithemius, and other Magi. What about the "wisdom-art" of Techellus which he praises? You find the magic of Artephius in the works of Cardano. The book of Artabel seeks "informant spirits." Is this not a way of learning? What would all the Paracelsians know if there had not been the first wise men, and their books, schools, and Academies? Crollius, on page twenty-eight, even posits this: *All true Philosophy, along with the other faculties, must be founded on the sacred scriptures.* When the Professors of this Philosophy propose these monstrous things that mutually overthrow each other, it immediately becomes clear what should be thought of them.

---

-> S E C T I O N  I I I. <-

Wherefore, we judge that a false foundation has been laid under the whole of Paracelsianism, and consequently, whatever is built upon it cannot be true. By this alone falls everything they shout against the ordinary Physicians, Physicists, and Philosophers of the Academies and Republics, and the "Monarchy of Paracelsus" vanishes into an empty fabrication. They drew the principles of their knowledge from the usual schools. Now that they seem to themselves to be somebody, they seek new—or rather, they dream up for themselves—foundations, and these are so uncertain that they do not know what should be established. They want others to learn in a way that they themselves never learned. What vanity is this? We know it is true that *all true wisdom is from the Father of Lights*, <note>original: "omnis vera ſapientia ſit à Patre luminum" - a reference to James 1:17</note> who also infused it into man at the first creation, and afterward often taught him immediately concerning divine matters and some other things; but after the Fall, it pleased Him not to always teach immediately. Therefore, He instituted the ordinary way by which children should learn from parents, the younger from the elder, and the unlearned from the learned, as all the histories of the world and the consensus of nations testify. This happens either by living tradition, or oral instruction, as in catechetical discipline; or primarily through monuments—that is, books, written partly in hieroglyphs and partly in common letters—which also hold authority by divine command. Meanwhile, once the basics have been perceived through teaching, the contemplation of nature must not be neglected. For in this too are instructions of hidden things and matters most worthy of knowledge, by which that which our ancestors left to us, established by reason and experience, is also partly confirmed. 
<margin>The Book of Paul the Hermit.</margin> <note>This likely refers to the "Book of Nature," a concept often associated with solitary contemplatives.</note> Therefore, *we have both the book of nature and of scripture.* In both cases, the younger require information from the elders who excel in wisdom and skill. For the slip from truth into opinion is easy, as is the slip from essential wisdom to that which only seems to be wisdom but is actually deception and sophistry. Add to this that the younger, on account of their lack of experience and their lightness and rashness in giving assent, are easily seduced by sophists; and that Satan, pretending to be an angel of light and distributing his apostles everywhere, busies himself with crushing the truth with lies. This is manifest from so many sects of sacred and profane matters, and indeed from the prophecies of the ancients, and from experience and ocular intuition, so that the older the world grows, the 
<margin>Paracelsianism: a chaos of Sophistical deceptions.</margin> greater the abundance of conflicts. In Medicine certainly, and in the Philosophical sciences, is not Paracelsianism a distinguished monument of falsehood? If Doctors and books are not to be used, why do the Paracelsians write, and why do they want us to agree with the opinions of Paracelsus—whom Crollius praises more than hyperbolically and no less vainly with the most evident lie—and, having cast aside Galenic Medicine, to profess Paracelsianism? Not in vain were the arts and sciences established and described through scientific axioms and a universal method; and if workshops and the shops of craftsmen are compelled to use instructional or masterly information, shall these things, which are the most difficult, be permitted to the judgment of anyone's imagination according to the external world? If there are as many opinions as there are heads once the ordinary way of learning is abolished, how much confusion will arise? Take away masters and books, and let each one philosophize by himself, and you will have a "beautiful" philosophy. Moreover, this is itself one of the devil’s attempts: to either abolish or pervert every order of learning, so that he himself may rule according to his own will. The Paracelsians also consume themselves with mutual conflicts. For the same thing does not please everyone. *Paracelsus had exploded Lully and other Chemists older than himself.* Crollius wonderfully commends Lully; truly, in a manifest matter, there is no need for more words, since this is also certain: that there is hardly any Paracelsian who understands Paracelsus without an interpreter; let us say nothing of the necessary languages, which do not exist without discipline. Crollius surely denies that his mind is open from the written letter, which he calls "dead," as if he had poured out oracles; indeed, so that they might find a cover for the impieties, follies, and absurdities heaped onto paper by him, they therefore ascribe mysteries to him.

---

-> S E C T I O N  I V. <-

<margin>Measure.</margin> Aristotle, in the 10th book of the *Metaphysics*, text 3, writes concerning measure: *besides that by which quantity is known, even in other things, as it is said generally, it is that by which each thing is first known, so that it is...*

<meta>catchword: so that it is</meta>

<summary>This page critiques the Paracelsian "Light of Nature" theory, arguing that true knowledge requires tradition, books, and teachers rather than internal "magic" or occult mirrors. The author asserts that the Paracelsian rejection of academic structure leads to intellectual chaos and is a deceptive tactic to hide their own logical inconsistencies.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of Paracelsian philosophy, moving from the internal "Light of Nature" to how knowledge is validated. The author compares the Paracelsian worldview to ancient Greek skepticism and defends the value of traditional medical and philosophical texts against the radical "newness" claimed by Oswald Croll and his followers.</meta>

# Paracelsian Magic according to Croll.
### B 2
<meta>catchword: ages</meta>

*...so that there may be a certain single principle, to which nothing should be added nor taken away, always uniform, from which both science and sense are said to be the measure of things, because through them we recognize something.* Protagoras wished man to be the measure of all things, as if he were saying that the one who perceives and knows is the measure of <term>πάντων <note>original: "panton"; meaning "of all things"</note></term>, because knowledge and sense are the measure. These ideas are understood in different ways. By nature, a twofold knowledge—or faculty of knowing—resides in man: one is *sensual*, the other *intellectual*. By the former, we know individual things which exist in matter; by the latter, we know universals abstracted from matter, such as ideas and mental images received from the senses. If the senses err, it is necessary that the intellect also errs, and the result is not truth, but mere opinion. 

If the Paracelsians <term>Paracelsians: followers of Paracelsus (1493–1541) who rejected traditional medicine in favor of alchemy and direct observation of nature</term> think that their own intellect and sense are the measures of things—such that things are to be judged and named exactly as they appear to their own senses and intellect, and not as they have been judged by the "Teachers" or interpreters of nature, both living and dead—then they must protect us from errors regarding their own senses and prove that they are absolutely infallible. 

God indeed granted this to Adam: that he might impose names upon things according to his own knowledge and sense, because he was then in possession of his full powers and was guided by divine protection. But we cannot attribute this to Paracelsus. If we follow him, we fall more easily into the factions of the Pyrrhonists, the Sceptics, Heraclitus, Plato, and similar thinkers, who wished man to be the measure of things in such a way that whatever "seemed" true to anyone was true. Since this would result in *as many sciences as there are heads*, everything would ultimately vanish into uncertainty. Even the Platonists, seeing the changeability of material things, withdrew all trust from the senses and gave the "New Academy" <term>New Academy: a period of ancient Greek skepticism that argued absolute truth is impossible to grasp</term> the occasion to decree that *nothing can be comprehended*. 

By this Crollian <note>Oswald Croll (c. 1563–1609) was a prominent Paracelsian physician and chemist</note> way of knowing, the Paracelsians have fallen so far that they seem to seek almost "all things in all things," and through infinite similarity, they reduce all science to uncertainty. They themselves vary wonderfully in their own "masteries," essences, and unique principles. If the things in man are to be estimated and described only by the similarities found in the "greater world" <term>greater world: the Macrocosm or the universe at large, which Paracelsians believed was mirrored in the "microcosm" of the human body</term>, it is impossible for the knowledge of man to be certain; thus, it would not even be science at all. For to "know" is not to recognize a thing based on some external comparison, but from inherent, proper, and immovable causes. These causes are understood through definitions, scientific principles, reason, and observed experience, and are validated by the judgment and sense of the wise, to whom the Holy Spirit has bestowed that peculiar gift (for the same is not granted to everyone, nor can everyone judge rightly of things within or outside themselves).

<margin>
*The Paracelsians believe things are exactly as they perceive them.*
</margin>

---

-> S E C T I O N  V. <-

<margin>
*Whether the variety among writers means that all books should be rejected.*
</margin>

**Y**ET the book *Arbatel* <note>a 16th-century magical manual or grimoire</note> objects, and the Paracelsians follow it, claiming there is such great discord among the opinions of the Doctors and among books both ancient and modern, that you cannot find a place to rest your mind, and thus you cannot profit from them in wisdom. Whatever Aristotle did not establish, another refutes, and those who follow always tear down the decrees of those who came before. 

Furthermore, they deny that the kindness of the ancients has exhausted the cause of knowing. They claim something is left for the "final world" <note>the current age</note> which, because it has departed far from the integrity of the former world due to the vice of old age, causes the observations of the ancients to fail. They argue that in the face of new diseases and a new constitution of the world, new medical principles and new remedies must be sought—not from the books of the ancients, but from the nature of the present world. They are fond of inventing such "splendid" reasons. 

To these we respond first with the words of Paul in *1 Corinthians 11:19*: *It is necessary that there be heresies among you, so that those who are approved may be made manifest among you.* If controversies are not lacking in sacred matters, yet it does not follow that truth succumbs; rather, it is tested more and more, like gold in the fire. We should not think that controversy will be or has been lacking in Philosophy, nor that truth is overwhelmed by it—rather, it shines more brightly. It is shameful to desert one's post when the enemy attacks. It is therefore shameful for medical and physical truth to be abandoned by its professors while sophistry spreads. 

Doctors and books disagree; but this disagreement, through comparison, is useful for recognizing the light of truth. Truth is constant and consistent with itself: *Everything counterfeit is varied and changeable.* There are, however, certain reliable norms for judging well, which we will discuss in the following pages. Your judgment does not become true simply because you despise the judgments of others. Simply because Paracelsus repudiated everyone before him and threw away their books, his decrees are not to be received as true. Just because he seems to himself to seek the foundations of Magic, Kabbalah, Chemistry, and Astrology from the "light of nature" of the greater world, it does not mean the things he seeks are actually foundations. 

Indeed, if the decrees of the ancients are no longer valid for us, why do the Paracelsians recommend the doctrine of Hippocrates, the writings of *Hermes* <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note> and others, and borrow both their facts and their vocabulary? Why do they differ from Avicenna, Aristotle, Galen, and others on various dogmas, yet still preserve their terms, as well as their simple and compound medicines for the most part? Is it because *Rhubarb* and similar things purged people for the ancients, but do not do so for us? Do we have different strengthening nutrients, antidotes, or poisons? Nature has changed in some respects, but not so much that we have a different sun, different herbs, and the rest than the ancients had. We live on herbs and bread; we eat milk, cheese, and meat, just like the descendants of Noah. If the ancients had that "universal chemical" <note>the Philosopher's Stone or Elixir</note> for a long life, does it not work for the same purpose among us? 

Is it a "new" thing that like is preserved by like, like is produced by like, and contrary by contrary—not only in qualities, but in the way arts are removed by themselves or by accident? Certainly, the faculty of finding new things has not been denied to us, since nature is inexhaustible; yet that does not do away with the praiseworthy and constant discoveries of the ancients. The Paracelsians wait for "Elias the Artist" <term>Elias the Artist: a legendary figure in alchemy predicted to appear and reveal all secrets of the art</term> who is to bring all things into the light. They understand this to refer to the obscure traditions of the ancients, especially regarding the Philosophy of the Stone. If the world has changed so much that ancient things are no longer valid, what is the purpose of this Elias as a "manifester"? 

It is a foul and shameful <term>κενοφωνεῖν <note>original: "kenophonein"; meaning "to speak empty words" or "vain babbling"</note></term> that they put forward concerning the change of the world—that new sciences and arts must therefore be invented. New diseases are "new" only in a certain respect. What does Ecclesiastes say in Chapter 1? *What is that which has been?* he says, *The same that shall be. What is that which has been done? The same that shall be done. There is nothing new under the sun, nor can anyone say, "Look, this is fresh." For it has already preceded us in the ages past.*

<margin>
*Controversies illuminate judgment; they are not a reason for the rejection of books.*
</margin>

<summary>This page critiques the Paracelsian rejection of traditional authority, arguing that their reliance on subjective "inner light" leads to skepticism and that nature remains constant enough that ancient medical wisdom is still valid.</summary>
<keywords>Protagoras, sensory knowledge, intellectual knowledge, Oswald Croll, New Academy, Arbatel, Elias the Artist, nature, medical tradition, Ecclesiastes</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the critique of Paracelsian philosophy, moving from the "measure of all things" to the argument that their "new" discoveries are merely recycled ancient errors. The author asserts that true knowledge comes through the "ordinary" path of education, logic, and tradition, rather than the "divine infusions" claimed by Oswald Croll and his followers.</meta>

# On Scientific Principles
### 16

*...centuries before us, etc.* It is a common saying: *Nothing can be said that has not been said before.* <note>Reference to the Roman playwright Terence and the biblical book of Ecclesiastes.</note> Is more faith to be placed in Paracelsus than in Solomon and public opinion? Aristotle in Book 1 of his *Meteorology*, chapter 3 says: "Not once or twice, nor rarely do we say that the same opinions return to men, but an infinite number of times." Therefore, these same fictions existed before Paracelsus. The devil played this fable through others before him, and he put it back on stage through Paracelsus, so that he invented nothing new. Our ignorance makes us think a disease is new, when it is not. The French disease <note>Syphilis</note>, the Hungarian disease <note>likely typhus</note>, and similar ones are not so significant that the principles of medicine must be changed. These diseases existed once before, and they are already healed according to the precepts of ancient medicine, which are by no means abolished by the new preparation of remedies—not to mention that chemical treatments are repeated from great antiquity and are not new remedies for new diseases.

### S E C T I O VI.

<margin>One must learn in the ordinary way where there are no singular revelations or infusions of grace.</margin>If we must speak in the ancient and ordinary way, used by the whole world, how can a student be certain that what he learns is true? The Paracelsians themselves teach by their own example that one must learn no other way. For who among them did not learn their letters from some teacher, whether public or private? How many of them do not hunt for titles of "Doctor of both laws" <note>Civil and Canon law</note> from academic privileges? For those whom they call "bull-bearing" <note>titled by official decree, often implying they bought the title</note> are Doctors in name but not in reality or by law; they often become such for money, which does not take the donkey out of a man. When they also boast of their travels and conversations with various craftsmen, is this not learning from men? As for certainty, from where you know your own literacy is certain, seek from that same source the certainty of the rest of the arts and sciences. You know it from precepts established by long use, and confirmed again by practice, and you know it by the judgment and consensus of the wise. So that you might be able to know, **GOD** has enriched you with the good of nature. Learn sincere Dialectic <note>Logic</note>, which is the rule for both discovery and judgment. Ask God for Wisdom and the illumination of the light of nature. Listen to the wise, and compare their decrees with the decrees of others and with the things themselves. You have the book of nature, which is perpetually the same and consistent with itself. Learn to discern accidents from essences. Inspect the book of Scripture, in which you find much for judging the creation correctly. Even Croll, on page 28, acknowledges this when he writes: *All true philosophy, along with the other faculties, ought to be founded on the sacred letters.* If you have grasped the principles of useful arts, deduce conclusions from them by infallible demonstration according to the legitimate use of reason; and compare appearances and falsehoods with what is necessary and true. From there, let the truth be revealed. He who learns in our time reaches knowledge more concisely than the first men. For he already has the principles discovered and established, which are not to be recklessly uprooted, but accepted unless they are refuted by the sacred [scriptures] or by more evident facts. To be taught immediately through divine infusions and prophetic visions belongs only to certain persons. Those who tempt God by rejecting the ordinary way fall into the magical snares of the devil or are seduced by their own opinions. Certain universal <term>criteria <note>original: "κριτήρια" (kriteria); standards of judgment</note></term> are commonly proposed, such as: knowledge of principles (partly innate, partly acquired), demonstration from principles, common sense, public experience, and the consistent judgment of wise men regarding the same thing for the same reason.

To these, certain keys are required, such as the understanding of the languages of the wise, the interpretation of the Doctors, comparison, the illumination of the spirit, and frequent exercise, which produces a habit—and, as was said before, after divine grace: Logic, Rhetoric, and Grammar. In sacred matters, we must not depart from **GOD's** commands and the Word, neither to the right nor to the left. For Truth is the Word of God, the beginning. Whatever agrees with the analogy of faith is right; what disagrees is rejected. Come then now to the physics and medicine of Aristotle, Plato, Hippocrates, Avicenna, Galen, etc. Doubt or wonder was the cause of philosophizing for all men in human affairs. Use the aforementioned <term>criteria <note>original: "κριτήρια"</note></term>, and separate the essential things from the <margin>See Plato in the *Philebus*, *Greater Hippias*, *Timaeus*, etc. on the ancients. Hippocrates's *On Ancient Medicine*, *On the Nature of Man*, *On the Sacred Disease*, *On Diet*, *On the Art*, etc.</margin>accidental. You will indeed find some things false and adulterated, but those things which are intrinsic to the sciences, you will find to be entirely true. Consider also: if you attribute to yourself the knowledge of external nature, this same knowledge was not lacking in them, even if your method differs; since you judge yourself to be sensible and intelligent, consider that they also possessed sense and intellect. You do not wish your own decrees to be trampled upon, even though they are not as esteemed as theirs. Why would you wish to despise their ancient works? What if it is a labor to understand them, and you err in your interpretation? Truly, either the Paracelsian ideas are the same as the ancient ones, differing only in the manner of tradition and preparation, or they are accidental, or they are false.

### S E C T I O VII.

<margin>Croll is a disciple of Paracelsus and P. Severinus: and thus of men.</margin>Let us come now to a more specific consideration of certain opinions regarding this matter. <term>Croll</term>, following the author <term>Severinus the Dane</term>, divides the knowable things of the macrocosm <note>the universe</note> (for he himself learned from a **man**; by what right, he ought to have seen) into things of the upper and lower spheres, and he wants two others in the microcosm <note>man</note> to correspond to them; the microcosm's task is to know those external things, and from there, as if from a mirror, to return into itself and contemplate its own invisible internal parts. He is a defender of the "Panspermia" <note>the theory that the seeds of all things are contained in all things</note> of Anaxagoras against Aristotle, as if *it were true that the seeds of all things are in all things*. (This is against that Physics in Aristotle, book two of *On the Soul*, chapter four, text thirty-four: *The most natural of works in perfect, univocal living beings is to make another like itself, or similar to itself*, although in the meantime nothing prevents Plato's "prime matter" from being a certain <term>Pandochæon <note>original: "Πανδοχεῖον" (Pandochēon); meaning "all-receiver" or universal container</note></term>, which is all things in nature potentially, etc. Book 3 of *On the Soul*, chapter five, text seventeen.) Without a doubt, he also wished to act as the Patron of <term>Hermes Trismegistus</term> <note>a legendary Hellenistic figure associated with alchemy and magic</note>, whom he did not fear to prefer even to some Christians,

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of Oswald Croll and Paracelsian philosophy. Having previously argued that Paracelsian "discoveries" are merely recycled ancient errors, the author now attacks their core epistemological claim: that "like is known by like." He contrasts the Paracelsian view of the macrocosm-microcosm relationship with Aristotelian logic and traditional physics.</meta>

# Paracelsian Magic according to Croll. 17
### B 3

...to bring forward, as did Empedocles. *Like is always known by its like*, says the <term>Pimander</term> <note>A foundational text of Hermeticism, often attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</note> in Dialogue 11. Empedocles too, as cited by Aristotle in the first book of *On the Soul*, text twenty-five, composes all things from the elements, and affirms that <note>original: "terramque terra cognosci, aquam aqua"</note> *earth is known by earth, and water by water* (that is, the lower globe of the <term>macrocosm</term> <note>The "great world" or the universe.</note> is known by the lower globe of the <term>microcosm</term> <note>The "little world," or the human being.</note>), *ether by ether, fire by fire, harmony by harmony, and discord by discord* (the upper globe by the upper), like by like, as stated in text 76 of the first book *On the Soul* (the refutation of which you may find in text 79 of the same book). If this was the mind of **Croll** derived from the *Pimander* and Empedocles, then he learned it from men, not from the nature of the macrocosm. They may say it is so, but by what sensible demonstration, or at least by what logic derived from unshakable principles? It is a common habit of the Paracelsians to <margin>Paracelsians either invent or assume the very principles they are trying to prove in their doctrines.</margin> *beg the question* <note>A logical fallacy where the conclusion is assumed in the premises.</note> regarding their principles and to create *fictions of uncertain hypotheses*; but they find no belief among wise evaluators of things. Let there be another opinion, namely: that the soul knows neither itself nor its body by its own power alone; yet it possesses a certain faculty that can "become all things," as Aristotle disputes regarding the intellect. Therefore, when the images of higher and lower things creep in through the external senses—as if through the windows of the soul—these images will indeed excite the motivating phantasms of the intellect, but they will not teach that there are things within the human being that are either the same as or similar to the external ones. For any species can, by itself, indicate nothing more than what it is, and how it exists in external things. Whether there is the same or a similar thing within the human being, it cannot show, unless (as has been said) we accept the opinion of the *Pimander* and Empedocles. The Paracelsians say this is so, but they do so by the "faith" of Paracelsus, without any underlying principle. Indeed, it would be a wonder if a human being could not know that he exists, <margin>A person ought to know himself first, and from there determine external things, not the other way around.</margin> and feels, and suffers, and understands, and does other things, unless he first learned it from the "greater world." And as far as medicine is concerned, it would mean a person would not know whether he is sick or healthy, or what disease he suffers from, or how he is nourished, etc., unless he was first instructed by the macrocosm. Who would dare to scatter such monstrosities in writing, unless he were a madman?

-> S E C T I O  V I I I. <-

<margin>Severinian paradoxes based on mere fantasy and false comparisons.</margin> Now, is it correct that the "firmament" or "fire" are contained in the upper globe of the macrocosm, while earth and water are in the lower? So the Paracelsians wish it. But by what principle? By which "microcosmic globes" did they learn this? By foolish ones. Their reasoning should have been: *In man, the heaven and the spirit are one globe. Therefore, they are so in the world.* But this was a backwards way of reasoning. For the knowledge of ourselves would then arise from within us, not from the macrocosm. If we must judge from the macrocosm, then their distribution is false. For in the "greater world," the upper globe is only the heaven, which ends at the place of the moon; the lower is the entire sublunary realm. I say nothing of how mathematical measurement and observation show the evidence of the matter; I say only that the globes are correctly distinguished by a perpetual and general division: namely, into celestial natures and elementary natures. <term>Chymia</term> <note>Early modern chemistry or alchemy.</note> teaches the same, as it separates the <term>quintessences</term> <note>The "fifth essence," a pure, refined substance distinct from the four elements.</note> from the elements by their natures and virtues. Aristotle also added the roles of Ruler and Subject: *the heaven is the ruler; the elementary things are ruled.* We feel the heaven "flowing in" (influx); we see the air and the elements <note>original: "systecha," likely a corruption of the Greek 'stoicheia'.</note> receiving this influx. The former is more formal, the latter are material, etc. There are altogether many arguments by which the same is demonstrated, and it is inherently absurd if the air is part of the "upper globe," for in it all corruptible things live and move (except for fish and the minerals of solid places).

Sacred Scripture sometimes designates the air by the word "heaven," but only as a figure of speech. A philosopher must distinguish natures scientifically. Plato assigned the air to neither the upper nor the lower globe, but placed it as a bond between both extremes. In the twenty-sixth chapter of Job, verse seven, he writes that GOD stretched out the North (that is, the heaven) over the empty space (that is, the air), which is called the "great void" by the poets and the ancient physicists.

One heaven is that in which the stars reside; another is the air beneath the heaven, so closely related to the corruptible elements that it is both born from water and congeals back into water. Nevertheless, it seems **Croll** used some argument besides the opinion of **Severinus** <note>Petrus Severinus, a prominent Danish Paracelsian physician.</note>. For those <term>Meteors</term> <note>In this period, "meteors" referred to any atmospheric phenomena, including rain, wind, and lightning.</note> which occur in the air are sometimes called the *fruits of heaven*, and sometimes *excrement* (for the stars are nourished, and they pour out the remains of their food into the air as if into a sewer, according to the judgment of **Paracelsus**, who did not learn this from the macrocosm, but took it from the microcosm and applied it to the macrocosm).

However, fruits ought to be joined to their own plants, unless perhaps they fall of their own accord, or are shaken off by force and seek another place. Therefore, the air is part of the upper globe. Both the principle of this reasoning and the conclusion are false. It relies on a doubtful similarity and an arbitrary comparison. Experience itself teaches, both in Chemistry and outside of it, that these "meteors" are the coagulations of vapors rising from the lower globe; it is established that the same happens in the microcosm according to analogy, as shown by distillations or <term>catarrhs</term> <note>A buildup of mucus; here used as a physiological analogy for rain or dew.</note> in humans, which <margin>Aristotle, *On the Parts of Animals*, Book 2, Chapter 7.</margin> Aristotle himself compared to external phenomena.

-> S E C T I O  I X. <-

But perhaps we labor in vain. For the Paracelsians, like **Vertumnus** <note>A Roman god of seasons and change, famous for his ability to transform into any shape.</note>, can transform themselves into a thousand faces so as not to be caught. It is not those mundane globes and bodies obvious to the senses that they mean, as some think. Their words are **Magic-Cabalistic**. Thus, invisible spheres enclosed within the visible ones like chests or boxes must be understood, based on the foundation of **Anaxagoras's** <term>Panspermia</term> <note>The theory that "seeds" of all things are present throughout the universe.</note> or the **Platonic chaos**. For all stars are in all things, as **Croll** also pleased to say; they are "secrets," "tinctures," "seeds," the "philosophical heaven" with fixed stars, "lulliones," eternal fires, and the like—such as are prepared in chemical laboratories and vessels, and elicited from the external elements.

He argues that the foundations of science are ancient and universal, rather than revealed through mystical infusions as the Paracelsians claim.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>
<header>On Scientific Principles</header>

*...centuries before us, etc.* It is a common saying: Nothing can be said that has not been said before. Is more faith to be placed in Paracelsus than in Solomon and public opinion? Aristotle in book 1 of *Meteorology*, chapter 3 says: *Not once or twice, nor rarely, do we say that the same opinions return to men, but infinitely many times.* Therefore, these same fictions existed before Paracelsus. The same drama the devil performed through others before him, he has restaged through Paracelsus, so that he has devised nothing new. It is our ignorance that makes us think a disease is new, when it is not. The French Pox <note>Syphilis</note>, the Hungarian disease <note>Likely typhus</note>, and similar ones are not so significant that we must change the principles of medicine. These diseases existed in the past and are now cured according to the ancient precepts of medicine, which are by no means abolished by the new preparation of remedies; to say nothing of the fact that chemical remedies are repeated from great antiquity and are not "new remedies for new diseases."

---

### SECTION VI.

<margin>One must learn in the ordinary way when there are no singular revelations or infusions of grace.</margin> If, therefore, we must speak in the ancient and ordinary way used by the whole world, how can a student be certain that what he learns is true? The Paracelsians themselves teach by their own example that one must learn in no other way. For which of them did not learn his letters from some teacher, whether public or private? How few of them do not hunt for titles in both laws <note>Doctor of Civil and Canon Law</note> from Academic privileges? For those they call "bullated" <note>Those holding a diploma or "bulla" without true study</note> are Doctors in name but not in reality or law; they often become such for money, which "does not remove the donkey" <note>A proverb meaning a degree does not make a fool wise</note>. When they even boast of their travels and conversations with various craftsmen, is this not learning from men? As for certainty: from wherever you know your literacy is certain, seek there also the certainty of the remaining arts and sciences. You know it from precepts established by long use and proven again by use; you know it by the judgment and consensus of the wise. 

So that you might know, God has endowed you with the good of nature. Learn sincere Dialectic <note>Logic</note>, which is the rule for both discovery and judgment. Ask God for Wisdom and the illumination of the "light of nature." Listen to the wise, and compare their opinions with those of others and with reality itself. You have the "book of nature," which is always the same and consistent with itself. Learn to discern "accidents" <note>Non-essential qualities</note> from "essences." Look into the book of Scripture, in which you also find much for judging the creature correctly. Even Croll acknowledges this on page 28, when he writes: *All true philosophy, along with the other faculties, must be founded upon Holy Scripture.* If you have attained the true principles of the arts, deduce conclusions from them by infallible demonstration according to the legitimate use of reason; and compare appearances and falsehoods with what is necessary and true. Then let the truth be revealed. 

He who learns in our time reaches knowledge more concisely than the first men. For he already has discovered and established principles, which are not to be rashly overturned, but accepted unless they are refuted by sacred authority or more evident principles. To be educated immediately through divine infusions and prophetic visions belongs only to certain persons. Those who tempt God by rejecting the ordinary way fall into the magical snares of the devil or are seduced by their own opinions. Commonly, certain universal <term>criteria</term> <note>original Greek: κριτήρια</note> are proposed, such as: knowledge of principles partly innate and partly acquired, demonstration from principles, common sense, public experience; and the judgment of the wise concerning the same thing for the same reason.

Certain keys are required for these things: such as understanding the language of the wise, the interpretation of Doctors, comparison, the illumination of the spirit, and frequent practice, which produces a habit; and as said before, after divine grace: Dialectic, Rhetoric, and Grammar. In sacred matters, you know not to depart from God’s commands and Word, neither to the right nor to the left. For Truth, the Word of God, is the principle. Whatever agrees with the "analogy of faith" is right; what disagrees is rejected. Come now, then, to the physics and medicine of Aristotle, Plato, Hippocrates, Avicenna, Galen, etc. Doubt or wonder was the cause for all to philosophize in human affairs. Use the aforementioned <term>criteria</term>, and separate essentials from accidents. <margin>See Plato in *Philebus*, *Greater Hippias*, *Timaeus*, etc. on the ancients. Hippocrates in *On Ancient Medicine*, *On the Nature of Man*, *On Fleshes*, *On Diet*, *On the Art*, etc.</margin> You will indeed find some false and adulterated things, but those which are intrinsic to the sciences, you will find to be entirely true. Consider also: if you attribute to yourself knowledge of external nature, consider that they did not lack it either, even if your method differs; but if you judge yourself to be perceptive and intelligent, think that they also had senses and intellect. You do not wish your own opinions to be trampled, even if they are not as well-regarded as theirs. Why would you wish to despise the old things? What if it is a labor to understand them, and you err in your interpretation? Truly, either Paracelsian ideas are the same as the ancient ones—differing only in tradition and preparation—or they are non-essential, or they are false.

### SECTION VII.

<margin>Croll is a disciple of Paracelsus and P. Severinus, and thus of men.</margin> Let us come now to a more specific consideration of certain opinions regarding it. Oswald Croll, following the author Severinus the Dane <note>Petrus Severinus, an influential Danish Paracelsian physician</note>, divides the things to be known of the <term>macrocosm</term> (for he himself learned from a MAN, by what right he should have seen) into things of the upper and lower globe. He wants two others in the <term>microcosm</term> to correspond to them, from which, as if from a mirror, one might return into oneself and contemplate one's own invisible interior. He is a defender of the <term>panspermia</term> <note>The theory that "seeds" of all things are present in all things</note> of Anaxagoras against Aristotle, as if it were true that *the seeds of all things are in all things*. (Against this, see Aristotle’s *On the Soul*, book 2, chapter 4, text 34: *It is the most natural of works for perfect, univocal living things to make another like itself.*) Nevertheless, nothing prohibits Plato’s "prime matter" from being a certain <term>Pandochaon</term> <note>Greek: πανδοχέα, "all-receiver" or universal container</note> which is potentially all things in nature (see *On the Soul*, book 3, chapter 5, text 17). Doubtless Croll also wanted to act as the patron of Hermes Trismegistus, whom he did not fear to place even before some Christians.

### with examples produced from Croll.

fever. From this, you might easily conclude that God has a double body and a single soul. But since you even make it fourfold, and you consider man according to the elements, and the elements within him and outside him, and you devise four principles as well, why do you not call it a "Quadriunity"? You would have an opportunity from the <term>Kabbalah</term><note>A school of Jewish mysticism; here the author refers to the four-letter name of God, the Tetragrammaton.</note> of "Jehovah," where there are four letters; and yet you wrote "IHSV" <note>original: "IHSV Hadiecta afpiratione"</note> with an added "H," undoubtedly based on the foundation of your magic. But you and your "Triunity" must be hissed off the stage—a Triunity which, as was shown above, you even make fivefold on page 18.

You assign the image of that Triunity to all bodies. *For the holy Triunity (you say) through its "Three-in-One" brought-forth word, "let it be," created all things as Triune, as the <term>spagyric analysis</term><note>The alchemical process of separating and recombining the components of a substance.</note> bears witness, etc.* Whereby all things will bear the image of the *Triune God*. You are ridiculous, however, when you indicate that some <term>spagyrists</term><note>Alchemists.</note> add a fourth—*namely, making a spirit necessary*—and you assume a *primary matter*, then soon include your Triunity within it, and thirdly *separate those three species into four elements*, in the same way that a craftsman makes <term>minium</term><note>Red lead.</note>, <term>ceruse</term><note>White lead.</note>, glass, and <term>spirit of Saturn</term><note>Lead acetate.</note>, etc., out of a single piece of lead; so that the world is "coagulated smoke" made of sulfur, salt, and mercury.

<margin>The Paracelsian constitution of man.</margin>
Again, when you architect man on page 30, you write that *he is a fifth being out of all created things, out of which he is fashioned and made Prince, and the end of all things; who on the part of the body, or the corruptible, bears the image of the sensible <term>macrocosm</term><note>The "great world" or the universe.</note> and the temporal; on the part of the soul, or the immortal nature, he bears the image of the archetypal world, or the immortal wisdom of God, etc.* How can man, through his three parts—*the elemental body, the sidereal body, and the soul*—be the image of the divine trinity, and yet with that same mortal body also be the image of the corruptible world? How is it that *only the soul* reflects the wisdom of the creator (which is the Son of God, the Word through which all things were made), while the other parts reflect only the world? Where is that Triunity now? When man is also decomposed into seven or eight corporeal parts after the soul departs, where is the Triunity? For when you say *all things were made Triune, are even the Angels and the devils, and the soul of man, and the ether and other simplest things Triune?* Are your principles triune, and the sun, and the rest of the stars? What about the "accidents" created? *What alchemical analysis proves that all created things (for you except nothing) are triune?* If indeed <margin>Chemistry separates more than three things from a mixture.</margin> <term>Spagyria</term> testifies that there are more than three things in mixtures, your Triunity falls: just as <term>Tartar</term> is separated from wine; and in it is Salt, and "virgin earth": then a *spirit* analogous to ether is drawn out: furthermore a *fatty oil*, and a *mercurial liquor*.

These are five things. From <term>vitriol</term><note>Sulfuric acid or a sulfate mineral.</note> first comes out an elemental *wateriness*: then a *mercurial liquor*: thirdly an *acid spirit*, fourthly a very sharp *red oil*; fifthly *Salt*: sixthly a *tincture* from red earth; seventhly the *earth* itself. The <term>phlegm</term><note>A watery distillation product.</note> and the final earth are reckoned among the elements: the other five are essentials.

Truly, God sent a "spirit of dizziness" upon those magicians so that they do not know where to rest. <term>Quercetanus</term><note>Joseph Duchesne (1544–1609), a prominent French physician and chemist.</note> and others also produce more things of different natures from Lead, Antimony, <term>Chalcanthum</term><note>Blue vitriol or copper sulfate.</note> and the like, so that you should not boast so much of your Triunity.

<margin>The two spirits of man.</margin>
18. Let us also consider those things which *Croll* cries out on page 31. *Man, he says, is visible according to the body: invisible according to the spirit. There are two spirits in man: one "sidereal" from the firmament: the other from the "breath of life," which is the intellectual soul inspired by God.* Thus man has three parts: *a mortal body, a sidereal spirit, and an eternal soul, which is the dwelling place of the image of God, or the Holy Spirit in man, etc.* Who does not see that the writer is raving? The Triune essence of God was previously prefigured by the three parts of man, so that the image in which man was created was in all three: soon it was in the three principles of all created things: now it is only in the soul of man; elsewhere even in the angels. What is true, or not true? *The soul is also called the dwelling place of the image of God, or the Holy Spirit.* Surely the primary reason for the "image of God" lies not in the essence of the soul, but in its qualities: holiness, justice, wisdom, leadership, etc.

Likewise, its principal functions: intellect, mind, will, and reason. Certain things are common to every body, such as sensory knowledge, dominion over beasts, etc. But as far as the "dwelling place" is concerned, first a distinction must be made between the godly and the ungodly: for the Holy Spirit does not dwell in both. Secondly, why are the Father and the Son excluded, who themselves will also come and dwell in the hearts of believers as if in temples? <term>Trismegistus</term><note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom many mystical and alchemical works were attributed.</note> says in Chapter 3 of the *Asclepius*: "One part of man is simple, a form of divine likeness." Chapter 4: "God made man first from himself, and second by himself." Chapter 6: "The first God is the Lord of eternity: the World is the second God, Man the third. Man knows himself and the world. There are two images of God: man and the world, etc." These impious things are more pleasing to the impious Paracelsians.

But what else does Magic decree concerning man? *When man lives by his own or an arrogant will, at least according to the flesh, he is sensually a brute, a dog, a fox, a sheep, a wolf, a pig, a viper, etc.* You err, Magician. Those metaphors or allegories do not arise from his *being*, but from a comparison of similarity. Furthermore, if a man lives according to the passions of the flesh, he does not cease to be a man; he only loses his reason and the name of "spiritual," and receives the title of "carnal." Thus the Corinthians, when arguing among themselves, were not brutes, but men called carnal because of their discord (1 Corinthians 3:2-3). As for what you say about the sheep and the viper, have you not read that the Apostles and the rest of the faithful are *called the sheep of Christ?* Are they not commanded to be simple as *doves* and *wise as serpents?* Do they cease to be men? What does it mean *to be sensually a brute or a beast?* Nebuchadnezzar, though raving, remained a man, even though he thought he was an ox and lived like one.
