<meta>Continuity from page 47: The author continues their polemic against the alchemist Oswald Croll and his contemporaries, shifting from a critique of their chemical "Triunity" to an attack on how these "Magi" (occult philosophers) distort Biblical language to justify their claims of human power over the cosmos.</meta>

# How the Magi Corrupt Biblical Scriptures

Both the beast and the human possess sensation, but the one in one way, and the other in another. The pagans, following nature as their guide, lived according to the flesh. Yet they did not perceive things in a beast-like way. He who is a beast perceives through the senses, because the senses and the faculty of estimation arising from them serve as the "form" for beasts; in its very essence, it is a beast, not merely by comparison. But <margin>When a human is truly human.</margin> when is a human truly a human? If he lives rationally (you say) and rules over the living creatures within his own body. This idea is indeed translated from Plato; but he speaks *morally*, not *physically*. Likewise, Plato’s human remains a human even when reason is carried away by affections and desires, just as he keeps his "Lion" <note>A reference to Plato's *Republic*, where the soul is compared to a many-headed beast, a lion, and a human.</note> under the dominion of reason, or the human element. A human is whoever consists of a soul capable of reason and a corresponding body.

To such a person the definition of man applies: *Man is a rational animal, capable of discipline and speech.* <note>original: "Homo est animal rationale, disciplinæ capax, & sermonis."</note> A human is therefore a human whether he reasons or not: whether sleeping, waking, even in the mother's womb, or as a newborn infant, a boy, a decrepit old man, a fool, one delirious from fever, a maniac, a frantic person, or a melancholic—even one who does not rule over the "living creatures" (that is, the beastly desires and affections) but rather gives them the widest possible rein. Would not even <term>GOD</term><note>The author uses capitalized "DEVS" to contrast the divine nature with the human.</note> be considered a man by this logic? The magician does not dare to say it in this place, although he could have done so without absurdity. He attributes to man dominion over the stars and all things. Certainly, he does so too liberally. For man’s dominion is not infinite, especially since he is now in a state of imperfection. But <margin>Man’s dominion over the stars.</margin> even in his state of integrity <note>The state of Adam before the Fall.</note> he received no more than dominion over the earth, the fish, the birds, and the beasts living on dry land, according to Genesis 1:28 and chapter 9:2. Over the stars he has no dominion, just as he has none over angels or evil spirits, except as much as he obtains through the protection of <term>GOD</term> and by a power not his own. For the wise man—that is, the pious man—does not subject the stars to himself by his own virtue, but by God's virtue, which he obtains through prayers or possesses as a miraculous gift from God. You might think "chemical stars" <note>Alchemical properties of matter.</note> were meant, if the rest of the text did not stand in the way. When does the Magician ascribe this to man? If he lives according to the <margin>God-like Spirit.</margin> <term>GOD-LIKE</term><note>original: "DEALEM"</note> Spirit. From where does this come? *From the tree of life.* And what is this? *The talent or treasure entrusted to him in a clay vessel.* I do not yet follow; speak more clearly: "if he observes the property of the image of God." Why is there a need for these ambiguities? Why not use Psalm 119: *Blessed are the undefiled in the way, who walk in the law of the Lord;* or as it is said in Psalm 1: *Blessed are they who keep his testimonies, and seek him with their whole heart (who love the Lord GOD with their whole heart, etc.). A young man shall keep his way pure by guarding the words of God.* <margin>What the "God-like Man" means to the magi.</margin> When one is said to walk here in Genesis 5:22: *You are my friends (says Christ in John 15:14) if you do what I command you.* These and other eloquent passages of Holy Scripture are filthy to the magi. *The God-like man is,* they say, *he who lives having observed the property of the image of God.*

But who ever lived thus in the world except <term>CHRIST</term>, and the first-formed man in his innocence? Who can live thus? Let the Apostle declare it throughout the whole of Romans 7. What is that *God-like spirit?* Why do you not call it the Word of God which the Holy Spirit suggests? The breath of the Lord's mouth—that is, the speech, the law, and the command<margin>Living from the tree of life.</margin>ment of <term>GOD</term>. What is it to *live from the tree of life?* In Genesis 3:23, God sent Adam out of paradise so that, having lost his created righteousness, he might not live forever in his misery by the benefit of the *Tree of Life*. Did He begrudge him life according to the image of God? Did He not want him to have a *God-like life?* Truly, just as the Magi have fictions suitable to their own folly, so they have their own words. It is said by way of comparison and through a figure of speech in Proverbs 3:18: *Wisdom is a tree of life to those who lay hold of her, and blessed are those who hold her fast.* *Blessed is the man who finds wisdom,* and it is often said that the keeping of wisdom is our life, as in verses 21 and 22: *Keep the law, and it shall be life to your soul.*

So too in chapter 4, verse 13, concerning discipline; and in Deuteronomy 30:20: *The Lord is the life of the faithful and the length of their days.* In Revelation 2:7: *Christ will give to the victor to eat from the tree of life.* In chapter 22:2: *In the middle of the street of the throne of God and of the Lamb, the river has the tree of life on both banks,* bearing Apostolic fruits, and *leaves for the healing of the nations.* In the sacred writings, therefore, the "tree of life" signifies both the cause of life and life itself. The Magician wanted to say that one who observes God's commandments, which are ordained for life, leads a holy life pleasing to God, and finally attains eternal life. But lest he seem to possess only common knowledge and speak with the simplicity of the Holy Spirit, monstrous phrases—tailored to the tastes of Paracelsus, Hermes the Egyptian, and other haughty magi—had to be babbled out so that he might seem to say something abstruse and singular.

<margin>The talent in the clay.</margin> What is the *talent entrusted in the clay vessel?* In Matthew 25:15, an *official talent* is committed to the Lord’s servants so that they may invest it profitably. Paul, in 2 Corinthians 4:6–7, says: *He carries the treasure of the knowledge of the brightness of God in earthen vessels.* Elsewhere, the good man *brings forth good things from the good treasure of his heart;* and there is a Treasure in heaven. He, therefore, rules over all things who performs the duty committed to him with fruitfulness, lives wisely according to God’s commandments, and stores up for himself a Treasure in heaven. But in this life, who is there who has attained this? Even the pious must enter the kingdom of heaven through the cross; we indeed conquer the power of death, but by the death of <term>CHRIST</term>, to whose image we must be conformed.

The wretched Magi claim dominion over all things with their words, yet they die like others and are afflicted like the rest; moreover, the treasures they imagine for themselves are nothing but mere fables. Would God have recommended these magical ravings to His faithful, to be shared with their neighbor? Not in the least. Thus it is written in Deuteronomy 18:10 and following: *Beware lest you desire to imitate the abominations of those nations; let there not be found among you anyone who makes his son pass through fire, etc.*

Likewise, *anyone who practices divination, who observes hours* (like the foolish astrologers with their "elections" <note>In astrology, "elections" are the choosing of auspicious times to act.</note>), *who uses enchantments, who consults sorcerers and magicians, or mediums, etc.*—that is, as we say in the manner of Paracelsus, those who practice Beryllistics <note>Divination using crystals or beryls.</note>, *who make consecrated mirrors and wish to know all things from them, who cling to characters, Gematria <note>A system of assigning numerical value to letters/words.</note>, Kabbalah, geomancy, aeromancy, ghosts, Melusines <note>Female water spirits.</note> and other abominations,* and who persuade men of the commerce of spirits, celestial attractions through secret magnetisms, and other mockeries of the devils, with which they color the impious magic of Hermes, the Platonists, the Enthusiasts, Paracelsus, and Trithemius

The author reinforces the distinction between human reason and divine power, citing Deuteronomy to condemn magical practices as demonic vanities.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: The text continues from the previous page's discussion of human nature, specifically naming famous occultists. The catchword "tem" from the previous page completes the name "Trithemii" (Johannes Trithemius).</meta>

...of Trithemius, Cornelius Agrippa, Artephius, Techellus, Pico della Mirandola, Pistorius, Scotus, and other most famous Magi—who were indeed immune from secular fire <note>referring to being burned at the stake</note>, but not from the eternal fire, unless they were corrected by repentance. Let us note, however, the clever syllogism of Croll. <note>Oswald Croll (c. 1563–1609), a disciple of Paracelsus and author of *Basilica Chymica*.</note> The theme or question is: *In man all things are comprised, and he himself carries all things within him.* (Page 24. As many species of minerals as there are in the world, so many are also in man. For there are as many kinds of diseases as there are species, bodies, and seeds of growing things, which seeds and stars—whether of the air, the heavens, the water, or the earth—are nurtured within the human elements. The first three <note>The "Tria Prima": Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury</note> are the causes of all diseases, etc. See there and other delusions, which rest on no other foundation than opinion, and the very wording is absurd. For if the three chemical principles are the causes of all diseases, there will not be as many kinds of diseases as there are species, bodies, and seeds of growing things; and since they say like is cured by like, the healing of all things would be very easy.) The argument for this theme is that man was made from the world, which contains all things. Hence the syllogism: *That from which something is made carries all things of that source within itself. Man was made from the world. Therefore, man carries all things of the world within himself:* and consequently, *everything whatsoever, because the world is the capsule of all things.* 

Well done, O Magician! Let us apply this to your own way of thinking: *That from which someone is made, he carries all its properties within himself. Man was made from the dust of the earth. Therefore, he carries all the properties of the dust of the earth within himself.* Or: *Man was made from the blood of one, as Paul says; Eve was made from the rib of Adam; Christ was born from the seed of David according to the flesh.* Do you see what could follow from that? What, I ask, is *generation*? Is it not a change in substance? What is *creation*? Is it not a making out of nothing, or out of that which is not a proximate and natural potentiality? *That from which something is made, it carries within itself. The world was made from nothing:* (and indeed, according to Croll's monstrous wisdom, MADE FROM THE "IDEAL NOTHING," as if negative nothingness were not universal) *Therefore, the world carries NOTHING within itself.* Conclude the same for the rest: *Therefore Eve carries within herself all things of Adam, even the right of the sword and virility. Therefore Christ carries within himself all things of David, even sin.* 

Learn, you most foolish Paracelsians, that matter stands as the genus, and that which is made from it as the species, in which there is more than in the genus. For it has its own form, qualities, and actions, which were not in the genus, nor in the seed in actuality, but only in potentiality. If a composition were made of things that remained unchanged, it could be said that such a composite has all the properties of the component parts. But not where there is generation, corruption, transmutation, and alteration. Then, to respond to the fallacy: man was indeed made from the world, but only particularly. Therefore, it is not necessary for him to contain all things of the world within himself. Thus plants and beasts were made from the world; thus minerals; but they do not encompass all things of the world. And how could plants contain the stars, since they existed before the stars? Light was commanded to exist out of darkness, yet it does not have darkness within itself. Art makes many things from the material of the world, yet it does not encompass all things of the world in its work. What if I were to say someone born of a fool turned out to be wise? Was wisdom then within the fool? But let us proceed.

<margin>Note the magical syllogism. The strange dreams of the Magi.</margin>

19. The good *Croll* attempts to prove that Paul's opinion concerning the spirit of the Thessalonians is Paracelsian, and that there is some sidereal spirit in man who is the true man, or the invisible inner man; this is truly wondrous. For logical proof does not suit Paracelsian Magic, since it rejects all Logic and commands it to be exiled from its science. Let the arguments proceed:

> If the Triune supreme Creator wished to be manifested in man, it is necessary that there be three essences in him, just as in the divine Trinity. The former is true; and it can be proved, not only by the image of God in which man was created (as if it were God from God, light from light), but also by the authority of the Apostle in the first chapter of Romans. Therefore the latter is also true; consequently, man has an astral spirit within himself, which is the true man, besides the soul and the elementary body.

<margin>That man has within himself a sidereal spirit, who is the true man.</margin>

<margin>The true man is within the man who is not true.</margin>

O, what cleverness! Let us grant to the Paracelsian Magi—Trithemius, Techellus, Artephius (who is said by his own testimony to have lived more than a thousand years), Cornelius Agrippa, etc.—that they had their own <unclear>irrational</unclear> <note>original: "απολογους" (apologos) - likely referring to irrational or familiar spirits</note> spirits or Socratic familiars, just as it is well known that many had incubi and succubi. But if we are speaking of man simply, and of him who was created in the image of God, and having lost it, put on the image of the Devil, and having been stripped of this again through Christ is renewed to the first image—we deny the connection. 

There is no need for reason in a negation against a Magician, since he does not prove his affirmations otherwise than by *saying* them. To our students, we say in true speech that the reason is this: God indeed wished to manifest himself in man, but that could be done even in one part of man, such as the soul, just as they say he revealed himself in the angels. Thus the skill of the craftsman is seen even in the shield of Minerva. Furthermore, we argue from the same source thus: *God the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit wished to be manifested when the sun was created. Therefore there are also three essences in the sun.* The same for the air and other things. Conversely: *If those three things—the physical elemental body, the sidereal spirit, and the soul of the Magi—are not in man, then there is no Trinity in God.* But the antecedent is true. Therefore so is the consequent. 

I prove the assumption because it is a fiction that within the external man there is another man, truly made from the stars or collected from the four natures of the world. This is nothing but a "begging of the question." <note>A logical fallacy where the conclusion is assumed in the premise.</note> Again: *If there are not three essences in the Triune God, neither are there in man; the former is true.* And thus the latter. Again: *If by reason of manifestation there are as many in one man as in the divine Trinity, there will be three individuals and three persons in man just as in the Trinity.* For otherwise that manifestation is nothing as to essence. But there are not three individual persons in one man. Will the Magician then deny the personal Trinity? Not everywhere there are three things is there a revelation of the divine Trinity. Otherwise Cerberus, Geryon, Diana or the Moon (which has three faces), the Clover, etc., would be images of God. We must not conclude the mysteries of the Trinity from our own fantasy, but from the principles of revealed faith. Otherwise, how easy it would be to make God corruptible in two parts, and to cut the Trinity not into persons, but into matter, form, and spiritual organs? Since, therefore, the Magician's invention of a sidereal spirit and the revelation of the eternal Triunity through it is futile and empty, it is proved that the Apostle’s opinion was by no means that.

<margin>Three essences in man.</margin>

Argument II. *Those who philosophize from the Light of Nature agree that there are three essences in man: the elemental body, the ethereal spirit, and the soul.* There are therefore just as many, and consequently, the Triunity...

<summary>This page critiques the alchemist Oswald Croll’s macrocosm-microcosm theory, specifically his claim that man contains all elements of the world and a "sidereal spirit" mirroring the Holy Trinity. The author uses logical refutations and reductio ad absurdum to dismiss these occult theories as fantasies that distort both nature and scripture.</summary>

<meta>The text continues the critique of occult philosophers, specifically completing the name of Trithemius (Johannes Trithemius) from the previous page's catchword "temij." # How the Magi Corrupt the Biblical Scriptures

Both the beast and the human possess sensation, but the latter in one way and the former in another. The Pagans, following nature as their guide, lived according to the flesh. Yet they did not perceive things like beasts. He who is a beast acts through the senses, because the "sensitive" and "estimative" powers <note>These are scholastic terms for the basic faculties of animal perception and instinct.</note> exist in beasts as their very form; in its essence, it is a beast, not merely by comparison. <margin>When a human is a human.</margin> But when is a person truly a human? *If he lives rationally (you say) and rules over the animals within his own body.* This idea is indeed translated from Plato; but he speaks *morally*, not *physically*. Likewise, Plato’s human remains a human even when reason is carried away by passions and desires, just as he keeps his "Lion" <note>A reference to Plato’s tripartite soul, where the "lion" represents the spirited or emotional part of man.</note> under the dominion of reason, or of the human. A human is anyone who consists of a soul capable of reason and an analogous body.

The definition of a human also fits: *A human is a rational animal, capable of discipline and speech.* Therefore, he is a human whether he reasons or not: whether he sleeps, wakes, is still in his mother’s womb, or is a newborn infant, a boy, a decrepit old man, a fool, delirious with fever, a maniac, frantic, or melancholic; even if he does not rule over the "animals"—that is, the beastly desires and passions—but instead gives them the widest possible rein. Would not even GOD be a human? <note>This is a sarcastic challenge to the Magus's logic.</note> The Magician does not dare to say that in this place, although he might have done so without absurdity. He attributes to man dominion over the stars and all things. This is certainly too generous. For man’s dominion is not infinite, especially since he is now in a state of imperfection. <margin>The dominion of man over the stars.</margin> Even in his state of integrity <note>The state of Adam before the Fall.</note> he received no more than dominion over the earth, the fish, the birds, and the beasts living on dry land (Genesis 1:28 and Chapter 9:2). Over the *stars* he has no dominion, just as he has none over angels or evil spirits, except as much as he obtains through the protection of GOD and by a power not his own. For a wise man—that is, a pious man—does not subject the stars to himself by his own virtue, but by God's power, which he obtains through prayer or holds as a miraculous gift from God. You might think he meant "chemical stars" <note>A Paracelsian term for the internal essences or "virtues" of things.</note> if other points did not stand in the way. When the Magician ascribes this to man: *If he lives according to the DIVINE-LIKE Spirit.* <note>original: "DEALEM Spiritum," a term used by Paracelsians to describe a divinized human state.</note> From where does this come? *From the tree of life.* What is this tree? *The talent or treasure entrusted to him in an earthen vessel.* <margin>The Divine-like Spirit.</margin> I do not yet follow; speak more clearly: *If he observes the property of the image of God.* Why the need for these riddles? Why not use Psalm 119: *Blessed are the undefiled in the way, who walk in the law of the Lord; as is also said in Psalm 1: Blessed are they that observe his testimonies, and seek him with their whole heart (those who love the Lord GOD with all their heart, etc.)* A young man shall keep his life pure by guarding the words of God. <margin>What the "Divine-like Man" is among the Magi.</margin> *When it is said in Genesis 5:22 to "walk with God." You are my friends (says Christ in John 15:14) if you do what I command you.* These and other eloquent passages of the Sacred Scriptures are filthy to the Magi. *A Divine-like man is one,* they say, *who lives having observed the property of the image of God.*

But who in the world has ever lived this way, except CHRIST and the first-formed man in his innocence? Who is able to live thus? Let the Apostle declare it in the whole of Romans 7. What is that *Divine-like spirit*? Why do you not call it the Word of God which the Holy Spirit suggests? The Spirit of the mouth of the Lord—that is, the speech, law, and commandment of GOD. <margin>To live from the Tree of Life.</margin> What is *to live from the tree of life*? In Genesis 3:23, God sent Adam out of paradise so that, having lost his created righteousness, he might not live forever in his misery through the benefit of the *Wood of Life*. Did He envy him a life according to the image of God? Did He not want a *Divine-like life*? Truly, just as the Magi have fictions suited to their folly, so they have their own words. It is said comparatively and through a trope in Proverbs 3:18: *Wisdom is a tree of life to those who lay hold of her, and blessed are they who hold her fast.* Blessed is the man who finds wisdom; and it is often said that the keeping of wisdom is our life, as in verses 21 and 22: *Keep the law, and it shall be life to your soul.*

So too in Chapter 4:13 regarding discipline; and in Deuteronomy 30:20: *The Lord is the life of the faithful and the length of their days.* In Revelation 2:7: *Christ will give to the victor to eat of the tree of life.* In Chapter 22:2: *In the middle of the street of the throne of God and of the Lamb, the river has the tree of life on both banks, bearing Apostolic fruits, and leaves for the healing of the nations.* In the sacred writings, therefore, the "tree of life" signifies both the cause of life and life itself. The Magician wanted to say that one who observes God's commandments, which are ordained for life, leads a holy life pleasing to God and finally attains eternal life. But lest he seem to possess only common knowledge and speak with the simplicity of the Holy Spirit, monstrous phrases—tailored to the tastes of Paracelsus, Hermes the Egyptian, and the arrogance of other Magi—had to be babbled out so that he might seem to say something abstruse and singular.

<margin>A talent in an earthen vessel.</margin> What is the *talent entrusted in an earthen vessel*? In Matthew 25:15, an *official talent* is committed to the Lord's ministers so that they may invest it profitably. Paul in 2 Corinthians 4:6-7: *He carries the treasure of the knowledge of the glory of God in earthen vessels.* Elsewhere, a good man brings forth good things from the good treasure of his heart; and there is a Treasure in heaven. He, therefore, rules over all things who performs the office committed to him with fruitfulness, lives wisely according to God's commandments, and stores up for himself a treasure in heaven. But in this life, who is it that has attained this? Even the pious must enter the kingdom of heaven through the cross, and we overcome the power of death indeed, but by the death of CHRIST, to whose image we must be made conformable.

The wretched Magi rule over all things with words, yet they die like others and are afflicted like the rest; furthermore, the treasures they imagine for themselves are nothing but mere fables. Would God have recommended these magical delusions to His faithful so that they might be shared with their neighbor? Not in the least. Thus it is written in Deuteronomy 18:10 and following: *Beware that you do not wish to imitate the abominations of those nations, lest there be found among you anyone who purifies his son...* <note>Referring to passing children through fire as a pagan ritual.</note>

Likewise: *those who practice divination, who observe hours (as do the foolish Astrologers with their "elections"), who cast spells, consult sorcerers and the dead, or soothsayers, etc.*—that is, as we say in Paracelsian terms: those who practice Beryllistics <note>Divination using beryl or crystals.</note>, who make "constellated" mirrors and wish to know all things from them, who cling to characters <note>Magical signs or symbols.</note>, Gemaheis <note>Likely referring to "Gemmahaia" or engraved planetary talismans.</note>, Kabbalah, Geomancy <note>Divination by means of earth or geographic features.</note>, Aeromancy <note>Divination by means of the air or atmospheric phenomena.</note>, specters, Melusines <note>Elemental water spirits or spirits of the woods in occult lore.</note>, and other abominations, and who persuade men of the commerce of spirits, celestial attractions through secret magnetisms, and other mockeries of the devils, with which they color the impious magic of Hermes, the Platonists, the Enthusiasts, Paracelsus, and Trithemius.

<summary>The author critiques the occultists (Magi) for using obscure, non-biblical terminology like "Divine-like Spirit" and "Tree of Life" to inflate their own status. He argues that true wisdom and "dominion" come from following God's law rather than the forbidden arts of astrology, crystal gazing, and spirit commerce.</summary>

<meta>The text continues the critique of occult philosophers, completing the name "Trithemius" (Johannes Trithemius) from the previous page's catchword "temii." The author is refuting the Paracelsian "microcosm" theory found in Oswald Croll's work.</meta>

# Examples Produced from Crollius

...of Trithemius, Cornelius Agrippa, Artephius, Techellus, Pico della Mirandola, Pistorius, Scotus, and other most famous Magi <note>The author is listing influential Renaissance occultists and Hermetic philosophers.</note>—who are indeed immune to secular fire, but not to the eternal one, unless they have been corrected by penitence. Let us note, however, the rather clever syllogism of Crollius. The theme or question is: *In man all things are comprised and he himself carries all things in himself.* (Page 24. *Just as many species of minerals as there are in the world, so many are also in man. For there are as many kinds of diseases as there are species, bodies, and seeds of growing things; these seeds and airy, celestial, aquatic, and terrestrial stars are nurtured in the human elements. These are the primary causes of all diseases,* etc. See there also other delusions which rest on no other foundation than opinion, and the wording itself is absurd. For if the three Chemical principles <note>The "Tria Prima" of Paracelsus: Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury.</note> are the causes of all diseases, there will not be as many types of diseases as there are species, bodies, and seeds of growing things; and since they say "like is cured by like," the healing of all would be very easy.) The argument for this Theme is that man was made from the world, which carries all things in itself. Hence the syllogism: *That from which something is made, of that thing it carries all within itself. Man was made from the world. Therefore man carries all things of the world within himself:* and consequently, *absolutely everything, because the world is the capsule of all things.* 

Beautifully done, O Magician! So we lead you to your own logical path: *From whatever someone is made, he carries all of its parts in himself. Man was made from the dust of the earth. Therefore he carries all properties of the dust of the earth in himself:* or: "Man was made from the blood of one," as Paul says; "Eve was made from the rib of Adam;" Christ was born "from the seed of David according to the flesh." Do you see what could follow from that? What, I ask, is *generation*? Is it not a change in substance? What is *creation*? Is it not a making out of nothing, or out of that which is not a proximate and natural potential? *That from which something is made, it carries in itself.* The world was made out of nothing: (and indeed, according to Crollius’s immense wisdom "OUT OF NOTHING FROM SOMETHING," <note>original: "EX NIHILO DE ALI"</note> as if a negative "nothing" were not universal) Therefore the world carries NOTHING in itself. Conclude likewise regarding the rest: Therefore Eve carries in herself all things of Adam, even the right of the sword <note>The "ius gladii" refers to legal and sovereign authority.</note> and virility. Therefore Christ carries in himself all things of David, even sin. 

Learn, you most foolish Paracelsians, that matter stands as the "genus," and that which is made from it stands as the "species," in which there is more than there was in the genus. For it has its own forms, qualities, and actions, which were not in the genus, nor in the seminal act, but only in potential. If a composition were made of things that remained unchanged, such a compound could be said to have all the properties of its component parts in itself. But not where there is generation, corruption, transmutation, or alteration. Then, to respond to the fallacy: man was indeed made from the world, but only particularly. Therefore it is not necessary for him to contain all things of the world in himself. In the same way, plants and beasts were made from the world; likewise minerals; but they do not embrace all things of the world. And how could plants contain the stars, since they existed before the stars? <note>According to the Genesis creation narrative, plants were created on the third day and the celestial bodies on the fourth.</note> Light was commanded to exist out of darkness, but it does not have darkness in itself. Art makes many things from the material of the world, but it does not embrace all things of the world in its work. What if I said someone born of a fool turned out wise? Was wisdom in the fool? But let us proceed.

<margin>
*Note the magical syllogism.*
All the wonders of the Magi.
</margin>

19. Good Crollius attempts to prove that Paul's opinion concerning the spirit of the Thessalonians is Paracelsian, and that there is some *Starry Spirit* <note>original: "Spiritus syderius"</note> in man, which is the true man, or the invisible interior man; may he have what is wondrous in himself. For logical proof does not suit Paracelsian Magic, since it rejects all Logic and orders it to be exiled from its science. Let the arguments proceed:

<margin>
*That man has a starry spirit, which is the true man.*
</margin>

> If the Triune supreme creator wished to be manifested in man, it is necessary that there be three essences in him, just as in the divine Trinity. The former is true: and it can be proved, not only by the image of God in which man was created (as if "God from God, light from light"), but also by the authority of the Apostle in the first chapter of Romans. Therefore the latter is also true; consequently, man has an astral spirit in himself, which is the true man, besides the soul and the elementary body.

<margin>
*The true man is in the man who is not true.*
</margin>

O, what cleverness. Let us grant to the Paracelsian Magi—Trithemius, Techellus, Artephius (who is said by his own testimony to have lived more than a thousand years), Cornelius Agrippa, etc.—that many of them had their own *familiar spirits* <note>original: "spiritus epidicus"</note> or Socratic companions, such as those known to be incubi and succubi; but if we are speaking of man simply, and him who was created in the image of God, and having lost it put on the image of the Devil, and having been stripped of this again through Christ is renewed to the first, we deny the connection. There is no need for reason in a denial to a Magician, since he does not prove his own assertions otherwise than by saying them. Turning our speech to our students, we say the reason is this: because God indeed wished to manifest Himself in man, but that can happen even in one part of man, such as the soul, just as they say He revealed Himself in the angels. In this way, the genius of the craftsman is seen even in the shield of Minerva. 

Then, we argue from the same source: *God the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit wished to be manifested by the creation of the sun. Therefore there are three essences in the sun.* Likewise for the air and other things. Conversely: *If those three—the physical elementary body, the Starry Spirit, and the soul of the Magi—are not in man, then there will be no Trinity in God.* But the first part is true. Therefore the second follows. I prove the assumption, because it is a fiction that there is another "true" man inside the external man, made from the stars or collected from the four natures of the world. There is nothing here but a "begging of the question." <note>A logical fallacy where the conclusion is assumed in the premise.</note> Again: *if there are not three essences in the Triune God, neither are there in man.* The former is true. And therefore the latter is as well. Again: *If by reason of manifestation there are as many in one man as in the divine Trinity, there will be three individuals and three persons in man just as in the Trinity.* For otherwise that manifestation is nothing in terms of essence. But there are not three individual persons in one man. Will the Magician then deny the personal Trinity? Not everywhere where there are three things is there a revelation of the Divine Trinity. Otherwise, Cerberus, Geryon, Diana, or the Moon (who has three faces), the Clover, etc., would be images of God. We must not conclude regarding the mysteries of the Trinity from our own fantasy, but from the principles of revealed faith. Otherwise, how easy it would be to make God corruptible in two parts, and to cut the Trinity not into persons, but into matter, form, and spiritual organs? Since, therefore, the Magician's fabrication concerning the starry spirit and the revelation of the eternal Triunity through it is futile and empty, it is proven that the Apostle’s opinion was not that at all.

Argument II. *Those who philosophize from the Light of Nature agree that there are three essences in man: the elementary body, the ethereal spirit, and the soul.* There are therefore just as many, and consequently, the Triunity...

<margin>
*Three essences in man.*
</margin>

# How the Magi Corrupt the Biblical Writings

<margin>Matthew 10:28; Ecclesiastes 12:7.</margin>
...man prefigures <note>original: "tem diuinam," likely completing "divinitatem" (divinity) from the previous page</note> divinity. We respond that it is worth more to philosophize from the light of the grace of the Holy Spirit than from the light of nature. 
<margin>To philosophize from the light of nature is to reason from the opinion of compared things and superficial similarities.</margin>
But the Holy Spirit attributes nothing to man beyond body and soul regarding essential perfection; all the wise physicists and doctors acknowledge and assert the same. Furthermore, nothing follows universally <note>original: "κθ. 9όλ8" (kath' holou), a Greek logical term meaning "as a whole" or "universally"</note> from a particular instance. Who are those whom the Magus says philosophize from the light of nature? The Paracelsians, who are destitute of the principles of their assertions. But true philosophers, judging nature and scripture together, feel differently, and in a way consistent with reality. Their testimony is worthy of trust. It should be noted, however, that <term>Crollius</term> <note>Oswald Croll (c. 1563–1609), a disciple of Paracelsus and author of *Basilica Chymica*</note> has no other author in this area than <term>Paracelsus</term> <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), the famous Swiss physician and occultist</note>. For <term>Hermes</term> <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the Hermetic corpus is attributed</note>, whom he 
<margin>In the manner of Paracelsus.</margin>
writes of on page 28—stating that regeneration through faith, hope, and love, etc., is hidden within the secrets of his religious breast in silence with others of pure heart (For who can find a clean person among the unclean? Job 14:4; Psalm 50:7) and holy life 
<margin>Hermes, according to Croll, holds that there are two essential parts of man.</margin>
(according to the Apostle in Romans 3), men illuminated by the Holy Spirit before the Word was incarnate—well, Hermes writes better than many Christians that man is only a "double animal," of which one part is the Mind (the form of the divine likeness) and the other is the Body made from the four elements, by which the divine part is covered. But <insert>Parac</insert>elsus on page 67 calls himself the first Physician of the <term>microcosm</term> <note>The "small world"—the idea that man reflects the structure of the entire universe</note>, claiming he alone produced the "double body of creatures" which Gentile 
<margin>Before Paracelsus there was no Physician, because he was the first.</margin>
philosophy passed over, etc. But we do not care for Paracelsian trifles. If <term>Trismegistus</term> is to be followed, one must philosophize about man differently.

<margin>Note the impiety of the Paracelsian Magus, and the stupidity of the Paracelsian "mud of the earth."</margin>
The third argument: *If man is created from the mud of the earth through the word "Fiat" [Let it be], and received from the mouth of God the eternal Spirit, which is God himself dwelling in us, it is necessary for man to be Triune. But the premise is true. Therefore so is the conclusion.* The connection is proven because: *The mud of the earth is one thing, and the eternal spirit, or the God of man, is another.* The mud of the earth is twofold, visible and invisible; from the location of the spirit it stood forth; from this came the **astral spirit**, which is the "true man," not flesh and blood. That Magus [Croll] writes these things so impiously, insanely, and shamefully that it is a wonder if anyone of sound mind still wishes to adhere to the Paracelsian sect. First, the logical connection is denied. Imagine that man was made this way—it still does not follow that he is triune. For if the word "God" is taken personally, there are only two things: God incorporated (or, as the Church says, incarnate) who has a body from the mud of the earth, and the breathed-in spirit or soul. Thus man would be "biune" [two-in-one]. The secondary argument regarding the "double mud of the earth" ignores the logical refutation and begs the question. For it is not true that the dust from which God constructed Adam's body, or the rib from which the woman was fabricated, consisted partly of elements and partly of ethereal spirit. Even if it did, it would not be necessary for two men—visible and invisible—to exist from it, just as various individuals are not conflated from the four elements, humors, and qualities, or from three, four, or more chemical principles when they are generated. So much for that proposition. Second: the assumption is an impious lie. Man, as far as the body is concerned, was made from the earth or dust, which the Hebrews call *Aphar min Haadamah* <note>Hebrew: עָפָר מִן־הָאֲדָמָה, "dust from the ground"</note>, "dry dust," such as ash from burnt wood, or what Aristotle says finally comes from rot, where he says: *but all things that rot come from that [earth], and their end is earth and dung* <note>original Greek: ἀλλὰ τὸ καὶ ἐκείνης γίνεται τὰ σηπόμενα πάντα, καὶ τέλος γῆ, καὶ κόπρος</note>. The human body was earth and 
<margin>The mud from which man was made.</margin>
returns to earth, and although it may become rotten dung before it crumbles into earth, the end is earth, as appears in the corpses in graves. The Latin Translator of Genesis 2:7 indeed rendered it as "Mud" [*Limum*], but he understood it as the dust of the earth, and he says so expressly in chapter 3:19: *Dust you are, and to dust you shall return: Until you return to the ground from which you were taken:* where the Hebrew has *Adamah* and *Aphar*. What then is mud? Softer mud and slime, such as sits in water; or dust diluted with water so that it can stick. If Adam was made from dry dust, he was not made from mud. But, you say, anatomy shows his structure consists of various things. I say in turn that the body of Eve and of animals is also made of various things after it is finished. The omnipotence of God also produced variety out of nothing. Again: *If that dry dust was solitary dry earth, it was not a mixture from heaven, or the stars and elements, much less was it "double mud."* This is one lie of the assumption. Another 
<margin>God did not make man through "Fiat," as the Paracelsian Magi lie.</margin>
is that it is said man was made *through the word FIAT*. From what Utopia did the Magus produce this dream? Sacred Scripture only uses that word three times, which the Hebrew expresses as *Iehi* <note>Hebrew: יְהִי, "let there be"</note>, "let it exist, let it be"; as in "let there be light," "let there be a firmament," "let there be lights" (where there seems to be a mystery, for the singular verb is constructed with a plural noun). Regarding man, it is written: *let us make man*, where the verbs are *Asah* and *Barâ* <note>Hebrew: עָשָׂה (to make) and בָּרָא (to create)</note> in Genesis 1:26, 27, and in 2:7 *Iatzar* <note>Hebrew: יָצַר, "to form"</note>, which is to shape. Regarding the woman in Genesis 2:22, the verb is *Banah* <note>Hebrew: בָּנָה, "to build"</note>. Nothing foreign should be introduced into the sacred letters. God could likewise have said: "Let man be made from the earth"; but that eternal wisdom knew the reason why he should pronounce not "let it be" [*fiat*], but "let us make" [*faciamus*], and the like. This is the second lie. The third is *that he inspired an eternal spirit into man*, in the sense which he later explains, as if it were nothing other than God. We have said above that this is impious. For it can be taken neither essentially nor personally. To be sure, there is a tropical [metaphorical] way of speaking about judges, that they are *Elohim* 
<margin>The three gods of Hermes.</margin>
or gods; but that this is something other than God and the divine essence is clear enough. He collected 
<margin>Impiety of Croll.</margin>
this impiety from Hermes the Egyptian, who writes in his *Asclepius* that there are three Gods: the Lord of eternity, the world, and man; whom he therefore says is divine by soul, sense, spirit, and reason, and thinks he can ascend into heaven because his humanity is partly divine. Likewise, "God made man first from himself, and second by himself." "God loved man as a part of his own divinity." "God made man from eternal and mortal nature." These and similar impieties these Magi admire, and they dare to oppose them to the sacred Philosophers and Physicians 
<margin>Whether man is flesh and blood.</margin>
as if they were the truth itself. As for the claim that *the astral spirit is truly man, and not flesh and blood*, we touched on that above. Certainly, Scripture describes man being begotten from blood after the first-created ones, and designates them with the same mark. For Adam said of Eve: *This is now bone of my bones, and flesh of my flesh* (Genesis 2:23; Matthew 19:5, 6). They are not two, but one flesh. Furthermore, it often puts "flesh and blood" for a man not divinely illuminated, or for the "natural" man <note>original Greek: ψυχικῷ (psychiko)</note>, or his nature simply accepted, who is both the carnal man and the "old" and "external" man with his sins and lusts—altogether one who is not yet regenerated, as in Matthew 16:17, John 1:13, 3:6, 1 Corinthians 15:45-50. But speaking physically, *flesh and blood* are taken for the material part of man; and a whole man cannot lack flesh to such an extent that it is necessary for even that corrupted flesh to rise again. Whence Job is to see God in his flesh. Aristotle especially...

<meta>This page continues the critique of Oswald Croll and the Paracelsian school, moving from general complaints about their interpretation of scripture to a specific refutation of their "sidereal spirit" (astral body) theory, which the author argues contradicts Christian doctrine regarding the body and the Incarnation.</meta>

# On Examples Brought Forward from Croll.

...he rightly establishes that the voice is a part of man according to the body. If you remove the figure of <term>synecdoche</term> <note>original: "synecdoches"; a rhetorical device where a part is used to represent the whole.</note>—specifically, using a limb to represent the whole—the human body certainly also pertains to the integrity of man, even if elsewhere the soul is more properly called "man" and the body a "tabernacle." That Paracelsian <term>ethereal spirit</term> <note>The "spiritus aethereus" or "sidereal spirit," which Paracelsians believed was an intermediate body between the soul and physical flesh.</note> cannot truly be man, because it is a magical figment and a phantom without essence. *Bad company corrupts good character: watch out for those dogs, those evildoers, those who mutilate the flesh* <note>original: "κατατομὴν" (katatomēn), a punning Greek term used by Paul in Philippians 3:2 to mock those insisting on physical circumcision.</note>, says Paul. Let theologians pay attention to this kind of interaction with the Paracelsists in our Commonwealths. If that flesh of which <term>Crollius</term> speaks—not from Paul, but from Paracelsus—is the body of man visible to the senses, and yet is not the "true man" or the true substance of man (namely his other half made by God from the dust of the earth and further propagated through natural generation from parents), but instead the "true man" is a *sidereal spirit* from the stars, then it will be easy for someone unskilled in Paracelsian cunning to be led away from the simplicity of faith. For what consolation will there be from the article of the Incarnation of our Savior, his passion, and his death, if his flesh is not part of the truth of what it is to be man? "Do not boast, O Jew!" the Paracelsist will cry out. "For you did not truly crucify Christ the man, but only his garment and a mask of a man." For that invisible man could not be crucified. To this we should apply what Paul writes in 1 Corinthians 15:47: that the first man was of the earth, earthy; the second Lord himself is from heaven, who is also called heavenly. Does that Paracelsian spirit not lie in wait for this doctrine? For if this [sidereal spirit] is that "second heavenly man," then Christ is not God and man together. Thus far regarding the arguments for a "human trinity" <note>The Paracelsian view that man consists of three parts: Soul, Sidereal Spirit, and Elemental Body.</note>.

<margin>1 Corinthians 15:33; Philippians 3:2.</margin>

20. Let us proceed in the examination of this Paracelsian spirit. He had said that the "true man" is *that sidereal spirit, which is also the internal man together with his external garment (as Socrates writes, following Hermes the Egyptian), made from the mud of the earth*. We understand it, therefore, to be something corporeal, though invisible. Now he calls it the *life of the body: and compares it with the Spirit of the Lord, which is the life of the intelligent soul*. It is well known into what darkness and "squid-ink" these Magi are accustomed to retreat when they are caught in a lie. They bring forth their Cabalistic trifles and "mystical senses." But if the meaning of these words in their <term>esoteric doctrine</term> <note>original: "acroamatica doctrina," referring to advanced or secret teachings intended for a private circle of students.</note> is what the words actually say, and if life is nothing corporeal, it is not being used as it is in the Holy Scriptures when Christ says: *I am the way, the truth, and the life*; or when Paul says, *Christ is our life*, or as the Church says: *Life dies upon the tree*. How can a "heavenly, sidereal, astral spirit" made from the mud of the earth be the true man? Croll will say: "I mean it in this sense: *that spirit is the cause of the life of the external body*." I say in turn: regarding the organic cause, I concede. But in that case, you have said nothing different from the "natural, vital, and animal spirit" of the Physicians. Furthermore, if it is the *cause of bodily life*, how can it be the "true man," the "internal man," the "sidereal," the "invisible"? The body of Adam before the breath of life was not living except in potential, just like the bodies in Ezekiel before the wind blew into them. If that spirit was made at the same time from the mud of the earth, it existed with the body before the soul, and the body lived before it began to live through the soul. At other times, the Chemists want their principles and essences to be the *first and closest subjects of vital powers*, and they call "life" the forces of things that are otherwise not living, just as <term>Cardanus</term> <note>Gerolamo Cardano (1501–1576), an influential Italian polymath.</note> does; it seems that this meaning was transferred from the book of Sirach. For he says in chapter 42, verses 23-24: *How desirable are all his works, and they are like a spark to be considered* (though we can hardly contemplate or understand a spark of them. Wisdom 9:13-16). *All these things live and remain forever, and in every need they obey him*. Aristotle, as well as Hippocrates in his book *On Ancient Medicine*, recognizes something else in the human body, something more divine than what are called the elements—a marvelous analog to the element of the stars, a spirit in the seed and the foamy body, and a nature in that spirit, which is also called "heat"; and in *On the Generation of Animals* Book 3, Chapter 11, it is said to be in the moisture, with animal heat existing in the universe, and coming from there to that spirit and moisture. If, therefore, this is the meaning—that *the sidereal spirit is that part of the body which, extracted through Chemistry, contains the powers within itself*, while the remains or dregs are exhausted by the extraction and have nothing besides elementary qualities—then the phrasing is tolerable; but then the other phrases that contradict this must be corrected. For it cannot be said that this is the "true man," that it came from the stars, or that it is the "internal man," since it is nothing other than the nobler part of the tissues of the body—not just the human body, but of all mixtures—nor does it make a man or a human body by itself, but is simultaneously part of the nature of the limbs. Nor is the recognition of this essence owed to Paracelsus, since both the higher Physicists and Physicians (though by another name) and the Chemists have posited it. Truly, one should not labor over the sense in which a Magus calls it the "life of the body" (as if he himself were not a body). For he adds a declaration: that it is so *just as the Spirit of the Lord is the life of the intellectual soul*. The life of the soul is both the vital power (or more rightly, the intellectual power) bestowed by the Spirit that creates all things, by whose force man was made into a living soul (Genesis 2:7, 1 Corinthians 15:45), and the "second act"—namely the intellect and its motion, considered in its vigor according to the truth of the matter. However, the Magus, following his Hermes, carves "essential life" out of the essence of God and breathes it into man, so that it is not through a procreating and preserving cause, but is *formally* life; yet this wording is contradictory, for it indicates that the "intelligent soul" is one thing, and its "life"—namely the intellect—is another. *For an intelligent soul is intelligent through the power of the intellect, and when it actually understands, it then exerts its life*. But every person experiences in themselves that they do not always understand in action or move by the intellect, but sometimes they rest. Let these comparisons now be put together. That sidereal spirit will thus become some power and virtue of the body through which it is called "living," whether in potential or in act, just as the intelligent soul is intelligent through the intellect bestowed by God. See now what kind of man this is, and what kind of "body within a body."

<margin>The true man is the life of the body, and the Spirit of the Lord.</margin>

<margin>The Paracelsian life of things.</margin>

<margin>The sidereal spirit in place of Paracelsus's Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury.</margin>

<margin>The life of man from creation.</margin>

<margin>The sidereal spirit vanishes into a potential power.</margin>

21. This is not enough. The same spirit is called the *soul, and indeed a mortal one in the body, since it is produced from light matter*. If this is so, it is nothing other than a power by which the body is so disposed that the soul—which is the first source and natural cause of life—can exercise vital actions through it by means of suitable instruments. This is evident in the body of Adam, in those restored in Ezekiel, and in the dead, or those seized by some grave disease like apoplexy or fainting. Certainly, you will find no life when the soul is absent. To call the vital or animal spirit "soul," or even to call the air we draw in by breathing "soul," is nothing new, provided you are not speaking of the essential form. If these things are so, throw away the "sidereal body" of Paracelsus,

<margin>The sidereal spirit as the soul.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the author’s systematic refutation of Oswald Croll and the Paracelsian school. The previous page questioned the definition of the "true man," and here the author attacks their interpretation of the Creation narrative in Genesis, specifically their claim that man is a "triune" being composed of a physical body, a "sidereal" (astral) spirit, and a divine essence.</meta>

# How the Magi Corrupt the Biblical Writings

<margin>Matthew 10:28; Ecclesiastes 12:7 <note>These verses refer to the distinction between body and soul, and the body returning to dust.</note></margin>
To philosophize by the light of nature is to reason from the opinion of comparisons and spurious similarities.
<margin>To philosophize by the light of nature is to reason from the opinion of comparisons and spurious similarities.</margin>
<margin>In the manner of Paracelsus.</margin>
<margin>Croll’s Hermes has two essential parts of man.</margin>
<margin>Before Paracelsus there was no Physician, because he was the first. <note>The author is likely sarcastically repeating a Paracelsian claim.</note></margin>
<margin>Note the impiety and folly of the Paracelsian Magus.</margin>
<margin>The Paracelsian mud of the earth.</margin>
...man prefigures the divine [image]. We respond that it is worth more to philosophize from the light of the grace of the Holy Spirit than from the light of nature. But the Holy Spirit grants nothing to man beyond the body and the soul regarding essential perfection, which all wise Natural Philosophers and Physicians acknowledge and assert. Furthermore, nothing follows from a particular premise <note>original: "κ. g. πόλ." — likely an abbreviation for "καὶ τὰ λοιπά" (and so forth).</note>. Who are those whom the Magus says philosophize by the light of nature? Paracelsians who are destitute of the principles for their assertions. But true Philosophers, considering nature and Scripture together, judge differently, and in a way consistent with reality. Their testimony is worthy of trust. It must be noted, however, that <term>Crollius</term> <note>Oswald Croll (c. 1563–1609), a disciple of Paracelsus and author of *Basilica Chymica*.</note> has no other authority in this matter than Paracelsus. For <term>Hermes</term> <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom many alchemical and philosophical works were attributed.</note> writes on page 28 that *regeneration is better known through faith, hope, and love, etc., hidden in the silence of one's religious heart along with others pure of heart* (Who can find a clean thing among the unclean? <note>original: "Quis mundum inter immundos inueniat?" referencing Job 14:4 and Psalm 50:7.</note>) *and men holy in life* (following the Apostle in Romans 3) *illuminated by the Holy Spirit before the Word was made flesh*, than by most Christians; he writes that man is only a twofold animal, of which one part is the Mind—namely the form of the divine likeness—and the other is the Body made of the four elements, by which the divine part is covered. But on page 67, he calls Paracelsus *the first Physician of the microcosm <note>The "microcosm" is the idea that man is a miniature map of the entire universe.</note>, who alone produced the twofold body of creatures, which was passed over by Gentile [pagan] philosophy, etc.* But we do not care for Paracelsian trifles. If <term>Trismegistus</term> is to be followed, one must philosophize about man in a different way.

The third argument: *If man is made from the mud of the earth through the word "Let it be" [FIAT], he is created; and if he received the eternal Spirit (which is God himself dwelling in us) from the mouth of God, it is necessary for him to be Triune.* The premise is stated; therefore the conclusion follows. The connection is proven because: *The mud of the earth is one thing, the eternal Spirit, or the God of man, is another.* They claim the mud of the earth is twofold, visible and invisible: from the first, the physical body emerged; from the second, the <term>sidereal spirit</term> <note>The "astral body" or "star-spirit" which Paracelsians believed mediated between the soul and the physical flesh.</note>, which is the "true man," not flesh and blood. This Magus speaks so impiously, insanely, and shamefully that it is a wonder if anyone of sound mind still wishes to adhere to the Paracelsian sect. First, the logical connection is denied. Imagine that man was made this way: it is still not necessary for him to be triune. For if the word "God" is taken personally, by the very terms set forth there are only two: namely, God incorporated—or as the Church speaks, Incarnate—who has a body from the mud of the earth, and the inspired spirit or soul. Thus man would be "bi-une" [two-in-one]. The further argument concerning the twofold mud of the earth ignores the logical refutation and begs the question. For it is not true that that dust from which God constructed Adam’s body, or that rib from which the woman was fashioned, consisted partly of elements and partly of ethereal spirit. And even if it had so consisted, it would not have been necessary for two men, a visible and an invisible one, to exist—just as various individuals are not split into multiple beings when they are generated from the four elements, humors, and qualities, or from three, four, or more chemical principles. This concerns the proposition. Second: the assumption is an impious lie. Man was made, as far as the body is concerned, from the earth or dust, which the Hebrews call *Aphar min Haadamah* <note>original: "אפר מן האדמה", meaning "dust from the ground."</note>, "dry dust," such as the ash from burnt wood, or that which Aristotle says eventually comes from decay, where he says: *From which and out of which all decaying things arise, and the end is earth and dung.* <note>original: "Ἀλφ’ ὅ το καὶ ἐξ οὗ γε γίνεται τὰ σπόρδωμα πάντα, καὶ τέλ’ ὅ γῆ, καὶ κόπρ’."</note> The human body was earth and returns to earth; and although it may become putrid dung before it crumbles into earth, yet the end is earth, as is apparent in the corpses in tombs. The Latin Translator of Genesis 2:7 indeed rendered it as "Mud" [*Limum*], but he understood it as the "dust" [*pulvere*] of the earth, and he says this expressly in chapter 3:19: *Dust you are, and to dust you shall return: Until you return to the ground from which you were taken,* where the Hebrew has *Adamah* and *Aphar*. But what is mud? *Slime and softer filth, such as settles in water;* or dust diluted with water so that it can stick together. If Adam was made from dry dust, he was not made from mud. But, you say, anatomy shows his frame consists of various things. I say in turn that the bodies of Eve and of animals also consist of various things once they are finished. The omnipotence of God also produced various things from nothing. Again: *If that dry dust was solitary, dry earth, it was not a mixture from heaven, or the stars and elements, much less was it a twofold mud.* This is one lie in the assumption. Another is that it is said man was made *through the word "LET IT BE" [FIAT].* From what Utopia did the Magus produce this dream? Sacred Scripture uses that word only three times, which the Hebrew expresses *per Iehi* <note>original: "יהי", meaning "Let there be."</note>, let it exist, let it be: as in "let there be light," "let there be a firmament," "let there be lights" (where there seems to be a mystery, for the singular verb is used with a plural noun). Concerning man, it is written, *Let us make man* [*faciamus hominem*], where the Hebrew verbs are *Asah* and *Barâ* (Genesis 1:26, 27) and in chapter 2:7, *Iazar*, which is "to form." Concerning the woman in Genesis 2:22, the verb is *Banah*, "to build." Nothing foreign should be introduced into the sacred letters. God could likewise have said, "Let there be man from the earth"; but that eternal wisdom knew the reason why he should pronounce not "let there be," but "let us make," and the like. This is a fertile lie. The third lie is *that he breathed into man an eternal spirit*, in the sense he later explains, as if it were nothing other than God himself. We have said above that this is impious. For it can be taken neither essentially nor personally. To be sure, it is a tropical [metaphorical] way of speaking about judges, that they are *Elohim* or "gods"; but it is clear enough that this is something other than God and the divine essence. He gathered this impiety from the Egyptian Hermes, who writes in his *Asclepius* that *there are three Gods: namely, the Lord of eternity, the world, and man;* whom he therefore says is divine in soul, sense, spirit, and reason, and thinks he can ascend into heaven because his humanity is partly divine. Likewise: *God made man first from himself, and second by himself. God loved man as a part of his own divinity. God made man from an eternal and a mortal nature.* These and similar impious things these Magi admire, and they dare to oppose them to sacred Philosophers and Physicians as if they were the truth itself. As for the claim that *the astral spirit is truly man, and not flesh and blood*, we touched on that above. Certainly, Scripture describes man as being generated from blood after the first parents, and designates the first parents by the same mark: for Adam said of Eve, *This is now bone of my bones, and flesh of my flesh* (Genesis 2:23, Matthew 19:5-6). *They are not two, but one flesh.* Furthermore, Scripture often uses "flesh and blood" to refer to a man not divinely illuminated, or for the "soulish" [natural] man <note>original: "homine ψυχικῷ" (psychikō), the natural or unspiritual man mentioned in 1 Corinthians 2:14.</note>, or his nature taken simply—who is both the carnal man, and the old and external man with his sins and lusts, and altogether one who is not yet regenerated, as in Matthew 16:17, John 1:13, chapter 3:6, 1 Corinthians 15:45-50. But speaking physically, *flesh and blood* are taken for the material part of man; and a whole man can no more lack flesh than it is necessary for that corrupted flesh to rise again. Whence Job says: *He shall see God in his flesh.* Aristotle most univocally...

<margin>The mud from which man was made.</margin>
<margin>God did not make man through the word "Let it be" [Fiat], as the Paracelsian Magi falsely claim.</margin>
<margin>The impiety of Croll.</margin>
<margin>The three gods of Hermes.</margin>
<margin>Whether flesh and blood is man.</margin>

He refutes Oswald Croll’s interpretation of Genesis by analyzing the original Hebrew and Greek terminology regarding dust, mud, and the specific verbs used in the creation of man.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s refutation of Oswald Croll and the Paracelsian doctrine of the "triune" man. Having previously addressed the definition of the "true man," the author now examines the Paracelsian concept of the "sidereal spirit"—an astral body supposedly mediating between the physical flesh and the divine soul—arguing that this concept undermines both medical science and orthodox Christian theology, specifically the reality of Christ’s incarnation.</meta>

# On the Examples Produced from Croll.

He <note>Referring to Oswald Croll, a prominent follower of Paracelsus.</note> rightly establishes that the voice of man is a part according to the body. Remove the figure of synecdoche <note>A rhetorical figure where a part represents the whole.</note>—namely, a member for the whole—and the human body certainly also pertains to the integrity of man, even if elsewhere the soul is more properly called the "man," and the body its "tabernacle." That Paracelsian ethereal spirit cannot truly be man, because it is a magical figment and a phantasm without essence. *Evil communications corrupt good manners: beware of dogs, beware of evil workers, beware of the concision* <note>original: "κατατομὴν" (katatomēn) — a punning reference used by St. Paul to distinguish between true circumcision and mere "mutilation" of the flesh.</note>, says Paul. Let theologians attend to such conduct of the Paracelsians in the Commonwealth. If that flesh, of which <term>Crollius</term> <note>Oswald Croll (c. 1563–1609), a German alchemist and professor of medicine.</note> speaks—not following Paul but Paracelsus—is the body of man visible to the senses; and if it is not the true man, or the true substance of man (namely, his other member made by God from the dust of the earth and further propagated through natural generation from parents), but instead the "true man" is a *sidereal spirit* from the stars, it could easily happen that someone unskilled in Paracelsian craftiness might be led away from the simplicity of the faith. For what consolation will there be from the article of the incarnation of our Savior, his passion, and his death, if the flesh is not part of the truth of man? "Do not boast, O Jew," the Paracelsian will cry out. "For you did not truly crucify Christ the man, but only a garment, and a mask <note>original: "laruam"</note> of a man. For that invisible man could not be crucified." To this should be referred what Paul writes in 1 Corinthians 15:47: that the first man is of the earth, earthy; the second Lord himself is from heaven, who is also called heavenly. Does not that Paracelsian spirit lie in wait for this doctrine? For if this spirit is that "second heavenly man," then Christ is not God and man together. Thus far concerning the arguments for the human Trinity.

<margin>1 Corinthians 15:33; Philippians 3:2.</margin>

20. Let us proceed in the examination of this Paracelsian spirit. He had said that the true man is *that sidereal spirit, which is also the internal man together with its external garment, (as Socrates writes, following Hermes the Egyptian) made from the mud of the earth.* We understand it, therefore, to be something corporeal, though invisible; now he calls it the *life of the body, and compares it with the Spirit of the Lord, which is the life of the intelligent soul.* It is not unknown into what darkness and "cuttlefish ink" <note>An ancient metaphor for a writer who obscures their meaning to escape detection, like a squid releasing ink.</note> the Magi are accustomed to retreat when they are caught in a lie. They bring forth their Cabalistic trifles and mystic senses. But if that understanding of the words in the secret <note>original: "acroamatica" — referring to teachings intended only for oral delivery to a select few.</note> doctrine is what the words themselves display, then "life" is nothing corporeal, nor is it understood as in the sacred texts, when Christ says: *I am the way, the truth, and the life*; or when Paul says, *Christ is our life*, or as the Church says: *Life dies upon the tree.* How can the Olympic, sidereal, astral spirit, made from the mud of the earth, be the true man? Croll will say: "I mean it in this sense: *that spirit is the cause of the life of the external body.*" I say in turn: regarding the organic cause, I concede this. But in saying so, you have said nothing different from the natural, vital, and animal spirit of the Physicians. Furthermore, if it is the *cause of corporeal life*, how can it be the "true man," the "internal man," the "sidereal," and the "invisible"? The body of Adam before the breath of life was not living except in potential, just like the bodies of Ezekiel before the wind blew upon them. If that spirit was made from the mud of the earth at the same time, it existed with the body before the soul, and the body lived before it began to live through the soul. Elsewhere the Chymists <note>Alchemists.</note> want their principles and essences to be the *first and nearest subjects of vital powers*, and they call "life" the forces of things that are otherwise not living, just as <term>Cardanus</term> <note>Girolamo Cardano (1501–1576), a polymath who argued that all metal and stone possessed a form of life.</note> does; and that meaning seems to have been transferred from the book of Sirach. For he says in chapter 42, verses 23–24: *How desirable are all his works, and they are as a spark to be considered* (though we can scarcely contemplate or understand a spark of them; Wisdom 9:13–16). *All these things live, and remain forever, and in every necessity they obey him.* Aristotle, as well as Hippocrates in the book *On Ancient Medicine*, recognizes something else in the human body, and that something more divine than what are called the elements—a marvelous analogue to the element of the stars: the spirit in the seed and the foamy body. Nature resides in that spirit, which is also called heat; and in the third book of *On the Generation of Animals*, chapter 11, it is said to be in the moisture, with animal heat existing in the universe, and coming from there to that spirit and moisture. If, therefore, this is the opinion—*that the sidereal spirit is that part of the body which, extracted through Chemistry, contains the powers within itself*—since the remains or dregs are exhausted by extraction and have nothing besides elementary qualities, then the phrasing is tolerable; but then other phrases that contradict this must be corrected. For it cannot be said that this is the "true man," that it came from the stars, or that it is the "internal man," since it is nothing other than the nobler part of the similar components of the body—not only of the human body, but of all mixed things. Nor does it constitute either a man or a body of a man by itself, but rather it belongs to the nature of the members. Truly, the recognition of this essence is not owed to Paracelsus, since both the higher Physicists and Physicians, as well as the Chymists, have posited it, albeit under another name. But we should not labor over the sense in which the Magus calls it "the life of the body" (as if he himself were not the body). For he adds an explanation: that it is so, *just as the Spirit of the Lord is the life of the intellectual soul.* The life of the soul is both the vital power (or more rightly, intellectual power) conferred by the Spirit that creates all things, by whose power man was made into a living soul (Genesis 2:7; 1 Corinthians 15:45), and also the "second act"—namely, its understanding and motion, considered in its actual vigor, according to the truth of the matter. However, the Magus, following his Hermes, carves an "essential life" out of the essence of God and inspires it into man, so that it is not through a procreating and preserving cause, but is formally "life" itself. Yet the phrasing is contradictory, indicating that the intelligent soul is one thing, and its life—namely the intellect—is another. *For the intelligent soul is intelligent through the power of the intellect, and when it actually understands, it then exerts its life. But everyone experiences in themselves that they do not always actually understand or move with the intellect, but sometimes rest.* Now let the similarities be assembled. Thus, that sidereal spirit will become some power and virtue of the body, through which it is said to be alive, whether in potential or in act, just as the intelligent soul is intelligent through the intellect conferred by God. See now what sort of man this is, and what sort of body-within-a-body.

<margin>The true man, the life of the body, and the Spirit of the Lord.</margin>

<margin>The Paracelsian life of things.</margin>

<margin>The sidereal spirit according to Paracelsus's opinion: Sulphur and Mercury.</margin>

<margin>The life of man from creation.</margin>

<margin>The sidereal spirit passes into potentiality.</margin>

21. Not enough. The same spirit is called the *soul, and that a mortal one in the body, produced as it is from light matter.* If this is so, it is nothing other than the potential by which the body is so disposed that the soul—which is the first source and natural cause of life—can exercise vital actions through it, using suitable instruments. This is evident in the body of Adam, and those restored in Ezekiel, as well as in the dead or those seized by some grave disease, apoplexy, fainting, etc. Certainly, you will detect nothing of life when the soul is absent. However, to call the vital or animal spirit "the soul"—or even that which we draw in by breathing—is nothing new, provided you are not speaking of the essential form. If these things are so, discard Paracelsus's body of the stars...

<margin>The sidereal spirit may be the soul.</margin>

<summary>This page critiques the Paracelsian "sidereal spirit," arguing that defining the "true man" as an astral body leads to theological heresy regarding Christ's physical incarnation and death. The author contends that what alchemists call a "sidereal spirit" is merely the refined chemical or biological "spirit" already recognized by traditional medicine, not a separate internal entity.</summary>
