<meta>This page continues the author’s systematic critique of the Paracelsian and Crollian doctrine of the "sidereal spirit." Having previously argued that man’s body is a real, physical substance, the author now addresses the claim that man is "triune" (composed of body, soul, and an intermediate astral spirit), dismissing the Paracelsian definitions as linguistic confusion and magical superstition.</meta>

### How the Magi Distort Biblical Writings

<margin>Why this Magic should be examined, even though its language is empty.</margin>...of Paracelsus, that he is truly man, the internal man, the second body of man, first brought to light by Paracelsus. 22. ***The spirit is the life of the soul: the spirit and the soul are the life of the body.*** Someone might say: Why bother getting exhausted by these delusions? Let these "offscourings" <note>original: "περιψηματα" (peripsēmata) — a Greek term for scrapings or refuse, often used by early Christians to describe themselves as the world’s outcasts.</note> be sent to the crows! To this I respond: Spirits must be tested even in matters of Philosophy, lest we condemn the innocent. If they are harmful, it is useful for them to be known in the State so that they may be avoided. It is not our pleasure to hunt down men; but we do not even understand these men when we judge their "spirit" and doctrine, although sometimes the names are exchanged, and "Paracelsus" is named in place of the spirit and doctrine of Paracelsus.

But to the matter at hand. The body by itself does not have a vital act, or life in actuality; however, it is so disposed that it can live by a life-principle implanted within it—unless you take "life" figuratively to mean the powers of any thing whatsoever. Now, if the spirit is a "second body" of man, how can it be his life, except as an instrument or a communicator? In the same way a blacksmith turns iron by means of tongs: here, neither the iron lives, nor the tongs, but the smith, in whom the knowledge of the smith’s art is the principle and the form. But this phrasing is not even tolerable. For natural instruments in the body are not a "second body" of man.

Indeed, who is that spirit which is the "life of the soul"? The Natural Philosopher does not, by himself, deal with the Divine efficient cause, as that is a Theological matter. Under that supposition, the natural cause is a living parent who begets a living child. For after God, those who are born draw life from their parents. Thus the soul is made naturally. I ask you, what natural spirit makes this soul live? Is it the "Olympic" spirit? Not at all. For that is not an animal principle, except in a certain respect, and certainly not by reason of the soul; the soul possesses the power of life in and of itself in such a way that it makes a corresponding body live with it, and exhibits the proofs of its life through that body. That the soul has life in itself is apparent from its immortality. For life is inseparable from it, even when it has been freed from the contagion of the body. It is well known that the soul communicates the act of life to the body to which it is essentially joined—that it sees with and through the eye, walks through the feet, hears through the ears, and so on. There is no "corporeal spirit" that can make the soul live in itself. That which instrumentally causes the soul to exercise vital acts in the body is the "organic spirit." But this is not "another man," nor is it that Paracelsian fiction, but a vapor or breath, a steward of life from the soul into the body. All sane Natural Philosophers and Doctors have acknowledged this, and there was no need for a Paracelsian reminder. Regarding powerful chemical essences, it was said before that their "life" is metaphorical, and they cannot cause a dead body to live. 23. The description of this spirit from page 36 of Croll is, for the most part, a description of the soul. ***For this is the living spirit, which in plants has at least vegetative power, in beasts sense and motion, and in man intellect and will or reason.***

<margin>The Soul.</margin>Here, however, it is neither a body nor a separation from the body. How does it become a "star" or the "internal body" of man, a medium between the external body and the soul, so that man is thereby **TRIUNE**? The "binary" <note>The number two.</note> number is infamous among the magi. Therefore <term>Crollius</term> <note>Oswald Croll (c. 1563–1609), a key figure in "Iatrochemistry" (medical chemistry) who blended medicine with occult philosophy.</note> calls a certain detractor who wrote against Paracelsus a "binary" <note>Implying the critic is double-minded or belongs to the flawed, dualistic world of matter.</note>. Lest every man be subject to the binary, they make him a "ternary" <note>A group of three.</note>—as if the number itself were of such great value! A sorceress in Virgil also uses the ternary; but is she so innocent in doing so? The Pythagoreans celebrated the "quaternary," which is twice-binary. 24. ***Bodies are the excrements of the stars.*** This is ridiculous folly (page 36): ***Bodies are nothing other than the excrement of the stars, produced into corporeal existence by their operation.*** Every star produces another star enclosed in a body by "imagining" and forming it from itself and through itself by its operation, etc. Therefore, corporeal effects are the "excrements" of the stars.

The Paracelsians say elsewhere that comets, rains, winds, various kinds of manna, and "shootings" <note>Meteors or falling stars.</note> are excreted by the stars (which they claim require nourishment). The common people take these to be "stellar purgings," like the snuffings pulled from burning lamps or candles, among many other things. But as for visible elemental bodies, which are found in minerals, plants, and animals—when were they ever in the stars, or when did they fall from them? The body of man was made from the dust of the earth; Natural Philosophers, along with the choir of Chemists, compose it from the elements. What, then, is the meaning here? This phrasing is taken from Chemistry improperly applied. When, as <term>Mesue</term> <note>A reference to Johannes Mesue, a name attached to influential medieval Arabic pharmaceutical texts.</note> says, the form is abstracted from the matter, or the essence is totally separated, the "empty earth" remains, and the segregated phlegm is comparable to elemental water. Others call it "elemental wateriness," as it is distinct from the nutritional juice that is not yet assimilated and the primordial substance-maker. Along with these two, they say there is air and a "fiery spirit," or at least that these depart with the essences. Thus, dead bodies would be the "remains" of extracted essences. Imagine those essences are "stars" or "sidereal spirits": even so, art <note>Alchemy or human skill.</note> has separated those elemental parts from them; the stars themselves did not "excrete" them. Nor did they proceed from the stars. For these elemental bodies are made and born at the same time as the essences. And so they are elsewhere called "capsules," not excrements. But the Magi think anything is permitted to them, whether they contradict themselves or others. 25. ***While this spirit is adorned with wonderful titles, you can scarcely restrain yourself from laughter. If it is an incorporeal man, how is it the "other part of foul mud"?*** How can bodies be its excrements? Why do you call it the "second body" of man? You speak relatively. Because the spirit is invisible, and the ancients called invisible things "incorporeal," therefore man is called incorporeal. If, I say, the mutability of perspectives has a place in axiomatic doctrine, you could easily make whatever you want out of anything. As for the ancients, they would have made even the vacuum, souls, angels, and devils corporeal. Do not tell me what the "ancients" (for whom Paracelsus cares little) have said, but explain the thing itself. It cannot be "man." (For man consists of body and soul.) It cannot be "man" if it is only something internal to man—and indeed not the soul, nor a power of the soul, but a spirit "cut off" from the stars.

***Gabalis*** <note>A Paracelsian term for the "sidereal man" or astral body.</note>, if there is a certain reason for the word and it is not a pure fiction, the name seems to be derived from a "boundary" and "shaping." For to the Hebrews, ***Gabal*** signifies a "limit," and to "shape" or "form" within a certain boundary. Thus, that internal spirit would be the "plastic" seminal shaper of the body while it is being generated. But because ***Gabalistic*** magic found its name there, and ***Gabul*** among the Rabbis signifies a certain treading of a man within determined boundaries, why should that not be the spirit through which devils are summoned by Magi standing within a certain circle? That is the attractor of spirits, as will follow. Hence you can say: Paracelsus’s *Gabalistica* is the summoning of demons from a certain circle to answer questions; or it is that by which <term>Trithemius</term> <note>Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516), a German abbot and polymath accused of demonic magic for his work on stenography and spirits.</note> transformed himself into various shapes, as certain sorceresses and infamous magi are accustomed to do—who are now fire, now a stone, a lion, straw, an ass, etc., like Proteus and Achelous in the Poets. Paracelsus, in his paragraph on Astronomy, derives it from the stars, and in the *Labyrinth*...

<margin>The binary [number two] stands for the elemental, the dung-like, or the foolish.</margin>

<margin>Gabalis: the astral Olympic man, a confused mix of sidereal spirits.</margin>

<summary>The author mocks the Paracelsian concept of a "sidereal spirit" and the "triune man," arguing that defining the body as "star-excrement" is unscientific and that the term "Gabalis" likely refers to demonic circle-magic rather than true philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, Oswald Croll, Gabalis, sidereal spirit, alchemy vs medicine, ternary vs binary, Johannes Trithemius, astral body, elemental excrement</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of the "sidereal spirit" (spiritus sydereus) concept used by Oswald Croll and Paracelsus. The previous page discussed the definition of man as a triune being; here, the author examines how these "Magi" use analogies like magnets and specific categories of magic to justify their claims that humans can "attract" knowledge and power from the stars.</meta>

### From Examples Produced by Croll.
[E 3]

...of the Physicians, he says, it reveals all the secrets of nature from the stars. In Book 3 of *On the Origin of Invisible Diseases*: "In the Olympic spirit lies the Gabalistic art; the assemblies of Olympic spirits prove the power of the imagination," etc. 26. *That spirit is the same as the firmament of the stars, just as the redness of wine is the same as the wine, and the whiteness of snow is the same as the snow.* This is truly strange. This identity is merely the agreement of a subject with its attribute. Let us, therefore, place the firmament of the stars within man—that is, all those essences which Chemists are accustomed to extract by their art from bones, flesh, and other things. In these is that sidereal spirit, as if in its own proper subject; just as in oil of cinnamon and water there is the *spirit of cinnamon*, and in an extract of Rhubarb there is the *spirit of Rhubarb*, and so on for the rest. The matter becomes clearer with a magnet. This stone attracts iron by a spirit clinging in its sulfur. Sulfur is a "star": the spirit is the ethereal nature of that star. If this is the meaning, it is poorly compared with colors, which arise from limited light and behave in quite varied and changeable ways; as the words sound and the force of the comparison indicates, the sidereal spirit is an inherent quality in the firmament of the stars. Therefore, it is not a substance. Elsewhere, the stars themselves are spirits. Now we perceive something different. In this way, these Gabalistic <note>original: "Gabalistici" — a term Paracelsus used to distinguish his "natural" magic from the Jewish "Cabalistic" tradition, though the author here treats it as a corruption of the term.</note> Magi transmute both themselves and their words into various monstrosities.

27. Although that sidereal spirit is said to be the same as the stars—by an identity that is simultaneously a diversity, as similar examples have shown, such that the stars are corporeal while the spirit in them is something incorporeal—nevertheless, it is soon called a "body." To see the truth of this, one must understand them joined together, just as *Aristotle in Book 2 of the Generation of Animals, Chapter 3, calls heat and spirit a "body" together with a nature analogous to the element of the stars, or ether.* To this internal body of man, something incredible is attributed. It is compared to a magnet. It is necessary that the external stars in the visible sky behave like *iron or something made of iron*. Between these two, a "sympathetic familiarity" is established, by which the spirit of the magnet seeks the spirit of the iron for food; when it has exhausted this, then the excrement is left behind—this is the empty rust, evacuated or deprived of all the spirit and essence of the iron.

<margin>Man attracts the forces of the external stars.</margin>
For in this way, the Paracelsian Magi philosophize about these things sometimes in one way, sometimes in another; so that by the stars of our body joined with the sidereal spirit, they might exhaust the macrocosmic stars and be nourished by their spiritual essence. However, it is necessary for nourishment to pass into the nature of that which is being nourished, changed by that very thing, just as blood is turned into flesh—not in the bone or the nerve, but in the flesh. It would be a fable, and a work of less than magic, if someone wished to reason in this way. What else then? The Magi *falsely attribute to the heavens admirable virtues and operations, and the power of conferring wisdom, arts, and all things.* Therefore, Paracelsus sees in the stars certain "Vulcans," or *mechanical spirits, acting as Epimethean and Promethean <note>Epimetheus (afterthought) and Prometheus (forethought) are titans from Greek mythology; here they represent the "builders" of human intellect and skill.</note> builders and givers of talents, arts, and other things.* From these spirits, Croll wants our spirits to attract powers. But on what foundation? None. Therefore, that whole matter is "vanity of vanities." Astrologers also devise ascendants, horoscopes, and other things from the rules of the pagans concerning the whole life, fortune, and the goods of the mind and body of the newborn, which they have dragged out of Chaldean Magic. But these men too are led by a spirit of vanity. For as God says *through Jeremiah in Chapter 10*: *The laws of the nations are vain.* From a vain principle, nothing but vanity comes. It should be noted, however, that these things, although varied, are discussed seriously by the Paracelsians so that they may give some color of legitimacy to the *Magic of Techellus, Trithemius, Pico, Agrippa*, and others who know the art of *Simon Magus* and *Tiridates the Armenian*, who hosted Nero with magical banquets while he gaped in vain for the art; <margin>The wise heaven of the magi.</margin> <margin>Pliny, Book 30, Chapter 2.</margin> <margin>See Paracelsus vol. 6, book on "Long Life" chapter 9, where more magi [are mentioned].</margin> as well as the Jews who preach the *Schemhamphoras* <note>original: "Schemhamphoras" — the explicit or "hidden" name of God in Jewish mysticism, believed to have great power.</note>; and those who make *constellated mirrors, images, homunculi, characters, coins, and other devilish things, and attribute all things to seals.* For they strive to persuade us—as did that famous magus *Scotus*—that *such things have natural causes*, and do not happen through commerce with unclean spirits and the deception of Satan, when in truth it is nothing else; the whole thing has been exterminated and prohibited by the Holy Scriptures and the Church, with Roger Bacon the Chemist himself detesting this same art.

28. From this comes *Gamaheos* <note>term: <term>Gamaheos</term> — naturally occurring stones that appear to have images or "signatures" on them, believed to hold celestial power.</note>, the fourth species of Magic, etc. Croll defines this magic as follows: *those things which nature perfects of its own accord (understand this in gems and naturally figured stones, to which the magi attribute the greatest powers, such as the serpent's egg, thunderstones, the vegetable adder's-tongue, shark teeth, the horn of Ammon, etc.), these can be performed far more easily by art, invisibly and spiritually.* But to serve the studious, we will repeat what Paracelsus has concerning *Gabalistica and Gamahea*. In Book 1 of his *Sagacious Philosophy*, he divides magic into six species. <margin>What Gamaheos are.</margin> <margin>Species of magic.</margin> The first he calls *Insignis* (Signal Magic), through which they bring forth some interpretation from the supernatural signs of heaven, such as new or prophetic stars, or the Apocalypse; as if they were interpreters of divine signs and prophecies. The second he wants to be *Metamorphosis*, the transformation of living bodies into others, just as Christ was transfigured. He calls this *Transfigurative Magic*. The third, he says, teaches how to make words that have all that power which is sent down from heaven into herbs, so that a physician might purge by words instead of rhubarb. The reason for this is: *Whatever the natural order effects by itself of its own accord, art does as well.* This is *Characteral Magic*. The fourth he writes teaches how to make *Gamaheu* (also written by others as Gamacheu, Gamahey, Gamahæa, Gamahæos. <margin>Gamaheu, Gamahæos, called the fourth magic.</margin> <margin>The marvelous power of Hamahæos against Witches and Hags when some are burned from... [unclear/fragment].</margin> <margin>Gabalistica. Another species of magic.</margin> Dorn says *Gamohæas* are images conceived from super-celestial influence. Truly, *Gamatheos* are stones marked with wondrous characters, images, and figures into which celestial powers are impressed, such as sometimes exist even in nature); all these perform those things which natural instruments can do, such as unlocking bars, inflicting wounds, protecting against blows, etc., and doing so invisibly. The fifth species is that which fashions images similar to those for whom they are intended; whatever is inflicted upon these images, he whose likeness they are suffers it. Thus, in a wax image or another, someone can be spoken to, pierced, killed, contracted, etc., which witches otherwise procure—such as making someone impotent and then healing them again. It is called "one into another." The sixth is the *Cabalistic art*—not that of the Hebrews, but the magical kind, which is also called *Gabalistica*, from *alla, Caballia, Gubanala*, named after him who practiced it powerfully; Paracelsus wants it to have flourished in Persia, unknown to the Hebrews and Greeks. These six species are recounted in the first book of his *Sagacious Philosophy*. In Volume 10, where he declares the whole of astronomy, he attributes five species to magic: *images, gamaheos, similitudes, words, herbs.* "Images" correspond to the fifth species, "gamaheos" to the fourth, "similitudes" to the first, "words" to the third; but first concerning the Gabalistic. *Gabalis*, says Paracelsus, *is a spirit which walks from body into body.* Croll calls it the sidereal spirit of man, which perhaps he wanted to exit from the human body, ascend into heaven, and from there assume the powers of the stars, and magnetically attract all knowledge.

The author dismisses these ideas as superstitious vanities and demonic deceptions, contrasting them with orthodox religious and philosophical views.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's polemic against Paracelsian magic. While the previous page examined the definition of the "sidereal spirit" as a link between the stars and the human body, this page focuses on how "Magi" (magicians) like Paracelsus and Oswald Croll use this concept to justify "Gabalistics" (astral magic) and the use of "Gamaheas" (magical talismans). The author argues these practices are not natural science but diabolical deceptions.</meta>

# 54 How the Magi Corrupt Biblical Writings
### 54

For Gabalis <note>likely referring to the *Comte de Gabalis*, a title often used to discuss elemental spirits, or a specific Paracelsian source</note> says on page 36: the sidereal, internal, olympic, incorporeal man—or Gabalis—is the same with the firmament of the stars, just as redness is the same with wine. He is the other part of man: the sidereal body, the genius of man, the household god, the visible shadow of the body, the Lar <note>a Roman protective household spirit</note>, the shadow-man, the familiar "homunculus" <note>a tiny, artificially created human</note> of the Wise, the good Demon, the internal *Adech* <term>Adech: Paracelsus's term for the inner creative or "internal" man</term> of Paracelsus, the specter, the light of nature, the presaging and prophetic *Enestrum* <note>original: "Enestrum" — a Paracelsian term for a premonition or an invisible vital force</note>, the imagination, enclosing all stars within himself, and so forth. *Whoever*, says Paracelsus, *knows how to lead his imagination to such a point that it becomes a born spirit <gloss>original German: "ein geborner Geist"</gloss>, he can rule the Gabalistic art.* We understand, therefore, that the Gabalistic spirit is not something implanted or created within man from the start, but must be "born" out of or through the imagination. What, then, is the consensus of these Magi? <margin>Croll does not agree with Paracelsus. Notable principles of the Gabalistic magic of hags and sorcerers.</margin> *Paracelsus pronounces that nothing is arduous or difficult for the Gabalist, as one who can do more than a magnet. Likewise, he says that Gabalistics demonstrates that we are ANGELS; our soul is eternal, while the body is dead and of no value. He claims that through Gabalistic speed, one who is in the West can speak with one dwelling in the East across several thousand miles; and what is otherwise accomplished over a long time is finished by this art in a short duration. Likewise, things born among us for a time, or made naturally in another way, can be transferred to a foreign region at an unusual time; this is the foundation of the weapon salve <note>original: "unguenti armarii" — a "sympathetic" medicine applied to the weapon that caused a wound rather than the wound itself</note>, of magical transportations—how harvests are carried away by incantations, how hags are transported, and how Trithemius <note>Johannes Trithemius, a famous abbot and cryptographer rumored to be a magician</note> sought a dinner from France or Italy simply by uttering the word: BRING IT.* The reasoning is this: *If a stork can transfer its young born in our lands to Egypt, and if physical or elementary things have such effects, how much more so can spiritual things?* Gabalistics uses magical and spiritual powers (indeed, hags have confessed that they were exported to very distant places in a short space of time), but a most powerful imagination must be added, which you might think is that spirit called upon by adjurations, characters, and other means—the one the pagans call *Jupiter Elicius* <note>original: "Elicium" — a title for Jupiter meaning "The Summoned," specifically through lightning or ritual</note>. You may see the formulas in the little book of Magic; but they were nothing but diabolical adjurations. Otherwise, that persuasion is false: that nothing is impossible for us by the benefit of Gabalistics. It is also false that everything can be sought from the stars through it, since the stars cannot give what they do not have. The foundation of Paracelsus is false: that a man can naturally, through the Olympic spirit, accomplish in a short time across vast spaces what is naturally done slowly and successively. For not even the devil can do all those things. Croll saw this and understood well that neither Paracelsus nor himself attained everything by the benefit of this Magic. Therefore, he joins *a certain incredible imagination and that natural faith*—which is called *miraculous* by theologians—with true prayers to God, a matter concerning which we shall speak in the following exercise. When the augur *Navius* split a whetstone with a razor, when *Numa* summoned Jupiter, when *Simon Magus* <note>a biblical figure associated with sorcery</note> deceived and maddened the Samaritans with magical arts and caused it to be said he was the *Great Power of God*, when Tiridates exhibited magical dinners, and in our own time when a certain *Scottish* Magus performed wonders, etc.—they procured whatever they performed neither by an implanted spirit received from the ether, nor by one born through imagination (unless it be the devil attracted by imagining), nor by natural imagination, nor by that natural faith. <margin>Gamahes.</margin> Let the Gabalistic art therefore be sent back to the devil, by whose chief help it is practiced. What is the *Gamahea* <note>original: "Gamahea" — often referred to as "Gamahez," these are stones or talismans believed to have natural or celestial powers</note>? Its foundation is first explained: that in heaven are *all the powers of all things*, which nature sometimes spontaneously imprints on lower things and reveals through wonderful figures. They say these must also be drawn down by art into a *Gamahey*, or a body; for example: In heaven is the power to heal all diseases. Heaven sometimes heals spontaneously. Therefore, through his art, the "Adept" Physician and his "Attained" medicine draw a similar celestial healing power into a body, by the application of which he may later heal all diseases. *Why not? Celestial nature imprints powers upon words, signs, and Gamaheas (that is, stones like Sapphire, Sardonyx, and other gems figured by art under a certain constellation); if she does this without art and without being asked, she will do it much more when solicited.* This is the foundation: but it is false. For the powers that the Magi ascribe to figured gems by the spontaneity of nature are mostly false. Secondly, the stars do not have those powers; thirdly, that drawing down from the stars is a vain fiction. *From man*, says Paracelsus, *through his spiritual nature, a Gamahea can be made in Magic—or a horse, a dog, a bird, etc.—in which the astral powers perform their work.* This is a lie, and as true as the *armor of Achilles* belonging to a certain similar Magus, by the power of which entire armies could be put to flight, and so on. In Book 9 of *On Natural Things*, he says that natural *Gamaheas* receive power from the seven Planets; but to recognize this requires great knowledge. Why is Chemistry silent here? Elsewhere it says: *whatever is hidden in a thing, it makes manifest.* But since nothing of the sort is within them, <margin>Egyptian Gamaheas as transporters of virtues. Concerning Alexander the Great, Isaiah ch. 23, and Cyrus, and other Monarchs according to the prophecy of Daniel ch. 4 and 12. Concerning Cyrus, Isaiah 44:28 and ch. 45.</margin> it can reveal nothing; and those natural figures arise from other causes. In Volume 7, writing *On the Carline Thistle*, he writes that the Egyptians transferred happiness from one person to others through *Gamaheas*, as well as victories. He claims that *Alexander the Great and Julius Caesar* did not conquer by their own art or resources, but received victories through *Gamaheas* stolen from others—which is an insult against God himself. For it is well known that those heroes were divinely sent into those victories. The Magi claim there are words, characters, seals, images, and wondrous effigies which they inscribed on "virgin parchment," or painted, or engraved on metals, especially *Gamaheas* made of Sardonyx, Sapphire, etc., in the form of humans or beasts, which were equipped with supernatural powers. *Techellus the Jew* <note>a legendary medieval author of a book on engraved gems</note> is said to have excelled in this art, along with the Israelites, to whom God revealed many things in the light of nature—though Paracelsus thinks otherwise regarding Gabalistics. Thus, against crocodiles, basilisks, and darting serpents, he says *Gamaheas* were made in the figure of a man treading on a serpent’s head with his left foot, concerning which *Techellus the Jew* wrote excellently. To these *Gamaheas*, in Volume 6 *On the Long Life*, he opposes a remedy from strong imagination. <margin>Commendation of Techellus. To make magical powers void. The Beryllistics of Paracelsus.</margin> *Whatever happens in a Gamahea and in imaginations*, he says, *with the Planets and signs approaching, must be referred to the superior signs, by which power some mortal might be rendered immune to death.* These and other things Paracelsus says about the magical *Gamahea*, so that you may know what spirit they are of. Add also the rest of what he has on *Beryllistics* <term>Beryllistics: scrying or divination using beryl stones or crystals</term>, which is the devil's oracle giving answers from crystals and similar transparent gems, likewise from glasses and mirrors; on *Thesaurinella* <term>Thesaurinella: the magical art of finding buried treasure</term>; on magical *Azoth* <note>the "universal solvent" or vital spirit in alchemy</note>; on the basilisks of the sky arising from the infection of female impurities, etc. Paracelsus, Volume 4, *On the Falling Sickness*: Gaballia <note>Gabalistics</note> does not arise from spirits, nor from incantations, but from the natural course of subtle nature. Man must be known, openly and magically, visibly and invisibly. See *On the Long Life*, Book 1, chapters 6, 7, etc., Volume 6. Magic is exercised either silently without external and conspicuous symbols, or through instruments...

<summary>This page critiques the Paracelsian concepts of Gabalistics and Gamaheas, arguing that these magical practices—ranging from instantaneous travel to the use of enchanted talismans—are actually diabolical rather than natural. The author dismisses the idea that human imagination or the stars can grant such supernatural powers, attributing the successes of historical figures like Alexander the Great to divine providence instead of magic.</summary>

# 55 Examples Produced from Croll.
### 55
#### E 4
<meta>catchword: hemen-</meta>

...visible elements. Although the devil performs both through the deception of the Magi, and while some well know they are using the devil’s commerce, they nevertheless pretend it is the <term>LIGHT OF NATURE</term> <note>original: "LVMEN NATVRÆ" — In alchemical philosophy, this is the innate wisdom of the created world, which the author here argues is being used as a cover for sorcery.</note>. It is the *Light of Nature to command the spirits of the world to be present for service; the Light of Nature to stir up storms; to transfer fates, just as mountains are moved through natural faith; to remove a man's head and put it back on again; to display magical dinners in a single moment; to affix horns; to drink from a sieve; to halt the long-dead; to pierce whatever you wish, even if unseen, with three arrows from a bow or lead balls from a musket; to see in mirrors all things past, present, and future—acts, writings, words, and thoughts; to heal across the greatest distances; to speak with one who is in Persia while you are in Spain; to read in the disk of the moon writings from someone a thousand miles away; to perform upon a specific person, absent and unaware, those things done to a wax "homunculus" <note>a small human figure or "voodoo doll"</note> or another image by which he is represented; to induce physical bindings <note>original: "ligaturas physicas" — magical charms used to prevent physical action, often sexual</note> and to take them away again; to procure enmities and favors, and to change the affections of men at will; to draw strength from one and bring it into another; to transfer victories; to turn great armies to flight by voice, a whistle, or imagination; to gather mice and flies, and also witches; to render oneself immune from all injury by characters, pentacles, seals, and other means; to make armor, swords, and martial shields which, once seen, rout hostile armies; to conjure cannons so they do not fire; to so affect horses <margin>* Paracelsus ascribes such horses to the Magi of the East at the birth of Christ in chapter 9, book 1 of "On Long Life."</margin> that they carry their rider with the greatest speed even through precipices and bring him back safe from the enemy; to have <term>GAMAHEAS</term> dogs <note>original: "Gamahæos" — usually talismans made of stone, but here referring to spirit-familiars in animal form</note> with which you may do wonders; and likewise to make attendant succubi, sirens, birds, and so forth; to make bells by which you may distinguish harlots; to change the human form into that of a lion, bear, wolf, horse, donkey, crow, etc.; to be able to play so well with dice, cards, and other games that you never lose; to understand the voices of birds, to cut skin with a razor [without pain], to change sticks into serpents, and to perform other unspeakable things such as those remembered in worldly histories.* Indeed, there is hardly any Magus in all of antiquity or in our own century whom Paracelsus and the Paracelsists do not commend, admire, and preach: Paracelsus names Muhammad; a certain Archeus of Hippocrates; Serellus; Styrus; Archalius; Techellus; Virgil, and many others. They themselves condemn wretched little witches as worthy of every fire, yet Paracelsus not rarely excuses them as those who use "ascendants" <note>astrological influences</note> and naturally draw out storms and perform their other deeds. You will better recognize and judge that magical spirit if you read what the ancient Church noted concerning <term>SIMON MAGUS</term> <note>the biblical sorcerer from the Book of Acts</note> and his disciples: <term>MENANDER</term>, <term>BASILIDES OF ALEXANDRIA</term>, <term>SATURNINUS OF ANTIOCH</term>, and others. These Magi did not wish to appear wicked, but boasted of the Apostles or apostolic men as their masters. See *Baronius* <note>Cardinal Caesar Baronius, a major Catholic historian</note> in volume 2 of the *Annals*, year 120, in the times of Hadrian, section 5 and following, where you will read things almost identical to the Hermeticism of Croll. <margin>The Magic of Basilides from his dogmas.</margin> <term>BASILIDES</term> said that <term>THE MIND</term> <note>original Greek: "τὸν νοῦν"</note> was born from the Unborn Father, and begat the <term>WORD</term> <note>original Greek: "λόγον"</note>, from which came <term>PRUDENCE</term> <note>original Greek: "φρόνησις"</note>, and from that <term>WISDOM</term> <note>original Greek: "σοφία" — which Paracelsus admires in magical matters</note>, and <term>POWER</term> <note>original Greek: "δύναμις"</note>, from which virtues, princes, and angels proceeded, which angels built the heaven. <margin>Abraxas.</margin> He said there are as many of these prince-builders of the world as there are days of the year, which, when understood by numerical letters, yield the magical word <term>ABRAXAS</term> or <term>ABRASAX</term>. He used magical images, incantations, and invocations. *Baronius* writes that an amethyst gem was found in Rome, engraved with Basilidian images and marks, such that it is a <term>PARACELSIAN GAMAHEA</term>. <margin>The Gamahea of Basilides.</margin> The form of the gem is oval; on one side is a man's effigy with many letters, a fork, and other things, under whose feet is written <term>ABRAXAS</term>, from which was later fashioned that magical <term>ABRACADABRA</term>, which the superstitious hang around the neck, written in a certain arrangement of letters, against fevers, just as the poet <term>SERENUS</term> <margin>Serenus Sammonicus, who is believed to have been a follower of Basilides.</margin> teaches. On the other side was a sculpted lion upon a staff topped with the head of a bull, by which it is believed a double celestial sign is signified, pertaining to the birth-chart of the one who was to wear that Gamahea, as *Baronius* notes. However, so that heaven, man, and spirit might correspond, that Magus fashioned as many spirits as there are heavens and members in man. Thus, it is persuaded that because of magnetic familiarity, celestial spirits can be easily drawn by the spirits of the members of man, according to the exposition of Croll. <margin>Another Basilidian Gamahea.</margin> The same *Baronius* expresses another Gamahea of the same Magus in the appendix to the second volume of the *Annals*, which is added to volume 12. It was an oval gem likewise found by chance in Rome, on one plane or surface of which was written the word <term>ABRAXAS</term> (the name, namely, of the magical prince of the 365 horoscopes or heavens), and on the other the figure of an armed man holding a shield in his right hand and a scourge or whip in his left, with a pointed helmet on his head. In place of feet, two dragons are sculpted, stretching their heads to the right and left, making a knot or circle in the middle, with their tails hanging from his cloak. On both sides, characters are added: one of a double cross, <image-desc>symbol of a double cross</image-desc> the other like a doubled N, <image-desc>symbol resembling a mirrored N or Z</image-desc> or a prone mark <image-desc>symbol of a horizontal line with a vertical stroke</image-desc> by which figure they designate Jupiter, with a small transverse line added. It is likely that this Gamahea was made for victory over an enemy, and against the poison of serpents, and other things. <margin>Croll's pentacle.</margin> <term>CROLL</term> teaches that a pentacle be made for a Gamahea, on one part of which is a Scorpion, with "constellated mirrors," of which we remember a certain diverse configuration left by a follower of the Assyrian in an inn somewhere. This is the same Magic as that of <term>HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA</term>, who also favored the Basilidian <term>ABRACADABRA</term> inscribed on a figure of the Pythagorean table, as well as the seal of <term>RABBI HAMA</term>, etc. It has the same foundations and persuasions which Croll proposes from Paracelsus, because he also commends Agrippa, adding that whatever God executes through angels, He does the same through the heaven and the stars: <margin>Jesus revoked the oracles of Satan and broke the power of Magi from the Devil.</margin> and especially he preaches the name <term>JESUS</term>, after which the Hebrews have found no more power in ancient names. The same author lists many masters of his Magic, among whom are <term>ZAMOLXIS</term>, <term>NUMA</term>, <term>HERMES THE EGYPTIAN</term>, <term>APOLLONIUS</term>, <term>ALBERTUS MAGNUS</term>, <term>SIMON MAGUS</term>, <term>PYTHAGORAS</term>, who represented letters he had written on the moon, <term>PASTER</term>, <term>SERAPIS</term>, the <term>GNOSTICS</term>, <term>VALENTINIANS</term>, <term>OPHITES</term> (the sect to which Paracelsus belonged, as his treatise *On the Mysteries of Worms and the Serpent* indicates), <term>CERDONIANS</term>, <term>MARCIONITES</term>, <term>MONTANISTS</term>, etc. These are indubitable Magi. The same [Agrippa] finally repenting of his Magic, wrote that he was not affected by his magic because of worldly cares, because a Magus must die or withdraw his soul from his body (which Socrates declared concerning his Philosophers), which few can do. He calls his magical work a school of crimes and impious trifles, or Cabalistic Theurgy...

<meta>This page continues the author’s examination of figures associated with magic and alchemy. While the previous page listed magical feats often dismissed as demonic, the author here shifts to Roger Bacon, attempting to distinguish Bacon's "natural philosophy" from the "vanity" of modern Paracelsian magicians.</meta>

# How the Magicians Corrupt Biblical Writings

<margin>Concerning Roger Bacon</margin>
...he strongly disapproves of and attacks the great magician, and finally *he writes that he had advanced so far in this vanity that he knows how to discourage others.*
<margin>Note: If [Oswald] Croll had done this, or if today's Paracelsians would yet do it, they would be wise. See concerning the exiled Agrippa in the 3rd colloquy of Maiolus in volume 2, where others are also discussed.</margin>
29. Among his magicians, Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa also lists the Englishman Roger Bacon, whose likeness—along with a Salamander—Croll added to the other pictures in the entrance of his *Chymical Basilica*, accompanied by a Philosophical Emblem from the aphorisms of the <term>Great Magistery</term> <note>the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone</note>: *Through the conversion of the elements, let the purified triad become the monad*; by which sentence the entire Chymical artifice is understood. Did Croll, therefore, also consider him a magician of the same sort as Paracelsus, whom he placed next in the sequence of pictures? As far as I know, three of his small works have been published: one called the *Mirror of Alchemy* (*Speculum Alchymiæ*), consisting of seven chapters. In the first, Alchemy is defined as *the science teaching how to make (by art) and to generate (by nature) a certain medicine called <term>Elixir</term>, which, when projected upon imperfect bodies (whether diseased as in chemical medicine, or imperfect metals), perfects them completely in the moment of projection.* In the second chapter, the two natural principles of the generation of minerals are treated: Quicksilver <note>Mercury</note> and Sulfur, from which the primary intention of nature is to make gold; other metals are produced *by accident* <note>as side effects or unintended results</note> depending on the purity or impurity of the principles. From there, individual metals are described. In the third, it treats those things from which the material of the Elixir is to be closely drawn—namely, those which consist of metallic sulfur and quicksilver, but truly from no animal, vegetable, or other mineral; nor from those alone, but joined analogically, or as they say, <term>anatically</term> <note>original: "anatice" — in equal parts, from the Greek "ana"</note>, just as they are already found together in nature, as in natural gold and silver. These must be prepared by art so that they become more than "imperfect," acting as a <term>ferment</term> <note>a substance that triggers a transformation in a larger mass</note>, whose fire is the closest power and is easily led into action once the appropriate external heat is applied to the mass being fermented. This mass is of the same nature as the ferment, but behaves materially and passively, while the ferment acts formally and actively. However, Roger thinks a substance or body can be found made of sulfur and quicksilver as pure as, or even purer than, that in gold and silver—in which nature has labored little—which can be cooked by our artificial fire to more-than-perfection. He says this is made from quicksilver and sulfur proportionally coagulated into a solid mass, in which the clean, clear, white, and red quicksilver is mixed with such sulfur; by ingenuity, these become thousands of times stronger than natural bodies. In Chapter IV, he proposes the mode of acting and the artificial regulation of heat for the proper concoction of the matter, in which the continuation of the fire and <term>Azoth</term> <note>a term for the universal solvent or the essential agent of transformation</note> are sufficient, staying within the "mother’s womb" <note>the alchemical vessel</note>, proceeding from lighter nutrients to stronger ones, and accommodating the degree of heat to each so that the food is thoroughly cooked. The heat should be mild in individual parts, but if the graduations are compared, the following stage should be stronger than the preceding because of the matter. In Chapter V, the quality of the vessel and furnace is described. The vessel of art should correspond to the vessel of nature, so that the heat applied from below can ascend and be constrained, making it long-lasting. Therefore, the vessel must be closed and made of glass without pores, of such a form that it reverberates the heat; it is notable that it should have a small neck that can be sealed or closed with bitumen and placed in a furnace where the heat touches the vessel mediately (that is, with something intervening, for the fire must be vaporous) and thus surrounds it so that it grips it equally from every side. *A triple vessel is required: furnace, crucible, and phial.* In Chapter VI, he reviews the essential and accidental colors; the first is black in putrefaction, after which the colors are accidental and inconstant until the other essential color, true whiteness, emerges. This happens by the movement of the matter itself, provided the proper heat is applied. In the progress of the cooking, redness emerges, which is preceded by *ash-gray*, just as the <term>Peacock’s Tail</term> <note>a stage where many colors appear at once</note> preceded the whiteness. The fire must then be intensified. *These things are for the work toward the red.* In Chapter VII, he touches upon <term>projection</term> <note>the final step of the alchemical process</note>, though it is still imperfect unless you repeat the work. *The Elixir produced is twofold*: one white, changing imperfect things into silver infinitely; the other *red*, likewise converting things into gold, and indeed those closer to perfection more easily; those further away more with more difficulty. Mercurial things are closer; less mercurial things are further away, and so it goes regarding purity, impurity, etc. Hence, some things require preparation. *Nature rejoices in nature; like joins with like, etc.* If the imperfect things are impure, melting alone is not enough, for the *purest medicine* does not enter them. Therefore, one must first project this onto 1000 parts of Mercury and cook them together in a vessel of fixation, as above, through degrees of fire increased over three days. One part of this medicine is again projected onto 1000 of quicksilver, and the *Elixir* is made in one day, which afterwards transmutes other things in one hour or a single moment.

<margin>Note that Paracelsus, in the preface to his book on the plague (Vol. 3, p. 125), calls Roger’s books fraudulent, so this author cannot help the Paracelsians at all.</margin>
<margin>The chapters of Roger's *Mirror of Alchemy* are only principles. Elixir.</margin>
<margin>The matter of chemical transmutation: Gold and silver. Some think antimony or magnesia is the "philosophers' lead." The fire of the great magistery.</margin>
<margin>The vessels of the art.</margin>
<margin>Colors.</margin>
<margin>Projection.</margin>

Thus far *Roger in the Mirror*, in which you see nothing of the six types of Paracelsian Magic. You see the wonderful power of art and nature, which itself is ascribed to magic—but to Natural Philosophical magic, not Paracelsian. Regarding the *matter of the Elixir*, it should be noted: if it can be found in nature by a student just as *Roger* described it, the matter is well; but if it cannot, another counsel must be followed, according to the verse:

> I would dissolve what was taken by a thing sufficiently suited to it.

<margin>Matter of the Elixir.</margin>
<margin>Philosophical mercurial water.</margin>
For a dissolving water is to be prepared from Mercury, which simultaneously becomes the matter from which the mass to be fermented is cooked. It dissolves gold or silver down to a tiny part like a point, which is insoluble. Thus the first matter is made from proportioned mercury and sulfur. This is cooked in an <term>athanor</term> <note>a furnace designed to provide a steady, uniform heat</note>, in one vessel, with one continued regulation of fire until the end. Yet *Roger* can also be understood this way. For if we look at the power of art in nature, truly nature has achieved little in its gold when it is still soft and has not suffered any external fire through the refiners. There is in it Mercury and sulfur in such a proportion that it would be impossible for a man to achieve. The *second work* is concerning the oil of antimony for health and long life, although he thinks the same can also be used for the separation of gold from silver and for transmutation; a view also held by the *Monk Basil* <note>Basil Valentine</note> writing about the stone of FIRE from antimony, though at least in a particular sense. The process is liquid. First, the dissolving water must be prepared through skillful distillation from a pound and a half of <term>Chalcanthum</term> <note>copper sulfate or blue vitriol</note>; one pound each of sal ammoniac and rock salt; and half a pound each of antimony and raw alum. Once mixed, let them be distilled...

<margin>In gold as if in fixed and red Mercury.</margin>
<margin>The Oil of Antimony of Roger.</margin>

<summary>This page analyzes the alchemical theories of Roger Bacon, specifically his work "The Mirror of Alchemy," detailing the stages of creating the Elixir and the transmutation of metals. The author distinguishes Bacon's "natural" alchemical philosophy from the more controversial and supposedly demonic magic of Paracelsus.</summary>

<keywords>Roger Bacon, Alchemy, Elixir, Quicksilver, Sulfur, Transmutation, Projection, Oswald Croll, Paracelsus, Antimony, Mirror of Alchemy, Natural Philosophy</keywords>
