<meta>This page continues the author's critique of alchemical and magical practices, specifically examining the works attributed to Roger Bacon and comparing them with the claims of Oswald Croll and Paracelsus regarding the preparation of antimony and the power of symbols.</meta>

# From the Examples Produced by Croll.

...let them be distilled gently and cautiously. In the water that comes out rectified, let the **best antimony** <note>Antimony, often called *stibium* in Latin, was a key mineral in alchemy used both for purifying gold and as a powerful, though toxic, medicine.</note> be dissolved, and immediately let the solution be decanted; let the **powder** <note>original: *calx*</note> be washed with the poured-off water so that the **tincture** <note>An alchemical extract or "soul" of a substance.</note> is not lost. From this powder, let the tincture be extracted using distilled vinegar by pouring on the vinegar and digesting it for forty days. Once it has turned red and been poured off, more vinegar is poured onto the remains, and the process is followed similarly, repeating it a third and fourth time, so that 160 days pass in total. This digestion takes place in a water bath or in warm dung. The dregs are discarded. The **collected tinctures** are distilled in a water bath so the vinegar can be separated. However, that same vinegar is poured back in if it is still sharp, or new vinegar is substituted for it. It is distilled until dry. The remaining essence is washed with sweet distilled water. Then it is dried again and drenched with **highly rectified spirit of wine** <note>Concentrated alcohol, essentially high-proof ethanol.</note>. It is digested in a water bath for four days. The spirit takes up the tincture; if it does not take it all up the first time, the first portion is poured into another vessel and new spirit is added. If anything remains with it, that too is discarded. The **tinted spirit** is distilled in a water bath, redistilling it <note>original: *cohubando*, a process of repeated distillation where the liquid is poured back over the residue.</note> so often until the colors rise, and with the heat increased, the **red oil** flows into the receiver. Once this distillation is finished, the spirit is separated from the red oil by a light distillation. **The spirit is put away for cases of ill health. The red oil is carefully preserved; it is administered drop by drop for various diseases.** It is said to grant long life if two drops are drunk from wine every year in spring and autumn (after the body has been purged). **To transmute metals**, some prepare gold in such a way that it can be dissolved by spirit of wine and, through frequent redistillations, be changed into an **oily** juice. They call this **potable gold** <note>original: *aurum potabile*; a legendary alchemical medicine thought to be liquid gold and a universal cure-all.</note>, for which various methods are shown, and there is one in **Croll's** work that should not be despised. **Roger** <note>Roger Bacon, the 13th-century English philosopher and friar often associated with early science and alchemy.</note> thinks that the **powder of Sun-Mercury** <note>A symbolic term for a specific preparation of gold and mercury.</note> should be substituted, which is made through a mercurial medicine. One part of this is drenched with two parts of oil of antimony; both are mixed in a closed vial and cooked in an athanor <note>A self-feeding alchemical furnace designed to maintain a constant heat.</note> at the first degree of heat for ten days. The material turns from black to ash-colored. With the heat increased to the second degree for another ten days, it turns white; at the third, it turns red; at the fourth, it is fixed. If you wish to multiply it, you add the Mercury of the Sun and cook it as before. So says **Roger** there. The **Monk** <note>Likely referring to the legendary alchemist Basil Valentine.</note> wanted to make the tincture from softened antimony, but **Roger** refutes him. There is nothing magical here either. For all these things are chemical, medical, natural, and artificial—though I hardly believe that this treatise is truly by **Roger**. For what does it pertain to labor in foreign material in such a foreign way for the sake of transmutation, when the philosophical work is described clearly enough and warns against mineral mediums like magnesia, marcasite, tutty, inks, etc.? **But antimony is Magnesia.** <note>In this context, "Magnesia" refers not to the modern element magnesium, but to a "primitive matter" or a specific alchemical mineral blend.</note> There is indeed in the tincture of antimony the color of gold, which can make pure silver look like gold; but to fix it so that it passes all the tests of gold is not within everyone's power.

<margin>
*The red oil of antimony, prepared essentially, is equivalent to potable gold.*
</margin>

<margin>
*The Rogerian tincture from antimony for making gold.*
</margin>

But is there a Magician in the third work? That will appear from the review. First, he puts forward those **illusionists** <note>original: *præstigiatores*</note> who, by a certain agility, are accustomed to perform wonders for the ignorant and persuade the unrefined that these things are done by a specific peculiar power, when it is actually **deceit**—although, since he includes <unclear>invase mutes</unclear> among them, the matter does not seem contemptible.

<margin>
*Roger’s third work concerning the wonderful power of nature and art.*
</margin>

Second, he speaks of those who call **wicked spirits** into a partnership of the work, thinking that they obey and are subject to their will, when that is impossible. This is diametrically opposed to Paracelsus, who writes that spirits were commanded by God to obey men.

Third, he blames others who try to make **familiar spirits** <note>Spirits or demons thought to serve a magician.</note> friendly to them through **natural things**, or invocations, sacrifices, etc., when whatever good is sought should be obtained from God or good spirits. He therefore rejects two kinds of impure magicians, both of whom are nevertheless Paracelsian, as is clear from **Croll’s** summary on the Imagination of man and his power over all things equal to God. He denies that Philosophers ever cared for these methods, although devils are usually ready if they are invoked.

<margin>
*Roger opposes Paracelsian-Kabbalistic magic, talismans, etc.*
</margin>

Fourth, he treats the question of the **power of charms and characters** <note>Inscribed symbols or occult sigils.</note>. He attributes no real power to these. For he says that **characters** were invented only for the sake of concealment through simulation, as if something great were being done, when they contribute nothing to the matter. He says the same of **charms**. He advises that one should then look to the **work of art and nature**, ignoring those additions. **Magicians**, he says, foolishly trust in characters and charms. Prayer to God for this or that thing is a different matter, asking that God might manifest something, as in the **ordeal by glowing iron and water**; which tests, however, are suspect, and therefore little credit is given to them. So in the past, the **trial water for chaste marriage** <note>A reference to the biblical "Water of Bitterness" used to test a wife's faithfulness.</note> was made. Although magical books may contain some truth, they are nonetheless to be kept away because of many falsehoods, even if they are attributed to **Solomon** and other wise men. In this way, wicked men forge many books with grand titles. Other **characters** are notes of words made from their intertwined letters: or they pertain to the study of the stars, using displayed figures.

<margin>
*Against characters and words used against things: where however Croll (p. 45) thinks that much is attributed by Roger Bacon to characters, words, etc., whether engraved or hung around the neck.*
</margin>

<margin>
*The ordeal by glowing iron and water. Magical books rejected.*
</margin>

Fifth, concerning **seals** <note>original: *sigillis*; astrological or magical talismans.</note>, he says that unless they are made at **their proper times**, they have no efficacy. Here he holds something of the magical. For he thinks that if they are made in the proper constellations according to the faces of the heavens, then both **characters and seals become effective by the power of art and nature according to the virtue of the heaven**. Yet he says there is much error in this, because few are able to arrange anything truly: by which word he greatly disparages the common Paracelsians—who are cruder than Arcadian asses—for their arrogance, whom **Croll** also notes. If those things were true which **Herlucius** <note>An obscure reference, possibly to a critic of astrology or a specific contemporary author.</note> says, that celestial discipline requires much correction, as proved by the great variety of prophets and the uncertainty and falsehood of predictions.

<margin>
*Roger is "magical" regarding seals.*
</margin>

Sixth, he proposes another **use of words and characters**, which is medical, when—to **excite confidence and strengthen the imagination**—the doctor murmurs something or adds signs that have no power in themselves. For some men are so devoted to superstitions that they attribute something even to trifles and nonsense if they are uttered with a religious appearance. For that purpose, **amulets** are sometimes used, and something is conceded to the patient's desire.

<margin>
*Seals could not be made as imagined from the heaven so as to be true.*
</margin>

Seventh, he brings forward a dogma from **Avicenna** <note>The famous Persian polymath Ibn Sina.</note> concerning certain powers sent out from oneself into others by heat and spirits, using the examples of the **Basilisk, the palm tree, a menstruating woman, those who fascinate <note>The "evil eye" or casting spells by looking.</note>, and those affecting others by contagion**, etc. Here he thinks that even the healthy, the well-off, and the joyful can bring some strength to the sick. He believes a greater harm can be inflicted, and a stronger force brought to bear, if he who desires to harm has a strong thought, desire, and intention to harm, which he directs by thought; just as a leper can infect another more if he simultaneously directs his thought, with the natural power in his limbs obeying his thoughts and desires, and this applies in good things as much as in evil.

<margin>
*Avicenna’s dogma on contagion through fixed thoughts. Here Croll has Roger agreeing to some extent.*
</margin>

<summary>This page details an alchemical process for creating "red oil of antimony" as a medicinal substitute for potable gold and critiques various forms of magic. It argues that while symbols and charms are largely deceptive or psychological, certain natural forces and astrological timing (seals) might hold genuine power.</summary>
<keywords>antimony, potable gold, Roger Bacon, alchemy, Oswald Croll, Paracelsus, talismans, charms, Avicenna, medical imagination, contagion</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's examination of natural philosophy and the boundaries between legitimate science and "magical" excess. Having previously discussed alchemical preparations, the author now turns to the works of Roger Bacon (Rogerius Bacchon) to distinguish between natural wonders and fraudulent magic.</meta>

# How Magicians Corrupt Biblical Scripture

<margin>Job 14:4; 15:14, 15, 16.</margin>...especially if the soul is clean of sins—if indeed a clean soul can even be found among the unclean. From this, it is concluded that great things can be accomplished through words and operations when the aforementioned causes align: because words are accompanied by breath, heat, and spirit, and are directed by thought, or imagination, intention, and a strong desire, and so forth. For this reason, when one person yawns, another yawns as well; the living voice possesses great power, and this happens not by magic, but naturally.

<margin>Paracelsian magic arises from excess and hyperbole regarding natural things. Purely magical books: *On the Offices of Spirits*; *On the Death of the Soul*; *On the Notary Art*.</margin> However, while these effects are specific and only effective if the recipient is also disposed to receive the impression, the followers of Paracelsus apply this to everything. From there, they imagine miracles even across vast distances, arguing that if such things occur within the elements and physical bodies, they must occur even more so in spirits. This is a magical excess. Conversely, some sin by "defect," attributing nothing to such influences and claiming they are mere deceptions. Roger Bacon <note>original: "Bacchon"</note> disapproves of both views. In the eighth [chapter] of his books, he writes that some works are purely magical, while others, though they are not, are regarded as such. He rejects the former because they contain neither the work of nature nor art, but mere fictions. In the ninth [chapter], while he approves of those things that can be achieved through art and nature, he indicates that the divine virtues of influences—or rather, the help and blessing of God—must concur. He rejects magic entirely.

<margin>Wondrous works of art and nature. (26 types)</margin> For it is not necessary to descend into magic, since art and nature, with God’s help, suffice for producing wonders. In the tenth [chapter], he declares the admirable power of art and nature through twenty-six varieties:

1. Making ships that can be moved by only one man more swiftly than those moved by many, even if they are vast.
2. In the same way, making <term>self-moving</term> <note>original Greek: *automates*</note> chariots, such as those he believes were the <term>scythed</term> <note>original: *falcatos*—chariots with blades attached to the wheels</note> chariots of the ancients; but he is mistaken. For Diodorus Siculus, in Book 17, regarding the battle of Alexander with Darius, indicates that these were pulled by horses.
3. Making a flying instrument that a seated man might govern. But this was attempted by someone and proved fatal to its creator.
4. Using a small instrument to lift oneself high, and thus escape from the prison of towers, provided the upper door is not closed. He believes this instrument is only three fingers wide and long. But what if you were held by chains, manacles, and shackles?
5. Making an instrument <term>by which one man might pull a thousand others toward himself</term> (unless they resist and allow themselves to be captured), and so on for other things.
6. Preparing instruments by the benefit of which one may <term>walk in the sea and under the water</term>. Dutch peasants are said to be skilled in this art, so much so that they even light fires and sing under the water. Egyptians and Indians can also remain underwater for a long time. There is something on this subject in the books of Flavius Vegetius.
7. Building bridges without piers or supports.
8. Constructing <term>wondrous mirrors</term>; concerning these, see the *Catoptrics* of Giambattista della Porta.
9. By the help of such mirrors, <term>producing the phantom of a huge army</term> and striking terror into men. This would be something to use against the Turks, if they allowed themselves to be terrified; if, however, the Turks used the same art, we would be lost, even if we wished to flee.
10. Other mirrors in which things at a very great distance appear very close. By this art, we could read the tiniest letters and see other hidden stars, just as through the <term>telescopes</term> <note>original: *perspicilia*—literally "seeing-through devices"</note> of the Dutch, by the use of which some believe they have seen the moons of Jupiter and spots on the sun. Others can be made in which nearby things appear far away. Julius Caesar is said to have seen the affairs of the Britons in huge mirrors, if it is lawful to believe it.
11. Other mirrors in which size and position are changed.
12. Manifesting things hidden somewhere in cracks or ambushes through mirrors, as is told regarding <term>Socrates’ Dragon</term>.
13. Ensuring that poisonous breaths and vapors are directed at enemies, in the manner of an upright <term>Basilisk</term>. It would be a wonder if the serpent of Moses and the stratagem of those who threw pots full of snakes at the enemy could not also be referred to here. Some think that by piling many poisons together, they can create a smoke which, if sent into enemy territory with a favorable wind, would kill everyone. The miracle of the <term>Cave of Smellen</term> in Olaus [Magnus] is cited here. But in that smoke, there is both danger and vanity. For fire dissipates poison, and smoke usually vanishes into the air. This is better: to hurl caustic waters from wall-mounted tubes at opposing enemies, or fierce flames from bituminous matter.

<margin>Automated chariots. Scythed. Flying by aerial rowing. Lifting weights. Wondrous attractor. Walking device. Stepping through the sea and under the water. Bridges. Mirrors. Army phantoms.</margin>

<margin>Sending poisonous breaths against enemies.</margin>

<margin>An apparition of golden and gemmed finery.</margin> 14. To produce a "plasma" or appearance of gold, silver, and gems in a chamber, which can be done through mirror reflection, so that a small portion of gold and a single gem might decorate an entire dining room.

<margin>An artificial sky.</margin> 15. Incendiary mirrors <note>burning mirrors used to focus sunlight</note>. 16. To transfer the heavenly family <note>the constellations and planets</note> onto a terrestrial globe and represent the whole celestial economy exactly, such as Archimedes, Drebbel, and others are said to have done. However, the description is not as valuable as the motion, for which a unique material and craftsmanship are required, so that the stars move for us just as they do in the heavens. Roger [Bacon] thinks the material can be found because certain things here move like the heavens, such as Comets, the tides of the sea, etc. But this is an opinion. Drebbel seems to have devised something else, following the example of the motion that occurs in the secret of a stone enclosed in glass, and so forth.

<margin>Lighting fires from a distance.</margin> 17. Pyrotechnics for lighting a fire from any distance: where the materials are saltpeter, petroleum, amber, naphtha, sulfur, etc. From this comes the stratagem of poured fire against an enemy attacking walls, and <term>Greek Fire</term>, by which ships are set ablaze under the water through channels, as well as those called <term>Petards</term>, and many similar things. For fire-projectiles <note>original: *pyroboli*</note> are also made which, when sent high into the air, first thunder there and explode like a lightning strike.

<margin>Perpetual lights. See the fame of the Rosicrucian Society.</margin> 18. Making perpetual lights, such as those read about in Hermes Trismegistus, or those remembered as being found in the tombs of Olibrius and Tullia, the daughter of Cicero—though Guibertus, that dense Galenist among the Lorrainers and a fugitive from the Paracelsian furnaces, does not believe it.

19. Making perpetually warm baths, in the likeness of natural hot springs. Some think these are prepared through quicklime and similar materials that boil with water. In this way, they also heat rooms. But those that burn perpetually are not without bituminous, sulfurous, and lime-like material, like the craters of Mount Etna, etc.

<margin>Making thunders. Job 26:11; 38:25, 34, 35.</margin> 20. To make thunders in the air louder than natural ones: which I do not believe. For Holy Scripture in Job and elsewhere ascribes this to God alone. However, when witches procure this with the help of demons, it does not happen without nature, which the devil compels. But Roger [Bacon] takes his conjecture from that small amount of matter which, when ignited, makes a horrible sound. This is powder of gold or iron, <term>Ceraunius</term> <note>likely "thunder-powder" or fulminating gold</note>, prepared through caustic waters from river salt. If such a sound is made in a small amount, how much will be made in a large one? This art seems to be touched upon near the end of the book, where saltpeter and sulfur are named; the rest are hidden by the abbreviation *Lu ru vopo vir can vtriet*: which I do not wish to guess the meaning of. <note>This is a famous anagram in Roger Bacon's work, which scholars decipher as "luru vopo vir can utriet" = "pulvere carbonum" etc., hidden instructions for the proportions of gunpowder.</note>

<summary>This page lists twenty "wonders of art and nature" described by Roger Bacon, ranging from early concepts of telescopes and submarines to pyrotechnics and automated chariots, contrasting these natural achievements with "magical excess." The author emphasizes that these wonders are possible through the combination of human ingenuity, natural laws, and divine blessing rather than demonic magic.</summary>
<keywords>Roger Bacon, Paracelsus, Drebbel, Archimedes, telescopes, pyrotechnics, Greek fire, perpetual lamps, automata, gunpowder, mirrors, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of "magical" claims by examining specific examples from the works of Oswald Croll and Roger Bacon. The author previously established a distinction between natural phenomena and the exaggerated or fraudulent claims of Paracelsian magicians.</meta>

# Examples Brought Forth from Crollius

Chosroes <note>original: "Cofroes," likely Chosroes II</note>, the King of the Persians in the time of Heraclius, had dining halls where he displayed thunder, lightning, and rain from the ceilings during banquets. Today, such machines are constructed by the fantasies of kings. However, one ought to consider that if a great sound can be produced by artifice in a small chamber, a similar effect can be achieved in the vast clouds and the wide air in the manner by which God thunders.

<margin>To destroy cities and armies.</margin>
21. He <note>Roger Bacon</note> believes the use of this art is *to destroy cities and armies*, and he cites the example of Gideon from Judges 7, as if Gideon had laid low the Midianites with an ineffable crash by smashing jars and lamps. Truly, that good soldier *Roger* <note>Roger Bacon, 13th-century philosopher often associated with early science and alchemy</note> did not read the history correctly. Those three hundred men blew trumpets and sounded the signal, they broke the jars, held the lamps in their left hands, and shouted, "*The sword of the Lord and of Gideon!*" What happened then? *The Lord set every man's sword against his fellow*. Thus, the clattering of jars was not so great a factor. But Roger acknowledges his own <note>original Greek: "σφάλμα," meaning a slip or error</note> error. He says, "*These things are wonders, if one knows how to use them fully in the proper quantity and material.*" But who is there who can? The Lord alone can, as seen in Psalm 18:8, Exodus 14:20, and Exodus 19:16. Magic, which desires to imitate divine things, is mocked in the Wisdom of Solomon, chapter 17, verse 7.

<margin>Magnetic miracles.</margin>
22. Another class of miracles is termed "Magnetic," which is not of great utility, but is nonetheless of wonderful delight. A book was written on these by *Giambattista della Porta*. Likewise, *Anselmus Boetius* recounted some in his book on gems; as did *Scaliger*, *Cardanus*, *Wecker*, and others. Paracelsus spoke some truths and told some lies. *Crollius*, following his opinion, applied this to the *miraculous kinship between celestial and terrestrial things*, and from there he constructed many magical theories—not without the most foolish vanity—which, if true, could defend all impure Magic. However, their vanity is made clear by the impotence of those who preach such things with great bombast. Roger says that *by a stone of gold and silver, all metals can be similarly attracted*. We understand this to mean that the "Philosopher's Stone" <note>the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold</note> embraces imperfect metals through a certain similarity of substance (insofar as they are mercurial) and transmutes them into its own nature. *For nature rejoices in a similar nature, desires to be united with it, and if it is stronger, even turns it into its own nature*. By this harmony, many things are joined, which is why *Roger* writes that *nothing is difficult for him to believe, provided one knows how to properly apply active agents to the appropriate passive subjects in the right way*.

<margin>To attract metals.</margin>
23. Another wonder is produced: not only to describe the governance of the heavens upon a terrestrial globe, but also to add the appropriate motion, as discussed before. 24. *To make an abundance of gold and silver at will*. This would be the highest of desires for most people, if the art lived up to the appetite. It proceeds, however, *from the foundation of "graduation."* <note>the process of refining or increasing the purity of a metal</note> The gold of nature is "perfect" at 24 carats. If someone could "graduate" it to 30 or more, he could later mix in copper or silver until it became gold again, which would have 12, 13, or 14 carats in one mark, and so on up to 24, which would be the perfection of nature. But who can graduate gold in this way? There are many Paracelsian graduations, but all are inconsistent. Thus, they have been held in contempt until now. If anyone wishes to graduate metals by the Philosopher’s Stone, he must first create the Stone itself. But in that case, I would not study graduations, but rather transmutations <note>the complete change of one metal into another</note>.

<margin>To increase gold and silver at will.</margin>
<margin>A marvelous art by which the affections of men are won over or alienated. To change an entire army.</margin>
Miracle 25. *To influence men according to one's opinion*: That is, to produce such affections in them as are most useful to us—such as making the malevolent benevolent, the angry mild, the calm irate, the brave timid, and vice versa. The same applies to the will: if someone denies us many talents of money, we might make him easy to give, if he has it. In this way, loves and hatreds could be changed. *Roger* intends for this to happen not only in individual men, but in *entire armies*. The ancients attributed much to eloquence. Magicians fruitlessly enlist *imaginations, intentions, and natural faith along with the breathing of the heavens*; others boast of *pipes, musical harmonies*, and similar things. Thus, some think that *Achilles' armor* could be made effectively. *Some study to cast enemies into terror and flight by blowing a pipe or a military horn*; others devise other things; all are vain and lack certain experience. There is great power in eloquence, music, and obedience: but the greatest is the power of God, who inclines minds where He wills and leads them through His angels. He it is who overthrows cities and armies, and He has performed this through admirable deeds that no magician—whether natural or spiritual—could imitate.

<margin>A long life is very short for those who promise it.</margin>
26. It is a miracle *to prolong life until the limit of the divine decree*. *Roger* writes much on the power of art in this matter, and there is no Paracelsian who is not profuse with promises to the point of ostentation. For an argument, they bring forward specific long-lived individuals, whether through a perfect regimen of life or through medicines like *oil of antimony, potable gold <term>Aurum Potabile: a medicinal oil believed to contain gold</term>, the Philosopher's Stone*, etc. What need is there for many words? The art fails in the professors themselves. If you throw this back at them, they claim *the limits of nature, a defect in the specific medicine*, and other frivolous excuses. *Crollius* ridiculously denies that *Paracelsus* was able to reach a long life because *he was taken away by poison*. But if chemical promises were true, he should have been fortified against poisons, like Mithridates. Why are we of short life? Moses answers in Deuteronomy 30:20: *The Lord is the length of your days*; and Psalm 90:7: *We perish in your anger, and by your wrath we are troubled. You have set our iniquities before you*, etc. Exodus 20:12: *Honor your parents, that you may be long-lived*, etc. See also Psalms 71, 49, and 31. These are the special "Rogerian" works by which he wished to declare that it is not necessary to aspire to Magic, since nature and art suffice. Thereafter, until the end, he writes certain enigmatic things so perplexedly *about the stone that is not a stone* and other matters, that no one would easily explain them; he gives as the reason for this perplexity that secrets are not to be made public. He died, therefore, with his secrets, and yet the World lives on by God's benefit, just as it had lived before *Roger* was born. Where is that Great Magician of *Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa*, or of *Paracelsus*, or of *Crollius* now? Nowhere. For although he [Roger] proposed some things very much in agreement with the words of the magicians, he was never so delirious as to *approve of Paracelsian Magic*.

<margin>The most lying Paracelsian lies about the omniscient heaven, and man drawing down all science and art.</margin>
30. Let us return to the spirit of Crollius. *In the stars of the firmament* (he says) *lies hidden all animal wisdom (of the "psychic man," as Tertullian calls him following Paul; Crollius understands all wisdom except Theology and Law), the arts, the sciences, and the knowledge of all things. This "sidereal man," or the "Olympic spirit" in man, has drawn this in, so that he himself receives knowledge, etc. For all things are most well known to the Heaven: all actions and events of men are depicted in the stars. These are the words of one who vomits lies like water.*

First, by what firm argument can it be taught that *the stars of heaven know all things*? By what principle can it be shown...

<summary>This page critiques the alchemical and magical claims of Roger Bacon, Paracelsus, and Oswald Croll, specifically regarding the simulation of weather, the transmutation of metals, and the prolongation of life. The author argues that these "miracles" are either natural phenomena, biblical misinterpretations, or outright frauds, asserting that true power over life and nature belongs solely to God.</summary>

<keywords>Roger Bacon, Paracelsus, Oswald Croll, Philosopher's Stone, alchemy, graduation of gold, sidereal man, longevity, magnetic miracles, Gideon, Chosroes</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the Paracelsian school, specifically targeting Oswald Croll and the theoretical underpinnings of "sidereal" or celestial magic. The catchword "Exercita-" from the previous page likely refers to the "Exercitationes" (Exercises) or discussions concerning the Turba Philosophorum mentioned later in the text.</meta>

### How Magicians Distort Biblical Scripture

Is it true that all human acts are inscribed in the heavens? By what valid reason can it be demonstrated that the arts, wisdom, and all science exist in the heavens? Secondly, even if it were granted that they are there, who can prove that **a Magician can attract them into a human being?** I hear talk of the "Magnet" and of "familiarity." But how am I to believe that such a familiarity exists between the stars of heaven and man, when astrologers render the whole of heaven hostile and dire to us for the most part, and God has subjected it to a curse and to vanity because of the sins of mankind? 

Then, **Crollius** <note>Oswald Croll (c. 1563–1609), a prominent alchemist and physician who advocated for the "signatures" of plants and celestial influence.</note>, did you wish to persuade others with portentous lies that you yourself did not even believe? Come now, if you truly believed it because you were a Paracelsian Magician—those who know, as is clear from your writing, *how imaginations were to be joined to sidereal spirits, in what way the intention was to be directed, and how natural faith was to be added*—why then did you not attain all medical, physical, and chemical science, and especially <margin>The ridiculous promises of magicians: though they claim to know the entire method of application, they cannot actually achieve the results.</margin> **potable gold and the Philosopher’s Stone?** Can any Magician show that he ever arrived at all human wisdom by his own sidereal spirit? Why does he assert with such vain words that which is denied to the whole of nature? 

When Cornelius Agrippa <note>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa (1486–1535), a famous polymath and occult writer who later expressed skepticism about the occult sciences in his work "De Vanitate."</note> asserted these same things, he nevertheless denied that he was able to become capable of fulfilling these promises; he claimed that because of a multitude of other cares he could not be entirely focused, or rather, the devil did not wish to give himself entirely to him. Just as, it seems, Nero could not learn the magical art from Tiridates. 

You say that *devout prayers to God* must be added. But, I say, these alone are sufficient. For in this way Solomon was made wise without the heavens, through natural means and ordinary discipline (see 1 Kings 3:9, 11, 12; 2 Chronicles 1:10, 11, 12; Wisdom 7:7, 13, 14). Throughout these texts, all things are said to exist not in the heavens, but in God, who gives wisdom (as in Psalms 16 and 17). So too in Wisdom 8:19–21 and chapter 9:4, 9, 11, 13, 14, etc., where one may see how a man is capable of his own thoughts. So also Christ preaches in Matthew 7:7–11, Luke 11:13, and James 1:5–7. 

But why, since you knew this medium of prayer, did you not pray devoutly? Why do the other Paracelsian Magicians not pray? Is it not because even if you did pray, you would still obtain nothing, because you would not be praying according to God’s will in spirit and truth? Furthermore, your prayer is polluted: it partly looks toward diabolical means and partly tries to accommodate itself to God, even though God does not admit the devil into partnership, nor can the devil stand before Him. Those who ask for wisdom according to God’s will pray rightly; and if they do not obtain what they seek, they know they must acquiesce to the divine will. For the Spirit divides His gifts as He wills. 

Paul so diminishes <term>natural wisdom</term> <note>original: "Sapientiam psychicam," referring to wisdom derived from the human soul/psyche rather than the divine spirit.</note> that he calls it foolishness. In Jeremiah chapter 10, he pronounces the **Laws of the nations, of Hermes,** and of the rest to be vain. Isaiah ridicules the astrologers in chapter 47:10: *Your wisdom and your science (O Babylon) have led you astray.* And in verse 12: *Stand now with your magicians... let the gazers of the heavens save you.* Take note, you Paracelsians! The more you preach those magical things of yours, the more you violate the First Table <note>The first part of the Ten Commandments, concerning duties to God.</note> and bring injury and slander against the Divine Majesty (See Isaiah 44:45).

31. There are many more abominations in **Crollius** in his magical preface, written in favor of the folly of Paracelsus and other magicians; but it is enough to have touched upon at least these few, so that our Paracelsians—**Scheuneman, Hartman, Graman,** and others—may see what kind of "Monarch" they boast of. 

Indeed, even the Philosophers have used the names of **Soul, Spirit, and Body** in the <term>Great Mastery</term> <note>The "Magnum Opus," the alchemical process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> of the mystic stone. But they use them neither in the theological sense of St. Paul, nor in the magical sense. There are two ways this terminology is used in the Mastery. 

**The first way** is when you compare either the parts of the composition or the progress of its refinement. One part is corporeal, the other spiritual, and the third <margin>In the philosopher’s stone: soul, spirit, body.</margin> is, as it were, "soulish" or animal. Thus, in the beginning, all things are crude and resemble a **body**; once worked upon, they become **spiritual**; and finally, like a **soul**, they become the source of motion.

**The second method** is more secret. When the matter is agitated while enclosed in its glass vessel, that which is at the bottom is the **body**; from there rises the **spirit, which carries the soul in its belly.** When this reaches the surface, it is reflected back onto its body and brings the soul down again. This happens with countless reciprocations until at last the body receives its soul and spirit, and all three **having been made from a monad, return into a monad.** <margin>From the five-fold, set aside the two-fold, and reduce the three-fold to the essential monad. Thus, from one through five, two, and three, a return to one is made.</margin> Thus from one there are three, and they return again into one. If you cannot prove this by the great metallic mastery, prove it at least through vegetable, animal, and other mineral elixirs, where you first dissolve the compound of nature, clean the parts, and refine them. For finally, they are joined together again in the "Glass of Hermes" <note>An airtight alchemical flask, the origin of the term "hermetically sealed."</note> by the industry of heat and the governance of weights. 

When the spirit and soul ascend, the body is said to die; when they return, it receives life. Arnold <note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist.</note> calls the purified air the "oil, tincture, gold, and ointment of the Philosophers," which colors, tints, fixes, and makes the matter flow. Indeed, the Chemists speak much of the "Soul of Gold," which is nothing other than the medicine and the perfect Stone itself in the Great Mastery, which is now able to transmute. That which extracts this soul from bodies is called the **spirit**; see that you recognize it in its proper part of the work. For it is not the same kind of thing as "spirit of wine" (alcohol) used for common extractions, though by analogy these names are also applied to them. 

The **Body** that results is now evident: it is that from which the soul is elicited through the spirit; yet it is not something to be discarded. For it must receive both the spirit and the soul through frequent ascensions, descents, and reverberations. In the *Rosary of the Philosophers* <note>The 'Rosarium Philosophorum,' a 16th-century alchemical florilegium.</note>, the stone is said to have a **body, spirit, and soul.** By "body," the author (believed to be Bernard) means the "unclean body"; by "soul," he means the "ferment"; and by "spirit," he means that which acts in the projection and is called the **Fifth Essence**, having the power of converting, etc. 

This is another opinion pertaining to the act of transmutation. The perfect tincture is the **soul**. By the first "projection" onto Mercury, it becomes the **spirit**. This is then projected into the "imperfect body." Alternatively, Bernard, in his book on the philosopher’s stone, says **earth** has the name of "bones"; **water and air** are the "spirit," in which the **soul** resides, which contains **fire and air**. 

In the *Turba* <note>The 'Turba Philosophorum' (Assembly of the Philosophers), one of the earliest Latin alchemical texts.</note>: the body cannot tint itself unless the hidden spirit is extracted from its belly, and the body and soul feel without the spirit—that is, the spiritual tincture, by whose power the colors appear. The *Exercise on the Turba* says: *The elixir is made from the body, soul, and spirit joined together: the soul gives life and existence to the body and spirit.* See more there, though they do not always convey a truly philosophical sense. Elsewhere, the **Soul is the ferment of the imperfect body.** In the *Turba*, they are also ordered to **make the bodies incorporeal like the spirit**, if we wish to extract the thinnest and tinting soul. **The Soul is the tinting spirit. The Body stands (it is the fixed part), the Spirit tints, the Soul vivifies,** etc. But of these things, more elsewhere.

<summary>The author critiques the Paracelsian belief in celestial magic as a distortion of Scripture, arguing that true wisdom comes from God rather than the stars. He then explains the alchemical allegories of Body, Soul, and Spirit used in the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>

# ANOTHER DISPUTATION
### Concerning the
# ABOMINABILI IMPIETY
# OF PARACELSIAN MAGIC
### EXPANDED BY OSWALD 
-> CROLL. <-

### SECTION I.

<margin>
Sections.
Matthew 22:37 & following.
Deuteronomy 6:5.
Leviticus 19:18.
Matthew 18:7.
2 Corinthians 10:5.
See Hermes, & the Platonists, as well as the vain laws of the nations.
Cornelius Agrippa.
Isaiah 60:2.
Jeremiah 10.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'C' featuring a seated figure, possibly a scholar or saint, within an ornate floral and architectural frame.</image-desc>That men devoted to Christ ought not even to admit into their minds anything of magical vanity, let alone spread it to the public through the printing press, is sufficiently taught by the law of the first and second tables of the divine commandments <note>The "tables" refer to the two stone tablets of the Ten Commandments; the first concerns duties to God, the second duties to fellow humans.</note> regarding loving God with all one’s strength, mind, soul, and affection above all things, and not offending one's neighbor. All our reason ought not to be led captive to the desires of the King of Darkness and the Prince of Magicians <note>The Devil.</note>, but rather into the obedience of faith as contained in the Holy Scriptures and brought forth from the heart of the Father by Christ, the true Son of God, beyond which one should not seek to be wise. In ancient times, magic was as subtle among the Chaldeans and Egyptians as any that can be devised by a Paracelsian: yet all those nations are said to have sat in darkness; light arose only among the chosen people of God, and the laws of the nations were vain.

Therefore, even the most refined magic was among those things considered abominable, and for that reason, it was forbidden by so many laws. It is all the more surprising, then, not only that **Paracelsus** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), the Swiss physician and alchemist who challenged traditional Galenic medicine.</note> once indulged himself so greatly as to teach **Magic**, but also that—even though his doctrines regarding magic have been cursed by most people until now—certain men have been found in recent memory who repeat them, illustrate them, expand them, and attempt to defend them with great effort. The power of the Devil, and how much is permitted to him in these final ages, is apparent in this.

<margin>
Two types of Paracelsians.
</margin>
Certain Paracelsians delight in being called such, some entirely so, and others only by reason of their chemical preparations, principles, surgeries, and mineral remedies, as well as their "tartaric" members <note>Paracelsus used the term "tartar" to describe metabolic waste or sediments (like stones or gout) that form in the body, comparing them to the crust in a wine barrel.</note>, which are not read about in this way in the works of **Hippocrates**, **Galen**, or other princes of medicine. The first group of Paracelsians are undoubtedly the "sons of Belial" <note>A biblical term for lawless or wicked people.</note> condemned by the Holy Scriptures and the Church, even if their writings are read in public with special privileges while the laws look the other way and the censors of books remain silent. As for the second group, they do not provide a sufficiently worthy or adequate argument for why they wish to be named "Paracelsians."

<margin>
Tartaric diseases
</margin>
Chemistry <note>original: "Chymica"</note> is traced back to the Arabs, and has been so polished by Europeans that we could easily do without the writings of Paracelsus. They bring nothing new besides the name. The "principles" <note>Referring to the *tria prima* (salt, sulfur, and mercury) advocated by Paracelsus as the basis of all matter.</note> are better derived from chemical separations than from **Paracelsus**. Indeed, we do not think one should labor over a name; but as far as its use is concerned in all matters of Philosophy (excepting jurisprudence and the Theology of Scripture), it is extended too far—as if the whole of natural philosophy must be explained through chemical **spagyria** <term>spagyria: The alchemical process of separating a substance into its components and then recombining them to "purify" it.</term>—and the treatment of these principles should be called back to its proper boundaries. Concerning their number, nothing is certain; for some, there might be more, and for others fewer effective essences extracted from a mixture, so that we should not care much for the **Trinity** [of principles], since groups of four, five, and other numbers can be elicited. **Crollius**, on page 65 of his preface, professes "freedom" in his words: for he wants a physician not to be bound to sects. Yet who is more addicted to Paracelsus, Hermes, Severinus, Agrippa, Roger [Bacon], and their like than Crollius? Who has ever attributed so much to Paracelsus and Lully, who nevertheless disagree in many things? Is this "philosophizing freely"? Certain people think that the principles of **Hippocratic** medicine are either imperfect or not suited to our times (in which new diseases require new remedies from the class of minerals), and that **Galenic** medicine is not at all true. But if they would wipe the glaucoma <note>The author uses "glaucoma" metaphorically for a blinding prejudice or clouding of vision.</note> from their eyes and set aside envy and hatred, they would see more rightly and judge more sincerely. We shall deal with this argument in what follows. The first Paracelsians, especially the **Severians** <note>Followers of Petrus Severinus (1541–1602), who synthesized Paracelsian ideas into a more coherent system.</note>, bitterly persecuted the general medical goal of curing by "contraries." Today’s followers accept both "similars" and "contraries," yet they cannot endure Galen's "qualities" <note>The four qualities: hot, cold, moist, and dry.</note>, which they revile rather than understand. They repudiate the Galenic elements, honors, and qualities; yet they themselves expand the elements, take names from the humors, and—whether they want to or not—are forced to embrace qualities.

Altogether, no suitable argument appears as to why a modest man would want to be called a **Paracelsian**. We have declared how much [impiety] is in that profession in the previous exercises; so that it may be revealed more fully and clearly, let us also add the following sections.

# How the Magicians Corrupt Biblical Scripture

...that all human acts are inscribed in the heavens? By what suitable reason can it be demonstrated that the arts, wisdom, and all science exist in the sky? Secondly, even if it were granted that they are there, who can prove *that a Magician can attract them from a man?* I hear talk of the "Magnet" <note>This refers to the theory of "magnetic" attraction or sympathy between celestial bodies and humans.</note> and "familiarity." But why should I believe that such familiarity exists between the stars of heaven and man, when astrologers render the whole sky hostile and mostly dire to us, and God has subjected it to a curse and vanity because of the sins of mankind? Then, **Croll**, did you wish to persuade others with portentous lies that you yourself did not believe? Come now, if you truly believed them because you were a Paracelsian Magician—those who know, as is clear from your writing, <margin>The ridiculous claims of the magicians, who, though they wish to seem knowledgeable of every method of application, nonetheless cannot partake in it themselves.</margin> *how imaginations were to be joined to sidereal spirits, in what way the intention was to be directed, and what natural faith was to be added—why have you not attained all medical, physical, and chemical science, especially* *that of drinkable gold and the Philosopher's Stone?* Can any Magician ever show that he reached all human wisdom through his sidereal spirit? Why does he assert with such vain words that which is stripped from all of nature? When Cornelius Agrippa <note>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa (1486–1535), a famous occult writer who later famously recanted his belief in the effectiveness of magic.</note> had asserted the same things, he nevertheless denied that he was able to become capable of these promises, because amidst the multitude of other cares he could not be entirely focused—or rather, the devil did not wish to give himself entirely to him. Just as, evidently, Nero could not learn the magical art from Tiridates. You say that *devout prayers to God* must be added. But, I say, these alone are sufficient. For in this way Solomon became wise without the heavens, through natural means and ordinary discipline (see **1 Kings 3:9, 11, 12**; **2 Chronicles 1:10, 11, 12**; **Wisdom 7:7, 13, 14**; and throughout that whole book, where all things are said to reside not in the heaven, but in God, who gives wisdom, as in verses 16, 17, and following. So too in **Chapter 8:19, 20, 21** and **Chapter 9:4, 9, 11, 13, 14** and following, where you may see how one is able through their own thoughts). So also Christ preaches in **Matthew 7:7, 8, 9, 10, 11**; **Luke 11:13**; and **James 1:5, 6, 7**. But since you knew this medium, why did you not pray devoutly? Why do the other Paracelsian Magicians not pray? Is it not because even if you did pray, you would still obtain nothing, because you would not be praying according to God’s will in spirit and truth? Furthermore, your prayer is polluted, for it partly regards diabolical means and partly accommodates itself to God; yet God does not admit the devil into partnership, nor can the devil stand before Him. Those who ask for wisdom according to God’s will pray rightly: and if they do not obtain what they seek, they know they must acquiesce to the divine will. For the Spirit divides His gifts as He wills. Paul so diminishes *soul-based wisdom* <note>original: "Sapientiam psychicam." In the biblical sense, this refers to natural, worldly wisdom as opposed to spiritual wisdom.</note> that he calls it foolishness. Jeremiah in **Chapter 10** pronounces the *laws of the nations, of Hermes, and of the rest* to be vain. Isaiah mocks the astrologers in **Chapter 47:10**: *“Your wisdom and your science (O Babylon) have led you astray.”* Verse 12: *“Stand fast with your magicians, etc. Let the prognosticators of the sky save you, etc.”* Look at yourselves, Paracelsists. The more you preach those magical things of yours, the more you violate the first table of the law, and you wrong and slander the Divine Majesty. See **Isaiah 44:45**.

31. There are more abominations in **Croll’s** magical preface on behalf of the folly of Paracelsus and other magicians: but it is enough to have touched upon these few, so that our Paracelsists—**Scheuneman, Hartman, Graman**, and others—may see what kind of "Monarch" they boast of. The Philosophers <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers to Alchemists.</note> have indeed used the names *Soul, Spirit, and Body* even *in the Great Mastery of the mystic stone*. But they use them neither in the theological sense of Saint Paul, nor in a magical sense. Truly, there are two modes of this usage in the Mastery. *The first* is when you compare either the parts of the composition among themselves, or the progress of their exaltation. One part is bodily, the second is spiritual, and the third <margin>Soul, spirit, and body in the Philosopher's Stone.</margin> is as if it were the soul. Thus, in the beginning, all things are crude and similar to a body; once worked upon, they become spiritual, and finally, like the soul, they become motive (capable of movement). *The second way* is more secret. For when the matter is stirred, enclosed in its glass vessel, that which is at the bottom is the *body*; from there rises the *spirit, which carries the soul in its belly*. When this reaches the surface, it is reflected back upon its body, and brings the soul down again. This happens through innumerable circulations, until finally the body receives its soul and spirit, and all three, *having been made from a monad, return into a monad* <note>The "monad" represents the alchemical unity or the completed work.</note>. Thus from one there are three, and they return again into one. If you cannot prove this by the great metallic mastery, <margin>From the number five discard the two, and reduce the three to an essential monad. Thus from one through five, two, and three, there is a return to one.</margin> prove it at least through the vegetable, animal, and other mineral elixirs, where you first dissolve the compound of nature, clean the parts, and exalt them. For at last they are joined again in the Glass of Hermes by the industry and instinct of heat and the governance of weights. When the spirit and soul ascend, the body is said to die; when they return, it receives life. Arnold <note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist.</note> calls the washed air "oil," "tincture," "gold," and the "ointment of the Philosophers," which colors, dyes, fixes, and makes flow. Elsewhere, the Chemists speak much of the "soul of gold," and it is nothing other than the medicine and the perfected stone itself in the great mastery, which is now able to transmute. That which extracts this soul from the bodies is called the *spirit*; see that you recognize it in its proper part of the work. For it is not such a thing as "spirit of wine" <note>Alcohol.</note>, by which common extractions are made, although by analogy these names are also adapted to those things. What the *body* becomes is now clear: namely, that from which the soul is elicited by the spirit; but it is not something to be thrown away. For it must receive both spirit and soul through frequent ascensions, descents, and reverberations. In the *Rosary of the Philosophers* <note>The "Rosarium Philosophorum," a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise.</note>, the stone is said to have a *body, spirit, and soul*. By *body* is understood the "unclean body" (says the author, believed to be Bernard), by *soul*, the "ferment," and by *spirit*, that which is used in projection and is called the *Quintessence*, having the power of converting, etc. This is another opinion pertaining to the act of transmutation. The perfected tincture is the soul. By the first projection upon Mercury, this becomes the spirit. That spirit is then projected into an imperfect body. Elsewhere, **Bernard** in his book says that in the Philosopher's Stone, "earth" has the name of "bones"; let the hidden "water and air" (the *spirit*) be extracted from its belly, and the "body and soul" feel it without the "spirit"—that is, the spiritual tincture, by whose power the heats appear. *The Exercise of the Crowd*: *“The Elixir is made from the conjoined body, soul, and spirit: the Soul vivifies and gives being to the body and spirit.”* See more there, which nonetheless do not taste of a Philosophical sense. Elsewhere, the *Soul is the ferment of the imperfect body*. In the *Assembly* <note>The "Turba Philosophorum" or "Assembly of the Philosophers," one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts.</note> they are also ordered *to make incorporeal bodies like the spirit*, if we wish to extract the thinnest and tincturing soul. *The Soul is the tincturing spirit. The body stands (it is the fixed part), the spirit tinctures, the Soul vivifies*, etc. But concerning these things, we shall speak elsewhere.

It also interprets alchemical terminology—Body, Soul, and Spirit—as metaphorical stages of chemical refinement and the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>

# ANOTHER EXERCISE
### Concerning the
# ABOMINABLE IMPIETY OF PARACELSIAN MAGIC
### EXPANDED BY OSWALD
-> CROLL. <-

### SECTION I.

<margin>

Leviticus 19:18.
Matthew 18:7.

See Hermes, and the Platonists, as well as the vain laws of the nations.
Cornelius Agrippa.
Isaiah 60:2.
Jeremiah 10.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'C' featuring a seated figure, possibly a scholar or saint, within an ornate floral and architectural frame.</image-desc>The law of the first and second divine tablets sufficiently teaches that men dedicated to Christ ought not to admit any magical vanity into their minds, let alone spread it to the public through printing. This law commands us to love God above all things with all our strength, mind, soul, and affection, and to not offend our neighbor. All our reason should not be led captive to the desires of the king of darkness and the prince of magicians, but rather into the obedience of the faith contained in the sacred scriptures—brought forth by Christ, the true Son of God, from the bosom of the Father—beyond which one should not seek to be wise <note>original: "vltra quam non sapiendum," a common theological warning against intellectual pride</note>. In ancient times, magic was just as subtle among the Chaldeans and Egyptians as any that can be devised by a Paracelsian today: yet all those nations are said to have sat in darkness; light arose only among the chosen people of God, and the laws of those nations were vain.

Therefore, even the most subtle magic was counted among the abominations and was thus forbidden by so many laws. This makes it all the more wonderous that not only did *Paracelsus* formerly indulge himself so much as to teach *Magic*, but that—even though his dogmas regarding magic have been cursed by most people until now—there have been found within recent memory those who repeat them, illustrate them, expand them, and attempt to defend them with great effort. The power of the Devil, and how much is permitted to him in these final ages, is clearly seen in this.

<margin>
Two types of Paracelsians.
</margin>
Certain Paracelsians enjoy being known as such, some in full, and others only by reason of their chemical preparations, principles, surgeries, remedies, minerals, and "tartarean" <note>The term "tartarean" refers to Paracelsus's theory that diseases like stones and gout were caused by "tartar" or sediment in the body, similar to the crust found in wine barrels.</note> members, which are not read about in this way in the works of *Hippocrates*, *Galen*, or other princes of medicine. The first type of Paracelsians are undoubtedly "sons of Belial" <note>A biblical term meaning worthless or wicked people</note> condemned by the sacred scriptures and the Church; yet their writings are read in public even with official privileges, while the laws turn a blind eye and the book censors <note>original: "expurgatoribus librorum"</note> remain silent. As for the second group, they do not provide a sufficiently worthy argument for why they wish to be called Paracelsians. Chemistry <note>original: "Chymica"</note> is traced back to the Arabs and has been so refined by Europeans that we could easily do without the writings of Paracelsus.

<margin>
Tartarean diseases.
</margin>
These "tartarean diseases" offer nothing new besides the name. Their "principles" <note>The three Paracelsian principles are Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury.</note> are better prepared through chemical separations than through *Paracelsus*. Indeed, we do not think one should labor over the mere name; but as far as its use is concerned in all matters of Philosophy—excepting the law and the Theology of Scripture—it is extended too far. It is as if all natural Philosophy were to be explained by chemical spagyria <note>term: "spagyria" (the alchemical art of separating substances into their components and then recombining them)</note>, and the treatment of these principles must be called back to its proper boundaries. There is nothing certain about their number; some extract more and others fewer effective essences from a mixture, so that the *Trinity* <note>Paracelsus often tried to align his "Three Principles" with the Holy Trinity.</note> need not be so greatly cared for, since a group of four, five, or other numbers can also be elicited. *Croll* on page 65 of his preface professes liberty in his words, for he wants the physician not to be bound to any sect. But who is more addicted to Paracelsus, Hermes, Severinus <note>Petrus Severinus, an influential Danish follower of Paracelsus</note>, Agrippa, Roger <note>likely Roger Bacon</note>, and their like than Croll himself? Who has ever granted so much to Paracelsus and Lully <note>Ramon Llull, a medieval philosopher whose name was often attached to alchemical texts</note>, who nonetheless differ on many points? Is this what it means to philosophize freely? Some think that the principles of *Hippocratic* medicine are either imperfect or—in our times, when new diseases require new remedies from the class of minerals—simply unsuited, and that Galenic medicine is not true at all. But if they would wipe the cataracts <note>original: "glaucoma," used here metaphorically for blindness or deception</note> from their eyes and put aside their envy and hatred, they would see more rightly and judge more sincerely. We shall deal with this argument in the following sections: the first Paracelsians, especially the followers of *Severinus*, bitterly persecuted the general medical goal of healing by contraries. Those of today accept both "likes" and "contraries"; yet they cannot bear the "qualities" of Galen, which they slander rather than understand. They repudiate Galenic elements, honors <note>This likely refers to the "humors," though the original Latin says "honores," perhaps ironically or as a printer's error for "humores."</note>, and qualities; yet they themselves also amplify the elements and draw their names from the humors, and whether they like it or not, they are forced to embrace "qualities" in the end.

It does not appear at all to be a suitable argument why a modest man would want to be named a *Paracelsian*. How much impiety is in that profession we have declared in the previous exercises; so that it may be more fully and clearly evident, we also add the following.

<summary>This page begins a formal theological and medical attack on Oswald Croll and Paracelsianism, arguing that their "magic" is a pagan abomination that contradicts Christian scripture. The author also dismisses Paracelsian medical theories, such as "tartarean diseases" and the "three principles," as either stolen from earlier Arabic chemistry or logically inconsistent.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s systematic dismantling of Oswald Croll’s "Preface of Wonders." Having previously argued that Christian law forbids magical vanity, the author now addresses Croll's specific claim that all human knowledge and arts are "inscribed" in the stars, contrasting this with the biblical tradition of wisdom as a gift from God. The discourse then shifts toward the technical vocabulary of alchemy—specifically the triad of body, soul, and spirit—reinterpreting these terms within the context of the "Great Work."</meta>

# How the Magi Corrupt Biblical Scripture
<meta>catchword: Exercita-</meta>

Are all human acts inscribed in the heavens? By what suitable reason can it be shown that the arts, wisdom, and all science are in the heavens? Second, if it is granted that they are there, how will the Magus prove they can be drawn down by a man? I hear of the "Magnet" and of "familiarity" <note>This refers to the concept of "sympathy" or a magnetic attraction between celestial and terrestrial bodies</note>. But why should I believe this familiarity exists between the stars of heaven and man, when astrologers render the whole heaven hostile and mostly dreadful to us, and God has subjected it to a curse and vanity because of the sins of men? Did you then wish to persuade others with Croll's monstrous lies, which you yourself did not believe? 

Come now, if you believed it to be true because you were a Paracelsian Magus—yourselves being the knowing ones, to whom it is clear from your writing how imaginations must be joined to the sidereal spirits, in what way the intention must be directed, and how natural faith must be added—why then have you not attained all medical, physical, and chemical science, especially <term>drinkable gold</term> <note>original: "auri potabilis," a legendary alchemical medicine believed to grant longevity</note> and the <term>Philosopher's Stone</term>? Can you show even one Magus who has ever reached all human wisdom by his sidereal spirit? Why does he assert with the vainest words that which is denied to all of nature? 

When Cornelius Agrippa <note>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa (1486–1535), a famous occultist and author of *De Occulta Philosophia*</note> asserted the same things, he nevertheless denied that he could become capable of the promises, because amidst the multitude of other cares he could not be entirely focused in attention—or rather, the devil did not wish to give himself entirely to him. Thus, it is said, not even Nero could learn the magical art from Tiridates. You say that devout prayers to God must be added. But, I say, these alone suffice. For thus Solomon was made wise without the heavens, through natural means and ordinary discipline. (See 1 Kings 3:9, 11, 12; 2 Chronicles 1:10, 11, 12; Wisdom of Solomon 7:7, 13, 14; and indeed through the whole book, where all things are said to be in God, who gives wisdom, as in verses 16, 17 and following.) So too in chapter 8:19–21 and chapter 9:4, 9, 11, 13, 14 and following, where you may see what man can achieve by his own thoughts. 

So also Christ preaches in Matthew 7:7–11 and Luke 11:13, and James in 1:5–7. But why, when you knew this means, did you not pray devoutly? Why do the other Paracelsian Magi not pray? Is it not because even if you prayed, you would obtain nothing, because you would not pray according to God's will in spirit and truth? Furthermore, your prayer is polluted, looking partly to diabolical means and partly accommodating itself to God, though God does not admit the devil into partnership, nor can the devil stand before Him. Those who ask for wisdom according to God's will pray rightly; and if these do not obtain what they ask, they know they must acquiesce to the divine will. For the Spirit divides his gifts as he wills. 

*Paul so diminishes "psychic" <note>original: "psychicam," referring to worldly or soul-centered wisdom as opposed to spiritual wisdom</note> wisdom that he calls it foolishness.* Jeremiah in chapter 10 pronounces the *laws of the nations*—those of Hermes and the rest—to be vain. Isaiah mocks the astrologers in chapter 47:10: "Your wisdom and your science (O Babylon) have led you astray." And in verse 12: *“Stand fast with your magicians, etc. Let the astrologers of the heavens save you, etc.”* Behold, you Paracelsians! The more you preach those magical things of yours, the more you violate the first tablet of the law, and you wrong and slander the Divine Majesty. (See Isaiah 44:45).

<margin>The ridiculous claims of the Magi, whose mode of application they describe, yet they cannot actually participate in them.</margin>

31. There are many more abominable things in Croll's magical preface in favor of the folly of Paracelsus and the other magi; but it is enough to have touched on these few, so that our Paracelsians—*Scheuneman, Hartman, Graman, and others*—may see what kind of Monarchy they boast of. 

Indeed, they have used the names *Soul, Spirit, and Body*, as have the Philosophers in the great mastery of the mystic stone. But they use them neither in the theological sense of St. Paul, nor in a magical one. Truly, there are two modes of this usage in the mastery. *The first is* when you compare the parts of the composition among themselves, or the progress of the "exaltation" <note>In alchemy, exaltation is a process by which a substance is raised to a higher dignity or potency</note>. One part is corporeal, the second spiritual, and the third as if animal (the soul). Thus, in the beginning, all things are crude and similar to a body; when labored upon, they become spiritual, and finally, like a soul, they become the moving force. 

*The second reason is more secret.* For when the matter, enclosed in its glass vessel, is agitated, that which is at the bottom is the *body*; from there rises the *spirit, which carries the soul in its womb.* When this reaches the surface, it is reflected back upon its body, and carries the soul down again. This happens through countless reciprocations, until at last the body has received back its soul and its spirit, and all three, *having been made from the monad, return to the monad.* 

<margin>In the philosopher’s stone: soul, spirit, body.</margin>

<margin>From the five (quinary), take away the two (binary) and the three (ternary) to reduce the essence to the Monad. Thus from one through five, two, and three, there is a return to one.</margin>

Thus from one there are three, and they return again into one; if you cannot prove this by the great metallic mastery, prove it at least through vegetable, animal, and other mineral elixirs, where you first dissolve the compound of nature, clean the parts, and exalt them. For at last, in the glass of Hermes, they are joined together again by the industry of heat and the governance of potencies. When the spirit and soul ascend, the body is said to die; when they return, it receives life. 

Arnold <note>Likely referring to the medieval alchemist Arnold of Villanova</note> calls the washed air "oil," "tincture," "gold," and the "ointment of the Philosophers," which colors, dyes, fixes, and makes things flow. And indeed, elsewhere many Chemists speak of the "soul of gold," which is nothing other than the medicine and the perfect stone itself in the great mastery, which is now able to transmute. That which extracts this soul from the bodies is called the *spirit*; see that you recognize it when it has gone out into its proper part of the work. For it is not such a thing as "spirit of wine" (alcohol) by which common extractions are made, although by analogy these names are also adapted to those processes. 

The *body* that is formed is now clear: it is surely that from which the soul is drawn out through the *spirit*; but it is not something to be discarded. For it must receive both the *spirit* and the *soul* through frequent ascensions, descents, and reverberations. In the *Rosary of the Philosophers* <note>original: "Rosario Philosophorum," a famous 16th-century alchemical text</note>, the stone is said to have a *body, spirit, and soul.* By "body" is understood the impure body (says the author, who is believed to have been Bernard <note>referring to Bernard Trevisan</note>); by *soul*, the "ferment"; by *spirit*, that which holds itself in the projection, and is called the *Quintessence* <note>The "fifth essence," the purest and most concentrated form of a substance</note>, having the power of converting, etc. 

This is a different opinion, pertaining to the act of transmutation. The perfect tincture is the soul. This, by the first projection upon Mercury, becomes spirit. That spirit is then projected into an imperfect body. Elsewhere, Bernard says in his book on the philosopher’s stone that "earth" has the name of "bones"; "water" and "air" are the spirit, in which the soul resides, which contains fire and air. In the *Turba* <note>referring to the "Turba Philosophorum" or Assembly of Philosophers, one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts</note>: the body cannot dye itself unless the hidden spirit is extracted from its womb, and it becomes body and soul without spirit—that is, without the spiritual tincture, by whose power the colors appear. An exercise in the *Turba* says: *“The elixir is made from the body, soul, and spirit joined together: the soul gives life and existence to the body and the spirit.”* See more there, which nevertheless does not taste of a Philosophical sense. Elsewhere, the *soul is the ferment of the imperfect body.* In the *Turba* they are also commanded to make the bodies incorporeal like spirit, if we wish to extract the thinnest and most dyeing soul. *The soul is the dyeing spirit. The body stands (it is the fixed part), the spirit dyes, the soul gives life, etc.* But concerning these things, we shall speak elsewhere.

<summary>The author critiques Oswald Croll's belief that wisdom is stored in the stars, arguing instead that true wisdom is a divine gift. He then explains alchemical theory, specifically how the terms "body, soul, and spirit" describe the stages of refining the Philosopher's Stone through repeated distillation and fermentation.</summary>

# ANOTHER EXERCISE
### Concerning the
# ABOMINABLE IMPIETY
# OF PARACELSIAN MAGIC
### AUGMENTED BY OSWALD CROLL.

### SECTION I.

<margin>
Sections.
Matthew 22:37 & following.
Deuteronomy 6:5.
Leviticus 19:18.
Matthew 18:7.
2 Corinthians 10:5.
See Hermes, and the Platonists, then Cornelius Agrippa.
Isaiah 60:2.
Jeremiah 10.
</margin>
That men devoted to Christ ought not even to admit into their minds any magical vanity, let alone scatter it to the public through the printing press, is sufficiently taught by the law of the first and second divine tablets. This law concerns loving God above all things with all one's strength, mind, soul, and affection, and not offending one's neighbor. Our whole reason must not be led captive to the desires of the King of Darkness and the Prince of Mages, but rather into the obedience of the faith contained in the Holy Scriptures, and brought forth from the bosom of the Father by Christ, the true Son of God, beyond which one should not be wise. In ancient times, the magical arts among the Chaldeans and Egyptians were as subtle as any that can be devised by any Paracelsian; yet all those nations are held to have sat in darkness; light arose only among the chosen people of God, and the laws of the nations were vain.

Therefore, even the most subtle magic was among the abominations, and for that reason forbidden by so many laws. This makes it all the more marvelous that not only did Paracelsus in the past indulge himself so much as to teach *Magic*, but also that, although his dogmas regarding magic have been cursed by most people until now, some have been found in recent memory who both repeat them and illustrate them, augmenting them and striving to defend them with great effort. In this, the power of the Devil appears, and how much has been permitted to him in these final ages. Certain Paracelsians rejoice to hear themselves called such, some entirely so, and others only by reason of their <term>chemical preparations</term> <note>original: "chymicarum præparationum," referring to the alchemical processing of medicines</note>, principles, surgical methods, mineral remedies, and <term>tartaric</term> members <note>original: "membrorum tartareorum." In Paracelsian medicine, "Tartar" refers to stony or crystalline deposits in the body, which they believed caused diseases like gout or stones</note>, which are not read as such in the works of Hippocrates, Galen, or other princes of medicine. The first sort of Paracelsians are undoubtedly "sons of Belial" <note>A biblical idiom for lawless or wicked people</note> condemned by Holy Scripture and the Church, even though their writings are read in public with official privileges, while the laws look the other way and the censors of books remain silent. The second sort do not show by a sufficiently worthy argument why they wish to be named Paracelsians. The <term>Chemical arts</term> <note>original: "Chymica"</note> are traced back to the Arabs and have been so cultivated by Europeans that we could easily do without the writings of Paracelsus.

<margin>
Two types of Paracelsians.
Tartaric diseases.
</margin>
Beyond the name, they bring nothing new. The "principles" are better produced from chemical separations than from Paracelsus. We do not think one should labor over a name; however, regarding its use in all matters of Philosophy (excepting jurisprudence and the Theology of Scripture), the claim that universal natural Philosophy must be explained through the <term>spagyric art</term> <note>original: "spargiria," a term for alchemical separation and recombination</note> of chemistry is extended too far, even if the treatment of "principles" has its own limits. Regarding their number, nothing is certain; some extract more and others fewer effective "essences" from a mixture, so that one need not care much about the *Trinity* <note>The Paracelsian "Tria Prima": Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury</note>, since groups of four, five, and other numbers may be elicited. *Croll*, on page 65 of his preface, professes freedom in words: for he wishes that a physician should not be bound to sects. But who is more addicted to Paracelsus, Hermes, Severinus, Agrippa, Roger <note>likely Roger Bacon</note>, and similar figures than Croll? Who has ever attributed so much to Paracelsus and Lully, who nevertheless disagree in many things? Is this "philosophizing freely"? Some think the principles of Hippocratic medicine are either imperfect or not suited to our times (in which new diseases require new remedies from the mineral class), and that Galenic medicine is not at all true. But if they would wipe the cataracts from their eyes and put aside envy and hatred, they would see more rightly and judge more sincerely. We shall deal with this argument in what follows. The first Paracelsians, especially the followers of Severinus, bitterly persecuted the general medical goal of curing by "contraries." Modern Paracelsians accept both "similars" and "contraries," yet they cannot bear the "qualities" of Galen <note>The traditional system of Hot, Cold, Moist, and Dry</note>, which they slander rather than understand. They repudiate the Galenic elements, honors, and qualities; yet they themselves also multiply the elements, draw names from the humors, and are forced to embrace the qualities whether they want to or not.

Altogether, there appears no suitable reason why a modest man would wish to be named a Paracelsian. How much is contained in that profession we have declared in the previous exercises, and so that it may be more fully and clearly open to view, let us add the following.

He critiques Oswald Croll for claiming intellectual freedom while remaining slavishly devoted to occult authorities, asserting that traditional Galenic medicine remains superior to "spagyric" innovations.</summary>
