### On the horrible impiety of Paracelsian Magic

...to Müntzer <note>Thomas Müntzer (1489–1525) was a radical German Reformation theologian and a leader in the Peasants' War. The author compares him to Paracelsus to imply they both stir up social and spiritual disorder.</note> and his associates, who filled Germany with the peasant uprising. There is no little old woman who does not magnify her own dreams; no witch, nor sorcerer who does not do the same.

<margin>Pimander or shepherd of oaks <note>original: "ποιμὴν δρῶν"</note>.</margin>
Fifth, when the guiding spirit was asked who he was, he called himself *Pimander* <note>Poimandres, the "Shepherd of Men," is the title of the first book of the *Corpus Hermeticum*, a collection of ancient philosophical texts attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</note>. We read of this name being attributed to the true God or his angel nowhere in scripture. Indeed, the name sounds the same as "Shepherd of men" or "Shepherd of oaks"—a King or a pastor. It is not the role of a King to profess the nature of things. Therefore, we understand that he has assigned himself a function that does not belong to him and has abandoned his own duties. Physics must be learned from physicists, not from the "shepherds of men" or the leaders of Republics. Therefore, when our Magi admire that writing, they show themselves to be deranged by a vain superstition, and they do not consider what kind of spirit they are following.

Sixth, he lies when he says *he is the mind of divine power*. Although this is said inconsistently with sacred things—because the divine power and the mind of God are distinct properties of God—nonetheless, if he were the mind, he would be the Wisdom of God. For to this Wisdom is attributed whatever wise doctrine exists anywhere, as seen in Proverbs 1, and chapters 8 and 9; Wisdom of Solomon 6, 7, 8, and 9; and elsewhere. Psalm 104:24 says: *God made all things in Wisdom*, that is, *in the Son, who was made wisdom for us*. (Colossians 1:16, John 1:3, 1 Corinthians 1:30). Hermes himself says: *The Word is the Image and mind of God. In him are hidden all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge* (Colossians 2:3). He himself gives to men what they know (Psalm 94:10), he who also knows that the thoughts of men (such as Hermes) *are vain* (ibid. verse 11). Now, however, Christ the Son of God cannot be understood to be in that apparition [of Pimander]. The reason is this: because although he revealed to us the secrets of God pertaining to salvation, he indicated nothing of such things: *He said nothing about such a union or conjunction with God*. Furthermore, the things contained in the *Pimander* fight face-to-face against the revealed mysteries of the Church; at least let these things from Dialogue XII be seen: <margin>The impious dogmas of Pimander.</margin> *Most of all,* (says that devilish Pimander) *the man who grasps God and is conformed to the divine essence is immortal. For to this man alone out of every kind of living thing does God himself join, by night indeed through dreams, and more frequently by day through portents, through all of which he foretells the future to himself BY BIRDS (Augury), ENTRAILS (Divination by organs) <note>original: "PER AVES... INTESTINA"</note>, the Spirit (the Pythonic spirit), and the Sibyl, because of which man is truly said to know things past and future. Likewise: Mind is God in men. Therefore, some from the number of men are gods, and their humanity is closest to divinity. The evil sin by fate, and they suffer so that they may suffer; therefore, a shameful thing is committed (which is the faith of Predestinations). The human soul is superior to fate. There is nothing in the world devoid of life. God willed the world to be a living thing.* <margin>The Paracelsian life of all things, and Plato’s world.</margin> From which it follows that the world must be God. Truly, there is no need for more. A piously learned man immediately detects the devilish lies and the dogmas of Heathen religion often forbidden by God.

Seventh: Hermes desires to learn the nature of things and to know God. If Pimander had been the spirit of truth, he would have begun from the true creation of the World and would have taught about the integrity and the fall of man. If he had described man as he is because of sin, he certainly would not have made him a "third God" or a "great miracle," much less elevated him into God himself. There was also the son of Sirach <note>The author of the biblical book Ecclesiasticus.</note> in Egypt. But what he thinks of man, you have in chapter 10, verse 9 and following: *where he is called earth, ashes, a slave of doctors, the spoil of death, the food of worms, serpents, and toads. What is man in the judgment of God?* Psalm 78, verse 39: *He remembered that they are flesh; a spirit that passes away and does not return.* See Psalm 90:7 and following; Psalm 103:14–16; Psalm 39:5–6; Job 7:7, chapter 10:9–10, chapter 11:11, chapter 13:28, chapter 14 throughout; chapter 15, 16, chapter 17:14, chapter 20 throughout, chapter 21:26, chapter 25:6. Behold the "great miracle" of Man! Where are you, Hermes? Where are you, Croll, who repeated that from Hermes? The beginning of pride is to apostatize from God. Thus fell Lucifer and the first-created parents. Pimander ought to have taught these things, and shown his Hermes the remedy from the revealed God, not from a hidden God. But as the ancient serpent seduced Eve, so Pimander seduced his Mercury <note>Hermes is the Greek equivalent of the Roman Mercury.</note>: who, while he desires to know God, is drawn furthest away from God toward heathen auguries and the worship of demons.

Eighth: Pimander commands that *he be comprehended by the mind*. He himself had said he was the mind of divine power; he commands that he be grasped by the mind, so that "like may be grasped by like." What the method of grasping and understanding God is, he explains in Dialogue II: *Contemplate, he says, God in himself, having all intelligences. Unless you make yourself equal to God, you will never understand God. For like is always known by its like*, etc. You see the fraudulent spirit. He persuades man of that which is impossible for him. Thus, that miserable Pimander certainly never arrived at the knowledge of God; and because eternal life is in the knowledge of God and Christ (John 17), it must be concluded that this little man [Hermes], whom Croll prefers over our Philosophers and even some Theologians, is a slave of eternal death, bound in the chains of eternal darkness. How then does this "Gabalistic" <note>Kabbalistic</note> Magic of Hermes please you, O Paracelsian Magi?

Ninth: the promise is vain. *I will instruct you,* he says, *in all things which you have desired.* <margin>Croll ought to have noted this, who thought that through his "spirit of the stars" all wisdom, arts, and sciences were drawn from the all-knowing heaven. What need then for the spirit Pimander and other means?</margin> It is a lie, I say. For God never decreed to manifest all things to man in this age, but only those things which he proposed in the book of nature—in which, however, the greatest things lie hidden—and in the scriptures pertaining to salvation. What is to be said of the future? Isaiah 64, verse 4. In 1 Corinthians 2:9, Paul takes true wisdom away from the princes of this age, but grants something to the pious ministers of Christ because of the revelation of the spirit and the mind of Christ; but how little that is, he teaches in Romans 11 near the end, and in 1 Corinthians 13:9–10, etc. Christ promises the Holy Spirit to his disciples in John, but the Spirit who will not say anything other than what is consistent with the doctrine of Christ. And not even Christ taught things other than what he had heard from the Father and held in his commands. Pimander, however, a most liberal and profuse promiser, proves by this very fact that he is not a spirit from God.

Tenth: This "Blessed" Hermes writes that all things (which he had desired concerning nature and God) were suddenly revealed to him; but he was miserably deceived. For he has not yet learned to know himself, and he has been cast headlong by his spirit into the pit of eternal damnation, as can be understood from so many impieties and follies of Pimander and Asclepius <note>Asclepius is another major text in the Hermetic corpus.</note>. And so much for these things from Pimander. He who has the *Amphitheater* of Khunrath <note>Heinrich Khunrath (1560–1605), a German physician and Hermetic philosopher. The "Amphitheater" refers to his famous work, *Amphitheatrum Sapientiae Aeternae*.</note>, in the sixth image...

<summary>This page contains a fierce theological critique of the Hermetic text 'Pimander,' labeling its guiding spirit a demon and its teachings as blasphemous lies that lead followers away from Christ and toward eternal damnation. The author specifically attacks the Hermetic idea that man can become equal to God or gain total knowledge of nature through magical or pneumatic means.</summary>

<page-num>69</page-num>
->...according to Croll’s opinion.<-

<margin>Thrasybulus's Magic <note>Thrasybulus is likely a reference to a character in a dialogue or a specific occult author known to the writer.</note>.</margin>
One can see in an image a globe consisting of a circle, a triangle, and a square in the middle, displaying three spheres in its circumference. In the center of these is the **ladder of union**, which encompasses *faith, meditation, knowledge, love, hope, prayer, conjunction, frequency, familiarity, and likeness.* No doubt he thought these should be observed even in that *Kabbalistic union with God*. If his explanation responded to the divine scriptures, something worth saying might have been uttered. Into the first sphere, he assigned the degrees of magical knowledge, which are: <margin>Degrees of magical knowledge.</margin> *the object, the medium, the external senses, the internal sense, common sense, imagination, lower and upper judgment, Reason, intellect, and mind.* If he thought that one could ascend in this manner all the way to the Archetype <term>Jehovah</term> <note>The "Archetype" refers to the original, divine pattern of the universe in Neoplatonic and Hermetic thought.</note>, he did not think in accordance with the Gospel scripture, which shows the true door and the true ladder to be Christ, and the Spirit as the guide through the doctrine of faith. *No one comes to Christ unless it is given by the Father, and whom the Father draws.* (John 6, verse 44). It is a wonder, however, that his memory failed him.

In the preceding figure, upon which he inscribed the Decalogue in Hebrew along with the names of God and others, he ascends through <term>En-soph</term> <note>original: "En-soph"; the Infinite or Divine Origin in Kabbalistic thought</note> to the magic triangle of **Jehovah**. The Magi also have 50 gates of intelligence <note>A Kabbalistic concept referring to 50 levels of understanding created by God.</note> derived from the five predicables and ten categories multiplied among themselves. In the second book of *Caelius Augustinus* <note>Caelius Augustinus Curio (1538–1567), an Italian humanist.</note>, who added some things to the hieroglyphics of *Pierius*, there is an image of a Capricorn leaping out of a certain house. This is said to be called <margin>The magical Gate of the Gods.</margin> the "Gate of the Gods" by the Platonists, because they claim that through it, souls released from the chains of the body ascend into heaven, where they become participants in the divine nature. This too is a magical dream pertaining to the Kabbalistic union; the demon is even accustomed to carry away hags and sorcerers to their solemnities in the form of a goat. But it is to be feared that they are being led by <margin>Whoever desires more should read the treatise of Jacob Heilbronner on Magical and Kabbalistic Demonomania, Pistor, etc.</margin> Mercury to the underworld. So much for the Kabbalistic union of the *created spirit with the uncreated God*, the finite spirit with the immense God, of a miserable little man—mere straw and stubble—with God the consuming fire, of a magical fiction with the truth, of Belial with Christ, and so forth, as Croll continues.

### SECTION VIII.

<margin>The double Word</margin>
God is addressed by the internal spirit, not by the power of words, but in a sacred silence without the opening of the mouth or the drawing of breath. Even the Philosophers recognize a double <unclear>Word</unclear> <note>original Greek: "λόγον" (logos)</note>. For there is <unclear>the internal word</unclear> <note>original Greek: "ὁ ἔσω" (ho eso)</note>, which is also <unclear>the internal thought</unclear> <note>original Greek: "ἐνδιάθετος" (endiathetos)</note>, and <unclear>the external word</unclear> <note>original Greek: "ὁ ἔξω" (ho exo)</note>, which is <unclear>the spoken word</unclear> <note>original Greek: "προφορικός" (prophorikos)</note>, occurring when the formal image conceived in the mind, mixed with material sound, flows forth into the air; there, multiplied by the divisibility of the air, it is carried to and perceived by the ears of those present. It is not necessary to emit shouts to God for His own sake, for He understands both thoughts <unclear>and the internal word</unclear> <note>original Greek: "καὶ τὸν ἔσω λόγον" (kai ton eso logon)</note>, as the example of Moses teaches in Exodus 14, verse 15. Truly, the affections of the soul and the circumstances also express a voice. It is well known how much the Magi attribute to words, characters, and other sensible signs at other times. Not infrequently, demons apply their signs to others, from which they easily understand what they want, <margin>See page 44 of Croll.</margin> although they primarily seek the glory of knowing hearts.

That is indeed impious and diabolical: but *Croll*, with the rest of the Kabbalists, dares to turn it into sacred prayers and sacred silence, *as if when the Magi seem to produce wonders, it happens through the power of silent prayers to God.* That is a part of *Pythagorean Magic* which they call <unclear>the vow of silence</unclear> <note>original Greek: "ἐχεμυθίαν" (echemythian)</note>. According to the opinion of the Chaldeans, <margin>The foundation of diabolical magic: the "Gabel-journey," that is, the Kabbalistic journey.</margin> *Agrippa* writes that a mind fixed with its whole focus upon God can be filled with the divine power; thus filled with light, it pours out rays through every medium even to this thick body, surrounding it also with abundant light and making it like the stars, lifting it high with lightness, and sometimes transferring it over a long distance. Others pass through closed doors. They claim God must be invoked through mediators—souls, intelligences, demons—and that loud prayer is useless, etc. *Croll* on page 34: "A mind elevated to the most high God and united with Him is preserved with God, and by that same work, it effects things; nor is anything found in man, not any disposition (even sin, and the evils arising from it?) in which something of divinity does not shine, nor is there anything in God that is not represented in man." On page 71: "If the soul returns within itself and turns toward the mind, it approaches God, sees all things, and has no need of external instruction, in the manner of the angels who learn all things from within," etc.

This is that precept of Pimander, in which he commands Hermes to embrace himself with his own mind and receive information on all things he wishes. The mind must be elevated through the fifty gates of intelligence on the Magical ladder into the <term>En-soph</term>, and from there, power must be brought down in turn, as it pleases other Magi. All this is done not by oral but by mental invocation. Thus Simon Magus the Samaritan performed miracles by keeping silent. Thus *Trithemius*, and *Laurentius* (who was Pope Gregory VII), a master in the Magical art according to Cardinal Benno; *Michael Sicidites* of Constantinople, according to Nicetas Choniates; *Zython* the Bohemian, as reported by Dubravius; *Diodorus Siculus* of Catania, reported by Fazello; *Pasetes* the Magician; *Zedechiah* the Jew, who lived in the time of Louis the Pious; *Eon* the heretic; *Apuleius's Innkeeper*; *Apollonius of Tyana*; and countless other Magi procured wonders, although not always without a voice. Therefore, whoever wishes to know what that *sacred silence of Croll* is, learns from this that it is a diabolical pretext for abominable and accursed magic, so that the simple-minded may be persuaded that what the devil executes at the command of magicians is actually done by the power of interior prayers. Such silence is accursed, not sacred. Even in invoking Him, God <margin>Croll's "sacred silence." 1 Corinthians 4.</margin> requires expressed words for the edification of others, as is done in the Church with the priest leading; and in the brightness of His glory even among the angels, and for the terror and flight of devils. Perhaps the Kabbalist *Paracelsus* will deny that his actions, done through silent prayers, are similar to those of these diabolical magicians; indeed, he will say his silence is sacred in essence, while theirs is through imitation. For the devil is accustomed to imitate the deeds of God and the Saints. But the histories of true miracles performed by the Prophets, Christ, and the Apostles do not mention this "sacred silence," but mostly indicate that words or external gestures were added. The Magi themselves confess that God created all things through the word "LET IT BE" (*FIAT*). Although God's speaking differs from human speech, it is nonetheless an argument that speech also contributes to miracles. Who would allow themselves to be persuaded that a silence is sacred whose foundations and effects are the same as those of the devil?

<meta>This page continues the author’s systematic debunking of Hermetic and Paracelsian philosophy. In the previous section, the author critiqued the "ladder of union" and the symbolic geometry used by Oswald Croll. Here, the focus shifts to the "Second Column" of Croll's system: "Natural Faith," which the author condemns as blasphemous for equating man’s power with God’s.</meta>

# On the Horrible Impiety of Paracelsian Magic

...are they consistent with the principles, pretexts, and deeds of the Magicians of the Chaldeans, Egyptians, and other peoples and times? Can those be true prayers to God which proceed from these Mages? *Simon Magus* <note>Simon Magus, or Simon the Sorcerer, is a biblical figure from Acts 8:9–24 who attempted to buy the power of the Holy Spirit; he is often cited in anti-occult literature as the father of all heresies.</note> was a Samaritan. Concerning the Samaritans, Christ says in *John 4:22: "You worship what you do not know."* If the Mages do not know the true God, how can they move Him with their secret prayers? For He is the true God who revealed Himself at the creation of the world, by the Word sent from heaven, and by His Son, the promised and manifested redeemer of the human race. This God hates and abominates the Egyptian magic and the Kabbalistic art <note>original: "Gabalisticam," a common early modern variation of "Kabbalistic."</note> of Paracelsus, and has strictly forbidden it; He is so far from loving it that He has never confirmed its hearings by any command or promise. Behold for yourself the "beautiful" magic of Croll and other Paracelsians: Behold Hermes, "pure in heart, holy, and illuminated by the Holy Spirit," as it is said on page 28 of Croll’s work. Such is the "first column" of their Kabbalah.

---

->SECTION IX.<-

<margin>Natural Faith.</margin> The second column is called NATURAL FAITH. Concerning this, it is said to be *wisdom communicated and inborn by God the Father to all men equally at creation, as a sort of private property and inheritance.* Likewise, on page 38: *through natural faith we are purified (made equal) to the spirits themselves, and through it, with the addition of imagination, all magical operations and all wonders are performed.* On page 46, Croll raves in this way: <margin>Note the horrible blasphemies</margin> *The true Kabbalist, whom Paracelsus calls the "natural divine," who is made equal to the prophets, and whose mind—united and made equal to God—DOES EVERYTHING HE WILLS; but he wills what God Himself wills, GOD-LIKE <note>original: "DEÄLITER," meaning to act in the manner of a deity.</note> above nature, with firm confidence and a miraculous faith in miracles. The Door is the single most Divine name IHSVH <note>IHSVH is the "Pentagrammaton," a Christian Kabbalistic version of the Hebrew name for Jesus, formed by inserting the letter Shin into the Tetragrammaton YHWH.</note> in which all things are recapitulated and contained; that is, in the miraculous word. Through the MIND (the third column of Kabbalah), FAITH (the second), and prayer (the first—namely, prayers made in spirit and truth), he executes everything in a moment.* (As he says a little later: For in these three principal things consists the entire foundation of Magic and the Kabbalistic art, as can be seen in Paracelsus, Book 3 of *The Signature of Things* <note>original: "de signatura rerum," a foundational text by Paracelsus regarding the hidden signs in nature.</note>, although Croll a little earlier had also endowed characters and "Gamaea" stones <note>Gamaea (or Gamahé) refers to stones or amulets naturally marked with figures or signs believed to have magical properties.</note> with the same power): *whatever a Physician effects naturally through the delay of time by the successive action of herbs, etc.* Page 83: *Firm belief, indubitable hope, love, and confidence toward the physician and the medicine contribute greatly to health. Natural faith, inborn from God the Father in the first creation—namely, our Imagination—is so powerful that it is able to heal and stir up diseases.* And a little later: *To the believer all things are possible (he attributes this faith to the "sidereal spirit" in man). Faith makes all certain things certain. God can in no way be defeated except through faith (he does not, however, as he says, wish to speak of the faith of Grace from Christ). He who believes in God, he works through God, and to God all things are possible. But how that happens cannot be scrutinized. Faith is at least the work of him in whom one believes.*

> <margin>Magical Progression. The Physician from faith.</margin> Thoughts surpass the operations of the stars and the elements. For while we think, and have faith in our thoughts, then faith perfects the work, and without faith nothing happens. Faith gives to the imagination, imagination gives to the star, the star gives the effect or the work. To apply faith to medicine gives the spirit of medicine; the spirit gives the knowledge of medicine; medicine gives health. From this it follows that the physician is prayed to from faith: according as he has believed, the spirit of nature—or the star of medicine—promotes and helps him, etc. Read more there. That Mage pours out all these things concerning natural faith most impudently and intricately—things worthy of clubs, the whip, or the flames.

From those writings, a "universal" natural faith is first elicited in the Kabbalistic art, through which all things that God performs can be effected by prayer and <margin>Natural faith of the Paracelsian mages.</margin> imagination, as if the Mage held God captive and dragged Him down to everything the Mage wills. Secondly, there is a "particular" faith in medicine. This is twofold: one in the physician, the other in the sick person. From this, he teaches that miraculous healings are procured, as in the *Panacea of Assylvanus* <note>The "Panacea Assyluani" refers to a universal remedy associated with the followers of Paracelsus.</note>, etc. If this faith should fail, he asserts that an impediment is created, and the efficacy hoped for from the medicine does not follow. If you ask: how is it that this faith is so rare? we receive the response: because a pure mind is required for it (page 27). From a man it makes one *all-knowing and omnipotent*, but one who is defiled by sins cannot exercise that power. <margin>Man as God from creation and relation to God. The power of sin.</margin> For thus he says (blasphemously): *We are from all things, and we carry all things in ourselves, NO DIFFERENTLY than (as) God our father himself. The Son (man) possesses all things equally with his father.* Therefore, all natural and super-supernatural goods are already within man: but just as that divine character is darkened in us by sin, so once sin is purged and expiated, it shines forth again more and more. In the center of the spirit all things lie hidden; but through sin we have fallen asleep to the gifts offered by God, so that we neither see, nor feel, nor believe those goods to be present in us. The intellect, oppressed by the body, lies hidden like fire under ash, etc.

We hear or see nothing but mere ravings and blasphemies. From this we argue: if sin stands in the way <margin>Against the magic of Croll from Croll himself.</margin> so that we do not feel the goods within us and cannot reach miraculous effects through them; in this life, Magic has no power. For man does not entirely strip off the flesh of sin in this life. *The former is true*, as Croll says: *Therefore also the latter.*

II. If no mind is more impure than the mind of a mage, and none more blind: nevertheless, the Kabbalistic Mages can do nothing of themselves. The reason is that for Kabbalistic works, a purity of mind is required; the greater it is, the more perfect the works exist. But the *antecedent* is true. Therefore also the *consequent*. I prove the premise: because he attributes to himself that which he is ignorant of, and which he cannot know because of the darkness, torpor, and sleep of sin. While knowledge according to nature is indeed co-created in terms of potential, it is so shattered that scarcely a shadow remains; and in divine or spiritual matters it is so much a "nothing" that all that the "natural man" <note>original: "homo psychicus," the soulish or unspiritual man mentioned in 1 Corinthians 2:14.</note> thinks he knows is called "foolishness" by the Holy Spirit. Furthermore, he has need of activation through the senses, to which the mage attributes nothing; moreover, he even says the body stands in the way of the intellect, following Socrates in Plato. Besides, he speaks insultingly against God when he equates man to Him as a son to a father: it is necessary that the mage's mind be darkened by more than Cimmerian darkness <note>A classical metaphor for total darkness, referring to the mythical Cimmerians who lived in perpetual mist.</note> and polluted by Tartarean dregs.

It is a most foolish and extremely reckless presumption to claim that man has all divine things within himself. <margin>A Lie.</margin>

<summary>This page critiques Oswald Croll's concept of "Natural Faith," where he claims humans can perform miracles and healings by realizing their "god-like" nature. The author rejects this as blasphemous, arguing that human sin and inherent limitations make such magical power impossible and offensive to Christian doctrine.</summary>

<keywords>Paracelsian Magic, Oswald Croll, Natural Faith, Kabbalah, IHSVH, sidereal spirit, Panacea of Assylvanus, imagination, blasphemy, Simon Magus</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s refutation of Oswald Croll’s "Second Column" of Kabbalistic magic, which Croll calls "Natural Faith." The author is arguing that the fall of man has stripped humanity of the divine image and any inherent "miraculous" power, making Croll's claims of inborn magical ability both factually wrong and religiously blasphemous.</meta>

# According to Croll’s opinion.

...it is impious, because it suggests that he carries those good things within himself and at least does not know he carries them. For it is certain that most things pertaining to the imagination of God have been lost, and so destroyed that in this life no perfect restoration is granted. Since those magnificent things are said of man simply, where will they be when the greatest part of humanity will dwell in hell? What shall we pronounce regarding the sayings of the Holy Spirit: ***O lying man!*** <note>original: "ô homo mendax"; see Romans 3 and 4</note>

III. From this we further conclude: *If these obstacles—namely sin, the thickness of the body, and the impotence of the senses—make it so that man cannot exercise perfect magical acts and perform all things, then there is no Mage of this worldly life.* That is the point. And thus this follows. It is said: "but by the sanctity of life the mind is expiated and illuminated by the Holy Spirit," as in Hermes. I respond: as far as it concerns magical operations, this is a "begging of the question" <term>petitio principii: a logical fallacy where the conclusion is assumed in the premise</term>, and a lie is committed against the Holy Spirit. For the Holy Spirit does not procure those Kabbalistic <note>original: "Gabalistica"</note> works, nor does He illuminate anyone to produce them. Furthermore, expiation is not for the sake of this [magical] grace, but for eternal salvation, the recovery of the divine image, and reconciliation with God; it happens through the blood of Christ by which we are cleansed, and we are reckoned just through the righteousness of faith received from Christ. Therefore, even reborn and justified men do not perform these magical acts, but rather abhor them. Add to this, that even if expiation were to occur to this extent, it could only be imperfect in this life, since the flesh always struggles against the spirit, etc.

<margin>A special examination of natural faith.</margin>
But let us consider that *natural faith* more specifically. I. It is indeed true that Christ several times commends *miraculous faith*, as in Matthew 17:20: *If you have faith, nothing will be impossible for you;* and chapter 21:21, where however verse 22 adds prayer joined with faith, so that it is clear one must not only believe, but first pray. Mark 9:23: *all things are possible to him who believes.* But Psalm 136:4, Psalm 86:10, and Psalm 72:18 stand in the way, where ***God alone*** is said to do great wonders: and as has been said, if the effect of miracles is to be obtained by prayer, *it is not a concreated gift* <note>A gift "concreated" (concreatum) is one that is built into human nature at the moment of creation, rather than being a special grace given later.</note> granted to all in common. Add that Saint Paul in 1 Corinthians 12:9-10 and verses 29-30 denies that miraculous faith, the grace of healing, and the working of miracles are granted to everyone. Hence follows an argument against the concreated and natural faith of the Mages: <margin>Against the natural faith of the Mages.</margin> *If God alone is the worker of miracles; if the operation of powers is a singular work of the Holy Spirit not granted to all, and that which comes upon one from Grace and not from nature; if it is obtained not by birth but by creation; if God works this gift through His Prophets and Apostles so that the preached word may prove to be from God; if, as Nicodemus says, "no one can do these miraculous signs unless God is with him"; if John the Baptist, otherwise most faithful, and Solomon the wisest, and David, a man after God's heart, and even the Apostolic Prophets could not procure miracles at their own whim: then it is not a common gift of Nature to all men.* The first part is true, and therefore the latter follows. The connection is clear: things common to nature are by themselves perpetual and are present in all men naturally procreated, unless an accident hinders them. If this nature were present, why did Adam and Eve not give proof of it in their integrity of faith? Or shall we only perform wonders in the next life? What should be said of the good angels? They are indeed ministers of miracles, but by themselves and from their own nature they can perform nothing. If they could, the Savior would have used a weak argument from miracles in John 5:36, 10:37, 14:12, and 15:24. <margin>Miraculous faith is not common.</margin> <margin>The Paracelsians wish to strip God of His glory.</margin> Indeed, Christ himself *refers his miracles to the Father*. Let us therefore recognize this Paracelsian spirit and execrate it as one that wants to take glory away from God and confer it upon man. That was the evil spirit who in paradise promised our first parents wisdom equal to God.

II. The definition of natural faith is foolish. For since it is the *power of doing miraculous works*, it cannot be "wisdom," which consists in contemplation and not in practice.

III. That it was implanted in all men at creation is false. For true and unambiguous miracles are a *proper gift of the Church*, and that for determined times. How great is the number of men outside the Church? Yet not even to all those within the Church, even the most pious, is it given to perform wonders. Often God works even through the impious, through angels, and other creatures according to His whim. The Devil is said to have performed miracles outside the Church, but those are not absolute miracles by their essence, but only in a relative sense and as a manner of speaking. Such things the false prophets, false Christs, and the Antichrist procure by the permission of God, along with mages and the devil himself. Paul calls them ***lying signs and wonders*** <note>original Greek: "σημεῖα & τέρατα ψεύδους" (semeia kai terata pseudous)</note>, which Christ indicates were prepared for seduction in Matthew 24. Therefore, Croll's "patrimony" is ridiculous; since he has performed no miracles, just as Paracelsus performed none, he is confounded by his own impotence. Now that two columns have been destroyed, does not the Kabbalah <note>original: "Gabalistica"</note> of Paracelsus collapse? <margin>The ruin of the second column of Paracelsus.</margin>

IV. It is a foolish saying that we are "pacified to the spirits" through natural faith (if miraculous faith is meant). Pure spirits possess the gift of miracles only ministerially, and not as something inborn or concreated, just as the unclean spirits do not. If it were innate to men, where is the equality? Perhaps the comparison is equal in this: that just as holy men, so also the pure spirits receive that gift particularly and from God. The unclean spirits, however, produce lying prodigies through their Mages and Antichrists, as it is written in 2 Thessalonians 2:9. Are not the Mages "pacified" to these? If it pleases them so, let them be equals, with our good leave.

V. It is a most vain fiction that ALL miracles are performed by the addition of imagination through natural faith. For neither the Prophets, nor the Apostles, nor Christ performed miracles by imagining and naturally believing; rather, all of them refer the performance to God the author. Concerning Christ, you have John 10:38 and 14:10: *The Father dwelling in me, He does the works.* Concerning the Apostles, Mark 16:20: *The Lord working with them*, and confirming the word with the signs that followed; likewise Acts 3:12-13, 4:10, and 4:30. Where indeed are these Paracelsian "Imaginators" and "Natural Believers"? Why can they do nothing—or if they can, why not without an evil spirit and through fraud?

VI. Let some of those wonders be recounted: those of Simon Magus <note>A legendary sorcerer mentioned in the Book of Acts who became a fixture of anti-magical polemic.</note>: *to create a new man out of air; to penetrate rocks like clay; to animate statues; to enter fire unharmed; to appear with two faces; to change himself into a sheep, a goat, etc.; to fly; to manifest a great amount of gold*, and like Pythagoras <margin>Magical Miracles.</margin>
->the thigh<- <note>A reference to the legend that Pythagoras had a golden thigh, a sign of his semi-divine status.</note>

<summary>This page presents a theological critique of Oswald Croll’s concept of "Natural Faith," arguing that miracles are exclusive gifts of God’s grace rather than innate human powers. The author dismisses Paracelsian magic as a delusional attempt to steal divine glory, equating its practitioners with biblical false prophets and sorcerers like Simon Magus.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s systematic attack on Oswald Croll’s "Natural Faith." Having previously argued that man’s fallen state prevents any inherent magical power, the author now lists famous examples of magical feats from history and folklore, categorizing them not as "natural" or "divine," but as diabolical deceptions.</meta>

# Concerning the Horrific Impiety of Paracelsian Magic

<margin>
* This is similar to the story in which a mage placed a wooden staff into a water vessel and, having taught it a verse, commanded it to fetch water and bring it into the house. When the master was away, a curious servant did the same; but since he could not recite the words to stop it, he seized an ax and split the staff. Instead of one water-carrier, he then had two, filling almost the entire house with water. <note>This is a version of "The Sorcerer's Apprentice," famously told by the ancient writer Lucian.</note>
Magic dogs.
The illusions of Sicidas.
The magic of Zyto the Bohemian.
The Pope as Mage.
Various transmutations in Apuleius.
</margin>
...a golden thigh: *ordering a scythe to go and reap, and to perform the task alone more abundantly than if it had been directed by reapers; to multiply the same image so that it might look out from all windows at once; to summon the souls of the dead with incantations; to make bread out of stones; to transform oneself into a column and various faces; to cause closed doors to open of their own accord; to have domestic utensils come to one's use voluntarily; to have many shadows leading the way; to flee in a changed form; to have a dog before the gates that devours all who approach uninvited (it is indicated that Mages delight in dogs by the magic dog of Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa; and <unclear>Khunrath</unclear> took care to have a dog painted for himself in his amphitheater) <note>Heinrich Khunrath was a famous alchemist; his "Amphitheater of Eternal Wisdom" contains famous engravings of his laboratory.</note> which dog was even able to speak; to change oneself into gold and a serpent; to exhibit ghosts of every kind at banquets, and so on. Michael Sicidites <note>A 12th-century Byzantine official accused of sorcery.</note> caused a man who was carrying pottery in a ship to think he saw a monstrous serpent lying upon the goods, about to devour him. The man, seizing a club to strike the serpent, smashed all the pottery, and the ghost of the serpent immediately vanished. Zyto the Bohemian <note>A legendary sorcerer at the court of King Wenceslaus IV.</note> appeared now with his own face, now with another’s, and in different heights; at one moment he was visible in purple and silk, at another in filthy rags. He sailed toward the King as the King walked on land; he caused his carriage to be pulled by roosters; at royal banquets, he changed hands into the feet of oxen or horses; he fixed stag horns onto those looking out of windows; he transformed thirty bundles of hay into fat pigs, which, when a baker drove them into the water, he restored into bundles of hay. When the baker sought him for judgment in a tavern, Zyto simulated sleep; when the baker grabbed his foot, Zyto left the entire limb pulled out in the baker's hand, and then used a legal action to force the baker to pay him for the torn-off foot. Laurentius Hildebrandi seemed to prophesy unexpectedly and interpreted the songs of birds; he reported what had happened the day before or the day before that in various corners of the world. Such things are also read in the *Golden Ass* of Apuleius; Turks and vestal virgins make things return by incantations; Diodorus of Catania, by magical power, attracted people located in far distant regions, just as witches do by means of goats; the same man escaped the hands of the law through the air, flying away from Catania to Byzantium. Simon the Samaritan <note>Simon Magus, often considered the first heretic and a master of dark arts.</note> boasted that he could do anything he wished and that all things obeyed him—which is the very voice of Croll as well. Zedechias the Jew used to throw a man into the air and restore him after he had been torn into limbs, just like another who beheaded youths and put their heads back on; and when another had loosened a magic knot, having produced a lily from a table and cut it through, he simultaneously cut off the head of his adversary without any remedy. Some devour horses, wagons, and hay; another devoured a cupbearer and excreted him behind the furnace; and when he had quickly swallowed a vessel full of water, he drenched that poor man entirely, who was finally found half-dead. With such art Zyto the Bohemian treated other Mages. For one is more powerful than another. Another was made to drink from a basket of lime, and when he wished to respond in kind, the magic bond was loosened and he was entirely soaked with wine. Tiridates exhibited magical dinners for Nero. Croll writes of Trithemius, citing Pico, that he turned himself into various forms; others tell different stories. Eon, a heretic and mage, made it seem as if he were dining with royal splendor and was surrounded by a great crowd of men. See the *Canicularia* of Maioli, volume 2, colloquy 3.

<margin>
See also volume 1 of the *Leisure Hours* of Camerarius, chapter 70 and following.
Psalm 77.
The Kabbalists are truly equated with the devil.
Kabbalists are "divine" because Paracelsus said so.
</margin>
In Homer, you have the story of Circe; in Virgil, the story of Moeris drawing the moon down from heaven and transferring sown crops. In the *Dæmonomania* of Bodin there are not a few examples, as also in other books concerning the examinations and trials of witches. The *Book of Azoth* by Paracelsus and Cornelius Agrippa; likewise the *Panoply of Achilles*. The *Book of Adam* handed down by Raziel, and similar books abound in magical promises, featuring deeds clearly identical to those of the ancient diabolical mages. Croll knows how to present all these things with the beautiful color of "natural faith" and imagination, although elsewhere he is very fierce against miserable witches—as is Paracelsus, who meanwhile manufactures "diabolized" mirrors, homunculi, characters, seals, *Gamahéas* <term>Gamahéas: stones engraved with astrological or magical signs</term>, and innumerable "familiars."

VII. Indeed, that a Kabbalist should be equated with diabolical prophets, and for that reason be called "divine," can easily be admitted. For such people are also found in the sacred scriptures. 1 Samuel 28, verse 8: Saul says to the woman with a familiar spirit <note>original: "Pythonissam"</note>: ***Divine for me by the spirit*** <note>original: "Diuina mihi in Pythone"</note>. Such people in verse 9 are called "soothsayers" and "mages." 1 Samuel 6, verse 2: The Philistines consult "diviners." Jeremiah 27, verse 9: diviners, prophets, dreamers, augurs, and sorcerers are held to be the same. Chapter 50, verse 36: it is written of the mages of the Chaldeans: ***a sword is against her diviners, and they shall become fools.*** Micah 3, verse 5: The prophets who lead the people astray, in verse 7, are called "diviners," whose divinations are darkness. Zechariah 10, verse 2: the diviners see a lie, and the dreamers speak vain things. 2 Chronicles 18, verses 21–22: it is expressed by what spirit such prophets are driven—namely, not by that which Croll sweetly imagines for himself, but by a spirit of lying, whom Christ in John 8 calls the devil. Although those prophets did not want to hear that they were being driven by an evil spirit instead of the spirit of the Lord, and therefore Zedekiah struck Micah on the cheek. Moreover, it is ridiculous for these things to be confirmed by the authority of Paracelsus the Mage: as if one devil would strip another devil of his testimony, or one liar another.

VIII. What does it mean, however, for a "Natural" man to be "divine"? Nature as instituted by God is one principle; that from which mages are called "divine"—namely, the evil spirit—is another. Are Paracelsian mages therefore such by nature, and born to this: to be the sewers of devils?

IX. We can concede that the mind of such a mage is united and made equal to his God. For mages prostitute themselves entirely to the devil, who is called "their God," and therefore they carry out the desires of the devil. To the true God, who fills heaven and earth, no creature can be made equal; and if the Paracelsian wants this to be understood of Him, he is a blasphemer. Moreover, it has been said above how those adhering to the true God become "one spirit" with Him through love, the observation of commandments, true faith, perfect holiness, and other means. If they are united to God through adherence, or service, worship, and love, they are not thereby made equal to Him in a magical sense.

X. *The mind of the mage does all things that it wants.* Truly, this is the voice of Simon Magus, whose disciples or descendants are the Paracelsian mages. So too Attus Navius thought he could perform what Tarquin was thinking.

XI. *But he wants what God wants.* Truly, the "magical god," or the devil, who holds the Mages captive to his beck and call, or his will (2 Timothy 2, verse 26). Whoever's servant one is, to that person's will he must obey. If the Mages observed the will of the true God, they would not depart from His commandments, neither to the right nor to the left.

He contends that the "divinity" claimed by Kabbalists is the same as that of the false prophets and mediums condemned in the Bible.</summary>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's theological assault on the "Natural Magic" of Oswald Croll and Heinrich Khunrath. Having previously argued that magic has no basis in the physical or spiritual laws of nature, the author now turns to Scripture and linguistic analysis to prove that the mages' use of the name of Jesus (IHSVH) is a blasphemous corruption rather than a source of divine power.</meta>

# According to Croll’s Opinion

<header>According to Croll’s opinion.</header>
<sig>G</sig>
<meta>catchword: to prepare</meta>

<margin>What the will of God is, you have in Matthew 11:25-26, chapter 18:14, John 6:39-40, 1 Thessalonians 4:3.</margin>
*to the left.* In the entire Holy Scripture, nothing is found regarding Paracelsian magic. If it were of the will of God, it would have been revealed. "I have called you friends," says Christ in John 15, verse 15, "because I have made known to you all things that I heard from my Father." And in Acts 20, verse 27, Paul did not shrink from declaring the whole counsel of God. Furthermore, the Holy Spirit was to lead the Apostles into all truth, as seen in John 16, verse 13. If this magic were of God's will, counsel, or truth, it would by no means have remained silent. That it is not of God is clear both from this argument and because it is instead prohibited and cursed; it is not of the Holy Spirit, but of the most wicked and worthless devil, who suggested this persuasion to our first parents <note>Adam and Eve</note> as if they could be like God—or as the Mage puts it, to be united and equaled to God, and to know and be able to do all things that God does.

<margin>The Mage confounded by himself.</margin>
XII. The claim that the mind of the mage—in a God-like manner <note>original: "Dealiter"</note> above nature, with firm confidence and miraculous faith—can do all things he wishes, is indeed a diabolical lie. This is refuted by experience itself, yet it is also a ridiculous fatuity and a foolish persuasion. If a Mage acts above nature, how is he a "Natural" divine? If he works in a God-like manner, how is it "natural"? Is God natural? If it is above nature, by what reasoning is "miraculous faith" considered a "natural faith"?

<margin>IHSVH</margin>
XIII. They claim the "Door" is the single, most divine name: IHSVH <note>This is a five-letter version of the name of Jesus used by Christian Cabalists, inserting the Hebrew letter 'Shin' into the middle of the four-letter name of God, YHWH.</note>. Surely here, too, the spirit of lies betrays itself. The Jewish mages, as well as others to whom divine things are known, attribute all things to the Tetragrammaton <note>The four-letter name of God, YHWH.</note>. Croll substitutes the unique name IHSVH, and he sins no less against the second commandment than the Mages of the *Schemhamphorasch* <note>original: "Schemhamphoristhæ"; refers to the "Exposed Name" of God in Jewish mysticism, believed to hold great power.</note>. But if this name is the only "Door," how could that Egyptian Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary Hellenistic figure to whom many alchemical and magical texts were attributed.</note> be the one whom Croll proclaims? The name of our Savior was unknown to him, as it was to Zoroaster, the prince of all diabolical magic. If this unique name is the door, to what purpose are the "Fifty Gates of Intelligence" in the scale of Pico della Mirandola, Johann Pistorius, and their like?

<margin>Concerning the corruption of the name of Jesus, or rather the adoption of a certain Magic Idol in place of the true Jesus, to the confusion of mages.</margin>
XIV. Truly, the spelling IHSVH is strange. Croll certainly transferred this from the "Magic Amphitheater" of Khunrath <note>Heinrich Khunrath (1560–1605), a famous alchemist and author of "The Amphitheatre of Eternal Wisdom."</note>, but how is the word to be read? In Latin or in Greek? If in Latin, it will be read as *ihsuh*; if in Greek, <unclear>ιησυη</unclear> <note>iesuē</note>. However, Christ Jesus, our Lord and Savior, was never called by that name. It is a magical corruption done to his insult and ignominy. Moreover, "Jesus" is the true name given to the Lord based on his effect and proper office, because he saves his people and cleanses them from sins (Matthew 1:21). It was never intended for Magic, but to strike the devil as if with the sword of the spirit and thus destroy his magical work—though the devil does not always care for the name, as may be seen in Acts 19:13, 15. In Hebrew, "Jesus" is derived by some from salvation <note>original: "ישע" (yasha)</note>; from which comes Jesus <note>original: "ישוע" (Yeshua)</note>. In the Quran, it is "Isaj" <note>Isa</note> for Jesus. So says Schindler in his Lexicon <note>Valentine Schindler’s "Lexicon Pentaglotton," a famous 16th-century dictionary of five languages.</note>, who thinks the letters were transposed by Muhammad for the sake of insult, just as the Jews write it with the letter *Ayin* <note>original: "ע"</note> removed. But the angel in Matthew derives it from "saving," which is *yasha* <note>original: "ישע"</note>; hence *jescha* and *jeschuah* mean salvation or redemption, etc. And *Moscha* means Savior, *Moschia* means Preserver or Messiah. *Iehosua*, *Iosua*, and *Ieschua* mean Savior or the author of salvation, as the same Schindler says under the root *yasha* <note>original: "ישע"</note>, where there is no letter *He* <note>original: "ה"</note>, just as there is none in the Arabic *jasâ*. From where, then, did it come into the minds of these Hebraizing Mages to write "IHSVH," unless the devil, their God, wished to mock Christ? Or perhaps our Jesus wished to confound them in their own wisdom, as it is written in 1 Corinthians 1:19. Or perhaps it was to guard against the profanation of that most sacred name, so that while they think they have Jesus, they instead have some foreign thing—a name suggested with Satan as the author. For our Jesus is not called IHSVH. There is an obsolete root *jaschah*; from which comes *jesch* <note>original: "יש" (yesh)</note>, meaning a thing or existing; also *Tushiyah* <note>original: "Thuschyah"</note>, meaning essence, wisdom, or virtue. <margin>Jehovah.</margin> It is connected to the root *hajah* <note>original: "היה"</note>, "he was," and *hauah* <note>original: "הוה"</note>, from which comes the essential four-letter name of God, Jehovah <note>original: "יהוה" (YHWH)</note>, who is Being absolutely and the cause of other beings. The Rabbis call this the "Exposed Name" or *Schemhamphorasch*, through which the faithless Jews falsely claim Christ performed miracles—by pronouncing that name over the dead and the sick. By its power, the Mage David El-Roi <note>A 12th-century Jewish pretender to the messiahship who claimed to use magic.</note> is said to have made a ten-day journey in a single day, and with it inscribed on his staff, Moses performed wonders in Egypt. Thus, so that these mages Croll and Khunrath might fashion something similar in the word "Jesus" that could support a belief in equal power, they added a Latin aspiration <note>the letter 'H'</note> translated from the Hebrew. In this regard, they are no better than the impious Jews, by which you may recognize the Paracelsian spirit. Let them also say this: since many "Joshuas" and secular "saviors" have acted as types of Christ, which of them used that name for Magic? They will find none.

<margin>Corruption of the Pauline sentence.</margin>
XV. It is claimed that in that word IHSVH, all things are recapitulated and contained. This is taken from chapter 1, verse 10 of St. Paul to the Ephesians, where he says: "God has made known to us the mystery of his will according to his good pleasure which he purposed in Christ... that he might gather together in one <note>or "recapitulate"</note> all things in Christ, both which are in heaven and which are on earth." The Apostle is speaking of Election and predestination made in Christ, so that through him and in him the Church of the Saints might be gathered, and both men living on earth and angels in heaven might meet with the Holy Trinity in one holy communion, with Christ being the head of the Church. The term "all things" cannot be extended to those things to which that blessed Holiness does not belong, nor is anything included in the statement that cannot fall under Christ as the head of the Church. It is clear, then, how blasphemous that mage is who dares to drag a Gospel sentence into magic itself, as if Paul meant that all things converge in the name of Christ so that Mages can do whatever they wish because all the power of all things is contained in it. Furthermore, even by that reasoning—since according to the judgment of the Cabalists this power resides in names—the power should be ascribed to the name "Christ," not "Jesus" or "IHSVH," since neither of those is placed there in the text. His foolishness and impiety are refuted first because Christ received the name "Jesus" not because magic power resides in the name itself, but because it denotes his office and brings singular consolation to all the faithful. That name is indeed of such majesty that at its mention every knee should bow, in heaven, on earth, and under the earth, for it is above every name (Philippians 2:9-11), nor can it be spoken spiritually without the Holy Spirit (1 Corinthians 12:3). But it does not follow from this that it can be magically abused to produce whatever mages want. As we said above, the devils in Acts 19 could not be expelled by the power of his name <note>referring to the Seven Sons of Sceva who tried to use Jesus' name as a magic spell and failed</note>, nor could Croll, Khunrath, and other mages obtain long life or other things according to their magical desires through its use.

<summary>The author critiques the alchemical use of the name 'IHSVH' by Oswald Croll and Heinrich Khunrath, arguing it is a linguistically incorrect and blasphemous corruption of the name of Jesus used for forbidden magic. He maintains that the name of Christ is for the salvation of the faithful and the glory of God, not a tool for mages to command nature.</summary>
<keywords>Oswald Croll, Heinrich Khunrath, IHSVH, Tetragrammaton, Cabala, Etymology of Jesus, Blasphemy, Paracelsianism, Schemhamphorasch, Ephesian 1:10</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s systematic refutation of Oswald Croll’s "Cabalistic" magic. Having previously attacked the linguistic misuse of the name of Jesus, the author now addresses the Paracelsian claim that a physician or "mage" can perform instantaneous miracles through a specific type of "natural faith" or "imagination."</meta>

# On the Horrible Impiety of Paracelsian Magic

<meta>Catchword: that it is so,</meta>

they were able to prepare. And what should be said of that "sacred silence" of Croll? The name *Jesus* signifies him who is the King of kings, and Lord of Lords, the Savior of the world, and the Creator in whose hand and power all things reside—as Paul speaks in Colossians chapters 1 and 2. But the mere sound of the pronounced voice, the characters, and the letters do not possess that power. The same is true for the sign of the Cross, to which the Mages ascribe no less significance, thinking that by its virtue enemies are defeated—as in the time of Constantine the Great (who for this reason added to his military standard, the *Labarum*, the symbol <note>The Chi-Rho, representing the first two letters of Christ's name in Greek.</note> by which Christ is designated)—and that other wonders are performed. See the annals of Baronius, in which there is much concerning the cross, and also Maiolus, volume 1, *Canicular Colloquies*, colloquy 21, etc. But all who attribute to created things that which belongs to God alone are confounded.

<margin>The sign of the Cross.</margin>
<margin>*</margin>

<margin>The miraculous operation of the Physician.</margin>
XVI. *Now the Physician is introduced.* It is said that by using that miraculous word *IHSVH* <note>The "Pentagrammaton," a Renaissance occult version of the name Jesus created by inserting the Hebrew letter 'Shin' into the four-letter name of God, YHWH.</note> he can magically *execute in a moment all those things which a natural physician—working through the ordinary power of herbs, gradually and naturally, applying his mind, prayers, and faith—effects as if the virtue from the name of Jesus were drawn through these instruments.* This is ridiculous. I argue thus: A physician working naturally through natural means cannot do all things in cases of disease, nor even in health; not to mention that outside of these means, he is not accustomed to undertaking anything memorable through his medicines. Therefore, neither can a Mage do all things in a moment by the virtue of the name *IHSVH*. If Croll could truly do all things by his Magic, what was the point of him describing eight causes of incurable diseases? How superfluous it would be to write a book on Chemical pharmacy!

<margin>Faith as Imagination.</margin>
XVII. Again, *natural faith in diseases is called Imagination.* But I say that one pillar of Cabalistic magic was *natural faith* (which is nothing other than a natural power in the will, by which we believe and assert something as true), while the other was *Imagination*. Now, therefore, there will be only two pillars or foundations, and Magic will go limping.

XVIII. That *faith is said to be "Ingenerate" by God the Father at the moment of creation.* From where, then, did it come to the Mages, who were not present at creation? You will say: they received it from their parents. But I say it is impossible that all parents, grandfathers, and great-grandfathers all the way back to *Adam* were Mages. Are the Paracelsians alone the natural children of Adam? If they are not the only ones, why does such a multitude of men not possess that miraculous natural faith? What Adam did not have, he could not transmit to his descendants. *Adam did not possess miraculous faith.* For the sacred history attributes no such thing to him. Furthermore, *if he had possessed it in his state of integrity, he would have lost it, or at least extremely weakened it, by sinning.* From where, then, does he get such great strength? By the gift of the *Holy Spirit*, you say. Therefore, I say it is neither "ingenerate" <note>Meaning innate or born-within.</note> nor common to all. Moreover, magical works are claimed to be *supernatural*; and indeed they must be, if what the Paracelsians propose is true. Therefore, they cannot be performed through natural faith. For nothing operates beyond its own kind. If it is "concreated" <note>Created alongside the soul.</note>, it would be from the preternatural gifts which man lost; it is reckless to boast of them now. While it is said to be ingenerate from God the Father, did the Son and the Holy Spirit have no power in it? *But through the Son, God established all things:* John 1:3, Colossians 1:16-17. Is God spoken of essentially as a "Father" in respect to Adam as a created son? *Eve* used her faith poorly when she believed the devil more than God. That faith was not of such great power then.

<margin>Mage-healers wish to be believed in.</margin>
XIX. *Firm belief and undoubting hope toward the physician contribute greatly to health.*) Bodin notes in his *Daimonomania* <note>Jean Bodin's 1580 work on demonology and sorcery.</note> that a certain Mage promised health to a certain sick man if he would believe in him. We read of Trithemius that he wished to help someone if that person believed he could do it. What else does Croll demand here? Why does he not also wish to be adored and, like *Simon Magus*, be held as a Great God? The principles of the Christian religion *claim firm trust and undoubting hope for God alone*, not for a healer-Mage. Wisdom 16:12-13, Sirach 38:2, 9, James 5:15, Isaiah 38:3, 2 Chronicles 16:12, 1 Kings 2:6, Tobit 13:2.

<margin>The claim that God can be conquered by natural faith is a lie. That faith is the victor which has Christ as the victor: Genesis 32:28.</margin>
XX. *God cannot be conquered except through faith.* However, he <note>Croll</note> protests that he is not speaking of the faith of grace. Therefore, he must be understood as speaking of *natural faith*. If this were true, there would have been no need for such great work in redeeming the human race, and the Paracelsian mages would occupy the seats closest to God—or perhaps they would even climb into the place of the "conquered" God. Are they not ashamed of these more-than-diabolical blasphemies? 1 John 5:4: *This is the victory that overcomes the world: our faith.* Now, Christ has overcome the world (John 16:33). It is necessary, therefore, that the victorious faith be one of grace. For by that faith we take hold of Christ. He who has the Son has also the Father; he who has Christ has also the Spirit of Christ. In this way we can be friends and children of God, and urge our Father through faithful prayer to do our will according to the promise of his benevolent will, if our will agrees with the will of God: Hebrews 13:21, 1 John 5:14. Excellent are the things which the author of the Epistle to the Hebrews (chapter 11) preaches concerning the virtue of faith. But he says nothing about magical effects achieved through faith, nor about God being "conquered." The power of prayer is also great if it is done out of faith toward God. James 1:5, chapter 5:16-17. But no one could so "conquer" God that He would either approve or assist his Magic. Whether there is any other way of obtaining God's favor than that magical faith—which is attributed to the internal spirit of man, or the "sidereal man" <note>In Paracelsian thought, the *homo sydereus* or "astral man" is the part of the human being influenced by the stars, distinct from the elemental body and the divine soul.</note> (that is, a pure and simple Paracelsian figment)—is easy to judge. That which is nothing but a magical delusion cannot conquer God.

<margin>Things that overcome God.</margin>
If we must speak of God in this way (as is sometimes done), *God was overcome by mercy, clemency, love toward the world, the promises of grace through the Son, and likewise the intercession and merit of the Son.* For what else could overcome the immense God other than He Himself? We become participants in His victory through faith in Christ. This being settled, can we then do all things magically, according to the Paracelsians? On the contrary: nothing. For this grace is not believed into Christ, nor did Christ promise us any such thing. His words about the possibility of miraculous faith signify something far different; such faith is a singular gift of the Holy Spirit and is by no means implanted in nature at the first creation, nor is it common to all men.

XXI. From what has been said, the argument is judged: *If all things are possible for God, they are also possible for the one believing in God.* The first part is true. Therefore, the second is as well. The connection is strengthened because the believer works through God. He who has an omnipotent cooperator or minister can do all things: which perhaps Paul also meant when he wrote that *he could do all things through him who strengthens him*, speaking of Christ: Philippians 4:13. But the response to this connection is that all things are indeed absolutely possible for God which do not overturn His truth or are not adverse to His will: *for that which He pleased to be, does not at the same time not exist, and that which He pleased...*

<summary>The author refutes the Paracelsian idea that "natural faith" allows a magician to perform instantaneous miracles or "conquer" God's will. He argues that true miraculous power comes only through divine grace and the Holy Spirit, not through magical manipulations of the divine name or the "astral" body of man.</summary>
<keywords>Oswald Croll, Paracelsianism, Pentagrammaton, IHSVH, healing miracles, natural faith, imagination, sidereal man, blasphemy, Adam, Constantine</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with an attack on Oswald Croll's use of the name of Jesus as a magical word, arguing that a physician cannot simply perform miracles by pronouncing a name. This page continues the theological critique by examining the limits of human power versus divine omnipotence, specifically refuting the "Cabalistic" claim that a believer or mage can do "all things."</meta>

# According to Croll’s opinion.

*It is so; it cannot be otherwise:* But for a person who believes, that word <term>ALL THINGS</term> <note>original: OMNIA</note> is very restricted. For it encompasses nothing beyond what is granted and promised by God, *to whom it was possible to create the world from nothing, and to call those things which are not as though they were; who likewise has the power of preserving and again destroying the world; who can cast into hell and lead back again, etc.* but this is not possible for man. The proof of this connection ignores the true point of the argument. Who was more closely associated with God than the <term>Protoplasts</term> <note>The "first-formed" humans, Adam and Eve.</note> in their state of integrity? Or after sin was brought in, who was closer than Abraham, Isaac, Jacob, David, Solomon, Daniel, Job, and other friends of God? Could they not do "all things"? Not even an Emperor or a prince allows himself to be so available to his subjects that they can do as much as he can, except by the will of God. <margin>* What man has the God of omnipotence as a familiar friend as Moses did? Yet he could do nothing except insofar as it was permitted to him by God, as is clear from the story of the striker <note>Likely referring to Moses striking the rock in anger, which led to his punishment (Numbers 20).</note>.</margin> *For grace is given to man according to the measure of the spirit;* thus there is a measure even for a son. For this reason, Moses was not able to do everything, nor was he heard in all things for which he prayed, *such as concerning the passage into the Holy Land.* Likewise, Paul did not obtain the removal of the <term>tortor</term> <note>Referring to Paul's "thorn in the flesh" or "messenger of Satan" mentioned in 2 Corinthians 12:7.</note>.

Therefore, no one becomes omnipotent just by having an omnipotent companion or associate, unless they either have that person under their power and can compel them to their whim, or that person agrees and consents to it. Moses wanted to enter the Holy Land, but he suffered a rejection. John, with his brother, wanted to destroy the inhospitable people with fire called down from heaven, but Christ did not permit them this—He who, although he was in the form of God and carried the entire fullness of the Deity dwelling in him bodily, nevertheless cried out on the cross: *My God, why have you forsaken me?* And in the garden: *Not what I will, but what You will.* <margin>Deuteronomy 2:25–26; Luke 9:54–55; Philippians 2:6; Colossians 2:9; Matthew 27:46; Matthew 26:39.</margin> <term>Whom the Lord loves, he chastises, and he scourges every son whom he receives.</term> <note>original: Quem diligit Dominus castigat, & flagellat omnem filium quem recipit.</note> Hebrews 12:6. *While we are judged, we are corrected by the Lord, lest we be condemned with the world.* 1 Corinthians 11:32. But how the Saints would wish that the *Cup of affliction* would not reach them! Consider the leading of the Israelites out of Egypt by Moses the leader, with whom God spoke face to face, who carried those departing as a man is accustomed to carry his little child on every path (Deuteronomy 1:31). Where, then, was this "magical omnipotence"? Why was it necessary for sedition and murmuring to break out so often if the Israelites could have done all things through God as their companion? Croll indeed warns his Cabalistic mage to want nothing except what God wants, so that omnipotence might be limited by the will; but it is clear from the facts themselves that what he says is false. For if he wanted only that which God wants, he would not be a Mage, nor would he seek anything beyond what is permitted in the scriptures. What should be said of our carnal lust and the imperfection of our will? To whom is the entire will of God open?

XXII. <margin>The ridiculous madness of the Mage concerning the method of magical omnipotence. Note the operation of the physician-mage.</margin> He says that one cannot investigate *how a person believing in God performs all things through God.* Clearly, that which is false, because it has no essence, cannot be investigated. Otherwise, that which God works through the Saints is clear through its effects. *Healing virtue went out from Christ into the believers and healed them.* Sometimes words were used; sometimes other ceremonies as well. It is situated in the will of God how He wishes to be effective through His people: *His ways are unsearchable.* And what does it matter to know the "how," if you are certain of the power? Nevertheless, that which the Mage had called "unsearchable," he soon describes so precisely and eloquently that you would think he had sat among the secrets of God for several thousands of years and had looked into and explored the entire Deity within. This is no Nicodemus asking how a man can be born again, or how the wind rises and disappears. *Instead, he has a familiar spirit by which he searches all things, even the depths of God;* unless his opinion deceives him (1 Corinthians 2:10). Yet there, it is the Holy Spirit who is the searcher of all things, but He does not manifest all things to man; rather, man knows only those things which are given and revealed through Christ, which it is proper to speak of not in the words of human magic, but in the doctrine of the Holy Spirit. Yet what is the reality? Explain your method, Mage: First, thoughts or imaginations must be established, and these must be *elevated on high above the Elements and the stars.* Second, it is necessary to have faith in those thoughts, or to believe certainly that what we think will come to pass. Third, that thought or imagination invades a star and brings it under its power. Fourth, the star takes up the work, produces it according to the opinion, and heals whom we want healed. These things are in the physician. Does the sick person do nothing? On the contrary, something: 1. there is a certain spirit of medicine; 2. that spirit brings the knowledge of what and how he ought to heal (for medicines are "knowing" things); 3. from this follows the healing. 4. However, it is necessary to capture, direct, and apply that spirit by faith, which is the work of God. Thus, finally, follows what we want. Not even Archimedes <note>The famous Greek mathematician and engineer.</note> would have calculated so exactly, nor Archytas <note>An ancient Greek philosopher and engineer known for building a mechanical dove.</note>—even though the former could bring the heavens down to earth like Drebbel <note>Cornelis Drebbel (1572–1633), a famous Dutch inventor known for his "perpetual motion" machines and early submarines.</note>, and the latter is said to have been able to count the sands. Truly, that Mage who forges "Achillean armor" out of seven metals also flies into the sky by thinking and imagining; there, he grasps death in its best position—which, however, has not yet been fully investigated even with the instruments of Tycho <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601), the most famous astronomer of the era, known for his precision instruments.</note>. *Once it is spiritually grasped into the metallic mass while it is being formed, he not only directs it most strongly with the intention of the mind, but also instills and breathes it in with words and characters.* From this, it happens that whole armies (perhaps of flies!) flee from one so armed. To this belongs the question from page 44: how God heals by a word through the Cabalists. 1. God lives; 2. the name of God lives; 3. there are living letters of the living name. As God lives in Himself, so He gave to the name to live in itself, and the name to the letters. Hence the power in the written word, characters, and seals. Such names are like little gods <note>original: numina</note> which have powers by reason of a virtue ordained by God or nature for such a name or character, etc. I ask you, what is emptier than this vanity? Are these "wise men" so doting like old women that they can either believe this themselves, or dare to persuade others of it and publish it through the printing press? Let them be sent to the <term>Cynosarges</term> <note>A gymnasium in Athens, here used as a place of dismissal for those with "illegitimate" or foolish ideas.</note>. Thus we have undermined the second column of Magic and reduced it to the dream of a shadow. The third follows.

### SECTION X.

<margin>On imagination, the third column of Magic.</margin>

1. This is Imagination, than which nothing is more wonderful in all of Magic. For it is described by Croll on page 39: *Imagination, strongly exalted, is a great and wonderful power: the proof: because Jacob’s sheep and pregnant women affect their offspring through it.*

2. The Imagination or Phantasy of man is like a Magnet in its nature: The proof: because it attracts the Phantasies of other men. For when one person yawns, another seeing it also yawns.

3. Violent imagination transforms both its own body and sometimes even another’s: The proof: <margin>How those who grate a file make things grate.</margin> because the sound of a file or of iron sets the teeth of the listener on edge and makes them also grate.

4. How does this happen? By a certain power through the mode of imitation. Moreover, the likeness of the thing possesses this power.

<summary>The author continues his refutation of Oswald Croll’s magical theories, arguing that even the holiest biblical figures were limited by God's will and could not perform "all things" at whim. He mocks the complex "scientific" process of magical healing and armor-making, dismissing it as superstitious vanity before moving on to analyze Croll's third "pillar" of magic: the power of imagination.</summary>
