<meta>This page continues the author's systematic refutation of Oswald Croll, a prominent Paracelsian physician. Having previously attacked the use of the name of Jesus as a magical charm, the author now addresses the Paracelsian concept of "natural faith" and the "sidereal man," arguing that these ideas are blasphemous attempts to grant humans divine power through magical means rather than through Christian grace.</meta>

## On the Horrible Impiety of Paracelsian Magic

<margin>The sign of the Cross.</margin>
<margin><image-desc>Chrismon symbol (Chi-Rho)</image-desc></margin>
...they were able to prepare. And what should be said of that "sacred silence" of Croll? The name *Jesus* signifies him who is the King of kings, and Lord of lords, the Savior of the world, and the Creator in whose hand and power are all things; as *Paul* speaks in Colossians chapters 1 and 2. But the sound of the spoken voice, and the characters and letters, do not have that power. Neither does the sign of the Cross, to which the Mages ascribe no less a power, thinking that by its virtue enemies are defeated—as in the time of *Constantine the Great* (who for this reason added to his military standard <note>The Labarum, the imperial standard used by Constantine.</note> the character <image-desc>Chrismon symbol (Chi-Rho)</image-desc> by which Christ is symbolized)—and other wonders are performed. See the annals of Baronius <note>Cesare Baronio (1538–1607), a cardinal and historian known for his multi-volume history of the Church.</note> in which there is much concerning the cross, or Maiolus, volume 1, *Canicular Colloquies*, colloquy 21, etc. But all those who attribute to created things that which belongs to God alone are thrown into confusion.

XVI. Now the *Physician* is taken up. He is said, by means of that miraculous word <term>IHSVH</term> <note>original: "IHSVH". This is the 'Pentagrammaton,' a Cabalistic version of the name of Jesus (YHSHW) used by esotericism to suggest the name's magical power over the physical world.</note>, *to magically execute in a moment all those things which a natural physician effects through the ordinary power of herbs, successively and naturally, by applying mind, prayer, and faith at the same time, as if through these instruments the virtue from the name of Jesus were drawn out.* This is ridiculous. I argue thus: A physician, working naturally through natural means, can neither do all things in cases of disease nor in health—to say nothing of the fact that outside of these, he is not accustomed to undertake anything memorable through his medications. Therefore, neither can a Mage do all things in a moment by the virtue of the name IHSVH. If *Croll* was able to do all things by his magic, what was the point of describing eight causes of incurable diseases? How superfluous it is to write a book on Chemical pharmacology!

<margin>The miraculous operation of the Physician.</margin>

XVII. Again: *natural faith in diseases is aided by Imagination.* But I say that one column of Cabalistic magic was *natural faith* (which is nothing other than a natural power in the will by which we believe and assert something as true), and the other was Imagination. Now, therefore, there will be only two columns, or foundations, and Magic will go limping.

<margin>Faith as Imagination.</margin>

XVIII. That faith *is said to be innate in creation by God the Father.* From where, then, did it reach the Mages, who were not present at creation? You will say: they received it from their parents. But I say it is impossible that all parents, grandfathers, and great-grandfathers back to *Adam* were Mages. Are the Paracelsians the only natural children of Adam? If they are not the only ones, why does such a multitude of men not possess that miraculous natural faith? What Adam did not have, he could not transmit to his descendants. *Adam did not possess miraculous faith.* For sacred history attributes nothing of the sort to him. Therefore. Furthermore, if he had possessed it in his state of integrity, he would have lost it, or at least weakened it extremely by sinning. From where, then, does it get such great strength? You say: *By the gift of the Holy Spirit. Therefore, I say it is neither innate nor common to everyone.* Moreover, magical works are claimed to be *supernatural*; and indeed they must be, if what the Paracelsians propose is true. Therefore, they cannot be performed through natural faith. For nothing operates beyond its own kind. If it is co-created, it would be from the preternatural gifts which, because man has lost them, it is rash to boast of. While it is said to be "innate from God the Father," could the Son and the Holy Spirit do nothing in it? *But through the Son, God founded all things:* John 1:3, Colossians 1:16, 17. Is God spoken of essentially with respect to Adam being created as a son? *Eve* used her faith poorly, believing the devil more than God. That faith was not of such great power then.

XIX. *Firm belief and indubitable hope toward the physician contribute greatly to health.* Bodin notes in his *Demonomania* that a certain Mage promised health to a certain sick person if he would believe in him. We read of Trithemius <note>Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516), a Benedictine abbot and scholar of magic and cryptography.</note> that he wished to help someone if that person believed he could do it. What else does *Croll* demand here? Why does he not also wish to be adored and, like Simon Magus, be held as a Great God? The principles of the Christian religion claim firm *trust and indubitable hope for God*, not for a healer Mage. Wisdom 16:12–13, Sirach 38:2, 9, James 5:15, Isaiah 38:3, 2 Chronicles 16:12, 1 Kings 2:6, Tobit 13:2.

<margin>Mage-healers want people to believe in them.</margin>

XX. *God cannot be overcome except through faith.* However, he [Croll] protests that he is not speaking of the faith of grace. Therefore, he must be understood as speaking of *natural faith*. If this were true, there would have been no need for such great work in redeeming the human race, and the Paracelsian mages would occupy the seats closest to God, or perhaps even ascend into the place of the "conquered" God. Is he not ashamed of these more-than-diabolical blasphemies? 1 John 5:4: *This is the victory that overcomes the world: our faith.* Now *Christ has overcome the world* (John 16:33). It is necessary, therefore, that the victorious faith be one of grace. For by it, we take hold of Christ. He who has the Son has the Father also; he who has Christ has also the Spirit of Christ. In this way, we can be friends and children of God, and urge our Father through the prayer of faith so that, according to the promise of His benevolent will, He might do our will—if it agrees with the will of God: Hebrews 13:21, 1 John 5:14. Excellent things are what the author of the Epistle to the Hebrews (ch. 11) preaches concerning the virtue of faith. But he has nothing to say about magical effects through faith, nor about God being "overcome." The power of prayer is also great if it comes from faith toward God (James 1:5, ch. 5:16–17). But no one could so "overcome" God that He would either approve or assist his magic. Whether there is no other way of obtaining God’s favor than *that magical faith which is attributed to the internal spirit of man, or the sidereal man* <note>The "sidereal man" (homo sydereus) is a Paracelsian term for the astral or "star-born" body that links humans to the heavens.</note>—which is a pure, unadulterated Paracelsian fiction—is easy to judge. That which is nothing but a magical delusion cannot overcome God. If one must speak of God in this way (as is sometimes done), *mercy, clemency, love toward the world, the promises of grace through the Son, and likewise the Son’s intercession and merit "overcame" God.* For what else would overcome the immense God but He Himself? We emerge as participants in His victory through faith in Christ. Once this is reconciled, are we able to do all things magically, according to the Paracelsians? On the contrary: nothing. For one does not believe in Christ for the sake of this grace, nor did Christ promise us any such thing, as His words regarding the possibility of miraculous faith signify something far different. Such faith is a singular gift of the Holy Spirit and is by no means implanted in nature at the first creation for anyone, let alone common to all men.

<margin>It is a lie that God can be overcome by natural faith. That faith is victorious which has Christ as the victor: Genesis 32:28.</margin>
<margin>Things that overcome God.</margin>

XXI. From what has been said, the argument is judged: *If all things are possible for God, they are also possible for the one who believes in God.* The first part is true. And therefore, so is the second. The connection is strengthened because the believer works through God. He who has an omnipotent co-operator or minister as an associate can do all things: which perhaps is also what Paul intended when he wrote *that he could do all things through him by whom he is strengthened*, speaking of Christ: Philippians 4:13. But the response to the connection is that all things are indeed absolutely possible for God which do not overturn His truth or are not contrary to His will: *for that which He pleased to be, does not simultaneously not exist; and that which He pleased to be thus,*

<meta>The text cuts off here with the catchword "sic esse" (to be thus), which will be the first words of the next page.</meta>

<summary>The author argues against Oswald Croll's Paracelsian view of "natural faith" and "miraculous magic," asserting that the power of the name of Jesus and the sign of the cross are not magical tools for humans to "conquer" God. He critiques the concept of the "sidereal man" as a fiction and maintains that true miraculous power comes only through divine grace, not innate human magic inherited from Adam.</summary>
<keywords>Oswald Croll, Paracelsus, natural faith, sidereal man, IHSVH, Chi-Rho, Constantine the Great, imagination, omnipotence, miraculous faith, Johannes Trithemius, Jean Bodin</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of Oswald Croll’s "Basilica Chymica," specifically targeting the claim that a "magical" faith allows a man to possess divine omnipotence. The author argues that even the most holy figures in scripture were limited by God's will and did not possess the inherent power claimed by the Paracelsians.</meta>

->according to Croll's opinion.<-

<sig>G 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: ...tude of things</meta>

it is so, and it cannot be otherwise: But regarding the believing man, that phrase <term>all things</term> <note>Referring to the biblical promise that "all things are possible to him who believes."</note> is very much restricted. For it encompasses nothing beyond what is granted and promised by God, who was able to create the world from nothing, and to call those things which are not as though they were; who likewise has the power of preserving, and again of destroying the world; who can cast into hell, and bring back again, etc. But this power is not for man. The proof of this connection ignores the logical fallacy. What was more accompanied by God than the <term>Protoplasts</term> <note>The "First-formed," meaning Adam and Eve before the Fall.</note> in their integrity, and after sin was brought in, <term>Abraham</term>, <term>Isaac</term>, <term>Jacob</term>, <term>David</term>, <term>Solomon</term>, <term>Daniel</term>, <term>Job</term>, and other friends of God? Yet they could not do all things. Not even an Emperor or a prince grants so much of himself to his subjects that they can do as much as he himself. <margin>* No man has the omnipotent God so familiar to himself as Moses did, and yet he could do nothing beyond the will of God. For grace is given to man according to the measure of the spirit: hence the measure is given to the son. Therefore Moses was not heard in all things which he asked, such as his passage into the holy land. Likewise Paul did not obtain the removal of the thorn, despite the power of the Almighty.</margin>

Wherefore, by an omnipotent companion or associate, no one becomes omnipotent unless he holds that being as either a supporter or a consenter under his own power, and can compel him to his own nods. Moses wished to enter the holy land, but he received a refusal. <term>John</term> with his brother <note>James the Great; see Luke 9:54.</note> wished to destroy the inhospitable with fire called down from heaven, but Christ did not permit this to them; though He was in the form of God, and bore the whole fullness of the Deity dwelling in Himself bodily, yet He cried out on the cross: <margin>Deuteronomy 2:25, 26. Luke 9:54, 55. Philippians 2:6. Colossians 2:9. Matthew 27:46. Matthew 26:39.</margin> *My God, why have you forsaken me?* And in the garden: *Not what I will, but what You will.* The Lord disciplines the one He loves, and scourges every son whom He receives. Hebrews 12:6. *When we are judged, we are disciplined by the Lord, so that we may not be condemned with the world.* 1 Corinthians 11:32. But how the <term>Saints</term> would wish that the <term>Cup of affliction</term> would not reach them! Consider the leading of the Israelites out of Egypt by <term>Moses</term> the leader, with whom God spoke face to face, who carried those departing as a man is accustomed to carry his little child in every way: Deuteronomy 1:31. Where then was the magical omnipotence? What need was there so often to stir up sedition and murmurs if the Israelites could have done all things through God as their companion? Croll indeed moves his <term>Cabalistic</term> mage to want nothing except what God wants, so that omnipotence may be limited by will: but it is clear from the reality of the matter that what he says is false. For if he wanted only that which God wants, he would not be a Mage, nor would he seek anything beyond what is permitted in the scriptures. What must be said of our carnal lust, and the imperfection of the will? To whom is the whole will of God open?

XXII. It cannot be investigated (scrutinized) *how the one believing in God works all things through God.* Naturally, because it is false and has no essence, it cannot be investigated. Otherwise, what God works through the Saints is clear through the effect. A *healing virtue* went out from Christ into the believers, and healed them. Sometimes words were applied; sometimes other ceremonies. It is situated in the will of God how He wishes to be effective through His own: *His ways are past finding out.* And what does it matter to know the method, if you are certain of the power? Nevertheless, that which the Mage had called "past finding out," he soon describes so precisely and eloquently that you would think he had *sat among the secrets of God for several thousands of years*, and had looked into and explored the entire Deity within. It is not <term>Nicodemus</term> asking how a man can be born again, or where the wind arises and disappears. *But he has a familiar spirit, by which he may search all things, even the deep things of God;* unless his opinion deceives him: 1 Corinthians 2:10. Where, however, the Spirit is indeed the searcher of all things, but all things are not manifest to man, since he knows only those things which are given and revealed through Christ; which things it is also fitting to speak, not in the words of human magic, but in the teaching of the Holy Spirit. Yet what is the matter? Explain your method, O Mage: <margin>The ridiculous madness of the Mage concerning the method of magical omnipotence. Note the operation of the physician-mage.</margin>
1. Thoughts or imaginations are to be influenced, and these must be elevated on high above the Elements and stars: 
2. It is necessary to have faith in those thoughts, or to believe certainly that which we think will happen. 
3. That thought or imagination invades the star, and brings it into its power: 
4. The star captures the work, brings it forth according to the opinion, and heals whom we wish to be healed. These things are in the physician. Does the sick person do nothing? On the contrary, something: 
1. There is some spirit of medicine: 
2. That spirit brings the knowledge of what and how it ought to heal. For medicines are "knowing" things. 
3. Then follows the healing. 
4. But it is necessary to capture, direct, and apply that spirit by the faith through which the work of God is done. So finally follows what we wish. Not even <term>Archimedes</term> <note>The famous Greek mathematician and inventor.</note> could have calculated so exactly, nor <term>Archytas</term>, though the former might have brought the heavens down to the lands, like <term>Drebbel</term> <note>Cornelis Drebbel (1572–1633), a Dutch engineer famous for building a submarine and other "miraculous" inventions.</note>; the latter is said to have been able to count the sands. Surely that Mage who forged the armor of <term>Achilles</term> from seven metals, by thinking and imagining, also flies into heaven, and there comprehends death in its best position—which nonetheless has not yet been entirely investigated even with <term>Tychonic</term> instruments <note>Instruments used by the astronomer Tycho Brahe for precise celestial measurement.</note>: *being spiritually grasped into a metallic mass, while it is being formed, he not only directs it most strongly by the intention of the mind, but also instills and breathes into it by words and characters.* Hence it happens that whole armies (perhaps of flies) flee from one so armed. To this belongs the question from page 44, how God heals by a word through the Cabalists. 1. God lives, 2. the name of God lives: 3. the letters of the living name are living. As God lives in Himself, so He also gave the name to live in itself, and the name to the letters. Hence the force in the written word, the characters, and the seals. Such names are divinities which have powers by reason of a virtue ordained by God or nature to such a name, or character, etc. What, I ask, is vainer than this vanity? Do these "wise men" rave so much like old women that they can either believe this themselves, or persuade others, and even dare to publish it through the printing press? Let them be sent to the <term>Cynosarges</term> <note>A gymnasium in Athens for those of "impure" birth; the author uses it here as a place for outcasts or fools.</note>. Thus we have undermined the second column of Magic, and reduced it to the dream of a shadow. The third follows,

### SECTION X.

<margin>On Imagination, the third column of Magic</margin>
This is Imagination, than which nothing is more wonderful in all Magic. For it is thus described by Croll on page 39: *Imagination, if strongly exalted, is a great and wondrous force: the proof: because the sheep of Jacob, and pregnant women, affect their offspring through it.* <note>Referring to the biblical story where Jacob used peeled branches to influence the color of sheep's wool (Genesis 30), a common historical example of "maternal impression."</note>

2. The imagination or Phantasy of man is by its nature like a Magnet: the proof: because it attracts the Phantasies of other men: *For when one person yawns, another seeing it also yawns.*

3. Vehement imagination transforms even one's own body, and sometimes even another's: the proof: because the screech of a file or iron sets the teeth of the listener on edge with numbness, and makes them also screech. <margin>How the screeching file makes the teeth screech: a multitude of things</margin>

4. How does this happen? By a certain virtue by way of imitation. Moreover, it has this similar virtue—

# On the Horrible Impiety of Paracelsian Magic

[simili]tude of a thing for transmuting a thing, which a vehement imagination moves: that is, he who hears the screeching of iron rubbed against iron, imagines a similar screeching for himself, and by the force of this imagination, he grinds his teeth <note>original: "dentes collidit"—literally "clashes the teeth," describing the physical reaction to a cringe-inducing sound.</note>. This is a transmutative likeness. The same man says this craftily.

5. The *force of imagination* is proven in another way. For in sadness and lack of confidence, some have summoned devils by their imagination, or drawn them to tempt or rule them.

<margin>How it is brought about that we suddenly conceive of God and prophesy, just as Saul was among the Prophets: 1 Samuel 19:9, 23 (or rather chapter 18, verse 10). See others on page 38.</margin>
6. Several people have brought it about through imagination that they were suddenly made into a temple of God (perhaps like the Enthusiasts and Ecstatics who feign being snatched up into paradise). How does this happen? 1. *Suspicion of impotence must be removed*: 2. *Imagination must be intensified*: 3. *Constant and firm faith in God must be held*: 4. *The mind must be raised to the highest level*. 5. *Infallible hope must be conceived*. 6. *Praying must be assiduous and ardent*. 7. *God must be worshiped religiously, as the more secret Theosophists know*. 8. *There are separated intelligences* <note>Non-corporeal beings such as angels or spirits.</note>. A mind burning with religious love is joined to these through the intention of the one invoking them with burning and devoted fear and trembling. Indeed, internal prayer, arising from a pious mind, continued with excessive affection and burning desire, unites the mind with God, and *learns and knows all things from God*. 9. For this, there is need for *supernatural influence*, etc. But more on imagination. For in this is the head and the tail, and the whole hinge of Magic.

<margin>In imagination is almost the whole of Magic, if you add a familiar spirit.</margin>
7. A man's Imagination depends entirely on the stars of the firmament, page 38; indeed, it is the same and remains with the stars. Therefore, the firmament also has an Imagination, though without reason, whereas man's imagination is with reason.

8. Imagination in man works *like the sun*. It is called incorporeal thought, which, just as the collected rays of the sun are intensified so that they turn into a flame and burn when matter is given, so it too works upon the subject spirit as if it were a visible instrument. 9. The imagination of man is a Magnet attracting over a thousand miles, and in its exaltation, it attracts from the Elements whatever it wishes.

10. How does this happen? Imagination, through its attractive power, must attract the conceived thing, so that it procreates from itself a *native spirit*, the architect of imagination. Afterward, the imagination, as if impregnated, leaves an impression which is not tangible, yet is corporeal like the wind. (I believe the sense is: that the imagination in man must give birth from itself to a certain spirit, which is also called imagination and has an attractive power. This spirit is corporeal, but subtle like a breeze. It is sent out to that thing which we imagine. It takes up the astral spirit of this thing and brings it back with it to the internal imagination of the one imagining. This is the impregnation by which the Idea of the imagined thing is conceived, etc. Or because an irrational Imagination is attributed to the firmament of the stars, perhaps it should be thought that the rational Imagination of man, reflected upon itself, generates an attractive force which, joined to the firmamental imagination, takes a star from there and carries it to the man, just as the *spirit of a magnet seizes iron as if with a hand and moves it to the magnet*.)

<margin>Pretext of the little men <term>homunculi</term>, seals, <term>Gemmahaae</term> <note>Paracelsian term for stones or talismans marked with natural signs or sigils.</note>, etc., of the Magicians.</margin>
11. A true magus, through imaginative operation, can draw the power of the stars into images, stones, and gems, so that they exercise the same power as the stars. By what argument? The burning mirror. For through it, the rays of the sun are drawn to us with heat. 12. Whatever we see with our eyes in the greater world <term>macrocosm</term>, this same thing imagination can also *produce, such as all vegetables and metals*.

<margin>What are the stars? The knowledge of all things.</margin>
13. Page 36. *Imagination encloses all stars within itself*, is all stars, and retains the same course, nature, and power as the heavens. It produces not one but many operations. *But the stars are the virtues of the angels who are the created wisdom of God*: page 46. The incarnate Word of God found in creatures by Paracelsus. He who has known the stars has known God; he who has known God also knows the stars; and he who knows the stars also knows the macrocosm; he who knows this, knows the microcosm <note>The "small world," referring to the human being as a reflection of the universe.</note>.

14. The names of Imagination are various, such as the *Sidereal body of man, the Genius born from the firmament, the Penates <note>In Roman religion, household gods; here used for indwelling spirits.</note>, the visible shadow of the body, the Lar <note>A guardian spirit.</note>, the Shadow-man, the familiar little man <term>homunculus</term> of the Sages, the good Genius, Adech <note>A Paracelsian term for the inner creative person.</note>, Spectrum, the Light of Nature, the presaging Euestrum <note>An astral or prophetic spirit.</note>*, etc.

15. Although imagination is incorporeal and invisible, yet when exalted and joined to the firmest natural faith (like the gate of miracles), it is the source of all Magical operations, having the power of giving birth to visible bodies and bringing forth any operations whatever, present or absent, beyond the grasp of human reason. It is explained on page 37: just as clouds and rain soon appear in a clear sky, and as God produced all things out of divine Nothingness from invisible darkness.

16. Imagination causes the plague and similar astral diseases; it induces diseases and health while the sidereal and animal spirit of man imagines.

17. The imagination *of the five spirits enclosed in the Elements* is also in man, but it can do nothing because those spirits are bound by the use of reason so that they rest with their imagination: page 34.

<margin>Plague from the emotion in epidemic conditions. Page 83.</margin>
18. Page 83, concerning the seventh cause of incurable things: our Imagination is the natural faith healing and exciting diseases. By this argument: because it excites the plague. How? A terrified man, out of his own imagination, generates the Basilisk <note>A mythical serpent whose gaze or breath was lethal; here a metaphor for a self-generated poison.</note> of his own heaven, which poisons that heaven or microcosmic firmament out of his superstition, just as faith assists. See there concerning the origin of the general plague from the <term>Iliaster</term> <note>Primeval life-force or matter.</note> or the <term>Euestrum</term> of the sun moved to anger by the sins of men, which is said to infect by participating uniquely with the <term>Euestrum</term> of man through the influence of the stars; hence the supernatural plague which *no medicine can resist*. 19. To whatever extent the sidereal spirit dreams and Imagines, it simultaneously lifts and conveys the body itself, by the argument of the Sleepwalkers <term>Noctambulones</term>.

<margin>Page 84.</margin>
20. Faith gives imagination, Imagination gives the star (it is called the marriage of the star and Imagination, which is also called thought), the star gives the work. *Through the faith of imagination, a sick person often does that which the best physician cannot*: By the argument: *The Panacea of Amwald <note>Referring to Georg Amwald, a doctor who claimed to have a universal remedy.</note>, and of the Meissen spring*. For the soul, having shaken off fear or a sinister imagination, is carried into a great excess of its desire, and overcomes nature itself when kindled by a vehement imagination, and *excites health and diseases* both in its own and in others' bodies: as in a pregnant woman marking the child <note>The old belief that a mother's sights or thoughts could physically mark her unborn baby.</note>, and those bitten by mad dogs <note>original: "lissodictis"—those suffering from rabies/hydrophobia.</note> imagining dogs in the water.

<margin>The formation of the child.</margin>
21. A pregnant mother forms the child with her imagination like a potter forms clay. This Imagination arises from fear and desire. With it, the microcosmic stars are moved, ascend, descend, and make an impression, just as a pestle stamps shapes. Thus the child is marked.

It argues that imagination can cause physical changes, healing, and even plagues by manipulating astral spirits and the "native spirit" within man.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of Oswald Croll’s theories on the "Force of Imagination." The previous page ended with a description of how internal prayer and intense mental focus are said to connect the mind with spiritual intelligences. This page concludes the summary of Croll's points before launching into a systematic refutation.</meta>

->according to Croll's opinion.<-
G 3
<meta>catchword: so</meta>

22. When the mind is powerfully exalted through imagining, a *constant credulity* <note>original: "constans credulitas"—a persistent state of belief or expectation.</note> (a natural faith such as that possessed by the woman with the hemorrhage and the Centurion) <margin>Working miracles.</margin> *works miracles* in opinions and intense operations. Imagination, when joined with faith, is capable of all things (page 85). These are essentially the things that Croll imagines to himself concerning the Imagination; of what spirit such things are must now be judged.

### S E C T I O N IX.

<margin>Judgment on the Crollian imagination.</margin> It is clear to any theologian or philosopher that this magical discourse on Imagination is wonderfully confused and so inconsistent that it is unworthy of the time spent in judging it. The arguments he uses partly beg the question, partly proceed from specific cases to general rules, and partly fall into the fallacy of the accident. The authority he relies upon is that of impious men—*Paracelsus, Cornelius Agrippa*, and similar dreamers. That infatuated *Croll* did not even consider that *Agrippa* eventually condemned his own magical writings and warned everyone not to allow themselves to be ensnared by the traps of that diabolical profession. For those things which those magicians hold as the highest and most hidden wisdom are nothing but *satanic delusions*, an injury to God himself, and all the more insulting and blasphemous because they confidently drag divine names, pronouncements, and matters into their Kabbalistic magic. If you survey all of nature, Imagination (or Phantasy) is either concerned with the senses or with reason; in the latter case, it is also called "thought," "deliberation," or "meditation." <margin>The scope of the Imagination.</margin> But our magician has one right hand and another left: one that is brute and devoid of reason in the "greater world" <note>The "Macrocosm" or the universe at large.</note> (not by the reasoning of beasts, but of the firmament and the Platonic world, which is asserted to be a living animal); a second joined with reason which exists in the firmament of man and his "sidereal spirit" <note>A Paracelsian term for a subtle body that connects the physical man to the stars.</note>; a third belonging to beasts; a fourth <margin>Irrational thought. The demons of Paracelsus.</margin> belonging to "genii" or demons, one type of which that most insane *Paracelsus* saw on the earth, another in the waters, another in the air, another in the heavens, and another wandering commonly, which he calls "plagues"—a society of many thousands of devils. He also posits a fifth under the name of "thought," which seems to be the same as phantasy and estimation, or at least not very different, since our Magician does not distinguish between them. But let us review each point so that we may thoroughly understand this Magic, and let us not be moved by the magnitude <margin>Hyperbolic praises of Paracelsus are most impudent lies. See pages 76, 77, 68, 69, 67, 56. Also in the preceding sections.</margin> of the praises which *Paracelsus* bestows upon this most corrupt magician, as they easily prove the impudence of his lying by their excessive immoderation.

**To the 1st point:** The refutation is that he argues from the specific to the general and from the accidental. The force of Imagination is great, but not as great as the Magicians proclaim. *Pregnant women and sheep* indeed experience the force of imagination, but it is neither magical nor supernatural. Furthermore, pregnant women do not imprint a mark upon the fetus by imagination alone, but often by a violent emotion, and sometimes by a fixed thought combined with emotion. As for the sheep, it is ridiculous to attribute magic to the Patriarch Jacob <note>A reference to Genesis 30, where Jacob used peeled rods to influence the breeding of Laban's sheep; the author argues this was natural or divine, not "magical" in the Paracelsian sense.</note>.

**To the 2nd point:** The comparison of phantasy to a magnet is false. For Imagination does not draw other things to itself as a magnet draws iron. Even if we grant the comparison: just as a magnet draws only one specific thing by its nature—namely iron—and consequently things attached to iron; so too would imagination only draw a single thing. Whence, then, comes this "miraculous power over all things"? Furthermore, *Imagination is a certain passive power*, since in sentient beings it does not exist without the sense, which occurs by being acted upon. If the imagination effects anything, it does so by accident—specifically through the spirit, heat, or emotion. Phantasy is excited by a pleasing appearance and is moved; thus, it is defined by motion. Then, the spirit and heat are struck, and moisture is joined to these in the emotions. These, once impelled, impel in turn. But what is said by that lazy argument—that it is "attractive"—is a foolish <margin>Lazy ones.</margin> logical error of ignoring the issue. For this does not happen mostly or always, and therefore not by its own nature. Furthermore, no "attraction" of the imagination occurs; rather, a sensible object excites a similar appetite, just as when one person urinating or drinking causes another person similarly disposed to urinate or drink. Thus *examples provoke*, as when one person eating voraciously causes others similarly affected to eat. There is no "attraction" here in the proper sense, but rather induction, invitation, and irritation through a sensible thing, which, being received by the external senses, moves the internal ones.

**To the 3rd point:** It is false that imagination transmutes either one's own body or another's. Change (not transmutation) happens in one's own body because of the connected appetite, emotion, and their instruments. But as for another's body—who could transmute it when they have no active force over it? Surely when *Apuleius* was changed into an *Ass* <note>A reference to "The Golden Ass" by Lucius Apuleius, a famous Roman novel of transformation.</note> by the force of a magical ointment (as the magicians claim), that did not happen simply by imagining it. The argument regarding mothers misses the point. For that force does not belong to the imagination of its own accord, but by accident; and fetuses attached to the womb are related to the mother as fruit to the trees on which they grow. *For they are one thing in actuality, though they are distinct in potential.* Mothers can affect the fetuses attached and united to them, but not those that are separated. Teeth chatter at the melody of a singer, and they are affected by the screeching of a file when it grinds iron, not <margin>Chattering of teeth.</margin> because of the imagination—as if our imagination "attracted" the screech. This assertion is ridiculous; rather, that is an affection of the nerves communicated to the hearing by the nerves of the teeth, and because there is a common passage from the windows of hearing and the palate, the suffering is also communicated. Conversely, the deaf cannot imagine the sound of a lute, since no appearance of it moves their hearing and thus their phantasy. If, however, they grip the neck of the lute with their teeth, a communication occurs not only of motion, but of sound. When someone was observing people talking and laughing about joking and ridiculous matters, he also laughed from a distance. When asked if he understood what had been said, he said no. "Why then do you laugh?" He replied, "I saw and heard you laughing, <margin>Laughing with those who laugh.</margin> and thinking there was something ridiculous, whatever it was, I laughed along." You foolish Magician, tell me what "attraction of imagination" happened here? Did that man "attract" the phantasies of the laughers, for whom the cause of laughter was not a motion of phantasy? And where is the "file" and the "iron" of the phantasy which he might attract in the phantasy of a man? Pain and unpleasant feelings do sometimes happen to some because of the weakness of the nerves and a certain disposition—though not always, nor do the blacksmiths filing the iron suffer such things—but it is by no means an "attraction": it is not a pursuit, but a flight. Truly, since this happens quite rarely, and scarcely once among many thousands, is it sufficient to establish a Universal magical power of phantasy? The Paracelsians are of such asinine stupidity that from these trivialities they dare to build massive and Universal theories.

<summary>This page refutes Oswald Croll's claims about the magical power of the imagination, arguing that physical sensations and psychological reactions like empathy or cringe are natural neurological responses rather than evidence of a "magnetic" magical force.</summary>
<keywords>Oswald Croll, Paracelsus, Cornelius Agrippa, imagination, phantasy, magic, refutation, magnetism, nerves, sympathy, pregnancy, Jacob's sheep, Apuleius</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the systematic refutation of Oswald Croll’s theories on the "Force of Imagination." Having previously summarized Croll's claims that imagination can work miracles or connect the human mind to the divine, the author now dismantles these points using scriptural evidence and logical arguments, specifically targeting the idea that one can "attract" the Holy Spirit through mental focus.</meta>

# On the Horrible Impiety of Paracelsian Magic

In this way, a response is simultaneously given to point IV. For it is false that a person, upon hearing the clashing of iron, imagines a similar motion within themselves and consequently suffers in a similar way. Why would this occur only in that specific instance and not in all things? If those fanatics had experienced this even once with proper attention, they would have recognized a far different cause, which you might also attribute to "antipathy" <note>An ancient medical and philosophical concept of natural "dislikes" or "repulsions" between certain substances or forces.</note>. Does anyone truly imitate that which is contrary to their own natural disposition?

Regarding point V: It is false that the devil is summoned simply by imagining him. Since this mental motion happens internally, it is unknown to the devil. Other signs are required. *Commonly they say the Melancholic head is the devil’s bath* <note>original Latin: "Vulgo dicunt caput Melancholicum esse diaboli balneum." This refers to the four humors theory, where "black bile" or melancholy was thought to make a person susceptible to demonic influence.</note>, and that the sad and afflicted are tempted by that spirit with God’s permission. This provides an opportunity for the devil, not an attraction. What impudence it is to prattle so recklessly about these metaphysical matters! Our adversary the devil, as Peter says, prowls about like a roaring lion, seeking someone to devour. He observes not only sadness, but also overconfidence, drunkenness, impious songs, conversations, and similar things. Judas the traitor opened himself up to greed; he did not "attract" the devil through imagination. St. Paul handed the unrepentant over to Satan. Christ permitted the demons to enter the pigs. Who would say that heretics summon the devil through imagination, when they think they are striving against him in everything they do? By what imagination did Eve attract him? You, impious and shameless Magus, foolishly open the doors to the devil through your senses, words, writings, seals, and other diabolical instruments.

<margin>Suddenly a temple of God.</margin>
Regarding point VI: Imagination does not cause someone to suddenly become a temple of God. And why are you, Magus, a temple of an unclean spirit, if one can become a temple of God simply by imagining it? Your lies, blasphemies, corruptions of God's word, and other abominations testify that you are not a servant of God, but a slave of the devil. Consider the examples: In Judges 14:6, the Spirit of the Lord rushed upon Samson (an impulse <note>original Greek: "κίνημα" (kinēma)</note> of heroic strength, indeed, with force). So too in chapter 15:14. Yet the Lord departed from him in chapter 16:20. If he had received the Spirit through imagination, could he not have recovered it through imagination? In 1 Samuel 10:6, it is predicted that the Spirit of the Lord would fall upon Saul, and it happened in verse 10. Likewise in chapter 11:6. Yet he loses it in chapter 13 through disobedience, and it is said to have departed in chapter 16:14, being directed instead toward David, who was thinking of no such thing. Saul, however, was invaded by an *evil spirit* even against his thoughts, opinion, and phantasy. If the power of imagination were so great, why did he not recover? On the day of Pentecost, by what imagination were the Apostles suddenly filled with the Holy Spirit? Christ himself explains the cause in Acts 1:4-5: the Father had promised it. In Acts 2:38, Peter says: *Repent and be baptized every one of you in the name of Jesus Christ for the forgiveness of sins, and you will receive the Holy Spirit.* In Acts 4:31, after prayer, the Church is filled with the Holy Spirit. In Acts 8:17, those baptized receive the Holy Spirit when the Apostles lay hands on them and pray. Simon Magus, or the followers of Croll, Paracelsus, Agrippa, Trithemius, Pico, Pistorius, and the like, should have suddenly become temples of the Holy Spirit by imagining it. But Simon—full of the devil—thought he could obtain not the Holy Spirit, but the favor of bestowing it through money. In Acts 10:44, Peter’s listeners are showered with the Holy Spirit even before they are baptized. Yet it is likely they knew very little of the Holy Spirit then, just like those twelve men in Acts 19:2. In Acts 11:15-16, Peter cites the promise of God, not an "attractive" or "magnetic" imagination; and indeed, it is called nothing other than a gift <note>original Greek: "χάρισμα" (charisma)</note> of God, which Luke 11:13 says is obtained from God the Father through prayer.

Show me, Magus, a single example of someone who obtained that gift through imagination. You, however, wish to cloak your "Enthusiasms" <note>In this period, "Enthusiasm" referred to the dangerous belief that one was directly inspired by God or spirits, bypasssing scripture or the church.</note> and diabolical raptures—which you Paracelsian Magi share with hags and witches—and refer them to the Holy Spirit, even though they are entirely the work of the devil. This is blasphemy against the Holy Spirit; if you die in this state without repentance, woe to you. It is well known from common calamity that heretics in the memory of our fathers used the same pretext—led by a certain Münster and later Carlstadt, John of Leiden, and other Anabaptists. The method you propose is the fiction of a foolish man, and furthermore, it is impossible. Why did neither Croll, nor Paracelsus, nor any other magus obtain the Holy Spirit through those nine points? Whoever wishes to obtain the gift of God must pray in the name of Christ according to the will of God. The will of God is clear in the scriptures. Tell me, Magus, in which scripture did you read those nine conditions? Who among all those who obtained the Holy Spirit ever observed them? You invented them barely yesterday. Some of them are not to be despised according to the literal word, but in their intended sense, they are all impious.

If you ask what this "suspicion of impotence" is that you are ordered to remove, it is a kind of presumption of one's own dignity and capacity, contrary to Paul’s Gospel in 1 Corinthians 4:7 and 2 Corinthians 3:4-6, where the Apostle ascribes gifts not to imagination, capacity, or his own glory, but to God the giver, through Christ. The *second condition* regarding the "discovery of imagination" is the ridiculous persuasion of Hermes <note>Refers to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary father of Hermeticism and alchemy.</note>, who thinks a man can be made equal to God through thinking and imagining. The *third* regarding faith is a fiction about the "natural faith" of a made-up, abstract man. The *fourth* regarding the highest elevation of the mind is an "Enthusiastic" dream. The *fifth* regarding infallible hope is contrary to the word of God. For divine hope, by which faithful men hope for future good things, is not that "magical" hope of conceiving God—the kind of hope you might attribute to Pythonesses <note>The priestesses of the Oracle of Apollo at Delphi, here associated with demonic possession.</note> or Apollonian Sibyls, who felt demonic inspirations and were filled by their "god" or evil spirit. Our hope is in the living God regarding eternal and invisible goods (Romans 8:24), not regarding the magical operations of this miserable life. The *sixth* regarding prayer is good in words, but abominable in reality. For we must not pray to be filled with a "magical spirit," but that other good things might come to us according to the promise and will of God. God never willed or promised Paracelsian Magic. The *seventh* requires a certain unique magical worship; but the worship in which God delights is clear enough in the Canonical scriptures. Through that worship, no one has ever obtained the "Pythonic" spirit belonging to Croll and Paracelsus. The *eighth* betrays magical wickedness. It is a diabolical persuasion about the "conjunction of the mind with separate intelligences"—which, if they are anything in the magic of Paracelsus, are nothing but deceiving and unclean spirits. "Magical union with God" is both ridiculous and foolish. Upon what man, I ask, does this "excessive affection, intention, and burning desire" fall for the purpose of "attracting" God? And what does he mean by the "conjunction of the petitioner with intelligences"? Are these separate intelligences to be invoked with fear and trembling? This is exactly what the devil wants, who even demanded adoration from Christ. Good angels reject worshipers (Revelation 19:10 and 22:8-9). Should God be invoked so that He might join us to demons?

<margin>Acts 2:4.</margin>
<margin>The cloaking of Diabolical Enthusiasm.</margin>
<margin>Examination of the conditions for obtaining the Holy Spirit.</margin>
<margin>Conjunction with separate intelligences.</margin>

He uses numerous biblical examples—from Samson and Saul to the Apostles at Pentecost—to prove that the Holy Spirit is a gift from God's promise, not a result of mental focus or magical technique.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the specific refutation of Oswald Croll’s numbered arguments regarding the power of imagination. The previous page ended with a discussion on whether the human mind interacts with demonic or celestial spirits; this page opens by concluding that such an idea is impious because a person should find everything they need by being united with God.</meta>

<header>According to Croll’s opinion.</header>
<sig>G 4</sig>

...with separate intelligences? <note>This sentence fragment completes a thought from the previous page regarding whether human thought connects to angelic or celestial beings.</note> But this is impious, and furthermore unnecessary, if we are united to God, in whom we have all things.

<margin>The imagination of the firmament.</margin>
To the VII. The claim that *the firmament possesses a brute imagination* <note>original Latin: "imaginationem brutam." In this context, "brute" means irrational or instinctive, like an animal, rather than intellectual.</note> is a foul lie. Therefore, our natural imagination (that is, the faculty of thought within the intellect) cannot depend upon it. It is also absurd for a nobler thing to depend on an ignoble one, or to be identical to it. If our thought were the same as the brute stars of the firmament, why would our thought itself not be brute? We are indeed familiar with what Aristotle writes concerning the "active intellect," <note>The "active intellect" (intellectus agens) is a concept in Aristotelian philosophy describing the force that allows the human mind to process abstract concepts.</note> but these ideas are not accepted by the greater Paracelsians. It is worthy of more laughter than even Democritus <note>Democritus was known as the "Laughing Philosopher" because he mocked human follies.</note> could muster to say that the heaven is all-knowing, and that all knowledge is drawn from it into us by a "starry spirit"—yet to claim the heaven itself possesses only a "brute thought," which they call "magical imagination." If this is how things stand, it is no wonder that mages are as brute as cattle. For they can draw no better imagination from their own heaven.

To the VIII. A ridiculous and stupid comparison. *The rays of the sun, when gathered through an instrument* <note>Such as a magnifying glass or burning mirror.</note>, *set flammable matter on fire* if they are directed toward it. Does *our thought also act upon external things?* Read what Christ argues in Matthew 6, verse 25 and following, concerning even the most intense worry. Can you, by worrying, add a single cubit to your stature? The Mages ought to have used their imaginations to warm the winter of the year 1613 and the year following, or to bury themselves in the midst of the snows. Imagine, I pray you, that the high price of grain might vanish; imagine a great abundance of corn or gold; in hunger, imagine fullness; in thirst, imagine drink. We shall see what you are capable of. There is no need for foolish and empty words. The belly has no ears. <note>original Latin: "Venter caret auribus." A common proverb meaning that a hungry person cannot be satisfied or persuaded by mere talk.</note> There is a need for the actual thing. We do not obtain these things by imagining them, but by praying and laboring, as is just.

To the IX. The claim that *the imagination of man is a magnet from the furthest heaven* (for imagine that it is distant by more than 10,000,000 miles) which attracts things, must be proven, not merely affirmed with vain words. If there is any *force of the stars*, it flows into us; it is not "attracted" by our imagining. If those who were freezing, or burning with fever, or sick, could do this, what great feats would they not attempt? As for the distance of the lands, surely this is how Adrian's Mage "attracted" things when he reported deeds happening in the East while he himself was in the West; or that possessed man mentioned by Camerarius; <note>Joachim Camerarius (1500–1574), a German scholar who wrote on portents and spirits.</note> or the Ecstatics, who even describe the state of hell and purgatory, such as Tundalus, the Emperor Louis, and others. The logic of "attracting" a plague is something else entirely. Thus, it is a childish madness to suggest that thought or imagination can attract whatever it wants from the Elements (heaven, air, water, earth). For first of all, "willing" belongs neither to thought nor to imagination, but to the *mind and reason*. Furthermore, a Paracelsian mage might perhaps attract demons from there—who delight in such company and have no need for attraction since they leap in of their own accord—but when will they finally manage to attract clear skies from the heaven and air, or fertility, and other good things? This is the same pretext used by the *impure magic of witches*, <note>original Latin: "strigum." This refers to folkloric witches or hags often associated with night-flying and malevolent magic.</note> who think they can stir up storms, even though those storms arise from another cause. Truly, those women are said to use certain incantations and tools, not imagination. There is no difference between them and the Paracelsian Mages, except that the latter use a more subtle pretext. In 1 Kings 12:17-18, Samuel brought forth a storm not by imagining it, but by invoking the Lord, who then gave the rains. Elijah did the same in 1 Kings 18:24, 36, 42. By the same power, he also brought down fire from heaven in 2 Kings 1:10, 12—which James and John later could not do by "imagining" it.

<margin>What the difference is between witches and Paracelsian Mages.</margin>

To the X. This is a foolish method and lacks any foundation. Therefore, there is no need to waste words against it.

To the XI. We also find this assertion lacking any proof. All Paracelsians are unworthy of life if they can do what they say and yet fail to provide good things. For they are found to be not lovers, but haters of their neighbor and of common Christianity, if they are able to draw the virtue of the warming sun into their images so as to warm the summer for the ripening of apples, grapes, and other crops, and yet they do not do so. If, however, they judge themselves worthy of life but cannot do good like a clear sun, let them acknowledge the weakness of their art and their lies. Truly, that Prophetic saying applies to them: *They are wise to do evil* (through the devil, whom they set up as God, and through their own inventions with which they try to corrupt the truth), *but to do good they know not.* Jeremiah 4, verse 22. They attribute everything to the stars. Therefore, they ought to draw all good things from them. But why did *Croll*, the patron of Magic, list eight types of incurable diseases if he is so powerful? Why does he propose *chemical secrets*? <note>original Latin: "Chymica arcana." This refers to alchemical recipes and hidden formulas for medicine.</note> Why does he desire true <term>potable gold</term> <note>original: "aurum potabile." A legendary alchemical medicine consisting of gold dissolved in a liquid, believed to grant longevity or cure any disease.</note> and the <term>universal stone</term>? <note>The Philosopher's Stone, supposed to turn base metals into gold and heal all ailments.</note> Is he then not a true mage? For a "true" mage is said to be able to do these things. If he is not a true mage, he is a false one, and a wicked impostor who wishes to persuade men of falsehoods.

<margin>Croll was not a true Mage. Therefore he established false precepts of magic.</margin>

The Paracelsians want doctrine to be sought from the *Light of nature and grace joined together*. Very well. But we have already found that human imagination is excited either by external objects or internal ones. I call "external" those things which come from the external senses, whether from near or far—even if the suffering is within us, as when the intestines, stomach, heart, or lungs ache, or when some emotion or desire stimulates them, as happens when we are thirsty or hungry. I call "internal" those instances when an image is received from the "common sense" or memory; or when we are moved by some hidden divine power, or through good or evil spirits; or when something recurs from the storehouse of reason and takes shape. Imagination is produced in those who are sleeping, waking, or in a middle state, and even in those undergoing an Ecstasy, whether physical or metaphysical. Now the Mages say: are all these objects true? It is very well known that men are agitated by countless false phantoms, especially those who are emotional, suspicious, and weak—and most particularly the melancholic, the frantic, the foolish, the drunk, the enamored, the envious, and so on. Plato argued, and after him the New Academics, that there is no certainty to be found in the external senses. Even if that is an overly bold assertion, it is not entirely false, especially in those I have mentioned, and those who are led by the snares of the devil to his will. To these you may add heretics, sophists, and any others who allow themselves to be driven by false opinions against the truth—a countless crowd, and within it a large portion of superstitious people, mages, witches, Paracelsians, and similar <note>original: "sterquiliniorum." Literally "manure-piles" or "refuse," a very strong insult used to describe the lowest dregs of society.</note> dregs of the human race. I add what Paul says: that this life possesses only a partial and imperfect knowledge, and that the devil masquerades as an angel of light and is powerful in the sons of distrust and darkness—such as these mages, who think the thickest darkness is the purest light.

<margin>The futility of Magic arises from the falsehood and uncertainty of magical imaginations.</margin>

If therefore...

He characterizes these claims as fraudulent, comparing the "magical imagination" to the delusions of the mentally ill or the deceptions of witches.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a systematic theological rebuttal of Oswald Croll’s "nine conditions" for achieving magical power through the imagination. The author argues that these conditions are not scriptural but are instead diabolical fabrications. This page specifically refutes points four through eight of Croll's system, contrasting them with the biblical account of how the Holy Spirit is received.</meta>

# On the Horrible Impiety of Paracelsian Magic

Indeed, this also serves as a response to the fourth point. For it is false that a person, hearing the screeching of iron, imagines a similar motion within themselves and thereby suffers in the same way. Why would this happen only in that instance and not in everything? If these "phantom-chasers" <note>original: "phantastæ"</note> had experienced this even once with careful attention, they would have recognized a far different cause, which you could also attribute to antipathy. Or does anyone imitate what is contrary to their own feelings?

Regarding the fifth point: It is false that the devil is summoned through imagination. Since this movement happens within, it is unknown to the devil; other signs are needed. *It is commonly said that a Melancholic head is the devil’s bath*, and that the sorrowful and afflicted are tempted by that spirit with God’s permission. There is an opportunity there, not an attraction. What impudence is it to prattle so recklessly about these metaphysical matters? *Our adversary the devil*, says Peter, *goes about like a roaring lion, seeking whom he may devour*. He observes not only sorrows, but also carelessness, drunkenness, impious chants, conversations, and the like. Judas the traitor exposed himself through greed; he did not attract the devil by imagining him. *St. Paul* handed the unrepentant over to Satan. *Christ* permitted him to enter the swine. Who would say that Heretics summon the devil by imagination, when they think they are striving against all that he stands for? By what imagination did Eve attract him? You, impious and shameless Magician, stupidly open the doors to the devil with your senses, words, writings, seals, and other diabolical instruments.

<margin>Suddenly a temple of God.</margin>
Regarding the sixth point: Imagination does not cause someone to suddenly become a temple of God. And why are you, Magician, a temple of an unclean spirit, if you can become a temple of God simply by imagining? Your lies, blasphemies, corruptions of God's word, and other abominations testify that you are a slave not of God, but of the devil. Consider the examples: In Judges 14:6, *the Spirit of the Lord* (a <term>gift of heroic strength</term> <note>original Latin/Greek: "Spiritus Domini (χάρισμα heroicæ fortitudinis)"</note> acting with force) *rushed upon Samson*. So too in chapter 15:14. The Lord departed from him in chapter 16:20. If he had received the Spirit by imagining, could he not have recovered it by imagining? In 1 Samuel 10:6, it is predicted that *the Spirit of the Lord would rush upon Saul*, and it happened in verse 10. So too in chapter 11:6. He lost it in chapter 13 through disobedience, for it is said to have departed in chapter 16:14; it was instead directed toward David, who was thinking of no such thing. *Moreover, an evil spirit invaded Saul* even against his thoughts, opinion, and fantasy. If the power of imagination were so great, why did he not recover it?

<margin>Acts 2:4.</margin>
On the day of Pentecost, by what imagination were the *Apostles suddenly filled with the Holy Spirit*? Christ himself explains the cause in Acts 1:4–5: for the Father had promised it. In Acts 2:38, Peter says: *Repent and be baptized every one of you in the name of Jesus Christ for the remission of sins, and you shall receive the Holy Spirit*. In Acts 4:31, after the church prayed, it was filled with the Holy Spirit. In Acts 8:17, the baptized receive the Holy Spirit when the Apostles lay hands on them and pray. <term>Simon Magus</term> <note>The biblical sorcerer who tried to buy the power of the Holy Spirit (Acts 8).</note>—the most learned prince of the followers of <term>Croll</term> <note>Oswald Croll (c. 1563–1609), a key figure in Paracelsian medicine and alchemy.</note>, <term>Paracelsus</term> <note>The Swiss-German physician and occultist whose theories are being attacked here.</note>, <term>Agrippa</term> <note>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, author of "Three Books of Occult Philosophy."</note>, <term>Trithemius</term> <note>Johannes Trithemius, a monk and scholar of cryptography and magic.</note>, <term>Pico</term> <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, a Renaissance philosopher of the Kabbalah and Hermeticism.</note>, <term>Pistorius</term> <note>Johannes Pistorius, a physician and Kabbalist.</note>, and others—ought to have suddenly become a temple of the Holy Spirit by imagining. But he, being full of the devil, thought he could obtain not the Holy Spirit, but the grace of the office, through money. In Acts 10:44, Peter’s listeners are showered with the Holy Spirit even before being baptized. Yet it is likely they knew very little of the Holy Spirit then, as did those twelve men in Acts 19:2. In Acts 11:15–16, Peter cites the promise of God, not an attractive or "magnetic" imagination; and truly it is called no other than a gift <note>original Greek: "χάρισμα"</note> of God, which Luke 11:13 says is obtained from God the Father through prayers. Produce, Magician, a single example of someone who obtained that gift by imagining.

<margin>The Cloaking of Diabolical Enthusiasm.</margin>
But you wish to cloak your <term>Enthusiasms</term> <note>In the 17th century, "Enthusiasm" was a derogatory term for claiming direct, private revelation from God outside of scripture.</note> and diabolical raptures—which the Paracelsian Magicians have in common with hags and witches—in this way; and, though the whole business is the work of the devil, you wish to attribute it to the Holy Spirit. This is blasphemy against the Holy Spirit; if you die in it without repentance, woe to you. It is well known from common calamity that heretics in the memory of our fathers used the same coloring: a certain <term>Münster</term> <note>Thomas Müntzer, a leader of the German Peasants' War.</note>, and later <term>Karlstadt</term> <note>Andreas Karlstadt, a radical reformer.</note>, <term>John of Leiden</term> <note>Anabaptist leader of the Münster Rebellion.</note>, and other Anabaptists. The method proposed is the fiction of a stupid man, and moreover, it is impossible. Why did neither Croll, nor Paracelsus, nor any other magician obtain the Holy Spirit through those nine points? Whoever wishes to obtain the gift of God must pray in the name of Christ according to the will of God. The will of God is clear in the scriptures. Tell me, Magician, in which scripture did you read those nine conditions? Who among all those who have obtained the Holy Spirit has observed them?

<margin>Examination of the conditions for the birth of the Holy Spirit.</margin>
You have only just fabricated them yesterday. There are some among them not to be despised according to the letter, but in their sense they are all impious. If you ask what this "suspicion of impotence" is that is commanded to be removed, it will be a species of presumption regarding one’s own dignity and capacity, contrary to Paul’s Gospel in 1 Corinthians 4:7 and 2 Corinthians 3:4–6, where the Apostle ascribes gifts not to imagination, capacity, or his own glory, but to God the giver—and that through Christ, through whom he asserts he has such confidence. The *second condition* regarding the "invention of imagination" is that ridiculous persuasion of <term>Hermes</term> <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary source of Hermetic philosophy.</note>, by which he thinks a man can be made equal to God by thinking and imagining. The *third, concerning faith*, is a fiction regarding the natural faith of the imaginary "astral man." The *fourth, concerning the highest elevation of the mind*, is an "Enthusiastic" dream. The *fifth, concerning infallible hope*, is contrary to the Word of God. For divine hope, by which faithful men hope for future good things, is not that magical hope of conceiving God, such as you might attribute to the <term>Pythonesses</term> <note>The Oracle of Delphi, associated with the "Pythonic spirit."</note> or the Apollonian Sibyls, who felt demonic inspirations and were filled by their god, or rather an evil spirit. *Our hope is in the living God regarding eternal* and invisible goods (Romans 8:24), not regarding the magical operations of this miserable life. The *sixth, concerning prayer*, is good in its words but abominable in intent; for we must not pray to be filled with a "magical spirit," but that other blessings may come to us according to the promise and will of God. God never willed or promised Paracelsian Magic. The *seventh* requires a certain unique "magical worship"; but what worship God delights in is clear enough in the canonical writings. Through that worship, no one ever obtained the "Pythonic spirit" which belongs to Croll and Paracelsus.

<margin>Union with separate intelligences.</margin>
The *eighth point* betrays magical wickedness. For it is a diabolical persuasion concerning the union of the mind with "separate intelligences"—which, if they are anything in the magic of Paracelsus other than deceptive and unclean spirits, then a magical union with God is both ridiculous and foolish. In what man, I ask, does this excessive affection, intention, burning desire, and the rest fall for the purpose of "attracting" God? What does this *union of the one who invokes with intelligences* mean? Are those separate intelligences to be invoked with fear and trembling? Indeed, the devil wishes it to be so, who even demanded worship of himself from Christ. Good angels rebuff worshippers (Revelation 19:10 and 22:8–9). Or is God to be invoked so that He may join us to demons...

<summary>This page contains a fierce polemical attack on the Paracelsian doctrine of imagination, arguing that the Holy Spirit is a gift from God's promise rather than a result of magical mental techniques. The author systematically critiques Oswald Croll's occult requirements, linking them to heresy and demonic possession rather than true Christian faith.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, Oswald Croll, Holy Spirit, imagination, magic, Simon Magus, Enthusiasm, Anabaptism, Pythonic spirit, Hermes Trismegistus, demonic possession</keywords>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the systematic refutation of Oswald Croll’s magical philosophy, specifically addressing his claims regarding the power of imagination and the influence of the stars. The previous page ended with a critique of how the devil interacts with human thought; this page moves into points seven through eleven of Croll's system.</meta>

->according to the opinion of Croll.<-
G 4
<meta>catchword: If therefore</meta>

...with separated intelligences or spirits? But this is impious, and furthermore unnecessary, if we are united to God, in whom we possess all things.

<margin>The imagination of the firmament.</margin>
Regarding Point VII: *To claim the <term>firmament</term> <note>The sphere of the stars or heavens.</note> possesses a "brute" or animal-like imagination is a foul lie.* Therefore, our natural imagination (that is, the faculty of thought in the intellect) cannot depend upon it. It is also absurd for a nobler thing to depend on something ignoble, and to be identical with it. If our thought were the same as the brute stars of the firmament, why would our thought not also be brute? We are indeed familiar with what Aristotle writes concerning the *active intellect*, but these ideas are not accepted by the more extreme Paracelsians. It is more worthy of laughter than even Democritus <note>The "laughing philosopher" of antiquity.</note> could provide to say that the heaven is all-knowing, and that all knowledge is drawn from it into us by a "starry spirit"—yet to claim that the heaven itself possesses only a "brute" thought, which they call "magical imagination." It is no wonder, if this is the case, that such magicians are as brute as cattle. For they can draw no better imagination from their own heaven.

Regarding Point VIII: *A ridiculous and stupid comparison. The rays of the sun, gathered through a lens, set fire to flammable material if directed toward it. Does our thought likewise act upon external objects?* Read what Christ argues in Matthew 6, verse 25 and following, regarding even the most intense anxiety. Are you able to pull to yourself a greater stature by it? These Mages ought to have warmed the winter of the year 1613 and the following year with their imaginations, or buried themselves in the midst of the snows. Imagine, I pray you, Paracelsian Mage, that the high price of food might vanish: Imagine a great abundance of grain or gold: In hunger, imagine fullness; in thirst, imagine drink. We shall see what you are capable of. There is no need for foolish and empty words. *The belly has no ears* <note>original: "Venter caret auribus"; a proverb meaning hunger cannot be satisfied by talk.</note>. There is a need for the actual thing. We do not obtain these things by imagining them, but by praying and working, as is just.

Regarding Point IX: *That the imagination of man is a magnet from the ultimate heaven.* (For suppose it were distant by more than 10,000,000 miles) attracting things—this must be proven, not merely affirmed with vain words. If there is any *power of the stars*, it flows into us; it is not "attracted" by imagining. If those who are freezing, or burning with fever, or sick, could do this, what great feats would they not attempt? As for the distances of the earth, surely this is how Hadrian’s Mage "attracted" things when he reported deeds in the East while being himself in the West; or that possessed man mentioned by Camerarius; or the <term>Ecstatics</term> <note>Those in a state of trance or out-of-body experience.</note>, who even describe the state of hell and purgatory, such as Tundalus, the Emperor Louis, and others. The logic of "attracting" a plague is far different. Thus, it is a childish madness to believe that thought or imagination can attract whatever it wants from the Elements (heaven, air, water, earth). For first, the act of "willing" belongs neither to thought nor imagination, but to the *mind and reason*. Furthermore, the Paracelsian mage might perhaps attract demons from there, who delight in such company and have no need of attraction since they leap in of their own accord; but as for when they will finally manage to attract clear skies from the heaven and air, or fertility and other good things—when will that be? This is the pretext of the impure magic of hags <note>original: "strigum"</note>, who believe they can stir up storms, when these actually arise from another cause. Truly, they are said to use I know not what incantations and tools, not imagination. There is no difference between them and the Paracelsian Mages, except that the latter use a more subtle pretext. In 1 Kings 12, verses 17 and 18, Samuel brought forth a storm not by imagining it, but by invoking the Lord, who gave the rains. Elijah did the same in 1 Kings 18:24, 36, 42. By the same power, he also produced fire from heaven in 2 Kings 1:10, 12—something which James and John later could not do by imagining it.

<margin>The difference between witches and Paracelsian Mages.</margin>

Regarding Point X: This is a foolish method and lacks any logical principle. Therefore, there is no need for words against it.

Regarding Point XI: We also find a lack of any principle of demonstration in this assertion. All Paracelsians are unworthy of life if they can do what they say and yet do not perform good works. For they are found to be not lovers, but haters of their neighbor and of common Christianity, if they are able to draw the power of the warming sun into their images—so as to warm the summer for the ripening of apples, grapes, and other crops—and yet they do not do it. If, however, they judge themselves worthy of life but cannot do good like the clear sun, let them acknowledge the weakness of their art and their lies. Truly, that Prophetic saying applies to them: *They are wise to do evil* (through the devil, whom they set up as God, and their own inventions, with which they try to corrupt the truth), *but to do good they have no knowledge.* Jeremiah 4, verse 22. They attribute everything to the stars. They ought, therefore, to draw all good things from them. But why did *Croll*, the patron of Magic, list eight types of incurable diseases if he is so powerful? Why does he propose <term>Chymical secrets</term>? Why does he long for *true potable gold* <note>original: "aurum potabile"; a legendary alchemical medicine.</note> and the *universal stone* <note>The Philosopher's Stone.</note>? Is he then not a true mage? For a "true" one is said to be able to do these things. If he is not a true mage, he is a false one, and a wicked impostor who wishes to persuade men of falsehoods.

<margin>Croll was not a true Mage. Therefore, he established false precepts of magic.</margin>

<margin>The futility of magic arises from the falsehood and uncertainty of magical imaginations.</margin>
The Paracelsians want their doctrine to be sought from the *Light of nature and grace joined together*. Very well. But we have already found that human imagination is excited either by external objects or internal ones. I call "external" those which come from the external senses, sometimes in a direct way, sometimes from a great distance, even if the suffering is within us—as when the intestines, stomach, heart, or lungs ache; or when some affection or lust goads us, as happens when we thirst or hunger. I call "internal" when an image is received from the common sense or memory; or when we are moved by some hidden divine power, or by good or evil spirits; or when something recurs from the storehouse of reason and takes shape. Imagination is produced in those who are sleeping, waking, or in a middle state, and even in those suffering a physical or metaphysical ecstasy. Now the Mages say: are all these objects true? It is very well known that men are agitated by countless false phantoms, especially those who are emotional, suspicious, and weak—and particularly the melancholic, the frenetic, the foolish, the drunken, the amorous, and the envious. Plato argued, and after him the New Academics, that there is no certainty to be found in the external senses. Although that is an overly bold assertion, it is not entirely false, especially in those whom I have mentioned, and those who are led by the snares of the devil to his will. To these, you may add heretics, sophists, and any others who allow themselves to be driven by false opinions against the truth—a countless crowd, in which is found no small portion of the superstitious, mages, witches, Paracelsians, and similar dunghills of the human race. I add what Paul says: that this life has only a partial and imperfect knowledge, and that the devil simulates an angel of light, and is powerful in the children of distrust and darkness—such as are the mages, who think the thickest darkness is the purest light.

<summary>The author continues his refutation of Oswald Croll, arguing that the Paracelsian concept of "magical imagination" is both scientifically absurd and spiritually dangerous. He mocks the inability of these "mages" to produce tangible results like food or warmth while accusing them of being deluded by demons or mental illness.</summary>
