<meta>This page continues the author's point-by-point refutation of Oswald Croll’s "basilica chymica" and the Paracelsian worldview. Having previously argued that the stars do not possess a "brute imagination," the author now attacks the reliability of human imagination when it claims to reach divine or magical heights without being grounded in scripture or reason.</meta>

# On the Horrible Impiety of Paracelsian Magic

If, therefore, among the objects by which the faculty of imagination is excited, so many thousands are false, or ambiguous and entangled—which even their own composition and confusion make obscure—then there is a very small number of those that can withstand the judgment and examination of truth. These are surely not what the Magicians bring forth from their own insane speculations, but what necessarily follow from the immovable principles of divine truth, which common sense and public experience demonstrate to be either perpetual or occurring for the most part. These truths are contained within authentic laws and the indemonstrable principles of the sciences, or the conclusions deduced from them. From all that has been said, it follows that *the Paracelsian Magician, through his imagination, obtains either nothing certain at all, or at least very little, and that which is confused and doubtful.* For a profession that errs in most things makes even the remaining things—if any truth were present—uncertain and doubtful. Just as dreams, which for the most part deceive us with images, do not easily allow us to confidently decide upon true dreams unless their source is truly divine and angelic. Why else are there so many complaints in the sacred scriptures about impostor spirits and prophets who, while it seemed to them they were <term>united with God</term> <note>original: "Dei vniti" — a mystical state claimed by Paracelsians where the human mind merges with the divine.</note> to predict true things, were actually deceiving both themselves and others? In 1 Timothy 4:1, it says: *in the last days some will depart from the faith, giving heed to deceiving spirits* <note>original: "spiritibus impostoribus"</note> (such as those of the Paracelsian Magicians, Heretics, the Antichrist, etc.). In Job 25:4: *He who is born of a woman (in the natural way) is not pure.* But without purity, no one can be united to God. How then can impure magicians be united through their foolish fantasies? The same is said in Job 15:13–16 and 4:18: *In his servants and even his angels, folly has been found,* and yet Paracelsian Magicians dare to attribute so much to their own insane imagination? Among the Israelites, many called themselves Prophets, and those who were truly Prophets were both despised and persecuted by them.

The Paracelsian Magicians are of this sort, through whom the Devil performs the same play. See Jeremiah 6:13, 8:8–9, and 23:9, 11, 15, 16: *Do not listen to the words of the Prophets* (the Magicians, Croll, Paracelsus, Agrippa, Hermes, Pistorius, Pico, etc., who prophesy to you and deceive you). *They speak a vision (imagination) of their own heart, not from the mouth of the Lord. For who has stood in the counsel of the Lord? etc. I did not send these Prophets—the Paracelsian Magicians who seize wisdom and power for themselves—and yet they ran; I did not speak to them, and yet they prophesied.* If the Paracelsians would think on the corruption of nature through sin, and what Paul writes of himself—to whom no magician can be compared—in Romans 7:10 and following, they would cast away their most foolish persuasions and hold the best parts of themselves as dung, as in Philippians 3:7–10. But let us turn to the remaining points concerning imagination.

<margin>The pretext of magical deceptions.</margin>
Regarding Point XII: It is a most vain pronouncement that *our imagination can produce all things that we see with our eyes in the greater world.* I do not think that Croll, or any other of such desperate insanity, believes that the very essences of things can be procreated by imagining them; however, their other meanings are equally dull. If the words are to be taken to mean that the imagination can generate within itself the images of all those things which are conceived by the eyes in visible form, the statement would be tolerable in some way; but even this does not withstand the balance of refined truth.

For our imagination does not produce the forms of things. Rather, the sensible things outside which move the senses are transmitted through an image to the internal sensitive faculties, and move them so that they are occupied with those things. Things that are absent from sight are not "made" in the imagination, but they excite that which is already made and existing in potentiality. You will say: "But I can imagine similar things, in the manner of Poets and tellers of fables, and those who describe <term>Utopia</term> <note>A reference to Thomas More’s fictional island; used here to mean an imaginary or non-existent place.</note> or <term>Monomia</term> <note>Likely a reference to an imaginary "single-rule" state or philosophical construct.</note>," or like Paracelsus, who falsely attributes mechanical craftsmen and disciples to the heavens who manufacture men like statues, and many other things he devises similarly. I believe, I say, that you can devise even those things which are nowhere and which you have seen nowhere. But if you imagine things similar to existing things perceived by the sense—as when, having seen one deer or one picture, you then make infinite species from it—you are not using imagination alone, but reason, which deduces like from like and compares things to one another. Truly, this is not magical. For it smacks of <term>Aristotle</term> <note>The author uses Aristotle here to represent traditional, rational philosophy as opposed to the "occult" methods of Paracelsus.</note>.

<margin>1 Kings 28:13 regarding the appearance of Samuel.</margin>
There is, therefore, another meaning: that a Magician, through his imagination—not exceeding the scope of his own brain—can display to outside spectators ruins, vineyards, gardens, gilded halls, beautiful maidens, queens long since dead, and absolutely everything, so that the spectators believe they are seeing true things when they discern nothing but deceptions. Simon Magus, Trithemius, Michael Scot, the Bohemian Magician, and others were industrious in that art. Witches are similarly maddened, so that when they have dealings with demons, they think they are loving handsome youths. At their Sabbaths, they eat toads, cats, and tasteless carrion, and other absurdities, which nevertheless seem to be the most sumptuous preparations of food. This is the "production" of the magical fantasy. Would you rejoice in such things if you were alive, Croll? Why does your fantasy not also produce the objects and species of the other senses? I believe your Devil could. But those things that strike the eyes are more evident. Therefore, so that you might paint your impure deceptions with some color, it pleased you to set down that magical principle. Albertus Magnus, at the time of the winter solstice in the palace at Cologne, displayed to certain princes a Paradise full of green trees, flowering herbs, singing birds, etc. See Lerchheimer, chapter 8, on magic. See there concerning Trithemius and other such "producers," as well as Agrippa, the disciple of Trithemius, who wished to persuade men that what he did happened naturally.

Regarding Point XIII: *The imagination encloses within itself all the stars, and is all the stars.* That we may understand the Paracelsian Magicians to be foolish triflers, who mix the highest with the lowest and have no regard for what they say or how they say it, this stupid maxim is proposed. For how can something enclose everything within itself and simultaneously *be* everything? The thing enclosing is one thing, and that which is enclosed is another. Sometimes something is said to be "all things" by composition; thus the imagination would be a confusion of good and evil. Sometimes by connection: like "Being." Thus it would be the highest indefinite genus. Sometimes by the power of perception, as the "passive intellect" is capable of becoming all things. Thus the "cogitative" faculty is able to think about all things; the "imaginative" to imagine all things. But if it were so, it would be an infinite thing and of no science, which the magician will not concede. If you say it is trifling vanity, you have hit the nail on the head. So too on page 30 he trifles: *Adam implanted within himself the whole world:*

<meta>This page continues the author's relentless critique of Oswald Croll’s *Basilica Chymica*. Having already dismissed the idea that stars possess their own imagination, the author now turns to Croll’s definitions of "stars" and "the internal man," arguing that these mystical concepts are logically inconsistent and ultimately blasphemous by equating human imagination and chemical processes with the Word of God.</meta>

### According to Croll’s Opinion

...world: *And: Man is all things made by God: All men are one man.* Invisible man is all the stars and one and the same exactly with the spirit of the world, he says on page 16. *The invisible body of the stars is the spirit of the world diffused into all the stars, or it is all the stars.* From these trifles it follows that anything can be in anything, and that all things are one. If the imagination of man is all the stars, and the internal heaven is likewise all the stars, then imagination and the internal heaven must be the same thing. And if, furthermore, man is also all the stars, then the imagination of man, and the <term>Olympic man</term> <note>A Paracelsian term for the "astral" or "sidereal" body of man, which was believed to be in contact with the celestial realms.</note>—or spirit—and the internal heaven will be the same; and since that internal man is a certain mortal body, as Croll believes, then the Stars, the Imagination, and the internal heaven will be invisible mortal bodies.

If you seek a starting principle for these trifles, you will find none. But what are those things called stars? <margin>The Crollian Stars.</margin> *He denies they are the "coals of heaven" (for thus the witty man calls the visible Planets).* On page 34, he says *spirits and stars in man are one.* Therefore, what we heard previously regarding the spirit of man, we must accept as being said about the stars. For that <term>sidereal spirit</term> <note>The "starry" part of the human soul in Paracelsian thought.</note> is the true astral man. Therefore, the stars will be the true astral man, and accordingly, if you ask what imagination is, what the internal heaven is, or what man is, the answer will be: it is the true astral man.

Again, on page 36: *The stars are the invisible body of all things.* From this, it follows that the imagination of man is the invisible body of all things. If, likewise, man is everything that God created, then Imagination will also be everything. From this they weakly conclude: Stars are the incorporeal species of all things, which are represented through imagining.

That which we imagine is an incorporeal spirit. This first passes into a likeness of a body, or a specter, and afterward it "embodies" further. Thus, whatever one thinks upon more fixedly becomes visible from the invisible without the need for deceptive magic. This is the height of fatuity. Page 25: *Stars are the secrets which the Chymist reduces from the ultimate matter into the first.* This must be a different meaning, unless you wish for imagination to be the <term>quintessence</term> <note>The "fifth essence" or purest part of a substance in alchemy.</note>, the magistery, and other chemical things. Page 19: *Stars are the bonds of things lurking in the invisible Treasuries of the elements; the spirit of the world diffused through all things is all the stars,* he says on page 16. To Severinus, Hartmann, and similar fantastical philosophers, those "Elements" in which the "Treasuries" are said to be are empty places—and yet they are fortified with a powerful force. For Hartmann, the spirit of the world is ethereal, flowing out chiefly from the sun of the macrocosm and received by the moon, from where it is sent into sublunary things. The sun is called the father of those being born, the moon the mother, etc., so that you may see the dreams of fools. There is absolutely nothing determined in this <term>Gabalistical magic</term> <note>A disparaging reference to Kabbalah, often used by critics to label occult philosophy as "confused" or "Jewish magic."</note>. Therefore, it is a science of a false name; and just as God is the author of order, so the adversary of God, the devil, is the author of confusion; as the former is of truth, so the latter is of lies. From this confused magic, you recognize its author: the Devil. What follows is of this same dreg: *Imagination retains the same course, nature, and power as heaven.* For it is a foul fabrication, whether you mean the internal heaven (which is said to be all stars and the spirit of the world diffused through all things) or the macrocosmic firmament. It is for us to dispute regarding the magical or Diabolical heaven. We gladly leave it behind. Since imagination occurs within men, it must be the internal heaven that is understood. But because this is said to be diffused through all things, how can imagination simultaneously be a spirit diffused through all things? And if it is diffused through all things, it will not have the same course, nature, and power as the external heaven, whose manner we know to be ordered, proceeding from an internal virtue and a regular nature, and not at all confused. One might concede, as if by accident, that imagination can produce many actions, if you speak of the true kind; but if you speak of that magical fiction, you should laugh. For that which does not exist can produce nothing. 

But it is not enough to have raved in Physics: the physical delirium must be augmented by theological impiety. A little before, it was said on Croll’s authority that *the stars are essences which the Chymist extracts from mixtures; in another way, they are the internal man, who is called the sidereal spirit; they are the spirit of the world; they are the spirit and invisible body of all things; they are imagination, which is known to forge more false things than true, and belongs as much to the evil as to the good.* Now we learn they are the "created wisdom of God"—that is, the power of angels; they are *the incarnate word of God; they are that by which, once known, God becomes known, as if God, Imagination, the power of Angels, and the rest were one and the same.* You will hardly find such blasphemy in any other writer. If the stars are the powers of angels, they will be nothing essential. How then can they be the sidereal body of man? <margin>Whether stars are the powers of angels. Whether angels are the created wisdom of God.</margin> If angels are the created wisdom of God, they are not essential spirits, but a certain "accident" of a rational creature, just as when God grants wisdom to Solomon. But if they were wisdom by their very essence, they would be God. For there is no essential wisdom except the Son of God, through whom all things were founded. Indeed, even the Devils would have been essential wisdom, they who nevertheless, having shaken off wisdom, plunged themselves into madness, so that in them was found only folly. If you wish to believe it, the "Gabalistical" stars of Paracelsus and Croll are the Devil himself, who through his prophets lies in wait under the honorary title of the Son of God. 

It is also false that he who has known God has also known the magical stars of Paracelsus. In the sacred scriptures, the knowledge of God is revealed through His Son; but there is nothing of the stars of Paracelsus there, nor anything through the stars. Much less true is that "gradation," namely, that *the knowledge of the stars gives birth to the knowledge of God, and makes the macrocosm of God known, from which the microcosm becomes known.* <margin>An impious gradation. Paracelsian magic and imagination is the ignorance of all things.</margin> Rather, the ignorance of all things follows from it. *For if you do not know the stars, you know neither God, nor the great world, nor the small world. But you do not know the stars.* The reason is that they are described so variously and confusedly that you cannot know what they are; but even if a consistent description were given, there would be no science of them. For there is no science of a non-existent thing. It follows, therefore, that Paracelsian magic is the ignorance of all things, and that "imagination" signifies the same. Furthermore, to say that the medicinal stars are *the word of God incarnate, which Paracelsus found in creatures, is both insane in its words and impious in reality, and insulting to the incarnation of Christ.* <margin>To Paracelsus, there is a twofold incarnate word of God. Page 46 of Croll.</margin> For he speaks thus: *Paracelsus wrote of the secrets of nature and the Miracles of God—that is, regarding the word of God incarnate to be found in creatures, which is medicine and the staff of our life. For through this word "FIAT," the seed of the whole world, heaven and earth were created, and that word is effective in all creatures, and to it the creatures are rightly subject as if to their own souls, etc.*

He asserts that Paracelsian magic leads to the ignorance of all things by conflating spiritual, physical, and divine categories.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of Oswald Croll’s "Basilica Chymica." In the preceding section, the author argued that Croll's definitions of the "internal man" and the "stars" were logically inconsistent. Here, he escalates his attack by labeling Croll’s mystical chemistry as outright blasphemy and demonic sorcery, specifically targeting Croll’s use of scripture and his concept of "Imagination."</meta>

### On the Horrible Impiety of Paracelsian Magic

We know from the Gospel how *John* writes that the Word was in the beginning, and all things were created through Him, and that He was God, who afterwards was made flesh. *Crollius*, speaking of Paracelsian medicine and magical healings through characters and stones—in which an <term>astral conjunction of superior and inferior things</term> <note>A core Hermetic concept: "as above, so below," suggesting that earthly materials can be linked to heavenly powers.</note> takes place—impiously abuses the most holy scriptures. Since the same Word cannot be both the Creator and a medicinal virtue within creatures, it follows that a "double incarnate word" is blasphemously established—to say nothing of the other impieties included in those same words. Where is it written that the Word, or the incarnate Son of God, is the word *Fiat* <note>Latin: "Let it be done." The author argues Croll confuses Christ (the Word) with the command used during Creation.</note>? Indeed, by that word is signified the departure from the temple of the Holy Trinity to create things; it is the edict of the powerful will of the Son of God. But it is not the Son of God himself, unless you say that He departed out of Himself. It is also explained otherwise: namely, that wherever it is placed, it is a mark of the decree of the entire Trinity. The Son later executed what is indicated by the words immediately following: "and there was light," and "God made the firmament," etc. Therefore, in no way can this [*Fiat*] be the Son of God. This fabrication is derived from the discipline of Hermes and the Platonic Magi, who likewise chatter vainly about the Son of God. *Heaven and Earth are parts of the world, not the seed of the world.*

<margin>Note what kind of imagination Croll attributes to man.</margin>
Regarding point XIV: The various names for imagination do not require refutation. For they confuse and refute both themselves and the whole of *Crollius’s* magic. How foolish it is that "imagination" should be the "body" of man? If it is a body, how can it be a <term>Genius</term> <note>In the classical sense, a "genius" was a guardian spirit or an innate driving force of a person's nature.</note>? The *Heathens* call the Genius the "God of human nature" or a certain innate power; others call it a man’s guardian angel. The Magi call it a "companion spirit," such as Socrates had, and such as *Crollius* presently calls the "familiar homunculus" of the wise (that is, of the Magi), a "Specter," or a "prophesying and prophetic Euestrum." This is surely a Pythonic spirit, like the one possessed by the girl at Philippi in Acts 16:16-17. Gather from this, therefore, what kind of thing this Paracelsian magic is, and what kind of "imagination" *Crollius* commends: namely, the Python Devil, a familiar spirit, such as Faustus, Trithemius, Agrippa, Simon Magus, etc., possessed. This is not a good genius, but a most wicked impostor, Satan, the ancient Serpent who seduces the whole world, the Prince of this world, the spirit of darkness, the prince of the power of the air, who is powerful in the sons of unbelief. He especially works through magi and, feigning to be an angel of light, deceives them remarkably—just as the dog deceived *Cornelius Agrippa* <note>A legend claimed Agrippa's black dog was actually a demon.</note>. That fictitious and spiritual "inner man" is called <term>Adech</term> by the Paracelsians. Since *Crollius* also calls this the "stars" and "imagination," he is consistent with himself. However, it differs somewhat from the <term>Euestrum</term>. For *Adech* is the "inner man," that is, the thinking intellect (if you wish to speak in Aristotelian terms) that proposes the "Ideas" of those things which a man wishes to accomplish externally—just as if someone, about to make a saw, first forms the image of it within himself through his skillful intellect. The *Euestrum*, however, is named the "Prophetic Spirit," or an interpreter or diviner who foretells something from a given sign. *Crollius* mixes everything together and confuses it into one Chaos. Thus, both the *household Lar* (that is, a domestic and familiar demon) and the *Penates* [household gods] are found in his "imagination." <margin>The Light of Nature.</margin> He ridiculously calls this the *Light of Nature*. How can that demonic and Satanic darkness be "light"? There are two lights for *Crollius*: one of the grace of the Holy Spirit; the other of nature. Since this latter light is the *Adech*, the *Euestrum*, the *Genius*, the *familiar homunculus*, and the "shadowy man of the invisible body" (for it is called by these names), what else can it be but the prince of darkness and an evil spirit? On page 14: *The Light of Nature is nothing other than a certain divine analogy of the visible world with the microcosmic body.* Is this, then, the shadowy man and the genius of darkness? On page 25: *The Light of Nature is that which naturally flows all things into man.* This description makes the "imagining man" a disciple of the Devil, as one who learns all things from a familiar homunculus, Euestrum, specters, etc. Look at yourself, *Crollius*, how you have betrayed yourself and your Magic like a thief.

Johann Hartmann, in Hermetic Disputation 11: "Nature, the Green Lion. Incombustible sulfur, the fire of wisdom, the living fire of nature, the fire of Hippocrates that moves all things, <unclear>the ever-living fire</unclear> <note>original: "πυρ αειζωον"</note> of Orpheus, the predestination of Paracelsus; the ethereal spirit in a more-than-glorified body, the fire, light, and divinity of nature, etc." He confuses all these things into one Chaos without any proof. In his diagram of the world, in the first empty circle, *Crollius* inscribed: "The finite good of the Light of Nature." Let some Paracelsian reconcile these statements: *Imagination is all the stars, and it is moved like the heaven; Imagination is the internal body of man; Imagination is a genius; It is the shadow of the visible body, it is a specter, etc.*

<margin>Imagination is a substance: body and not a body. Magical works.</margin>
Regarding point XV: Imagination is now said to be incorporeal; previously, it was the invisible body of man, though simultaneously the "dream of a shadow." It is "exalted"—that is, intensified—which is a quality denied to substances. Yet it must be a substance if it is a spirit, and a man, and a body, and a genius. When it is intensified, it must be joined with "miraculous faith." What happens? *Magic performs all things.* But these are magical works such as those of *Trithemius*, who represented his deceased wife to *Maximilian I* as if she were alive (no differently than the Witch of Endor summoned Samuel); or those of that man who produced a vine with grapes from a table, which, when the guests tried to cut them with knives, nearly everyone cut off his own nose; or another, who made a lily arise from a table, and after it was cut, he deprived a lesser magus of his head because he was hindering him from restoring the head taken from a servant; or the one who claimed he could know all things happening in the most remote places within 24 hours; or the one who provided oysters in the shortest time at the wrong time of year and place; or *Trithemius*, who, finding an inn poorly supplied, knocked on the window and said: *"Bring it!" And immediately a flask of good wine was present, with a side dish full of cooked fish;* or Paracelsus, who taught that all things could be known through three mirrors; or *Crollius*, who suddenly heals all diseases through characters, <term>Gamahaeas</term> <note>Stones naturally marked with images or patterns, believed to have magical properties.</note>, words, and other diabolical things.

This "imagination" of man—that is, the genius, familiar spirit, Python, etc.—works when a man intensifies his "natural faith," which is to say, when he firmly believes that the Devil will do those things which he imagines. (For as *Bodin* indicates, Magi want to be believed in, just as the Devil does; and as *Lercheimer* is the author, *Trithemius* wanted to stop a thief for a certain Frenchman, if the man believed he could do it.) This is the source of all operations...

He lists various legendary feats of magicians like Trithemius and Paracelsus to illustrate that their "miracles" are actually the work of familiar spirits and the Devil.</summary>
<keywords>Oswald Croll, Paracelsus, Blasphemy, Imagination, Adech, Euestrum, Genius, Light of Nature, Johannes Trithemius, Cornelius Agrippa, Demonic Magic, Pythonic Spirits</keywords>

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s systematic rebuttal of Oswald Croll’s "Basilica Chymica." Having previously attacked Croll's Christology, the author now focuses on the Paracelsian concept of "Imagination"—the idea that the human mind can physically manifest reality—labeling it a tool of the Devil rather than a natural or divine power.</meta>

->According to Croll’s opinion.<-

...power of the magicians having the capacity to beget visible bodies: Rather, they are specters, deceptive images, and illusions of bodies, as history teaches us. Otherwise, no created imagination can produce bodies or essences. It can do even more. "What more, I ask?" <margin>The most impudent lie of the magician: that whether present or absent, he can do all things.</margin> Whether present or absent, it can perform all operations, even those which surpass the grasp of human reason. You lie impudently, Satan! For to no created power has this been granted. It is indeed beyond the grasp of human reason, those things which the Devil effects when solicited by Magicians; for example: *we do not understand how a Bohemian magician was able to pull a baker's entire large foot out of his hip; how <term>Trithemius</term>, <term>Scotus</term>, and others could exhibit a feast in a moment at a cold hearth of an inn; or how in a single hour the same Abbot [Trithemius] taught a certain prince the Latin language, and transformed himself into every appearance of things, etc.* <note>Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516) and Michael Scot (c. 1175–1232) were famous scholars often accused of practicing "black magic" or "demonic arts" due to their immense learning.</note> But if these things exist beyond human grasp from an external principle and the power of an evil spirit by divine permission, they do not happen naturally, nor are they works of faith or natural imagination. The highest faculty in man is intellect and reason. *Natural faith and imagination* are subordinated to the intellect, and imagination is quite material in respect to the intellect. Nothing supernatural can proceed from natural causes. It is evident, therefore, that this Crollian imagination is nothing other than the procurement of the Devil, who accommodates himself for a time to the will of the Magician, until he snatches him away with him into hell. The manner of this demonic production is partly foolish and partly impious. *If the imagination of the Magician produces all things, just as clouds exist in a clear sky from settling vapors, or as clouds are brought in from elsewhere, it does not actually beget visible bodies.* For that production is the coagulation of pre-existing bodies there, a manifestation from the unmanifest and the indeterminate, a visible termination, a bringing-in. Neither, however, is the work of imagination, but—as far as Magic is concerned—it is the work of an evil spirit abusing nature by divine permission. *But if the Magician wants it to be done just as God founded the world out of nothing: he is impious toward the Creator*, whose glory he transfers to the Devil and the Magician. In the history of *Elijah*, we read that by praying he produced a drought of three years and six months, and again brought clouds of rain rising from the sea. Clouds rushed into a clear sky <margin>3 Kings 17:1; Ecclesiasticus 48:2; James 5:16.</margin> *not made out of nothing, but brought in by divine power*, by which at the same time the air was condensed, the vapors and exhalations presenting themselves again. In this way also the general flood arose. God did not wish to produce anything out of nothing after the rest of the first six days. When things become visible from the inconspicuous through <term>emphasis</term> <note>original: "Emphasin," here meaning a sudden appearance or manifestation.</note> or <term>systasis</term> <note>original: "Systasin," a Greek-derived term for a gathering or "setting together" of matter.</note>, they occur either by a natural principle (of which the Physicists speak) or a divine one (of which the Theologians speak). Job 26:8 and chapter 18:26; Psalm 146:8. The Devil, through his witches and magicians by God's permission—since he is the prince of the air (Ephesians 2:2)—likewise moves nature so that storms, tempests, hail, etc., may happen. It is an "excellent" praise of Paracelsian Magic if it can do the same as an evil spirit according to the will, or imagination and faith, of hags.

The <margin>The triple nothingness.</margin> **Triple Nothingness**—Divine, negative, and privative—turns Croll’s wisdom into foolishness. For what is called the *Divine Nothingness* <note>original: "Nihilum Deale." Croll used this term to describe the state of God before creation, which the author here critiques.</note>, which indeed preceded every creature—what else is it than the negative of all nature? For it denies all created Beings. Elsewhere the same Magician says that *God produced all things out of darkness?* St. Paul writes in 2 Corinthians 4:6 that God commanded the light to shine out of darkness <note>original: "ἐκ σκότους φῶς λάμψαι"</note>, which darkness in Genesis is said to have been over the face of the abyss, and to which a place hidden from man is attributed in Job 38:19, and which is called a creature of God in Isaiah 45:7. If darkness was created, it did not exist before the beginning of creatures, nor can it be matter. If they are that "Divine Nothing," as the Magician says, they are the privation of light, because this at least followed after them, by that motion which is from one contrary to another. *But the first "nothing" does not only deny light, but all created things in the universe.*

To the 16th point: *That the plague is effected by the imagining astral spirit of man* has no more truth than the rest of the lies. The Paracelsians confuse themselves by their own variety. For now it is said to arise from the *menstruum of women*, now from the *Basilisk of the heavens*, and other monsters about which no more need be said. It is an inept way of speaking to say that *man is imagination itself*, and at the same time something different from imagination. For the imagining man is one thing; his imagination is another. If this plague is produced, and it is the same as a Spirit, a Specter, an <term>Euestrum</term>, or an <term>Adech</term> <note>Paracelsian technical terms: "Euestrum" refers to a prophetic or astral body; "Adech" refers to an inner creative spirit or "inner man."</note>, the plague will be demonic, concocted by Satanic Magicians through the Devil; and indeed God punishes the sins of men by the permission granted to evil spirits. He does not, however, always work through those ministers. For in the plague of David and the slaughter of the Assyrians, he is called the Angel of the Lord, as in Psalm 34:8. They are not evil angels who, in Psalm 103:20, are said to be mighty in strength, doing the word of the Lord; they were not evil angels who overthrew Sodom, having first been the guests of Abraham, where also the Lord rained from the Lord: see Genesis 19:1, 15, 22, 24; nor was it an evil angel in Exodus 14:19. Through imagination—whether magical or some other human kind—God never sent a plague. <margin>The origin of the plague.</margin> <margin>Evil eyes/Witchcraft.</margin> It does sometimes happen that a disease is induced in one's own body by imagining it, but not without some underlying affection and disposition. In this way, mothers can deform the fetuses connected to them. When horses, lambs, tender children, etc., are <term>bewitched</term> <note>original: "fascinantur," referring to the "Evil Eye" or fascination.</note>, it happens by the impression of poisonous spirits and vapors into vulnerable bodies by means of the spirits of the same. A strong spirit easily infects a weak spirit, especially where it is open, such as in the eyes. These spirits can be directed by focused thought. But for these things, no Magic is necessary, nor is imagination sufficient. Otherwise, who I ask would be safe from evil men? Imagination—or rather thought, confidence, and the affection of a sick person—can likewise help him toward health without magic. All these things, however, happen in particular cases and not in isolation. For many things must concur so that the effect follows. Therefore, those magicians are laughed at who conclude a universal rule from a rare, accidental, and particular occurrence, as if that nature were produced through imagination. Croll brings forward the *histories of the Sylvanian Panacea and the spring of Meissen*. He would have done well to join to them the *miracles of Paracelsus’s cures* at the beginning. Just as those later vanished, and as the glory of that other spring by the river Jagst; so also the efficacy of Paracelsian Medicine has dissipated. If you asked a Theologian about the cause, he would answer: not miraculous faith, which is a singular gift of the Holy Spirit conferred upon men, but some hidden operation of such spirits.

<summary>The author critiques the Paracelsian idea that human imagination can physically create matter or cause plagues, arguing these are either demonic illusions or natural phenomena wrongly attributed to magic. He further attacks Croll's metaphysical "Divine Nothingness," contrasting it with a traditional biblical understanding of creation and divine providence.</summary>
<keywords>Imagination, magic, Paracelsus, Oswald Croll, demons, Trithemius, plague, Nihilum Deale, creation, Elijah, fascination</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s systematic rebuttal of Oswald Croll’s "Basilica Chymica." Having previously attacked Croll's Christology, the author now focuses on the Paracelsian concept of "Imagination"—the idea that the human mind can physically manifest reality—labeling it a tool of the Devil rather than a natural or divine power.</meta>

# On the Horrible Impiety of Paracelsian Magic

...of such people, as they are, who with wondrous cleverness lead man away from trust in God toward created things. The reason is that it is well-known that the same things happen at the statues of the superstitious, and formerly happened at the idols of the Pagans—the worship of demons and the like.

<margin>The Paracelsians, attributing much to their own faith and imagination, can perform nothing without the help of an evil spirit.</margin>
Regarding what Christ says—that all things are possible to the believer—this is by no means magical, nor a work of human fantasy, as experience also proves. If **Crollius** <note>Oswald Croll (c. 1563–1609), a disciple of Paracelsus and author of the *Basilica Chymica*.</note> had been able to cure diseases by his fantasy alone, he would have proposed chemical remedies in vain. If he had been unwilling to do so [despite having the power], he would have violated the law of love toward his neighbor. It remains, therefore, that this "magical healing" is a mere fiction. Some are healed by chance, through a certain simplicity of belief that settles the disease-causing spirits and strengthens the restorers of health; like those women who took the drying powder of ink in the Scriptures <note>original: "atramenti in Scripturis." This likely refers to the folk-practice of scraping ink from holy books to use as medicine.</note> against various evils, thinking it was powder made from crab-stones <note>term: <term>crab-stones</term> (oculi cancrorum), which were calcified stones found in the stomach of crayfish, used as an antacid in early modern medicine.</note>, etc. But what does this have to do with the diabolical imagination of **Crollius**? Why did **Trithemius**, **Agrippa**, **Faustus**, and other mages not use these healing arts, but rather incantatory and conjuring magic which they share with witches?

<margin>The mortal spirits of the elements. See Cardanus, Book 16, *On Variety*, concerning Psellus's demons divided into the elements.</margin>
To point XVII: Just as the stories **Paracelsus** babbles about mortal spirits said to be enclosed in the elements are fables, so too are his claims about their imagination. Earthly, watery, airy, and fiery demons do not harm by "imagining" when they do harm, nor are they even able to do so. Furthermore, by what argument can it be proven that spirits have a "phantastic" faculty <note>The power of forming mental images.</note>, which does not exist without a body? For that "Crollian" imagination—which is called the "ethereal body," the "stars," the "shadowy man" <note>original: "homo umbraticus"</note>, and the rest—is not an inorganic power of souls, angels, and demons; indeed, it is not even the same as the organic faculty of a whole human being. For that faculty by which spirits grasp images of things is laid open, and does not need the mediation of external senses or phantastic shaping. Imagination is, in a way, material. Incorporeal spirits are "naked forms." To say that the power of elemental demons is bound by human reason is a foolish utterance. Why did the perfect reason of **Adam** and **Eve** not bind the imagination of **Satan** when they were deceived by him? By what principle are these things demonstrated? By the foolish opinion of Paracelsus.

<margin>Private plague.</margin>
To point XVIII: Previously we heard of three separate pillars of Kabbalistic Magic: *prayer, faith, and imagination*; now faith and imagination make one pillar, <note>original Greek: "to einai," meaning "being."</note> "being." For imagination is said to be faith. The prayer of a delirious Mage is itself delirious. Regarding the plague, much has been said before. Here a discourse is established concerning one's "private and personal" plague, which lacks both head and tail [logic]. For how do you prove that a "private plague" occurs when a fearful man creates one for himself by imagining a Basilisk? What is this Basilisk? It is believed to be a most venomous serpent, yet it is full of poetic and magical fictions. Who has ever seen such a thing as it is described?

<margin>The pestilential Basilisk.</margin>
<margin>Paracelsus, Treatise 2 on the Plague, volume 3, page 73.</margin>
<margin>The impiety of Croll, robbing God of His honor.</margin>
*General plague* is foolishly imagined to exist solely from the *Euestrum* or Prophetic spirit, or even from the *Iliaster* <note>Technical terms in Paracelsian philosophy for the vital or stellar spirit of a thing.</note>, whose dull "chirping" <note>original Greek: "teretismata"</note> you find in **Dornæus's** Paracelsian Dictionary. Is it truly so? By what argument do you prove that the sun has an *Euestrum* which can be moved to anger because of the sins of men? Is this not a suggestion of the devil, who by this cleverness leads man from the Creator to created things, so that we do not think God is angry at sins, but rather a fictitious spirit of the sun? What remains except that we worship the sun and reconcile ourselves to it? By what reasoning do you teach that this spirit of the sun participates with the spirit of men? How do you teach that it infects through the influence of the stars? If the participation and influence are natural, how could it produce a supernatural plague? *Nature is the cause of things natural in themselves.* If no medicine can resist such a plague, let the Paracelsians stop boasting of fixed astral remedies made from minerals, such as **Scheunemann** <note>Henning Scheunemann (c. 1570–1615), a Paracelsian physician and chemist.</note> recently did. This will be the ninth cause of incurable diseases according to the Paracelsians, which will soon shrink their Panaceas and "Universals" [universal cures] into something very brief.

<margin>Incurable plague.</margin>
<margin>The pretext of magical transportations.</margin>
To point XIX: If, while the sidereal spirit is dreaming and imagining something to itself, the body is simultaneously dragged toward that which it fancies in its dreaming and imagining, how often would Magicians be forced to change their beds and be in many places in a single night? Surely this is how witches dream when they are carried away? Thus did **Trithemius** dream, of whom they say he celebrated three masses in places not slightly distant from each other in a single night. Is it not clear by this argument also that **Crollian** Magic is a *pretext for the Diabolical*? **Giambattista della Porta** writes, and **Bodin** reports in Book 2 of *On Demons*, chapter 5: *that a certain witch, having collapsed from an ointment, was dreaming and jumping about; but her body always remained in the same place.*

<margin>Sleepwalkers.</margin>
The same is confirmed by many examples, nor is this "bodily transportation" believed by **Lercheimer** and several others, although it may happen to some. Let **Crollius** then tell his stories to little boys playing "odds or evens" and riding on long reeds. As for sleepwalkers, the example is very different. For they, being half-asleep, are indeed struck by imaginations, but the pursuit is of a stronger power that presides over the locomotive faculty, emotion, and anxiety, etc. In Acts chapter 12, Peter, cast into prison, sleeps between two soldiers. An angel of the Lord is present and leads him out, while he does not know that he is truly progressing as a free man. For he thought he was seeing a vision (<note>original Greek: "edokei horama blepein"</note>). But the angel was truly leading him. The movement was not involuntary, for he followed the leader of his own accord, and moved his body by the animal spirit, nerves, muscles, and the instruments of walking. Furthermore, imagination did not do these things alone; indeed, he imagined to himself that he was seeing an empty vision without substance.

What if an evil spirit leads some, like that man who descended into a well at night? Furthermore, imagination does not reason. Sleepwalkers do reason and use their minds, though imperfectly and cloudily. For they do not think about danger and other circumstances. Therefore, they are often seen to achieve things impossible for those who are awake—like the man who raised himself by a rope at night to the roof of a house, and there, having captured a magpie's nest, brought the chicks back with him into bed. This seemed horrible to him when awake and considering what he had done. Sleepwalkers cannot do those difficult things, nor do they dare to attempt them when awake. But Mages and witches fly out while quite awake, if indeed they are transported bodily—and that not by the force of imagination (by which they are carried nowhere), but by the force of an unclean spirit.

However, bodily transportations are not to be denied, although they do not occur in all cases. For it is only too well known whom...

<summary>In this passage, the author continues his attack on Paracelsian magic, specifically debunking the idea that human imagination has the power to heal or transport the body. He argues that such phenomena are either natural accidents (like sleepwalking) or the work of demons, rather than the divine or natural powers claimed by Oswald Croll and Paracelsus.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, Oswald Croll, imagination, magic, witchcraft, sleepwalking, Basilisk, plague, demons, Trithemius, Agrippa, Faustus, Giambattista della Porta, Jean Bodin</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of Oswald Croll’s "Basilica Chymica," specifically refuting the Paracelsian claim that human imagination and "starry spirits" possess the power to transport bodies, perform magic, or shape the fetus in the womb.</meta>

### ...according to Croll’s opinion.

<margin>The power by which a possessed person is carried.</margin>
...that certain people have been snatched up and carried away by the devil. Thus *Simon Magus* <note>A biblical figure (Acts 8) often portrayed in later legends as a sorcerer who could fly.</note> flew, and so did *Trithemius* <note>Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516), an abbot and famous occultist.</note>. Another Magus finished a ten-day journey in a single day. I heard of a possessed man who, after being healed, affirmed that he felt as light as if he were the wind or a feather. With that lightness, he covered great distances very quickly, even over water.

Behold here "imagination" supposedly moving physical bodies! If that were true, magicians would care nothing for prisons. *The father of Cardanus* <note>Fazio Cardano, father of the famous polymath Girolamo Cardano (1501–1576).</note>, as a Magus, suffered physical disturbances and other things through a "familiar spirit" <note>A supernatural entity or demon believed to assist a magician.</note>, not through imagination. <margin>Cardanus, *On Variety*, book 16, page 1072.</margin>

**Regarding Point 20.** Prayer, imagination, and faith were said to be the different pillars of Magic; yet a little earlier, faith and imagination were called the same thing. Now faith is said to be the *cause* of imagination. To what end do these foul ideas tend? Who is it that transfers and carries Magicians over long distances in a short time? A "Star" <note>original: *Sydus*.</note>. (I understand this to be not that star which is in the external firmament, but the "starry spirit" <note>term: <term>starry spirit</term> (*spiritus sydereus*), a Paracelsian concept of an astral body or subtle essence within man that connects him to the heavens.</note> which is attached to man.) <margin>The marvelous fickleness of Croll in his assertions: when the devil transports magicians, it is called a "star" doing it.</margin> What excites this spirit? Faith. Believe, therefore, O Magician, that you will immediately be with your body in the court of the King of France celebrating a wedding—as they record regarding a certain *Faust* <note>The legendary German doctor who made a pact with the devil.</note>, and as is certain regarding witches creeping into wine cellars—and your imagination will excite a demonic spirit in you, and it will carry you wherever you wish. I believe you in this, even without an oath! For because your imagination is one and the same with your familiar spirit, phantom, or genius, whatever your imagination fashions, the familiar spirit will execute. It will lead you through the very vestibule of hell—where *Paracelsus* disputed with *Avicenna* <note>The great Persian physician (980–1037).</note>—down to the very bottom of the underworld. <margin>Paracelsus was supposedly carried to the vestibule of hell; he even wrote that he received letters from hell sent by Galen. It is a wonder if Galen wrote that there is someone among men by whom he is torn apart; it is a wonder also by what courier he sent the letters, etc. See volume 2, page 11, and volume 6, page 399.</margin>

**Regarding Point 21.** It is a charming piece of nonsense to claim that *imagination, stirred by affection and appetite, moves the stars and causes them to imprint a mark upon an infant—just as those who pound barley-water in a mortar, or those who shape something by striking a sieve-like instrument, or as a potter shapes his clay*. Where now is that "excess of great desire" moved by imagination, by which a mother supposedly brings health or disease to her offspring? It is not enough to seek an example from art; there is a need for a suitable and fitting one. One must demonstrate by some self-evident principle that it happens in a mother exactly as it does in the work of a potter, or in the formation of armor for a brave hero by the frequent striking of Vulcan's hammer, as seen in Homer and Virgil.

Why do you not say it is formed as a stone statue is by a sculptor? Mothers are often vexed by imaginations—false, true, or ambiguous—and sometimes not without stubborn distress. But the fetus does not always receive a mark. We attribute more to a strong affection (especially sudden terror) and to strong longing or desire—sometimes to fear and anxious worry—which stir the spirit <note>In early medicine, "spirits" were subtle fluids that carried life and sensation through the body.</note> to make an impression. We also attribute it to fixed thought, through which the spirit, moisture, and heat are directed toward that part of the body which the mother touches during her emotional state. In beasts, the "estimative power" <note>term: <term>estimative power</term> (*aestimatiua*), the animal instinct to perceive helpful or harmful qualities.</note> has a similar effect. There is no reciprocal "ascending and descending" of fictitious stars; rather, the spirit, along with heat and moisture in an affection stirred by the senses, moves the "plastic spirit" <note>term: <term>plastic spirit</term> (*spiritus plasticus*), the formative force believed to organize matter into living shapes.</note> with a single impulse. This changes the submissive and easily-shaped matter according to the conceived mental image. *Monsters are made this way*, which *Paracelsus* writes elsewhere are the works of "apprentices" wandering astray in the "Microcosmic Forge" <note>original: *Vulcania microcosmica*.</note> of the human heaven.

Indeed, it must be confessed that there are many things in this matter which human skill and the edge of the mind—greatly dulled in this life—cannot fully explore. We barely grasp a spark of God's works (Job 26, 1 Corinthians 13:9). Therefore, Scripture usually speaks of the formation of man in a way that grants little to our science (Job 10:8–11; Job 31:15; Psalm 139). It is a kind of rashness to assert those things about "fabricating stars" ascending and descending, and turning themselves in a circle like the heavens within the "lesser world" <note>The human body as a "microcosm" of the universe.</note>, and thus forming the fetus in the womb. If the stars are that "starry spirit," the internal man, the genius, the phantom, or the imagination, what will be the manner of its ascent and descent when a man is born? If this movement is natural to it, what is it like when something happens contrary to nature? How does it mark the surface of the visible elemental body, and not rather an "inner man" like itself? Or by what principle are all these things known and can be certainly asserted? Are we led by fictions? Art is an external principle of a work. Nature’s power is indeed declared through an analogy or likeness to art, but meanwhile, nature has its own peculiarities which are not the same. Job compares his own formation to the making of pottery and cheese. These are matters of art, just as our origin is compared to the work of a sculptor. If someone wants to conclude from this that a similar "craftsman" hides within human seed, fabricating individual limbs from the flowing matter according to the principles of his science and the idea he conceived, he has explained natural propagation and generation in a way that is not natural at all. He would be speaking of it as if it were a house, which an architect builds and leaves for an inhabitant to dwell in. But what would be the method of generation according to the soul? Adam begat a son in his own image and likeness, which is most natural in perfect things where nothing happens by chance. The method by which a male procreates in a female, and she within herself, is inexplicable. There, it would be necessary to imagine two imaginations, two Vulcans, two starry mannikins, *Adech*, *Euestra* <note>Technical Paracelsian terms for the internal "architect" and the "ethereal image" of a person.</note>, phantoms, and whatever other monsters Croll confuses with the "imagination"—which does not exceed its seat in the primary sensory organ, nor descend into the womb. <margin>Job; Romans 9:21.</margin>

How Cardanus leads and directs souls into bodies through demons, you have on page 1075 of his *On Variety*. Regarding Scaliger's opinion, we shall speak in the "Vital Philosophy" of Hartmann. But let these things suffice.

**Regarding Point 22.** *On the production of miracles*, much has already been said. In sacred matters, the *Word of the Lord* <margin>Miracles.</margin> confirms true miracles, and they are performed by the Holy Spirit through his ministers and worshippers—unless human unbelief hinders it. The "imagination" of the one through whom God works wonders is not always necessary, as is indicated by the story of the woman with the hemorrhage and by Peter in Acts 3:12, where it was not a matter of imagining...

<summary>The author refutes Croll's Paracelsian theories of "Magical Imagination," arguing that supernatural feats are the work of demons rather than the mind, and that fetal development is a biological/divine process rather than the result of internal "starry spirits" or maternal fantasies.</summary>

<keywords>Oswald Croll, Paracelsus, imagination, starry spirit, fetal development, birthmarks, Simon Magus, Trithemius, Girolamo Cardano, maternal impression, monsters, miracles</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's systematic dismantling of Oswald Croll’s "Basilica Chymica." Having previously argued against the physical power of "imagination," the author now refutes the idea that "natural faith" or "astral spirits" are the source of biblical miracles, instead attributing such wonders solely to divine power and the name of Christ.</meta>

# On the horrible impiety of Paracelsian Magic

...the Apostle attributes the miracle neither to his own power <note>original: "nandi," likely a fragment of "gubernandi" (governing) or "dominandi" (ruling) from the previous page</note> nor to any other virtue of his own, but to faith in the name of Christ. *By faith in his name, he has made this man strong, whom you see and know; and the faith which is through him has given him this perfect health in the presence of you all.* <note>Acts 3:16</note> What sort of "imagination" could there have been in Peter’s shadow, by which the sick were healed? <note>Acts 5:15</note> Or in Paul’s handkerchiefs and aprons? <note>Acts 19:12</note>

<margin>2 Kings 13:21 <note>The author uses the Vulgate numbering (4 Regum); in modern bibles, this is 2 Kings.</note></margin>
What imagination was in Elisha’s bones, or in the washing in the Jordan, about which even the Syrian <note>Naaman the Syrian</note> doubted? <note>2 Kings 5:11</note> It was the double spirit of Elijah resting upon Elisha that performed these works. And so, Elisha was mistaken when he thought God would be effective through his staff in raising the boy. <note>2 Kings 4:31</note> Elijah, however, used prayer, not imagination, in restoring life to the boy. <note>1 Kings 17:21-22</note> If these wonders were the result of "imagination" and "natural faith," they could have been hindered by their opposites. Since Simon Magus, Elymas, the Pythoness, and others—those magicians in the Book of Acts—could have, according to the opinion of Crollian Magic, intensified their imagination to the highest degree and thus rendered the apostolic miracles void, why did no such thing happen? Therefore, the claim that *imagination with natural faith can do all things* is a hollow fiction. Whether one considers the natural faith and imagination of the one who heals, or the one who is healed, or those standing by (as seen in the story of the paralytic), no true miracle occurs without divine faith and trust, nor without the power and will of God. That those "natural" factors must also be present is a claim that must be proven by stronger arguments than those of the Kabbalists. Many things happen by coincidence. So much for the third column or pillar of the Kabbalistic magic of Paracelsus.

---

-> S E C T I O N XII. <-

<margin>Isaiah 47:9 and following; chapter 19:3, 10, 11, 12, 13.</margin>
With all the pillars now collapsed, how can the building placed upon them stand? Where are your magical healings now, Croll? Where is your omnipotence? Your wisdom, with which you have polluted both divine and human things, has been turned into foolishness. Astronomy—which you said was tied only to the heavens and the visible stars by "unfortunate error" of Mathematicians or Physical Astrologers, claiming it should also be sought in other things of both the macrocosm and microcosm—has vanished. No other astronomy appears to exist than that true version of the ancients.

<margin>Magical stars.</margin>
Your "magical stars" are a figment of the most foolish Paracelsus, and in reality, they contain nothing but lies, since there is no internal, invisible, starry, corporeal man who is all the stars and can do all things. Yet the Paracelsians are not ashamed to found their medicine upon these trifles, deceiving both themselves and others.

<margin>The structure of Paracelsian medicine demolished, and the false monarchy destroyed.</margin>
If those things which a Chemist extracts from mixtures are to be called "stars"—namely, the essences of natural things analogous to the element of the stars, in which there are certain virtues for preserving and restoring health, as well as other hidden properties that Physicians attribute to the *entire nature and substance* of a thing—then we too admit that one pillar of Medicine is "Astrology." But this is an Astrology that is *metaphorical* <note>original: *parabolica*</note> rather than literal, since it otherwise belongs to Pharmacy and depends on the Physics of natural things, in which it yields the fruit of contemplative science. For the theoretical Physicist also examines the natures of things and provides the Physician with the material to fulfill his practice.

<margin>In medicine, many things happen by the power of hidden causes which cannot be specifically grasped by the rules of art.</margin>
However, because various things happen during healing—sometimes even the unskilled and reckless achieve something; sometimes emotion, thought, and desire have more power (for these are three faculties in man according to Plato, otherwise called the *vegetative*, the *sensing*—comprising the internal and external senses and locomotion—<gloss>the spirited part</gloss> <note>original: τὸ θυμικὸν (to thymikon)</note>, and the *rational*, to each of which adhere their own appetites or pursuits of good and flight from adversity) than the tools and works of the Physician; and sometimes something divine occurs in diseases, or by another principle with God’s permission—all of this cannot be grasped by scientific principles, and therefore it is not part of the essence of the art. Thus, there is no "science" of such things. For who, I ask, could grasp truly divine miracles through the scientific principles of medicine, when they belong to Theology? Who could grasp those diabolical magical acts, full of vanity, uncertainty, and impiety? Who, finally, could grasp even the accidental events, the certain reasoning of which is not open to man? This is a kind of confusion, not a methodical treatment of medicine, which Paracelsus gathered from every corner, pure and impure, mixing the Real and the magical with the medical entity, along with other foreign things which are ultimately found in practice to be alien to the essence of the art, even by the confession of the Paracelsians themselves. For how many are there, unless they be a Magician, who do not heal through natural medicines, if they heal at all?

I cannot wonder enough at what indulgence has seized men—otherwise not unlettered or foolish—that while they eagerly find, propose, and describe natural remedies (Chemical and non-Chemical, universal and particular) in great number, and do not neglect to investigate others; and while not only the old remedies from the school of the Arabs are at hand, but also the recent ones of Lully, Arnold, Ulstadius, Quercetanus <note>Joseph Duchesne (c. 1544–1609), a French physician.</note>, Croll, Penotus, Ruland, Beguin, and others in a long line; they still attribute so much to the Magic of Paracelsus. This magic is not only abhorrent to the ordinary laws of nature, but is also prohibited by God, by man, by the Church, by the magistrate, and by heads of families. The more one loves God and neighbor, and the more one is devoted to public honesty and common utility, the more he execrates and abominates these magical practices. They differ in no way from the works and opinions of necromancers and witches. Under the pretext of a certain "divine Philosophy" from the "light of nature" (as it seems to them) and grace, they dare to build out of our most corrupt and blind nature after the Fall things that were not even granted to nature in its integrity. They imagine everything is certain based on a most uncertain and deceptive imagination, led by their own opinions about things they can neither demonstrate with true principles nor complete in work without Satanic help. Indeed—what is admirable to the point of stupor—they bring PARACELSUS onto the stage as an authentic author...

<margin>They call the magical darkness of the prince of darkness the "light of nature."</margin>

<summary>The author continues his attack on Paracelsian medicine, arguing that miracles come from God rather than human imagination and dismissing the "starry" anatomy of man as a dangerous fiction. He rejects Paracelsus's "light of nature" as a demonic deception, contrasting it with traditional physics and chemical pharmacy.</summary>

<keywords>Paracelsus, Oswald Croll, imagination, miracles, faith, astrology, light of nature, chemical medicine, medical philosophy, Joseph Duchesne, Ramon Llull, Arnold of Villanova</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of vitalist or "living" philosophy, specifically targeting Paracelsian and Hermetic views on the soul and form. The author is contrasting these views with traditional Aristotelian logic regarding how living things are generated.</meta>

# Examination of Living Philosophy
<meta>catchword: species</meta>

It can exist neither in a common principle, nor in the parts depending on it. And just as the soul is entirely in every part because it is simple and indivisible, so too the soul, the center of the world, is the center everywhere. Have I touched upon the matter? But it stands in the way that you *fix that prior center into an unformed mass, and call it prime matter.* <note>The author is critiquing the opponent for treating the "soul" or "center" as a physical ingredient (prime matter) added to a shapeless lump.</note> The heart is not a raw mass, but the vital instrument and seat of the soul; nor can its vital faculty be the prime matter. You ought to enjoin your disciples to a Pythagorean silence of five years and bring back into use that phrase *he himself said it.* <note>original: "αὐτὸς ἔφα" (autos epha). This refers to the Pythagorean tradition where the master's word was absolute and students were forbidden from questioning him for five years.</note> Otherwise, they will laugh at you and your fictions.

<margin>Interior form.</margin>
5. What sort of thing is the interior form? Is it the *Vulcan of Paracelsus*, or the *mechanical archaeus* <note>The "Archaeus" in Paracelsian thought is a sort of internal "chemist" or "blacksmith" (Vulcan) that governs the functions of the body.</note>, who sits, stands, or lies (for it is uncertain) inside as if in an unformed, raw, and light-filled workshop (which they are accustomed to call the "limbus"), and because it contains all the causes of its own species, it fabricates its own body for itself using those causes? This is Severinus’s <note>Petrus Severinus (1542–1602), a Danish physician and follower of Paracelsus.</note> delusion: they do not perceive rightly who say that generation is a progression from non-being to Being. For those things which are made new daily were already hiding in the seed beforehand. Those things which seem to be corrupted do not hasten toward non-being, but return to their sources. (Prove this by immovable principles.) We say that the form of each thing exists *through itself* <note>original: "ἀνθ' ἑαυτὴν" (anth' heautēn)</note>, not that it approaches from the outside, nor that it is infused by a "giver of forms" after a suitable mixing of the Elements, nor that it is derived from the position of the stars; but rather that it hides in the seeds themselves, etc. How then is it *through itself*? Nothing generates itself; nor does it exist naturally by its own power. It must be from another, and indeed as a potentiality in the seeds where generation happens through seed. Is that so? Then the soul of Socrates would be older than his body, so that a thing exists before it actually *is*. Furthermore, if generation is a symbol and type of creation, and thus the world-soul fabricated its own body for itself just as the interior form fabricates its own: then God will not be the creator of the world in any other way than by making that soul. For this soul would have afterwards created its own body for itself within the *uncreated prime matter or chaos of Hermes, Plato, Democritus, Hesiod*, etc. Do you believe these monstrosities? Or rather, as happens in the generation of the Microcosm <note>The human being, viewed as a miniature version of the universe.</note> according to *Job and the Psalmist*, where the whole is born at once with God working providently, was the world also made first roughly, and afterwards elaborately? Just as a man tends from imperfection toward perfection, when previously nothing of him existed in act, but the whole was in the potentiality of the seed and the blood? For as it is to be generated here, although from pre-existing matter, so there it is to be created from nothing, or from matter of the most remote potentiality. If the world has some soul (suppose it for the sake of argument), it was made at the same time as the world itself; it did not exist prior and fabricate its own domicile for itself. You ought not just to say these things, but to prove them by some immovable principle. The *Archaeus and the mechanical Vulcan of Paracelsus* are the dreams of a madman. For a thing is not naturally generated in the same way an architect builds a house for himself or a swallow builds a nest—especially since the members of an animal or plant must be alive, or have within themselves the faculty to perform their own actions, with the soul presiding as the general principle and primary agent through common instruments. Or have you forgotten that life is not from art or an external principle, but from nature, which is an implanted and innate principle? Your "form fabricating a body for itself" is some kind of craftsman, not nature. Moreover, a craftsman who is not omnipotent cannot produce a work that is living by nature. Your *Pythagoras* performed wonders, but by no other means than those of *Simon Magus*, who attributed movement and speech to statues, or by which the river *Causus* addressed *Pythagoras* saying, *“Greetings, Pythagoras,”* <note>original: "Πυθαγόρα χαῖρε" (Pythagora chaire)</note> and that too with a *human-like* <note>original: "ἀνθρωπομορφικῶς" (anthropomorphikōs)</note> voice, such as you also seem to have heard in the courtyard.

<margin>A natural living thing cannot proceed from the art of a creature, nor from any external principle unless it be omnipotent.</margin>
<margin>The form, which is the cause, contains within itself all the causes of its species, that is, even the forms.</margin>
6. *Your form contains in itself all the causes of its own species.* I understand it this way: it is the nature of form to contain, and of matter to be contained. In order, therefore, for matter to become one, singular, final, and now sensible thing, there is need for the work of form. This, however, is not from an external mover which is not specific [to that thing], but from an internal one which, having received power from the generator, makes something like itself according to nature; this is the spirit and heat in the body of the offspring. That spirit, along with heat in the primogenial humor <note>The "first-born" fluid or essential moisture of life.</note>, is neither an "assistant" nor an "informing" form, but the instrument of the generator from which it flowed with the power of movement to produce something like itself. If, therefore, you wish to discourse naturally about things being born, and not represent to us some magical or mechanical craftsman, this is how you must do it. Here you find all the causes. There are External causes, universal and particular, of which you do not speak; and there are internal ones. For the nature of that spirit and heat in the inborn humor is the efficient cause of a thing similar to the parent. It is the instrument or medium of generation. The *Matter* is the body of the offspring which grows by the proportional fluid of the womb. The *Formal* cause in the seed is that by which it is seed: the reason for which is the potential to be like the generator. *For the seed holds the rationale of both the efficient and the material cause*, and by the fact that it is called the seed of this or that thing, it is simultaneously what it is. The *End* <note>The final cause or purpose.</note> is the act and perfection of that which is made, which did not exist before it was made. You, however, place the form and perfection of what was to be in the future as already in act before it exists, or while it is still in the process of becoming, and at the same time you profess that this same form is both the efficient and the final cause. It cannot be the matter. You will struggle to teach how a thing can both be in the process of being made (for a thing exists according to its form) and exist at the same time, and contain IN ITSELF all the causes.

<margin>Cause.</margin>
7. You expose yourself further when you say that it "contains the causes of its own species." We understand "species" universally. For it is a part of a genus. Certainly, the form of the parent possesses every power of producing its like. But it is not the future form of that which is being produced. *Generation is more a matter of the individual.* For "this" is produced from "this." Look into *Aristotle* if you have time; for I do not think you have thrown him away entirely. In *The Generation of Animals*, Book 4, Chapter 3, it says: *It generates both that which is particular and that which is a genus: but more so that which is particular. For this is the (primary) substance. For that which is generated, although it is a "such-and-such," is nevertheless a "this something," and that is the substance.* <note>original: "ἀλλὰ τόδε τι, καὶ τόδε ἡ οὐσία" (alla tode ti, kai tode hē ousia)</note> For this reason, from the faculties that are in the seeds of all such things: even the movements of ancestors are present potentially in the seed of the grandchildren; but that which is particular is always more proximate—I mean Coriscus and Socrates, etc. Coriscus is not only a Male, but also *such* a Male; nor is he only a male, but also a man. This is what the Philosopher [Aristotle] means when he says it generates both the genus (as a man generates a man) and the particular (the individual <note>original: "τὸ καθ' ἕκαστον" (to kath' hekaston)</note>), namely that an individual man generates an individual. He gives the reason: because the movements in the seeds of all such things are from the powers <note>original: "ἀπὸ δυνάμεων" (apo dynameōn)</note> through which the individual generates an individual—this man generates this one. By a more remote power, there is even a movement toward the grandchild in the seeds of the great-grandfathers or progenitors. From this, it is gathered that the traces of great-grandfathers are noticed in grandchildren. If your craftsman, or interior form, has in itself all the causes of generating its own species (its own species...

<summary>This page critiques the Paracelsian concept of the 'Archaeus' or internal craftsman, arguing instead for an Aristotelian model of generation where form and potentiality exist within the seed. The author emphasizes that life comes from an innate natural principle rather than an external or 'magical' architectural force.</summary>
